1. A Canyon Cut by a River

1Where to Find the Canyon. The Grand Canyon is in Arizona, in the United States. Phoenix is a city in that state. The Colorado River runs through the canyon. One side is the North Rim. The other side is the South Rim. This land is Grand Canyon National Park. Many people rank this canyon among the great natural wonders on Earth. Northern Arizona's high desert is the place where it lies. The canyon is so large that people can see it from space. There is a good reason for the name Grand Canyon. The place is dazzling.

2The word canyon means a valley with great depth. Its sides are steep. A river usually cuts a canyon. This national park covers about 1,900 square miles. In metric measure, that is 4,921 sq km. In some spots the depth passes 1 mile. That depth is 1.6 km. A trip through by river raft can take two weeks. Rocks in the walls are millions of years old. National park status came to this canyon in 1919. National parks are special places. The land in them is protected. The animals that live in them are protected too. Every year, millions of people visit this park. The first president to take steps to protect the Grand Canyon was Theodore Roosevelt. Look up. Giant birds called California condors live here. These birds came close to dying out. People caught them and kept them safe so they could breed. Hundreds of these birds now live in the wild. In the summer, many of them come to the Grand Canyon.

3How the Canyon Formed. Time and erosion shaped the Grand Canyon into the place we see today. Erosion is what happens when wind or water wears bits of ground away. The Colorado River flowed across this land for millions of years. It wore the earth down. In time, the river cut a canyon. The Colorado River still wears away soil, rock, and other materials. Every day, that wear makes the Grand Canyon deeper. The walls show stripes of different colors. Those stripes are layers of sediment. The layers piled up, one on another, over millions of years. Each layer marks a different time in the history of Earth. Nearly forty layers of rock make up these walls.

4Scientists study the rock formations in the canyon. Animal footprints from millions of years ago are part of the study. The layers tell about the past. From the layers, scientists learn the ways Earth changed as time passed. Nearly two billion years is the age given to the rocks at the bottom. People Who Came Here. For thousands of years, people have made this canyon their home. Native groups have hunted in this place. They have built homes here. They have grown food here. Several tribes still call this area home. Havasupai is one tribe. Say that name hah-vuh-SOO-py. Navajo is another tribe. Say that name NAH-vuh-hoh. Hualapai is another tribe. Say that name WAH-luh-py. Hopi is another tribe. Say that name HOH-pee. Zuni is another tribe. Say that name ZOO-nee. Apache is another tribe. Say that name uh-PA-chee. Life for them is not the same as life for relatives long ago. Even so, they have handed down many stories. They have handed down many traditions.

5By the 1850s, settlers still had little knowledge of this canyon. In 1869, a research team went through the canyon with John Wesley Powell in the lead. He learned the canyon's history from local people. They also gave him tips on how to stay safe. Native people usually shared their history by speaking it. His team was first to write that history on paper. Powell took a second river trip in 1871. Maps of the area came from the help he gave on that trip. The beautiful landscape drew more visitors with each passing year. In 1919, government leaders set the canyon aside as a national park. Local tribes were forced to leave. For a long time, they were kept out. Today they help keep the canyon and its history safe. Some work as guides. They tell their stories to visitors. Two Rims. The South Rim draws the most visitors. People can go there on any day of the year. The North Rim is on the far side of the canyon. The drive to it covers 212 miles (342 km). Fewer people go to the North Rim. For most visitors, the trip takes longer. The North Rim sits much higher. More snow falls there. The closing time runs from mid-October to mid-May.

6Plan Your Own Trip. Today this national park welcomes millions of visitors. Some hikers spend weeks exploring the canyon. Cabins give visitors a place to sleep. At the canyon bottom, hikers can see waterfalls. They can also see pools of many colors. Mules carry some visitors down into the canyon. A ride by raft on the Colorado River is popular too. In some places the water is rough. It can be fast. It can be full of rocks. That makes a grand adventure. Walking the trails along the rim is also a favorite. Miles of colorful walls make people marvel. They watch sunlight slide across the landscape. They watch shadows slide across it too. A twisting river runs through here for about 277 miles. That length is 446 km. A tour guide can help visitors on a raft. The Skywalk is a bridge made of glass. It reaches out over the canyon and gives an amazing view.

7Big horn sheep live under protection in the Grand Canyon. Many other kinds of animals are protected there too. A Wonder of Nature. Few sights in nature match the Grand Canyon. Its land will stay protected. Its peoples will stay protected. Its animals will stay protected. Its history will stay protected. A visit to this national park means an adventure. The view is unlike any other on Earth. Winter turns the national park into a beautiful sight. Erosion means rock or soil is carried off by nature. Water can do it. Wind can do it. Ice can do it. A landscape is all the visible features of a piece of land, seen together. To marvel is to feel wonder and surprise. A national park is land that the government owns. It is kept safe for its history, its natural landscape, or its wildlife. Protected means guarded from harm or danger. Sediment is bits of dirt and rock. Water, wind, or ice can carry those bits and drop them in a new place.

2. A Giant Ship on the Ocean Floor

1The sea was calm. A huge new ship was crossing the Atlantic Ocean toward New York City. Just before midnight, the crew and passengers felt a hard thud. The ship had struck an iceberg. In less than three hours, it broke in two and sank. Was the Titanic gone forever?

2Men beside the huge propellers show the Titanic’s size. Built in 1911, it rose twelve stories and was as long as three football fields. Ships were then the only way between Europe and the United States. Builders added watertight doors to keep the largest passenger ship from sinking. Most of an iceberg’s danger lies under the water. It left Southampton on April 10, 1912, hit an iceberg on April 14, and never reached New York.

3The ship’s side scraped the iceberg, and seawater poured in. Open compartments let water flood from one area to the next. More than 2,200 people were aboard, but there were not enough lifeboats. Women and children filled the few boats, and many people below could not reach them. At 2:17 am the Titanic split in two, and both parts sank. About 1,500 people died, and the deep, dark, cold sea hid the mystery.

4As a boy, Robert Ballard was curious about the ocean. He grew up in southern California and explored the beach and tidal pools. His parents and teachers encouraged him, and he learned to fish, snorkel, and scuba dive. He studied geology, rocks, minerals, and other sciences. In the U.S. Navy he got more training, then worked at Woods Hole in Massachusetts. He studied Earth’s oceans, grew interested in shipwrecks, and decided to find the Titanic.

5By 1985, new technology allowed remote exploration of the ocean. Robert and other scientists searched where the Titanic had sunk. They used a small video camera and sonar, which finds distant things with sound waves. Sonar showed marks, then pieces of a ship and its hull. Old photos proved it was the Titanic, so they cheered and later held a memorial. They videotaped the wreck until rough winter seas made the work too dangerous.

6In 1986 the team returned with Alvin, a small submersible for tight underwater places. Jason Junior could be controlled by a person above the water. Alvin viewed the decks and rooms while the team recorded more video and pictures. The bow and stern lay 1,900 feet, or 579 meters, apart. Between them, a debris field held shoes, a safe, a bathtub, and part of a doll. The iceberg had struck the hull, water rushed in, and its weight sank the ship.

7A mechanical arm sifts through shipwreck debris. Robert tried to stop treasure hunters from taking things that belonged to the ship or its passengers. The next year, salvagers removed thousands of items, and some were placed in an exhibit. He wants governments to agree that nothing may be taken, so the wreck remains a memorial. The United Kingdom and the United States have signed this treaty. After the tragedy, crews trained better and ships carried enough life jackets and lifeboats.

8A doctor’s bag was collected from the wreckage. Original artifacts tell what really happened to those 2,200 passengers over one hundred years ago. Removing something or adding something changes that history. Millvina Dean was nine weeks old when she boarded with her parents and brother. Her father hoped to open a tobacco shop, and Millvina, her mother, and her brother survived. She was a celebrity on the rescue ship, returned to England, drew maps in World War II, and later attended conventions.

9A bow is the front of a ship, and a stern is the back. The hull is a ship’s outer body, and a debris field holds items from a wreck. A maiden voyage is a ship’s first trip. Sonar uses sound waves, and a submersible operates fully under water. Scuba gear and a snorkel help people breathe in the water. Artifacts are objects people made, salvagers recover wreck items, and a tragedy brings great loss.

3. A Life with No Backbone

1This is a lion's mane jellyfish. Imagine a body like a bell, with long tentacles streaming down. You are mostly water and cannot live away from water. You live in the ocean and float on currents. A jellyfish is not a fish at all. It is an invertebrate, an animal with no backbone.

2You and your cousins live in every ocean, and some live in lakes or ponds. Some like warm water, and others prefer cold water. Some have survived for 650 million years, long before dinosaurs. You have no brain, heart, blood, or bones, only water, muscles, and nerves. Eyespots and a nerve network help you sense light, dark, and up and down. Your bell has tentacles, a mouth, a digestive sac, and sometimes oral arms.

3The smallest jellyfish is as tiny as a pearl. The lion's mane is the largest, with a bell wider than a bed. Its tentacles can be as long as a school bus. Jellies come in many shapes, sizes, and colors. Surface jellies are often clear, and deep ones may be red, purple, or glowing. You drift on currents, then fill your bell, squeeze, and shoot forward.

4Some live in open ocean or near shore, and some bask while others avoid light. You are not social, even when found together in the same place. Mom released an egg, and dad fertilized it. A planula floated for weeks, stuck to a rock, and became a polyp. The polyp cloned itself by budding, and disks swam off as medusas. The moon jellyfish is one of almost 200 kinds.

5The upside-down jellyfish lies in shallow warm oceans and grows algae in sunlight. You like to eat fish, crabs, shrimp, and tiny marine animals. Stinging cells catch prey, and some jellies glow or use sticky gel. Many animals avoid stinging cells, but sea turtles, fish, and birds do not. You hide or sting, yet many jellies are eaten young or as adults. Jellyfish are the main food of leatherback sea turtles.

6The Australian sea wasp is one of the most poisonous animals in the world. Its sting can kill a person in three minutes. Never touch a jelly on the beach, even a dead or dying one. Most stings cause pain and a red rash, but some are deadly. You may get swelling, nausea, fever, chills, sweating, or body pain. Rinse with seawater, use vinegar, remove tentacles, and see a doctor.

7Scientists learn new facts about jellyfish every year. They often discover new kinds of jellies. In 2003, Big Red was found off California, with oral arms but no tentacles. People enjoy watching jellyfish float at zoos and aquariums. Watching them gently float is fun and relaxing. Even without brains, they must be pretty smart to have survived for millions of years.

4. A Long Walk from Georgia to Maine

1Six parts make up this book. The reason for the walk comes first. Getting ready comes second. People on the path come third. Hard days come fourth. A look back comes fifth. Word meanings come last. Ben is my name. The year 2015 brought a big choice. A dream from childhood was still open. One very long walk was that dream. Appalachian Trail is the name of the route. Hikers use a short form too. They say A.T. A mountain chain is linked by the A.T. Appalachian Mountains is the name of that chain. The eastern United States is where the chain stands. Highest points of the chain carry the path. The year 1937 is when the path was finished. No hiking-only footpath anywhere was longer then. No hiking-only footpath is longer now. Georgia is the first state. The direction after that is north. The length is over 2,000 miles. The same length is 3,000 km. Fourteen U.S. states lie on the route. Maine is the final state. Many kinds of land showed up on the hike. Steep mountains were one kind. Rolling fields were another kind. Pennsylvania is the home of fields like the ones here.

2A map lays out the full A.T. The United States is labeled on that map. The opening day was April 4, 2016. Springer Mountain was the first peak. Georgia is where that peak stands. North Carolina is marked. Tennessee is marked. Brushing teeth still happened. It happened during the walk, not only at camp. Virginia is marked as well. Maryland and West Virginia are marked. Pennsylvania is marked. Rocky ground in Pennsylvania made each step painful. A pair of sneakers wore out. That happened just before New Jersey. A fresh pair replaced them. The worn pair and the fresh pair are both shown. New York, Massachusetts, and Connecticut are marked. New Hampshire is marked. Vermont appears too. Southern Maine holds Mahoosuc Notch. Slippery boulders sit there in a jumble. No other mile was as hard. Maine is the state for the next stretch. Kennebec River is the name of that water. A canoe was required at that river. The crossing date was October 1, 2016. Mount Katahdin appears on the map. Canada appears beside it. Walking a whole long trail inside a single year has a name. Thru-hiking is the name. Each year, thousands set out to finish an A.T. thru-hike. Success is uncommon. Only a few hundred people succeed. My own hike was a success.

3Several months went into preparation. Practice hikes used a heavy pack. Bad weather was part of the practice. Work at an outdoor store was part of the plan. Learning hiking gear was the point of that work. Saving money was another aim. The first footstep came on April 4, 2016. The place was Springer Mountain in Georgia. Days just after the start were among the hardest. My body had to learn the daily hiking. My mind had to learn it too. Setting up a tent took practice. That practice was indoors, in an apartment. A picture from the first day shows Springer Mountain. The Appalachian Trail starts on that peak. Here is the gear list. A wash kit held a toothbrush plus toothpaste plus a towel. Clothes were a hat plus gloves plus a bandanna. More clothes were underwear plus long underwear plus a T-shirt. More clothes were shorts plus pants plus a long-sleeved shirt. A balaclava was clothing too. Foot gear was hiking sneakers plus camp shoes. More foot gear was camp socks plus sock liners. More foot gear was wool socks plus gaiters. Small gear was a headlamp plus a smartphone plus headphones. More small gear was a GPS tracker plus backup batteries. Big gear was a backpack plus a tent plus trekking poles. Rest gear was a sleeping bag plus a sleeping pad plus a sitting pad. Storm gear was a down jacket plus a rain jacket plus a poncho. More storm gear was a rain skirt plus a pack cover. Carry bags were waterproof bags plus a bear food bag plus rope. Cook gear was a camping stove plus fuel plus a lighter. Water gear was bottles plus a water filter plus purification tablets. Safety gear was a first aid kit plus a repair kit plus a mini-pocketknife. Earplugs were packed as well. Average weight for a loaded pack was 21 pounds, which is 9.5 kg. One day's food was inside that weight. Some stove fuel was inside that weight. One liter of water was inside that weight. Gear cost came to nearly $2,500.

4Virginia holds McAfee's Knob. Cameras point at this spot more often than at any other spot on the path. Childhood was when I first heard of the A.T. Joining its community of hikers became a wish from then on. All ages use the A.T. each year. All backgrounds use it. All ability levels use it. Sad events send many people onto the path so they can recover. Whole families turned up on the trail. They were walking the entire route side by side. A reason of some kind brought every person there. Group hiking is the choice of some thru-hikers. Solo hiking was my usual choice. Meeting new people was a pleasure. Setting my own hours was a pleasure too. Lonely hours still had a cure. Other hikers were always there to be friends. Doing the hike on every single day was very hard. The hardness was the repeat itself. Sunrise was my wake-up on most days. Walking continued until the day's mile goal was met. Camp arrived a little earlier than sunset. Food had to fit a thru-hiker's needs, as it does for most of them. Light weight was one need. Easy preparation was another need. Food that would not spoil was another need. Shelters sometimes had no open space. A tent went up instead. A forest in Vermont was one of those tent spots. Daily miles changed when the weather changed. Daily miles changed when the terrain changed. Personal goals changed the miles too. Tiredness changed the miles too. A good camp had a lean-to nearby. It had water nearby. It had an outhouse nearby. Missing those things meant the small tent came out.

5Rain changed the plan for a day. The goal was a shelter with space still open. Some rain refused to pause. Several days in a row could pass under that rain. Full drying-out waited for a town. A town stop came every few days. More food got bought there. A shower got taken there. Laundry got done there. Rest turned out to matter. It mattered as much as the walking. The right supplies mattered just as much. Most shelters were small. A Pennsylvania shelter in one picture is that small kind. Crowds filled some shelters. A Smoky Mountains shelter in another picture is that crowded kind. Count the hike as six months. Each of those months is about 365 miles, or 587 km. Staying safe mattered more than anything else. Bears appeared. Snakes appeared. Ticks appeared. Calm was the goal. A safe gap stayed between me and those animals. Drinking water went through a filter. Staying dry was the aim. Staying warm was the aim. Staying clean lowered the chance of getting sick. Good shoes helped prevent injury. Paying attention to body limits helped prevent injury. Gear stayed organized. An emergency would find me ready. Contact with loved ones stayed part of the plan. Food-scented items do not stay in camp. Hikers hang them on bear poles. Bears then take less interest. Happiness was the usual feeling. Fun was not always part of the day. That gap was one of the largest challenges. Hunger showed up often. Tiredness showed up often. Soreness showed up often. Boredom showed up at times. Support came from people nearby. I learned to lean on them. New York grew very hot. Most water sources there dried up. Puddle water went through the filter.

6Both ends of the hike were hard. Fall had arrived by the finish. Nights were growing cold. Hunger was worse than at any earlier point. Maine's mountains were the toughest climbs of all. Nearly six months of walking led to the finish. The A.T. was complete. October 1, 2016, was the summit day. Mount Katahdin was the peak under my feet. Those last days were hard in a new way. They did not feel like the opening days. Strength was there. Confidence was there. Sports were rare in my childhood. Pride came after the thru-hike. Athlete was a name I could finally use for myself. The distance walked was more than 2,000 miles. That is more than 3,000 km. About 20 pounds came off. That loss is 9.1 kg. One night was the worst of the whole trip. Morning after that night brought beautiful views. Roan Highlands was the place. Tennessee is the state. Favorite memories are what people ask about now. One memory is a solo night. The camp was on a mountaintop. Wind was strong enough to matter. Rain was heavy. The tent collapsed inward. Frustration came. Fear came. Sleep was abandoned at 3:00 a.m. Gear got packed. Breakfast got eaten. Walking started. A sunrise followed. No earlier sunrise had been so amazing. Something scary changed its form. It turned beautiful. People ask about the method. The method matches any other large job. One step comes, and then another step. People also ask about the reason. Looking back changes the answer. Reasons for continuing were not the original reasons. Love came from people around me. Support came from people at home. Stopping no longer felt possible.

7These pages are being written now. Several months have gone by since the thru-hike. Knee pain is still here. Five million steps are the cause. Loved people were far away. Six months was the length of that separation. Was the risk worth it? A large chance got taken. The thing done was a long-held wish. Planning happened. Money got saved. Spending stayed inside a tight budget. Every carried object gained value. Life itself depended on those objects. Challenges were real. The ending feeling is still clear. An A.T. thru-hike is an amazing experience. Beautiful places return in memory often. Maine's Mount Katahdin is one of them. Word meanings come next. A budget is a plan that tells how money will be spent. A set period of time is part of that plan. Filtered describes a pass through a device or a material. Unwanted pieces get pulled out. Liquids can be filtered. Gases can be filtered. A footpath is a narrow walking trail made for people. A lean-to is a small shelter whose roof slopes. Rocks can hold the roof. Posts can hold it. Trees can hold it. Spoil is what happens when food turns unfit to eat. Summited means a climber stood at a mountain's top. Terrain is the natural shape of a piece of land. The word also means the ground. Thru-hiking means a long-distance trail gets walked from its start all the way to its end. One hiking season holds that walk. Transformed means form has changed. Appearance can change too.

5. A Pen Pal Writes from Canada

1Email subjects give this book its shape. Hello from Canada comes first. Questions come next. Family comes next. A game question follows. Roots and a trip follow. A caribou question follows. A visit invitation closes the letters. A glossary is last. Subject: Hello from Canada. Stephanie, yesterday’s email was a thrill. I had wanted a pen pal for a while. I am glad your teacher contacted mine. Your teacher is based in Omaha. That city sits in Nebraska, correct? My teacher is based in Vancouver. That city sits in British Columbia. Does that location sound familiar?

2Canada has 10 provinces, and British Columbia is one. Canada also has three territories. States belong to the United States, not to us. Your last email mentioned a report on Canada. Will this kind of information serve that report? If it will not, please be more exact. I hope to be useful. Jacqueline, writing as your pen pal in Canada. A map prints a set of names. Greenland is printed. Alaska is printed. Nunavut is printed. Yukon Territory is printed. Northwest Territories is printed. Labrador is printed. Newfoundland is printed. British Columbia is printed. Saskatchewan is printed. Alberta is printed. Manitoba is printed. Quebec is printed. Prince Edward Island is printed. Ontario is printed. Nova Scotia is printed. The United States is printed. New Brunswick is printed. Subject: Questions I need answered. Jacqueline, it is nice to get your letter. Here are the report topics my teacher assigned. They show what I am hunting. One topic is people. One is economics. One is government. One is history. One is culture. One is religion. One is physical geography. I hope the list is not too heavy. What does your family look like? My dad’s sales are animal feed. My mom’s job is waitress. Her truck stop is outside of town. I have no brother. I have no sister. Do you have either? I am thankful for the help. Next month I can describe Omaha for your report. I can describe the United States too. Your friend, Stephanie.

3Subject: About my family. Stephanie, that list covers a wide field. Here is how I will start. Family comes first. Your people section can use those details. Other sections may pick up answers too. Is the plan all right? Dad fishes in English Bay. He is gone before my school-day wake-up. He wants the Bay while the fish are still asleep. On exports, no North American port beats Vancouver. Freezing hardly ever happens there. Dad can therefore work the whole year. Fish count as a major resource. Dad says about 80 percent of Canada’s fish goes to other countries. More resources are on the list. Iron ore is one. Nickel is one. Zinc is one. Copper is one. Gold is one. Silver is one. Coal is one. Oil is one. I read that list at Statistics Canada. You could look there too. Mom drives me to school each day. She drives my brother too. She teaches French. The school is the one my brother attends. English has official language status. French has official language status. He is in grade 12. College comes the following year. Some classmates use the bus. Some use the train. A few use bikes. The opening time is 8:30. The closing time is 3:00.

4I am in grade 4, the same year as you. My top class is social studies. We are on the Canadian government right now. Your report can use that luck. These lines are from my notebook. Canada’s government is a democracy. In a democracy, people are the ones who run things. The United States follows that same idea. We hold a constitution of our own. We hold a bill of rights of our own. A republic is the United States form. A constitutional monarchy is the Canadian form. Our chief of state is England’s king or queen. Our Parliament is led by the prime minister. A president is absent here. Parliament contains two houses. The Senate holds 105 members. The House of Commons holds 308 members. I hope the notes help a bit. I need to leave. Mom is calling me to lacrosse. The other answers can wait for my return. Talk soon, Jacqueline. A further map marks places. British Columbia is marked. Ottawa is marked. Toronto is marked. Vancouver is marked. Florida is marked. Nebraska is marked. Omaha is marked. Miami is marked. Subject: Tell me about lacrosse. Jacqueline, many thanks. Lacrosse is unfamiliar. Soccer is the game I play. Do the games match? My mom asks about your grandparents’ origins. Mine come from Germany. What do you enjoy? Each summer sends my family to Florida. The beach is where we rest. Does your family travel on vacations? Canada seems excellent. I hope the practice went well. Your friend, Stephanie.

5Subject: Roots and a family trip. Stephanie, practice wore me out. I feel pretty tired. Hunger is ahead of the tired feeling. Mom is cooking upstairs. The meal is caribou. Grandpa taught her that dish. He was Inuit. Your heritage questions are already getting pieces of an answer. Soccer needs a line first. I do play. Gym is the only setting. Lacrosse is the one I enjoy more. The games share some traits. Our sticks end in baskets. The ball moves from one player to another. A goal is what we want. A soccer goal is far bigger than ours. Back to family. Dad’s ancestors began in England. He follows them to 1897, the year of the Klondike Gold Rush. Mom’s father was Inuit. I already told you that. Her mother came from French descent. Mom’s strong French comes from her. On religion, Roman Catholic is the largest group in Canada. Protestant describes many other people. Protestant describes our family. Some people follow a different Christian religion. Muslim faith belongs to only a small group. Jewish faith belongs to only a small group. Buddhist faith belongs to only a small group. Hindu faith belongs to only a small group. Two Englishmen appear in a picture, panning for gold. The time shown is the Klondike Gold Rush.

6Yes, family vacations are part of our year. This past summer’s goal was Mount Logan. That peak stands in Kluane National Park in the Yukon. Its rise is 5,959 meters. The same rise is 19,551 feet. No point in Canada is higher. I wanted a loop around the whole country. The area is 9,984,670 square kilometers. The same area is 3,854,045 square miles. Mom and Dad called that distance too far. In size, Canada is second among countries in the world. The land regions number eight. The Canadian Shield is a region. The Hudson Bay Lowlands is a region. The Western Cordillera is a region. The Interior Lowlands is a region. Another region is known as Great Lakes-St. Lawrence Lowlands. After that region comes the Appalachians. The Arctic Lowlands is a region. The Innuitians is a region. We left Vancouver heading straight north. We stayed inside the Western Cordillera. Scenery there is amazing. Mountains crowd the view. Your physical geography section can use these notes. A map of regions carries labels. Western Cordillera is labeled. Canadian Shield is labeled. Innuitian is labeled. Interior Lowlands is labeled. Hudson Bay Lowlands is labeled. Appalachian is labeled. Arctic Lowlands is labeled. Great Lakes is labeled.

7Two regions stay on my life list. I want the Canadian Shield. I want the region called Great Lakes-St. Lawrence Lowlands. Canada’s biggest land region is the Canadian Shield. About half the country is inside it. Plains appear there. Mountains appear there. Ancient rocks appear in large numbers. Rolling lowlands form most of a land region called Great Lakes-St. Lawrence Lowlands. This area holds Niagara Falls. It also holds the St. Lawrence Seaway. That seaway is Canada’s main river. Heartland is a frequent name for the area. Farming output is centered here. Industry is centered here. Could Omaha be similar? Dinner is ready, so I must run. The smell is great. Leftover questions can wait until I finish. Caribou stew is waiting. Jacqueline. A picture places a caribou next to a moose. Subject: Caribou beside moose. Jacqueline, the size of Canada was a surprise. Caribou was a new name to me. Pictures on a screen make them seem like moose. Is the likeness real? Deer are plentiful where I live. Friends and I sometimes spot them behind our houses. What are the friends around you like? I will write again soon. Your friend, Stephanie.

8Subject: Please come to Canada. Stephanie, the lacrosse team holds many friends of mine. Li is my closest friend. He is not on that team. China is where he is from. A lot of people immigrate here from countries in Asia. Every new school year, I scan the classroom. The number of Asian kids looks higher. Last year’s Math class put Li and me on an immigration graph. We counted arrivals in Canada during 2003. You will get a copy of what we drew. Mom helped scan the page onto the computer. Write if the file stays shut. The Philippines total is 11,978. The Pakistan total is 12,330. The S. Korea total is 7,086. The India total is 24,560. The United States total is 5,990. The People’s Republic of China total is 36,116. The Iran total is 5,648. The Romania total is 5,465. The Sri Lanka total is 4,442. The United Kingdom total is 5,194. I checked the topic list from the start. History is the piece we have not covered. I will ask Dad. He cares deeply about history. He scanned a timeline out of a history book he owns. I will attach the scan. Artifacts from 9,000 b.c. place native Canadians inside today’s Ontario area. In 986, marked a.d. 986, a Viking is first among Europeans to view the coastline of Labrador. Bjarni Herjolfsson is his name. Quebec City is founded in 1608. The founder is Samuel de Champlain. His title is Father of New France. That city is the earliest permanent settlement the French made in Canada. Henry Hudson’s exploration of Hudson Bay is dated 1610. Queen Anne’s War in 1702 sets the French against the British. The 49th parallel is fixed as the Canada-U.S. border in 1818. An Act of Union in 1841 links Upper Canada with Lower Canada. The link creates the Province of Canada. In 1867, Sir John A. Macdonald becomes Prime Minister for the first time. He heads the Dominion of Canada. A gold rush opens in the Yukon in 1897. In 1931, full authority for legislative matters moves from Great Britain to Canada. A fresh flag for Canada appears in 1965. The Canada-US Free Trade Agreement gets its start in 1989. Nunavut is created in 1999. It is the Inuit Territory.

9I hope your report got real help. Next summer, try to persuade the family toward Canada. Leave Florida for another time. Ottawa could be a stop. It is the capital of the nation. Toronto could be a stop. It is the city with the most people. Vancouver is still the prettiest summer choice. You would enjoy it. News of Omaha next month will be welcome. News of the United States will be welcome too. Take care. Your friend, Jacqueline. Ancestors are earlier people in a family line. An Inuit person is part of a native people. Those peoples live near the Arctic. Northern Canada is one home. Greenland is one home. Alaska is one home. A caribou is a reindeer of large size. Very cold country is its home. Parliament is made of officials in government. Lawmaking is their power. Changing laws is their power too. A constitution sets out basic laws. The laws belong to a state or to a nation. They explain the running of government. Provinces are parts made when a country splits its land. A constitutional monarchy keeps a king or a queen. A parliament oversees the power those royals hold. A republic has a president as leader. Elected representatives do the governing. A democracy lets people vote. The votes pick others who will represent them. Territories are lands under another country’s control. Ownership by another country is another form. Heritage is what an older generation hands to a younger one.

6. A Skyline That Rose Again

1A worker in 1930 balances on a beam. He bolts together the frame of the Empire State Building. In another view, a policeman of the Port Authority watches traffic. He sits in an electric patrol car. The place is the Holland Tunnel, in New York City. Try to imagine Paris with the Eiffel Tower gone. Dubai would not look like itself without the Burj Khalifa tower. Structures this famous, plus the buildings around them, draw an outline on the sky. People call that outline a skyline. A skyline marks a place. Fingerprints mark a person in a similar way. A fingerprint does not change. A skyline does. Dubai, Paris, and New York appear in photos. Manhattan is part of New York City. Its skyline has changed in slow ways and in fast ways. The shift in New York has been dramatic. Structures have climbed. They have come down. They have climbed once more.

2A drawing from 1884 records Manhattan's skyline of that year. Settlement of New York City began in the seventeenth century. The nineteenth century brought a sudden jump in population. After the Civil War, people who had been enslaved came north. Work was what they hoped to find. Ireland later sent immigrants. Poland sent immigrants. Italy sent immigrants. Manhattan Island became home for many of them. Late in the 1800s, no city in the country was more densely populated than New York. By 1925, no city anywhere held people more tightly packed. Homes were needed. Workplaces were needed. Shops were needed. Growth moved toward the north. Stores rose there. Factories rose there. Houses rose there. The island of Manhattan covers only about 23 square miles. That is 59.6 square kilometers. Open land ran out. Upward was the only direction left for growth. Builders had to change their methods. Older buildings used walls to carry the weight. Walls at ground level had to carry every floor stacked above. They had to bear the roof too. Height stayed limited because of that demand. No practical wall could be made both thick enough and strong enough for many floors. A steel frame became a new way to carry the load. Huge beams of steel were locked together. They formed a skeleton. Walls hung from those beams. The walls were fastened to the floors. Upper walls stopped pressing so hard on the walls beneath. The beams took the weight. Buildings could now reach heights never used before. Near the opening of the twentieth century, the city already held many of these huge structures. The name for them was skyscraper. They rose one after another. Builders competed with architects. The prize was the tallest building on Earth.

3A chart names well-known buildings on this city's skyline. The Park Row Building dates from 1899. It stands 391 feet high. That height is 119 meters. It contains 29 stories. The Singer Building dates from 1908. It stands 670 feet high. That height is 204 meters. It contains 37 stories. The Met Life Tower dates from 1909. It stands 700 feet high. That height is 213 meters. It contains 50 stories. The Woolworth Building dates from 1913. It stands 792 feet high. That height is 241 meters. It contains 55 stories. The Chrysler Building dates from 1930. It stands 1,048 feet high. That height is 319 meters. It contains 77 stories. The Empire State Building dates from 1931. It stands 1,454 feet high. That height is 443 meters. It contains 103 stories. The North Tower at the World Trade Center dates from 1970. It stands 1,727 feet high. That height is 526 meters. It contains 110 stories. One World Trade Center dates from 2014. It stands 1,776 feet high. That height is 541 meters. It contains 104 stories. Add the spire and the full reach is 1,792 feet. That added reach is 546 meters. In 1928 the Chrysler Building's builder had a goal. The building would have seventy-seven stories. He wanted it to be the tallest on Earth. A tall spire was fixed to the top. In 1930 the building took the title of tallest structure. It was also alone in standing above 1,000 feet. That measure is 305 meters. The lead did not last. Only a few blocks off, another giant was climbing. Before long the Empire State Building held the title of tallest. It became a famous landmark of the city as well. The Chrysler Building rises with the others.

4January 1930 is when work began on the Empire State Building. The workers numbered more than three thousand. They finished only 410 days later. Steel and granite are the materials. The floor count is 103. Height passed 1,400 feet. That is 427 meters. Working elevators numbered sixty-four. The ride to floor 80 took under a minute. An outdoor viewing deck sits on floor 86. An indoor deck sits on floor 102. One photo catches the building still under way. Another photo catches it complete. Each week, four and a half stories were added. The tower turned into a symbol of the city. Many books featured it. Many movies featured it. Nearly forty years passed with it as the world's tallest building. Then a pair of giant towers rose in Lower Manhattan. Skyscraper work slowed once this building was complete. The cost of giant structures was high. The economy of America was in poor shape. Not until 1970 did the skyline climb higher. During the 1960s, officials chose a center for commerce between nations. Lower Manhattan would hold it. The body in charge was the Port Authority, the one for New York and New Jersey. People called the project a world trade center. Minoru Yamasaki was the architect who designed it. His plan used twin towers. They would sit inside a complex of several buildings. This authority had been formed in 1921. Its purpose is developing sites for trade and for transportation. It also runs those sites. New Jersey is one place it serves. New York City is the other. It still owns much of the World Trade Center complex. It still manages that complex. The site covers 16 acres. That is 64,750 square meters. The same authority led the building of the George Washington Bridge. Manhattan and New Jersey are linked by that bridge.

5New structural ideas would go into the towers of 110 stories. Tube-frame construction was one idea. Think of each tower as a square pipe that is hollow. Columns stood close together on the outer walls. Inside support columns were needed less. Usable floor space grew because of that. Sky lobbies were a second idea. Elevators must be numerous in a building this tall. Yamasaki copied the pattern of the city subway. Express elevators were included. Local elevators were included. The subway, in the same way, runs express trains and local trains. Reaching the highest floors was a puzzle. Hundreds of stairs were a poor answer. Elevators solved the problem of height. Safety fell short in the earliest elevators. Elisha Otis repaired that weakness. The year 1854 brought his first safety elevator. A broken cable would drop the platform only a few inches. Engines to drive the car were his design too. Tall buildings depended on what he invented. Ride an elevator and look around. The name Otis may appear somewhere inside. At the peak of the work, over 3,500 people helped raise the twin towers. An express car could rise straight to a sky lobby. That lobby was on floor forty-four. Riders then moved to a local car. The local car stopped on every floor higher up. Offices were reached that way. Moving people straight up and down became efficient. Fewer shafts were required. The year 1966 started the work. Four years were needed. December 1970 brought the finish of the North Tower. Its spire tip reached 1,727 feet. That is 526 meters. Windows on the World, a restaurant at the top, gave diners a view that took the breath away. The South Tower measured 1,362 feet. That is 415 meters. Its finish came seven months later. A garage for cars lay beneath both towers. Rail lines and subway stops lay beneath the garage.

6The Twin Towers made a strong new shape on the skyline. Thirty years passed with them as New York's tallest buildings. Disaster arrived after that. Morning on September 11, 2001, brought terror in the air. Two jets full of passengers were flown into the towers by terrorists. The South Tower began falling in under an hour. It broke apart as it fell. The North Tower came down soon after that. New Yorkers looked on and could hardly believe the sight. Heaps of rubble, still burning, replaced the enormous buildings. Lives lost came to nearly three thousand. Two aircraft struck the Twin Towers that morning. The blow was devastating. Grief covered the city for neighbors who were gone. Grief also covered the loss of the landmark. A promise was made to build the site again. Digging out the ruins took a long while. Planning the ground took years. Ideas for new buildings were many. Opinions about the right plan were strong. In time the Port Authority revealed the design of a new complex. A memorial park was set aside. It remembered the people who lost their lives that day. Pools now mark where the first towers stood. Waterfalls drop inside those pools. Objects saved from the old towers sit in a memorial museum. The memorial's two reflecting pools hold a record. They are the largest waterfalls made by people in the United States.

7A tower of 104 stories has gone up close by. Freedom Tower is the nickname people gave it. The rise is an astonishing 1,776 feet. That is 541 meters. The number matches the year the United States broke free of British rule. A spire on top is lit. Miles away, that light can still be seen. More tall buildings are rising on the site. A new memorial has been completed. A museum has been completed too. New York's skyline does not stop changing. It shows a city that keeps adapting. It shows a city that keeps growing. One World Trade Center was done in 2014. It tops the first Twin Towers by more than 400 feet. That gap is 122 meters. An architect is a person who designs buildings. Commerce is the buying of goods and the selling of them. It is business, and it is trade. A complex is several buildings set close together for one shared purpose. Efficient describes a good use of time. It also describes a good use of resources. Excavating means digging out or uncovering. An innovation is a new idea. It may be a new product. It may be a new way to do a job. A landmark can be an important building from the past. It can be an important site. It can be a land marker that identifies a spot. Populated means living things have settled there. A skeleton is the frame that holds a structure up. A building can have this kind of frame. A spire is the pointed cap of a tower. Other buildings can have a spire too. Unveiled means something was shown after being covered. Vertically means in a straight line up and down.

7. A Summer Watching for Smoke

1Steve hikes along a ridge. The ridge is not far from his tower. The tower stands on Bear Mountain. A forest fire can spread fast. It can do a great deal of harm. Fire lookouts help keep forests safe. The workers are men and women. They spend the summer deep in the woods. They watch closely for smoke. They stay ready to alert firefighters. They send word as soon as danger appears. Steve Kalling has worked as a fire lookout since 1990. His last name sounds like KAL-ling. Each summer, he spends three months up on a tower. The tower is in the Selway-Bitterroot Wilderness. This huge forest covers part of Montana. It also covers part of Idaho. A map of the area is labeled Wild Country. The map marks Montana. It marks Missoula. It marks Idaho. It marks Bear Mountain Peak. It marks the Selway-Bitterroot Wilderness. It marks Darby. It marks Salmon. This wilderness is very large. In size, it ranks third among US wilderness areas outside Alaska. It covers 1.3 million acres. That area is 5,300 sq km. Part of it lies in the Bitterroot National Forest. Part lies in the Clearwater National Forest. Part lies in the Lolo National Forest. Part lies in the Nez Perce National Forest.

2Interviewer: What path led you into lookout work? Steve: I was out hiking. I met a ranger who worked in the deep woods. We talked about the work she did. She hiked from place to place. She checked the trails and the campsites. I thought that would be a fine way to pass a summer. The Forest Service was not taking on any rangers. That same morning, a fire lookout had quit. I walked into the ranger station and asked if the lookout job could be mine. Back then, the answer came that simply. Interviewer: What does your station look like? Do stations come in more than one style? Steve: Mine is a tower built of wood. The tower is 50 feet tall, or 15 m. A cabin sits on the top. The cabin measures 14 by 14 feet, or 4 by 4 m. The roof comes to a peak. A deck runs around the outside. Windows fill four walls. When the light is good, I can see for 60 to 80 miles in some directions. That reach is 97 to 129 km. Other lookouts use other styles. Some are cabins set right on high ground.

3A helicopter comes down to land. It carries enough supplies for another month. Interviewer: How do you get to the station, and how do you leave? Steve: The walk is 8 miles, or 13 km. At the start of the year, a helicopter flies me up with every supply I have. Each month, more supplies are dropped off. A helicopter does that drop almost every time. The crew brings food. They bring things such as paint. If I have broken a window, they bring a new one. Interviewer: What does a day at the station look like? Steve: I wake when daylight comes. The first look after dawn is a good time to study the land. I might notice a small fire that I can watch later that day. After that scan, I walk about half a mile, or 1 km. I collect water from a spring. Then I cook breakfast. A forest ranger calls on the radio and reads out the weather. Wind worries firefighters most. High heat worries them too. Low humidity worries them too. Those conditions can make a fire worse. Blackened trunks show where flames passed in an earlier year.

4Inside the tower there is a bed. There is a kitchen. There is gear a lookout uses. The gear helps him report the exact spot of a fire. After breakfast, repairs are a good task. Painting is a good task too. Fires are more likely in the afternoon. By then the day has grown warm. When the eight-hour duty day ends, I am free. I can go for a hike. I can do whatever I choose. I am a musician. I bring several instruments with me. I practice them while I keep watch. If rainy days stretch on, I read books. In the evening I climb back up. I cook the night meal. I watch the sun go down. Interviewer: Besides the radio, can you reach other people? Steve: A cell phone gets no signal on my tower. I also have no Internet there. Some other towers have both of those. A photo shows Steve with his radio. He sends details about a fire. The fire is far off. He studies it through binoculars.

5A small fire can turn large in a hurry when conditions are right. Interviewer: In far-off country, what sets the forest alight? Steve: Weather starts most of them. When the air is hot and dry, the danger of fire is higher. In weather like that, a chain may drag behind a truck and throw sparks. A careless camper can start a fire. A logger can start one too. Sometimes lightning starts a fire, and the storm may drop only a little rain. The rain holds the fire small for days or even weeks. Then the fire flares up. After any lightning, watchers need to look with extra care. Interviewer: After you see a fire, can you still help the firefighters? Steve: Yes. Once I have called a fire in, I keep watching the smoke for changes. The color and the amount of smoke tell me things. White or light blue smoke means a small fire that is not very hot. As a fire grows larger and hotter, the color moves from white to blue, then to brown, then to black. If I can see where the fire meets the ground, I can describe the way the fire is moving. Interviewer: Did a fire ever force you to flee? Steve: Yes. It has happened three times. Two of those times were at Bear Mountain Lookout, in 1994 and in 2012. The tower did not burn on either day. I was not truly afraid while I was still there. The fear hit after a helicopter was carrying me away. Before that, I had to stay on my job and tell the firefighters what the fire was doing.

6Interviewer: How often do large fires come? Steve: They come more often than they used to. The rise has been in the last few years. The climate has changed. The weather is hotter, drier, and windier. Often, crews let a small fire keep burning. A small fire belongs to a healthy ecosystem. Last summer the land was extremely hot and dry. The moment a fire was seen, it grew very fast. Smoke pours up from a large fire as it moves over a far ridge. Wild land offers moments of beauty that nothing has spoiled. Interviewer: What is the best part of being a lookout? Steve: The best part is life on top of a mountain. I spend the whole summer with earth below me and sky above me. The days run very long, and they pass slowly. At sunset, light slides in from the side of the windows. That light is beautiful!

7Another wonderful part of the work is care of the land. I help look after nature and make sure it will still be here later. Large wild places matter to people. People use them for recreation. Those places are also the source of drinking water and a source of oxygen. Our environment needs protection. Taking part in that care feels truly good. One of the hardest parts is the return when summer ends. Leaving my family and my friends is hard. Coming back into daily life is jarring. Everything around me moves very fast. The world feels busy, loud, and cluttered. Quiet becomes what you are used to. Indian paintbrush flowers color a hillside in view from the tower. Here are word meanings from this book. Alert is a verb on page 3. It means to give a warning or pass on news. Climate is a noun on page 13. It means the pattern of weather in an area over a long time. Damage is a noun on page 3. It means harm done to a person or a thing. Ecosystem is a noun on page 13. It means living things in one community, together with their habitat. Environment is a noun on page 15. It means every condition that affects a living thing in one area, or the natural world. Humidity is a noun on page 8. It means how much water the air holds. Jarring is an adjective on page 15. It means unpleasantly surprising. Lookouts is a noun on page 3. It means people whose work is to watch for danger. Remote is an adjective on page 11. It means far away or fully cut off from other people.

8. A Visit with the Honey Man

1Russell Buzz Riopelle is a beekeeper in the Cleveland, Ohio, area. He has been keeping bees for 30 years. Did he get the name Buzz because he keeps bees? No, his parents started calling him Buzz before he was born.

2Beekeeping takes lots of work, and it must be done at the right time. A delay of one or two weeks can hurt the honey crop badly. Buzz checks that the queen is laying eggs and looks at the bees every two or three weeks. He lifts heavy honeycomb onto his truck and extracts the honey in the honey house. The truck carries boxes of 35 to 45 pounds of honey and a blower that blows the bees out. He wears a bee suit and veil, and smoke from a smoker makes the bees fill with honey and stay calm.

3Even after smoke, bees may still sting Buzz's bare hands as he moves the boxes. He does not wear gloves, because they are bulky, hard to clean, and can spread disease. He gets stung a couple thousand times a year, but he no longer swells up. He works near two o'clock on sunny days, when half the bees are out collecting nectar. Queens and workers are female, drones are male, and every bee comes from the queen. In spring she lays 1,500 to 2,000 eggs a day, and drones only gather outside to mate.

4The queen makes short flights to mate, and otherwise she leaves only to swarm. If a second queen develops, she stays, and the old queen leaves with half the bees. They fill up with honey, build a new comb, store the honey, and lay eggs. Eggs in the cells look like tiny grains of rice standing on end. Bees make wax on their bellies and royal jelly, a special food for the queen. People use beeswax for candles, lip gloss, moisturizer, decorations, and toilet seals.

5Queens live two to three years, but summer drones and workers live only four to six weeks. In winter they live longer, eat honey, and shiver to heat the hive. At 5 degrees below zero outside, the hive center can be 95 degrees Fahrenheit. A summer hive holds 60,000 to 80,000 bees that feed, clean, and care for the queen. Bees notice the sun and use it to find the hive, but they do not fly on cloudy days. The hive is a large box of frames where bees raise young and store pollen and honey.

6There are bumblebees, carpenter bees, hornets, European wasps, potter wasps, and spider wasps. Caucasian bees come from the Caucasus Mountains, Italian bees come from Italy, and some come from Africa. These are honeybees, but America had no native honeybees. Pilgrims and settlers brought bees from Europe, and the bees spread across the United States. Africanized bees were bred in South America, get angry easily, and can take over a hive in a week. People have fought these bees for thirty years, but they still do the same job as honeybees.

7Both queens and workers have stingers, but a worker's hooked stinger pulls out and kills her. A worker stings only if her life or the hive is at risk, while a queen can sting again and again. Some people are allergic, so their throats may swell, and they must carry special medicine. A stuck stinger leaves a venom sac that makes the sting hurt. Bees collect pollen and nectar, but they make honey from nectar, not pollen. A bee stores nectar in her honey stomach, spits it into the comb, and bees fan it thicker.

8Making honey is not the biggest purpose of bees in this country. They pollinate alfalfa for animal feed, plus melons and almonds. An anther makes pollen with male cells, and a pistil makes seeds. Flower parts are petals, pistil, anther, and stem, and bees carry pollen from anther to pistil. Different flowers give different honey, like apple-blossom or orange-blossom honey. Buzz rents bees to orchards at night for a week or two, then takes them home.

9One hive gives 60 to 350 pounds of honey a year, or 27 to 159 kilograms. Some bees work harder than others, just as some people get up early. Honey sits on the outside of a frame, capped eggs form a light circle, and pollen is in the center. Buzz cuts off the wax cap and spins the frame so the honey flies out. He returns the comb for the bees to fill again. A heating tank thins the honey, a filter bag removes wax, and then he fills jars.

10Honey never spoils. Bacteria do not grow in honey because it has too much acid. That acid is antibacterial, so it keeps those bacteria from living in the honey. Because of this acid, honey can stay good instead of spoiling. Putting honey on a burn or a wound can help prevent infection. The same honey can also help the wound heal faster. A bee may leave a flower covered in pollen. She will spread that pollen to other flowers. This helps those flowers produce seeds.

9. A Writer Named Shakespeare

1Experts are not sure that any portrait of Shakespeare was painted in his lifetime. Some people believe one picture shows the writer. William Shakespeare was an English playwright and a poet. His life ran from 1564 until 1616. Four hundred years after he died, his words are still very much alive. In the history of English, many people rank him as the greatest writer.

2People enjoy Shakespeare for many reasons. They admire his plays because the stories are exciting. The stories reach people's feelings. His writing can make a person feel happy. It can make a person feel sad. It can make a person feel shocked. It can make a person feel scared. His stories also carry morals. Good and evil are among the large ideas those morals ask people to think about. Other writers do not handle language the way he does. Writers who want to tell a good story still treat his beautiful writing as the benchmark. His parents were John Shakespeare and Mary Shakespeare. Home for the family was Stratford, in England. William went to a good school there. School like that was rare in those days. Many people could not read. Many people could not write. His wife was a woman named Anne, and the wedding was in 1582. Stratford was also home to the couple and their three children. Sadly, their only son died while he was still young.

3At some point from 1585 to 1592, he left the family in Stratford. He moved to London. He worked as an actor. He also worked as a playwright. By 1594, he owned part of a popular theater group. Queen Elizabeth herself enjoyed watching the group. He helped open a London theater called the Globe in 1599. That theater staged many plays he had written. Plays in his time tried to wow the crowd. Sword fights were part of the show. Beautiful costumes were part of the show too. The crowds were loud. They shouted boo at the villains. They cheered for the heroes. They even tossed food at actors they did not like. Most plays were put on in the daytime. Singing was one skill an actor needed. An actor had to be able to dance. An actor had to be able to fight. Only males were allowed on the stage. Boys usually took the women's parts.

4Shakespeare wrote three main kinds of plays. The kinds are tragedies, histories, and comedies. In a tragedy, the hero makes mistakes, and those mistakes lead to disaster. Macbeth is a grim tale about power and one family's hunger for it. His history plays mostly honor the kings of England. His comedies were often love stories that made people laugh. Those plays end happily. A Midsummer Night's Dream uses a magic potion. The potion makes characters love the wrong person. The mix-ups are funny. Later writers have treated his stories as a guide. They write plays, poems, and books. In California, students act a sword fight taken from Henry V. Actors in England act out that same fight. For hundreds of years, people all over the world have read his plays. They have acted in them. They have watched them. His amazing characters make people laugh. They also make people cry. The stories last because they show what it means to be human. Readers often see evil in Iago, a man in the play Othello. Even so, Iago is still a human being. Sir John Falstaff is proud. He is also cowardly. He is clever. He is also a fool. He is a complex character. Romeo and Juliet, a love story, has been retold hundreds of times. People have told it in many different ways, including movies and television.

5Students perform in Shakespeare's plays. Many actors say a part in one of his plays is the hardest they have ever played. They also say it is the most important. He wrote his characters to seem like real people. Showing that complexity takes hard work from the actors. One of his plays is watched outdoors in Calgary, Alberta. More than one hundred languages have a version of his writing. Festivals of his work around the world still draw big crowds. Some actors speak the plays in the English of Shakespeare's own time. That English sounds very different from the language of today. Other actors set the stories in the world of today. Doing that shows how timeless the stories are. Famous actors perform his work. Children put on his plays too. There is something in Shakespeare for everyone.

6The number of sonnets he wrote is one hundred fifty-four. A sonnet is a special kind of poem that rhymes. Each sonnet has fourteen lines. Most of his sonnets are about love. The best-known sonnet opens with this line. Shall I compare thee to a summer's day? People still say they have had too much of a good thing. People still say they were sent on a wild goose chase. Those two sayings come from Shakespeare. Most people do not know that. When he could not find a word he needed, he made a new one. We still say many of those words. Buzzer, gloomy, and hint are three of them. He made hundreds more. He also joined words to build new ones. Eyeball, bloodstained, and leapfrog are three of those. After he died, his friends gathered his plays into a book. The book held thirty-six plays. For eighteen of them, that book was the first time they were published. His plays might have vanished if his friends had not kept them. The book grew popular in many countries. His friend Ben Johnson wrote about the work in that book. Johnson's words were these. Not of an age, but for all time. Johnson was right. People still feel close to his work hundreds of years afterward. Many visitors go to the church where he is buried.

7His plays are exciting. They are fun. They also reach people's feelings. His characters show how hard life can be. They also show how beautiful life can be. People will go on loving these works for hundreds of years more. In 1583, Anne gave birth to their first child, Susanna. In 1585, the couple's twins were born. The twins were named Hamnet and Judith. In 1623, a book of his plays was published. An actor is a person who plays a part on a stage, on television, or in a movie. A benchmark is a mark used to judge how good similar things are. A comedy entertains with funny characters. The ending is happy. Complex means made of many different parts. It also means hard to understand. History means events that happened in the past. A playwright is a person who writes plays. Published means a piece of writing was made ready for the public. Popular describes something many people like. Tragedies are plays, movies, or books that end sadly.

10. Abigail Adams and Her Ideas

1The year 1795 produced an oil painting of Abigail Adams. Who was Abigail Adams? The time was the mid-1700s. Most women in North America lived one kind of life. They cared for their husbands. They cared for their children. They cared for their home. The life of Abigail Adams was not the usual sort. She ran a farm. Child-raising fell mostly to her alone. She held many ideas. The ideas were about the United States. She thought about what the country should be. She thought about how the country should be. The 1795 painting shows her. Home care filled the lives of most women then. Husbands, children, and the house were that work. A farm was Abigail's work. The children were her work too. Ideas about the United States set her apart. She wanted a say in what the country should be. She wanted a say in how it should work. That mix made her life different from most women. North America in the mid-1700s is the setting.

2The childhood home of Abigail Adams is in Weymouth. Weymouth is in Massachusetts. November 11, 1744, is the birth date of Abigail. The birthplace was Massachusetts. Massachusetts was one of thirteen American colonies. Great Britain ruled those colonies. New Hampshire was one colony. Massachusetts was one colony. Rhode Island was one colony. New York was one colony. New Jersey was one colony. Connecticut was one colony. Pennsylvania was one colony. Delaware was one colony. Maryland was one colony. Virginia was one colony. North Carolina was one colony. South Carolina was one colony. Georgia was one colony. The Atlantic Ocean lay beside the colonies. Abigail never attended school. Most girls then were kept out of school. Her mother taught her at home. Her mother was named Elizabeth. Elizabeth taught Abigail to read. Elizabeth taught Abigail to write. Abigail was a smart girl. She loved to read. She learned about the world from books. The books belonged to her father. He owned many books. November 11, 1744, is her birthday. Thirteen colonies sat under Great Britain. The Atlantic Ocean bordered that colonial coast. Home lessons from Elizabeth took the place of school. Her father's many books opened the world to her.

3Abigail married John Adams on October 25, 1764. They lived on a small farm that had been in John's family for many years. John worked as a lawyer, and Abigail managed the farm and the money. In 1768 they moved to Boston so John's law business could do better. John was often away because of his work. Their children helped with chores on the family farm. Their oldest son, John Quincy Adams, became the sixth president of the United States. A stamp showed that a tax had been paid to Great Britain. A painting of John Adams was made in 1783. Soon after the wedding, Great Britain began to treat the colonies unfairly. People had to pay many high taxes. Many people in the colonies, including Abigail and John, wanted to break away. They wanted the colonies to become their own country.

4In 1774, John was elected to represent Massachusetts at the First Continental Congress. The Congress talked about how the colonies could become an independent country. Abigail stayed home while John was away. She ran the farm and taught the children. A woman running a business was not common in those days. She hired people and bought land in John's name, because women could not buy land on their own. While they were apart, they wrote many letters. John often asked for her advice on politics and on other subjects. He respected her ideas. John was also elected to the Second Continental Congress. In her letters, Abigail told how people in the colonies thought and felt. The leaders listened to what she had to say. Abigail and John wrote more than one thousand letters during their marriage.

5In 1775, Congress declared the colonies independent from Great Britain, and a war began. During the war, Abigail made meals for soldiers and cared for ones who had been hurt. The war did not end until 1781. The Adams farmhouse in Massachusetts is part of the Adams National Historical Park. The colonies won the war and became an independent country. George Washington was elected the first president of the United States in 1789, and John became the first vice president. John and Abigail moved to Philadelphia, which was the capital at the time. Abigail did not like the capital and soon moved back to the farm. They continued to send letters.

6John became president in 1797. As First Lady, Abigail gave her husband advice on many things. She spoke out against slavery and argued for equal rights for women. Some people in the government did not like it when she spoke out. They sometimes called her Mrs. President. Abigail never stopped talking about what she believed was right. John and Abigail were the first president and First Lady to live in the White House. It was called the President's House at the time. A portrait of Abigail was painted in her later years. She was happy when John's four years as president were over. She wanted to return to the farm and live a quiet life. She was sick during many of her later years. She died at home on October 28, 1818.

7A statue of Abigail Adams stands in Boston, Massachusetts. Her ideas lived on after her death. In time those ideas helped make the United States a better place for many people. She showed that a First Lady can speak up and work for important causes. Her life and work are still remembered. Advice means suggestions or guidance. A capital is the city where a government's main offices are located. Colonies are areas or countries ruled by or belonging to another country. Elected means chosen by a vote to join the government. Equal rights are freedoms and powers that match those of other groups. First Lady means the wife of a president or another head of state. Independent means not controlled or ruled by others. Politics means activities of government. Respected means treated with honor.

11. Acadia, Where Mountains Meet the Sea

1A map sets Mount Desert Island on the coast of Maine. Six parts make up this book. Early sunrise comes first. Land against the ocean comes second. Wildlife comes third. A park built piece by piece comes fourth. A closing comes fifth. Word meanings come last. Rocky shore at Acadia catches the rising Sun. Few spots in the United States see sunrise sooner than this park. A map explains the head start. Maine sticks out farther east than the rest of the forty-nine states. Tall peaks stand in Acadia. Those peaks take the earliest rays.

2Yearly visits to Acadia National Park pass two million. Small space holds a lot. Beautiful coast is here. Islands are here. Forests are here. Lakes are here. Rocks are here. Water adds beauty. Stone adds beauty. Trees add beauty. The year 2010 put President Barack Obama on Cadillac Mountain. His family hiked with him. Mountains here climb out of the sea. Cadillac Mountain is the tallest. Height is 1,530 feet, or 466 m. The view is amazing. Crowds are the result. No other spot in the park is more crowded. Coastline runs past 40 miles, or 64 km. Low tide leaves pools behind. Sea stars live in the pools. Sea cucumbers live in them. Hot pink seaweed lives in them. Other sea life lives in them too.

3Tide pools vanish and return. Tides do the same. Go while the water is falling. More time then remains for looking. A child studies a tide pool at Acadia. A starfish rests in a pool. Sea star is the other name for a starfish. Acadia's smallest falcon is the American kestrel. Bird-watching here ranks among the country's best. The bird list has reached 338 kinds. Autumn sends thousands of raptors over the park. Canada is where that flight begins. Winter is where the flight is headed.

4A metal band goes onto a peregrine chick's leg. A scientist does the fitting. Bands let scientists track actions and routes. Speed puts peregrine falcons near the front of all animals on Earth. Old nesting sites were Acadia's mountains. Speed did not save them from people. Human actions killed a great many. The 1960s found the park empty of these falcons. Years of work aimed at bringing them back. Chicks returned in 1991. Thirty-five years had passed without them. Every year since, the park has had new chicks. Baby foxes live here too. The American mink lives here. The porcupine lives here. Acadia covers less land than plenty of other national parks. Large mammals are few. Small mammals are many. Foxes are among them. Porcupines are among them.

5Becoming a national park at all may be Acadia's most amazing fact. Wabanaki people, who are Native, lived on this land across 12,000 years. People of the Dawnland is what Wabanaki means. Canoes were built by them. Fishing was part of life. Hunting was part of life. Plants were gathered round about. Five tribes make up the Wabanaki today. Northern Maine is home for them. Parts of Canada are home too. Near Acadia, canoes and baskets are still made as ancestors made them. Hunters who visit can hire guides. Smoked shellfish is sold. Teaching happens each year at the Native American Festival. A basket makers' market happens there as well. Birch trees nearby supply canoe bark. The 1600s brought French settlers who took Wabanaki land. The 1700s brought British settlers moving north. They fought the French. Fighting killed many Wabanaki. Diseases from the Europeans killed many too. Others were pushed out. Statehood for Maine arrived in 1820. Wealthy buyers wanted the rocky coast. Fancy summer houses went up. George Dorr owned one of those large houses.

6Protection of the land had been a Wabanaki practice for a very long time. Wealthy owners later wanted protection too. George Dorr gave land to the government. Others gave land too. The year 1919 turned that gifted land into a national park. Dorr's house was grand. Mount Desert Island is where it stood. He lived alone. Saving the area meant buying small pieces, then giving those pieces away. Death came in 1944. His money was gone by then. The dream was not gone. Acadia stood as a national park. Growth continued, piece after donated piece, in the manner of a many-patch quilt. Gifts of nearby land still arrive from landowners. Dorr looks across the park his work helped create. Sand Beach holds two young visitors. They are walking after a hike. Sea and mountain meet in this park. People come for that meeting. They come for sunrise too. After the new day is greeted, more remains. Hiking is one choice. Climbing is one. Fishing is one. A bicycle ride is one.

7Cadillac Mountain is a sunrise seat for visitors. If a visit can happen, it is worth making. Word meanings come next. Donate means to give something as a gift. A fund or a cause can receive it. Contribute is another word for this. Mammals are animals that are warm-blooded. Hair or fur covers them. They nurse their young. Babies are born live. Migrate means a move between habitats or between regions. The move comes at a set time each year. A national park is land owned by the government. Protection is the point. History can be the reason. Nature can be the reason. Raptors are hunting birds. Small animals are their food. Tides are the ocean rising and falling without a stop. Gravity from the Moon helps drive them. Gravity from the Sun helps drive them too.

12. Animals of the Ocean Community

1Many different types of animals live in the ocean. They do not live as separate strangers. An ocean community is the group they make. Some of these animals stay in the water all the time. Others leave the water for part of the time. They spend that part on land. This book asks you to meet some of them. Mammals make up most of the animals in these pages. Some of the animals are fish. Some of the animals are reptiles. A mammal is one group you will meet. A fish is another group. A reptile is another group. All of them belong to the ocean community. Water is home for every animal in these pages. Land is also part of life for some of them. You will meet animals that swim. You will meet animals that also use land. The community is full of different types. The pages ahead introduce some members. Keep the three groups in mind. The groups are mammals, fish, and reptiles. Some members never come out of the water. Some members do come out. Both kinds still belong to one ocean community. Let us meet them.

2I am a dolphin. My body is sleek. My tail is powerful. Body and tail help me swim fast. They also help me swim with strength. I live with other dolphins. Our group is called a pod. We whistle to communicate. We click to communicate. We squeak to communicate. I am a mammal. I am not a fish. I do not have gills. I swim up to the surface so I can breathe. I do that a few times every minute. My brain is large for my body size. People call me one of the ocean's smartest animals. I like to eat fish. I also eat jellyfish. I also eat shrimp. I also eat squid. I also eat octopus. Now a different animal speaks. I am a walrus. Every walrus lives as I do. I live on ice. I live in cold water. Warmth comes from my thick fat layer. My tusks are ivory. Very long teeth are what those tusks really are. I use the tusks to dig for clams. I use them when I move around. I use them to protect myself. Whiskers grow around my mouth. The whiskers help me sense food. The food sits on the dark ocean floor. My flippers are flat. Flat flippers let me swim. They also let me flop around on land. I make loud snorts. I make barks. Those sounds let me communicate with other walruses.

3My head shape gives me my name. I am a hammerhead shark. Each eye sits at the end of a lobe. I have two lobes. That eye placement lets me scan the ocean. I scan for prey. My large head pins stingrays down. I pin them to the ocean floor. Stingrays are my favorite food. Daytime is when I go along with other hammerhead sharks. We travel in large schools. At night I prefer to hunt alone. Now a different animal speaks. People know me for a bark like a dog. California sea lion is my name. People think I am noisy. People think I am playful. I am also intelligent. My fur is short. My flippers feel like leather. Those traits let me swim very fast. Other sea lions share my home. We live in colonies. Our colonies sit on rocky coastlines. We lounge on rocks. The sun shines on us there. We dive into the water to catch food. We catch fish. We catch octopus. We catch squid. I can stay underwater while I swim. That swim can last ten minutes.

4I am an octopus. My body is soft. I have eight arms. Suckers cover those arms. I have no skeleton. I can squeeze into very small spaces. An arm may be lost. A new arm grows in its place. I can shoot black ink. I can change my skin color. Ink and color help me hide from predators. They also help me hide from prey. Now a different animal speaks. Great white shark is the name I carry. I am large. I am a fierce predator. No animal of the ocean hunts me. I have almost three thousand teeth. The teeth are sharp. A tooth may fall out. A tooth may break. A new tooth grows back. I am a skilled hunter. I can sense blood in the water. Even a tiny amount is enough. I can sense it from far away. I eat seals. I eat dolphins. I eat fish. My name comes from my white underbelly. My back is gray. Gray lets me blend into the dark ocean. Then I can surprise prey.

5My brain is bigger than the brain of any other fish. That is why I am intelligent. Every manta ray is intelligent in this way. No ray in the world is larger than I am. My body is huge. My body is flat. My body is wide. My fins look like wings. Stingrays are my relatives. I do not sting. People like to watch me leave the water. I jump out. I make a big splash.
