Now a different animal speaks. I am a sea horse. My name confuses people. I am not a horse. I am a fish. I have no teeth. I have no stomach. Eating all the time still pleases me. Shrimp is a food I especially want. I do not chew. I swallow food through my snout. I am the slowest fish. Only a small fin sits on my back. That fin helps me move through the water. I can hold still in one place. I latch my tail onto coral. I can also latch onto seagrass. Trouble may come. Then I change color. I also change shape. A male sea horse carries the eggs. The female does not carry them. He keeps the eggs in a pouch. He keeps them until they hatch.

6No turtle in the world is bigger than I am. I am a leatherback turtle. Most turtles have shells. I am different from them. Leathery skin covers my body. Egg-laying time comes. I crawl onto the beach. I dig a hole. I lay the eggs in that hole. I lay from eighty to one hundred eggs. I cover the eggs with sand. I dive deep in the ocean. I can stay underwater for a long stretch. The stretch is longer than an hour. I travel long distances. Jellyfish are my favorite food. Now a different animal speaks. I am a blue whale. No larger mammal has ever lived. My tongue is huge. My tongue has the same weight as an elephant. I send messages to other blue whales. I make deep sounds. The sounds travel through water. They travel a long way. I have no teeth for biting. I have no teeth for chewing. I have rows of baleen. Baleen hangs from my upper jaw. Baleen works like a strainer. I gulp a large amount of water. My tongue pushes that water out. Baleen traps my favorite food in my mouth. The food is tiny animals. People call those animals krill.

7A reptile is a cold-blooded animal. Scales may cover its body. Horny plates may cover its body. Prey are animals that get hunted. Predators are the hunters. Predators eat the prey. A predator hunts other animals so it can survive. Pollution can be an act. Pollution can be a result. Harmful materials go into the air. They may go into the water. They may go into the soil. A mammal is a warm-blooded animal. Hair may cover a mammal. Fur may cover a mammal. A lobe is a body section. A lobe can also be part of an organ. The section is rounded or curved. Latching means fastening onto something. It can mean attaching to another object. Endangered means a living thing is in danger. The danger is dying out completely. Blend means not being noticed. Likeness in looks is the reason.

13. Around the World on the Equator

1Java holds a Buddhist temple. No other Buddhist temple is larger. Pack light clothes for the trip. Pack a hat so the Sun does not burn you. Bring insect repellent. In the places you will visit, the bugs are big. Add an umbrella. Rain falls often. Bring a camera. Monkeys might appear. You might see parrots. You might see pythons. The trip follows zero degrees latitude. That line is the equator. Say it ee-KWAY-ter. The equator is an imaginary line. It divides the Northern Hemisphere from the Southern Hemisphere. It crosses many continents. It crosses many countries. It crosses many cultures. A map in this book marks the places you will read about.

2Singapore has many skyscrapers. It also has a busy harbor. Your plane nears an airport in Southeast Asia. Singapore appears as you look down. Shiny towers make this small island country seem very large. Hundreds of ships sit in the harbor. One of the busiest ports in the world is here in Singapore. You start to explore the city. You can hardly breathe in the tropical heat. You find an outdoor market. The plants for sale tell you the climate is hot. Sugar cane grows well here. Limes grow well here. Papayas love the hot, wet weather. Orchids love it too. Charts show the climate at one degree north latitude. They give average temperatures for six months. January is 85°F. February is 87°F. March is 88°F. April is 89°F. May is 88°F. June is 88°F. The charts also give average precipitation. January has 7.8 inches. February has 6.1 inches. March has 6.7 inches. April has 5.6 inches. May has 6.2 inches. June has 5.5 inches.

3Volcanoes built the island of Java. Active ones are still among them. Your next stop is Java. Java is one island among the thousands in Indonesia. You ride a train through the lowlands. The train has no air-conditioning. The ride is hot. Far away, volcano tops poke through the clouds. Paddies pass by your window. A paddy is a watery field. Rice grows in those fields. The soil from volcanoes is good for crops. Small villages stop the train through the whole day. At every stop you see tall spires. The spires belong to mosques. Say that word MOSKS. A mosque is a place of worship. It belongs to the Islamic religion. Most people in Indonesia are Muslims. Five times each day, men call people to pray. Their calls sound beautiful. Muslims meet at the mosques in Indonesia to worship.

4People on Bali cut terraces into the land. They farm on hills. They farm on mountains. A short ocean ferry takes you from Java to Bali. Volcano slopes are home for many people on the island. The terraces look like giant stair steps. The steps make flat fields. Farmers grow rice in those fields. Hindu temples stand everywhere. The people of Bali are Hindus. They leave food at the temples. They leave flowers too. The gifts are offerings for the gods. Then you leave Indonesia. The flight heads east, still on the equator. For hours, the only sight is the vast Pacific Ocean. The plane lands in Manaus, in Brazil. Say Manaus mah-NOWS. The city stands on the Amazon River. In the old part of the city, you find a fish market. Shoppers in Brazil buy fresh fish there. The market runs for two city blocks. Row after row of tables holds thousands of fish. Some of the fish are six feet long. That length is 1.8 m.

5A small river carries your boat. That river flows into the Amazon. The rainforest makes a jungle symphony. Birds call. Monkeys call. Insects answer one another. Huge trees bend over the water. Ferns bend over it too. Vines do the same. A houseboat moves along the Amazon River in Brazil. At the edge of the river, a house stands on stilts. The water can rise by as much as thirty feet. It can also fall by that much. People put homes on stilts because of that change. The homes are wood. The roofs are made of leaves. The rainforest has very few roads. Most people live beside the waterways. Farms are almost nowhere in sight. The forest is too thick for farms. The soil is too poor. Thunder booms without warning. Black clouds open. Heavy rain falls. The rain lasts fifteen minutes and then ends. It ends as suddenly as it started. The steamy heat comes back.

6You are still in Manaus. You check the weather in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. Rain gear will be needed. The flight goes all night. The flight crosses the Atlantic Ocean. You wake as the plane is about to land. The city is Kinshasa. Kinshasa is the capital. It sits on the Congo River. The Congo is a long river. Central Africa's rainforest is drained by this river. Walking in the city, a new kind of music reaches you. The sound leads you to a cafe. People say the music is ndombolo. They also call it Afro Pop. People in the Congo created this popular music. People all over the world know it. In a Kinshasa market, a woman carries fresh vegetables.

7In the Congo, a traditional meal is goat stew served with foufou. You are hungry. You ask the waitress what dish is usual there. She brings foufou. The dish is a paste. The paste may be plantain. It may be yam. It may be cassava. The cook rolls the paste into a ball. Then the ball is fried. The meal may come with salted fish. It may come with barbecued goat meat. Mount Kenya rises above the forest. Snow covers the mountain.

8The next stop is Kenya. Africa's east coast is where you find Kenya. You leave the capital, Nairobi. Say Nairobi nye-ROH-bee. A five-day trip begins. It starts in the town of Nanyuki. Sirimon Park begins after you step across the equator. On the first day you hike through bamboo. Bamboo is a grass that grows like a tree. Hot, wet weather makes it grow fast. Day two has no trees. Low grasses cover the land. The third day brings another change in the land. It is rocky. Little plant life grows there. Breathing is hard. You feel freezing cold. Heavy clothes do not warm you. You wonder how this can be. You are standing on the Equator. Your feet are in snow. The trail leads up Mount Kenya. The mountain rises 17,040 feet. That height is 5,194 m. Away from the equator, day length changes with the season. Summer days grow longer. Winter days grow shorter. On the equator, each day stays close to twelve hours. Each night stays close to twelve hours too. The season does not change that pattern. There is something special about the equator. It gets lots of sunshine. It often gets lots of rain too. It is not in the north. It is not in the south. Winter, summer, spring, and fall do not differ much. On this journey, heat made you drip with sweat. Heavy rain made you run for cover. You saw huge rivers carrying water out of rainforests. Snow on Mount Kenya was part of what you saw. Outside a village in Bali, rice farmers work terraced land. The trip showed many ways to live. In a big city such as Singapore, some people work in cool buildings. Those buildings have air-conditioning. In the Amazon rainforest, other people raise their homes above the ground. They do this so the homes stay dry. The trip along zero latitude showed a clear lesson. Variety shows up all over the world. That is true even on one line of latitude.

9These words help you read the journey. Cassava is a plant grown in the tropics. People can eat its roots. The equator is a made-up line around Earth. It sits halfway between the poles. A substance called insect repellent sends insects away. It discourages insects that bite or sting. A mosque is a worship place in the religion of Islam. A plantain is a kind of wild banana. It grows in the tropics. A rainforest is a thick forest. It usually grows in a tropical place. It gets a great deal of rain. Many kinds of animals and plants live there. Stilts are posts. They hold a building above the ground. They can also hold it above the water. A symphony is a classical work for a whole orchestra. In this book the word pictures a mix of sounds. A terrace is a wide step cut into a hillside. The step makes flat ground where people can farm. Tropical describes a hot, humid climate. That climate is common at latitudes near the equator. The journey names these places. It names Kinshasa and Democratic Republic of the Congo. It names the Amazon rainforest and Bali. It names the Congo River. It names Manaus in Brazil. It names the equator and Mount Kenya. It names Indonesia, Java, and Singapore.

14. Art You Can Find Nearby

1You can read about painters, sculptors, potters, and glassblowers. You can also read about fiber artists. Some art in this book is unusual. Other parts tell where art is found. The book begins with an introduction to art.

2Art is all around us in many forms. Artists may use paint, paper, glass, clay, stone, computers, or plants. An artist can make almost anything into art. Alfred Quiroz painted a picture of himself. It is a self-portrait. Painters may paint objects or famous people, use a brush, splash or drip paint, or use mud.

3Painters paint on every kind of surface. Some paint on dishes, windows, or furniture. Murals are large paintings on a wall or the side of a building. Sculptors may carve wood, stone, ice, or wax with sharp tools. Others weave soft sculptures from fabrics, yarns, or leather strips. Some cast shapes and statues in metal.

4Metal casting can use bronze. First, sculptors carve a clay model of the shape. Wet plaster covers the clay and hardens into a mold, like a cast on a broken arm. Hot liquid metal is poured in, cools, and hardens. Then they remove the plaster and polish the art. Potters pinch, push, and pull wet clay on a fast wheel to make pottery.

5The clay is baked in a very hot oven called a kiln. The heat bakes out the water, and the dry clay is called ceramic. A glaze is special paint that makes ceramic shiny and colorful. Melted glass is gooey, like heavy syrup. A glassblower blows a bubble with a blowpipe, a long hollow metal tube. The hot bubble can be rotated, swirled, or made wide, long, thin, or tall.

6Hot glass is soft and can be shaped in many ways. Artists may add colorful streaks, swirls, a handle, or knobs. Glassblowers work quickly, but they must be careful. When glass cools, it breaks easily. Fiber artists use thread, yarns, and fabric pieces. Weavers loop yarns on a loom to make rugs, blankets, mats, wall hangings, and cloth.

7Patterned quilts and dolls are popular fiber art. Quilters sew pieces of cut fabric together to make quilts. Years ago, new fabric was expensive, so people used scraps for bed covers. Some artists make found object art from junk, car parts, old toys, wire, and scrap. Others put an everyday object, like a water fountain, in a museum. A funny face used a wooden cutting board, a circuit board, telephone bells, a keypad, industrial springs, and wire.

8Many sand castles are true works of art. Some artists make huge shapes on beaches with rocks and sand. Others use driftwood, or branches, rocks, and plants, in a garden or park. An installation makes a whole room or building into art. The room may hold objects, colors, shapes, sounds, and lights. You can walk inside, look, and touch parts of the work.

9The oldest art was painted in caves or scratched into rocks. These simple images tell stories about life long ago. Scientists find pottery, wall murals, carvings, and tile mosaic floors in ancient ruins. A Pueblo pot is one kind of art. Art is everywhere, so explore your town. Visit museums, galleries, and art fairs, and see public art in many shapes and sizes.

10A mural of a lighthouse can create an interesting illusion. Elephant sculptures can add fun to a park. Look for outdoor sculptures in a park and murals on a walk or a bike ride. You can draw on sidewalks with colored chalks. You can sculpt with clay, or make a snow sculpture or a sand castle. Glue shells, stones, and wood to a frame for a favorite photograph.

11Art does not have to be serious or hard to do. Art can tell a story or help you say how you feel. It can just be interesting, funny, or beautiful. Art makes our lives more fun. Look, and you will find art all around you. It is everywhere!

15. Bamboo Bears of the Mountains

1On all fours, a giant panda stands 70 to 80 centimeters tall. That height is 28 to 30 inches. Standing up, it can reach 170 centimeters. That height is 66 inches. From nose to tail it measures 1.83 meters. That length is 6 feet. Wild pandas weigh 90.7 to 109 kilograms. That weight is 200 to 240 pounds. Zoo pandas weigh more. Males are a bit larger than females. Giant pandas are some of the best loved animals on Earth. Thick fur makes them easy to know. The fur is black and white. A giant panda is a kind of bear. Wild ones remain in only one region. Forested mountains in central China are their only wild home. Rarity has turned them into a sign of a bigger idea. People use them as a sign for endangered animals on Earth. A map shows where they used to live. The same map shows where they live now.

2Other bears hibernate. Pandas do not. A panda must eat through the whole year. Only then does it get enough nutrients. The animals chew bamboo. Panda is a name for an eater of bamboo. Bamboo is a very tall kind of grass. It grows in plenty where pandas live. Those mountains are cold. Those mountains are damp. Almost every meal is bamboo. Bamboo holds few of the nutrients a panda needs. So the animal must eat a huge amount of it. An adult may spend sixteen hours of a day at this meal. It may eat up to 18 kilograms of bamboo. That amount is 40 pounds. A newborn panda is only the size of a hamster. The baby is 900 times smaller than its mother. Giant pandas are mammals. Every mammal gives birth to live young. Every mammal nurses those young. A female may start having cubs at age five. She may start at age six. After that, she has only one or two cubs. Years pass between births. Caring for even one baby is a full-time job. She usually raises a single cub. Any other cub is ignored. The ignored cub dies because no one cares for it.

3A newborn panda is tiny. It is blind. It needs its mother for everything. Predators can harm it because it is so helpless. Growth is slow. Walking starts only at four months. Nursing ends around the first birthday. Bamboo eating starts then. For two years, the cub stays near her. Forest living is one lesson. It works out which plants make the best food. It learns which animals are friends. It learns which animals are enemies. Leopards are a young panda's greatest danger. Wild dogs are another great danger. One photo shows a mother raising two cubs. That mother is not a wild panda. A wild mother usually raises one cub at a time. By age three, a panda is grown. It settles in a home range. It eats alone inside that area. Each panda warns others to stay out. One warning is a smelly liquid from its body. The panda rubs that liquid on tree trunks. Scratch marks on bark send a warning too. The scratches sit at the edge of the home range. Are these bears truly giant? They are actually among the smaller bears. The word giant sets them apart from red pandas. Red pandas share the bamboo forests of China. A picture sets a grizzly bear beside a giant panda.

4In a fight, male pandas do not bite. They do not scratch. They wrestle. A female usually lives alone inside her home range. Males travel farther. Males notice their neighbors more. When mating time comes, a male knows where females are. He also knows where rival males are. Males fight over which one may mate. They push one another. They bump one another. Farmers in China often cut bamboo forest. They want the land for fields. Giant pandas could disappear forever. Nobody knows the exact number left. The animals are hard to find. They are hard to count. Scientists think about 1,864 still live in the wild. Loss of forest homes is the biggest threat. China's population is growing quickly. People clear bamboo woods for timber. They clear them for farms. Hunters are another threat. They want to sell the coats. A slow panda is an easy target. China now has strict laws that guard pandas. More than sixty preserves guard the habitat.

5Scientists raise cubs in zoos so pandas can survive. Many zoos run breeding programs. Scientists study ways to help wild pandas last. People in many countries work hard at this goal. They want giant pandas to stay alive. The two pandas are not close kin, even though the names sound alike. Raccoons are the red panda's family. They share the bamboo forests where giant pandas live. Endangered means an animal could be gone for good. Extinct means wiped out completely. A habitat is the natural place where a plant or an animal lives. Nutrients are parts of food. Those parts feed the body. A predator is an animal that hunts other animals for food. A preserve is land set aside to guard living things. Vulnerable means easy to hurt.

16. Bands of Color in the Sky

1Six parts make up this book. Tricks that light can play come first. Places and times for rainbows come next. The making of a rainbow comes after that. Colors come next. A closing comes after the colors. Word meanings come last. A rain shower can end. The Sun can then return. That is a good moment to go outside. A giant rainbow may be waiting. It may curve over the sky. A big painting is what it can resemble. A window can break sunlight into a surprise. A small rainbow may appear close to you. Storm clouds can have a rainbow near them.

2Water that splashes can hold a rainbow. A splash rainbow usually fades quickly. What you see is an optical illusion. Eyes can find it. Hands cannot hold it. Walking around it is impossible. A side view is impossible. Step toward an outdoor rainbow and it seems to retreat. Light is the maker. Water is one thing the light must pass through. A clear object can play that role too. Outdoors, rainbows usually need rain and sunlight in the same place. They are lovely tricks. Science has solved their secrets. A bow made by the Sun has a fixed side of the sky. Turn so the Sun is behind you. The bow sits in the sky on the far side. Check the weather at the next sighting. Rain or mist should be ahead. Sunshine should come from the rear. The bow itself sits inside that wet air. Two needs must be met in nature. Air must hold water. Light must be direct. A sky packed with clouds will not produce a bow.

3Morning is often the best hour for a sighting. Late afternoon is often just as good. The Sun is then in the best spot to build a bow. Luck can add a second bow. Luck can even add a third. Study the outer band when two bows show. Color order there runs the other way. It does not match the inner band. Waterfall mist can hide a bow as well. A splashing fountain can build a small one. A lawn sprinkler in sunshine can stage a bright show. Even a bright moon can build a bow. Moonbow is the name for that kind. Dimmer light is what a moonbow gives. Rarity is greater too.

4Cameras have gone high into the air. They flew above a waterfall among the largest on Earth. The picture they caught was a rainbow in a full circle. Someone on the ground sees a curved arch. A half circle is the usual look. The true shape is the whole circle. Ground level usually hides the lower half. An airline pilot can be lucky. From the air, the full circle may be visible. Myths keep rainbows too. Viking stories made a rainbow into a bridge. Earth stood at one end. A kingdom of Viking gods stood at the other end. Irish tales speak of leprechauns. Those elves, the stories say, watch over pots of gold. Rainbow ends are the hiding spots. Indra, a Hindu god, is drawn with a rainbow. He treats it as an archer's bow. Lightning is what his arrows are made of. Rainbows fascinated a French scientist. His name was René Descartes. Say it as re-NAY day-KART. More than four hundred years have passed since his birth. He wanted a deeper understanding. Sunlight was his tool. A large round mass of water was his other tool. He learned a rule about the viewer. Only the correct angle lets the bow appear. His ideas are still studied. New work still grows from them.

5Traveling light is the key to a rainbow. Speed is not the same in every medium. Picture a run on solid ground. Then picture a run through water up to the knees. Water makes the runner slower. Light acts in a matching way. Air lets light move faster than water does. Water is the denser material. Thicker is another word for denser. Entry into water makes light slow down. The path also bends away from the old line. Refraction is the name of that bend. A pencil in a water glass shows the bend. The pencil seems to kink where it meets the water. Nature builds a rainbow at a meeting point. Direct light must strike raindrops. Each drop bends the sunlight on the way in. Inside, the light bounces off the rear wall of the drop. A mirror sends light back in that same style. Exit brings a second bend. Millions of drops do this together. Every color of the bow comes from that shared action. A diagram uses three stages. Stage one is entry. Sunlight bends on the way in. It then separates into colors. Stage two is the bounce off the rear of the drop. Stage three is the second bend, during the exit.

6Four things make the recipe. Sunlight is one. Raindrops are one. Light that has already bounced is one. A viewer is one. Every rainbow color is already inside sunlight. Raindrops disperse the beam. Split is what disperse means. Each separate drop does the splitting. Colored light then leaves toward the viewer. The angle differs a little from drop to drop. Drops along the top send back red. Their position is right for that color. Drops along the bottom send back only violet. No other color comes from those drops. Bands sometimes blur together. A bright bow can show all seven colors in a clean way. Top-to-bottom order does not change. A double bow has an outer band that reverses the order. Memory has a helper name. The name is ROY G. BIV. Seven main colors sit inside that name. Red is first. Orange is second. Yellow is third. Green is fourth. Blue is fifth. Indigo is sixth. Violet is seventh. A homemade bow is possible. Use a clear glass. Fill it about three-quarters of the way with water. Put the glass where sunlight can travel through the water. Hold a white sheet of paper in the light that comes out. Move the sheet until the bow appears. Colored pencils can mark each band on the paper. Count the bands. Note the color that leads.

7Most people stop when a rainbow appears. Awe is the feeling. Art through the ages has grown from these colors. Poetry has grown from them too. Stories around the world include them. Fairy tales include them too. Science can say how the illusion works. Water drops bend light. They split light. They send light back. Many colors then reach our eyes. Magic is still how the sudden beauty feels. Iceland has a waterfall with a rainbow at its base. Tourists stop there to admire it. Word meanings come next. An angle is the viewing position. It is also the direction of an approach. Indigo is the light color found between blue and violet. Mediums are substances that can carry something through them. Optical illusions are images. They fool the mind into a view that is not the real one. Reflects means light, sound, or heat is sent back toward its source. Refraction is a bend in light or in another energy wave. Certain mediums cause that bend.

17. Beasts from Greek Myths

1A map draws ancient Greece around the year 750 bce. Macedonia appears on it. Troy appears on it. Asia Minor appears on it. The Aegean Sea appears, along with Delphi and Athens. Mycenae appears. Europe, Italy, and Sparta appear. Crete appears. Africa appears. Heracles is a hero from Greece. His name is said HAIR-uh-kleez. An old picture shows him petting Cerberus. Cerberus is the watchdog of the underworld. The dog may have two heads. It may have three. Myths are stories people use to grasp important ideas. Good is one of those ideas. Evil is another. Fantastic creatures held an important place in Greek myths. One vase shows a battle. Heracles fights in it. The goddess Athena fights beside him. The enemy is the Hydra. That monster is made of snakes.

2Centaurs joined a human head to a human chest. A horse’s body completed the form. Ancient Greeks felt these beings were unlike them. The centaurs seemed animal-like. Feelings, not calm thought, drove them. Their story opens with Ixion, a king of Greece. He went to live among the gods. Zeus learned that Ixion loved the wife of Zeus. A test was Zeus’s answer. He made a cloud. The cloud copied the looks of his wife. Ixion gave his love to the cloud. He was sure it was the wife he desired. The first centaur was born from that cloud. Zeus set Ixion onto a wheel after that. Round and round the wheel would turn, and it would never stop. Old artwork often shows centaurs defeating Greeks in battle. One sculpture of that victory comes from Athens. The piece stood on a temple built in the fifth century bce. Pictures of myths let Greeks express feelings. The feelings concerned events of their own time. One enemy was the Persians. Greeks connected that enemy with the centaurs. A guest and a host were tied by a duty that mattered. Ixion’s tale, and the birth of the first centaur, shows this duty. Ixion had come as Zeus’s guest. What followed was a warning. Bad treatment of a host could end in harm.

3Sirens had heads like human women. Their bodies were the bodies of birds. Lovely songs pulled sailors into a crash on the sirens’ island. The myth points to a Greek concern. Self-control was that concern. In the tale, the sirens had once been human maids. They served the goddess Persephone. Her name is said pur-SE-fuh-nee. She was gathering flowers when a god took her away. The sirens used wings to search. They never found her. An island then became their home. Many sailors were drawn to that island by the songs. Few sailors managed to pass by. One man made music so loud the song could not reach him. Another man was tied onto his ship. His crew blocked their ears with wax. An ancient Greek vase shows Odysseus in that fix. He is tied to the ship. The song reaches him, yet he survives. These beings gave us the word siren. A siren is a device that sends out a loud warning. They also gave us the phrase siren song. It is something so pleasing that you want it anyway. You want it even though it is dangerous.

4The sirens were famous because they were beautiful. Medusa was famous because she looked frightening. Medusa and her two sisters carried the name Gorgons. Life without end was promised to the sisters. Medusa herself could be defeated by a human. Looking into her eyes could turn a person to stone. An evil king begins the tale. He was in love with a boy’s mother. The boy’s name was Perseus. The king hoped to move Perseus aside. He ordered Perseus to bring back Medusa’s head. Perseus had promised obedience. Any task the king named, he would do. The king expected failure. Help was given. With that help, Perseus removed Medusa’s head. Two creatures came out of her body. Some people see her death as a drought coming to an end. In that view, the creatures stand for good things that arrive next. Crops are one of those things. Water is the other. Greeks believed Gorgon faces could protect them. The faces could also hold evil back. Pictures of Gorgons were put in many places. Shields were one of those places.

5The Minotaur mixed human parts with animal parts. The head belonged to a bull. A man’s body formed the rest. Fear was so great that a maze became the prison. The maze was beneath a palace. The palace belonged to the king of Crete. A party of young people was sent to the monster. Seven of them were boys. Seven of them were girls. Theseus was a brave boy. He asked to join that group. The princess of Crete saw him. She fell in love. Her wish was to save him from the Minotaur. She handed him yarn wound into a ball. He was told to unwind it on the walk through the maze. A weapon was given to him as well. He defeated the monster. The line of yarn then guided him out. Bulls mattered greatly to a goddess of Crete. They may also have represented the island. Some readers see a meaning in his win. The win stands for Greeks taking control of Crete.

6Typhon was a giant, and the name is said TY-fon. The Minotaur was tiny when set beside him. Wings grew on Typhon. Snakes served as his legs. He carried one hundred heads. Only one of those heads was human. The remaining heads came from other creatures. Typhon stood for nature when nature is at its strongest. Zeus was the only match for him. Zeus ruled as king of the gods. Their battle caused the entire world to shake. Zeus won. He then locked Typhon underneath the earth. Greeks held Typhon responsible for powerful winds. One belief brought comfort. Zeus, they felt, was stronger than those natural forces.

7These creatures were not placed in myths only so people would be entertained. They were not placed there only so people would feel fear. The beings, and the tales about them, helped listeners understand the world. That world was the one surrounding them then. In the Greece of long ago, mythical creatures mattered. The amazing tales are still loved now. Logos for many companies borrow creatures out of Greek myth. Versace is a company that makes clothing. It uses Medusa’s head. The name is said vur-SAH-chee. Mobil is a company that sells gas. It uses Pegasus. Pegasus is the horse with wings that belonged with Perseus. A siren is the logo chosen by Starbucks Coffee Company. A creature is an animal. The word fits best when the animal is strange or unusual. To defeat someone is to win a victory over that one. A drought is a long dry stretch. Rain may be scarce. Rain may be missing entirely. Enemies are people or things that hate others. They may hurt others. They may threaten others. Events are happenings of importance. Myths are stories handed down over time. They can explain events in history. They can explain things in nature. They can explain social customs. Supernatural elements often appear in them. Self-control is the power to manage emotions. It is also the power to manage behavior. Settled means a person moved and then made a home. Unwind means to roll something open. It can also mean to uncoil it.

18. Bessie Coleman Learns to Fly

1This book’s parts come in order. First is an introduction. Next is a Texas childhood. Then a move to Chicago. Then a journey to France. Then a triumphant return. Then barnstormer work. Then Coleman’s legacy. A glossary ends the book. During the 1920s, only a small number of women flew planes. Their country was the United States. Coleman was Black. She was also Native American. She was a woman. A pilot like that seemed unthinkable. She flew anyway. Discrimination blocked the dream of piloting. She fought the block. Determination opened a door for later people. Women could enter. Black Americans could enter. Native Americans could enter.

2The birth year was 1892. Atlanta, Texas, was the town. Her parents were both Black. Native American descent belonged to her father too. Sharecroppers describes the parents. Thirteen children filled the family. Bessie was among them. Two years later the family moved. Waxahachie, Texas, became home. The spoken form is WAK-sah-HACH-ee. Her father wanted good jobs from that busy town. School for Bessie started there. Miles of walking each day led to an all-Black school. A white school was closer. It would not admit her. She loved reading. She loved books. Math made her a whiz. Texas was the home state. Waxahachie, her childhood town, lies close to Dallas. Racial barriers in Texas troubled her father. He expected kinder treatment in Indian Territory. That region now lies inside Oklahoma. His wife refused to leave with him. He left at Bessie’s ninth year. Older brothers became adults around then. They left too. Her mother earned pay as a housekeeper. Younger sisters were in Bessie’s care. Between 1911 and 1919, one picture shows that town’s courthouse.

3Cotton was a major business across Texas. Harvest sent everyone in the family into the fields. Bessie hated picking that cotton. The labor was hot. The labor was boring. Black children lost school during harvest. She still completed the local school. All eight grades were finished. College was her decision after that. Washing clothes for pay was the job she took. Money went into savings. About four years of saving opened the door. She enrolled at Langston, Oklahoma. The school name was Oklahoma Colored Agricultural and Normal University. Langston University is the name now. One year used up the money. Home was required again. A picture shows cotton workers. Colored once named people who were not white. The word is out of use. People hear it as an insult. African American is preferred now. Black is preferred now. Two brothers had already traveled east. Chicago, Illinois, was their city. They sought work. They sought a better life. One brother sent word one day. He thought a good job waited in the big city. Coleman was twenty-three when she boarded the train. Chicago was the destination. The ride lasted a long time. Comfort was poor. Black passengers were put on hard wooden benches. Those seats were kept apart from white passengers. A picture shows Chicago in 1919.

4Chicago near the start of the 20th century kept Black people and white people in separate neighborhoods. Most Black residents had to accept that split. Every Southside business had Black owners. Barbershops were included. Coleman’s workplace was a barbershop. Stores had Black owners too. Restaurants did too. Banks did too. Nightclubs did too. Wealth reached many owners. She moved in with brothers Walter and John. Both wives lived in the same home. Five people used one small apartment. A class trained her as a manicurist. A job came quickly. White Sox Barbershop hired her. The shop was on Chicago’s Southside. World War I then drew America in. The years of fighting were 1914 to 1918. Her brothers enlisted. The army sent them to fight in France. Coleman followed the European fighting in newspapers. Brothers’ safety worried her. Brave pilots appeared in those papers too. Eugene Bullard, a Black pilot, flew for France. His example gave her a strong spark. A picture shows him. Homecoming brought war stories from the brothers. Teasing came with the stories. French women, they said, had more bravery than American women. Those French women, they said, could fly. The pilot stories fixed her aim. Piloting was the life she chose. She asked for teachers. Nobody would train a Black woman in flight.

5Coleman kept going. Robert Abbott, a friend, edited a newspaper for Black readers. His advice named France as the place for flying lessons. Large sums were required. Passage cost money. Lessons cost money. She began managing a restaurant. Saving continued. French study was added. The language came quickly. Abbott paid part of the passage. Other friends paid part too. A picture shows Abbott. A ship carried her to France in 1920. The Caudron brothers ran the school she found. They agreed to train her. She had crossed an ocean for the lessons. The trainer was a biplane. Wing pairs on it numbered two. Open cockpits on it numbered two. The front cockpit held the instructor. The rear cockpit held Coleman. She studied how he moved the controls. Wind made a roar. The engine made a roar. His directions were hard to catch. She still learned well. A few months brought her to solo flying. Her age in 1921 was twenty-nine. No Black American woman had been a pilot before her. She earned an international pilot’s license. No Black person had earned that license earlier. No Native American had earned it earlier. Controls on the oldest planes were extremely simple. A stick, much like a joystick, handled up and down. Side-to-side motion came from a rudder bar. The bar lay against the floor. Feet pushed the bar. The push went back and forth. Labels in the picture say Stick and Rudder bar.

6One picture shows a stunt plane at a modern air show. Coleman’s return to the United States came in the fall of 1921. Black Americans heard of her feat. White Americans heard of it too. Reporters asked her questions. Special events asked her to come. People offered applause. People offered respect. The pilot goal was not her last dream. She wanted a flying school that others could enter. Black people were the students she cared about most. The school needed money first. Air shows paid pilots better than other work. Her picture is dated September 4, 1922. During the 1920s, airplanes were almost unseen. Even fewer people had ridden in one. The planes were small. The planes were dangerous. People did not use them for trips. Two jobs covered most planes. Battles were one job. Air shows were the other job. Those shows ranked among the biggest events of the time. They ranked among the most exciting too. More French lessons had to come before she could join a show. Stunts were in those lessons. Tricks were in them too. Abbott then helped her arrange a show near New York City. The day was September 3, 1922. No air show before that day had a Black woman flying the plane.

7Word of her flying crossed the country. Barnstormer was the new name for her work. A barnstormer tours with air shows. The grounds were fields that farmers owned. Every crowd liked the act. The loop-the-loop was one trick. The plane climbed straight up. The plane turned upside down. Large loops crossed the sky. A picture shows the view from inside a loop-the-loop. Parachute jumps were famous too. She offered rides. Many Black passengers took a first flight with her. She also pressed against discrimination. Her rule was strict. Every person could attend events that included her. Race was no barrier. Gender was no barrier. A white-only crowd got no show. A white-only crowd got no speech. The picture’s early parachute matches the kind she used on jumps.

8A Curtiss Jenny is the pictured plane. Early shows depended on borrowed planes. Enough money later let her buy one. The purchase was a Curtiss Jenny. The engine jammed soon after. A crash followed. A leg was broken. Ribs were broken. Cuts were numerous. Three months passed in a hospital. As soon as she was released, she went back to flying. Women after her gained a path from that courage. Mae Jemison was one of those women. Space had not held a Black woman before Jemison. She traveled on the shuttle Endeavour. The year was 1992. Jemison says Coleman’s courage wins her admiration. April of 1926 brought a show Coleman accepted. She was thirty-four. The city was Jacksonville, Florida. A parachute jump was included. William Wills, her mechanic, went up with her. They planned to choose a jump spot. She did not fasten the seat belt. Leaning out of the plane was required. She was hunting a good place to jump. Without warning the plane spun. Coleman fell clear of it. The fall killed her. Then the airplane crashed. Wills was killed in that crash. People called it a tragedy. A true pioneer of aviation was lost. One of the country’s favorite pilots was lost too.

9After her death, a stamp from the U.S. Post Office honored her. One street in Chicago received her name. Chicago names May 2 as Bessie Coleman Day. Three years after the death, a chief dream arrived. The Bessie Coleman Aero Club opened its doors in Los Angeles. Black Americans had lacked a flight school until then. A coin honoring her was proposed in 1998. Only a small number of coins were made. Her short life still held big accomplishments. She remains an inspiration. She was a pioneer who broke new ground. She refused to let an undone thing stop a dream. American history places her first among Black women who piloted. It also places her first among Native American pilots. Passion stands level with those record-breaking achievements. Motivation stands level with them. Courage stands level with them. Biplane: a biplane carries its wings as two pairs. Discrimination: discrimination means treatment that lacks fairness. A single person may face it. A whole group may face it. Race, age, sex, religion, or other differences may drive it. Enlisted: enlisted can mean signed up or enrolled. Gender: gender is the way a person is counted as male, as female, or as somewhere between. Society and culture supply the roles and the expectations for that identity. Manicurist: a manicurist looks after fingernails, and polish is part of the care. Mechanic: a mechanic repairs machines such as cars, planes, and other moving vehicles. Race: race sorts humans into groups by skin color and other physical features. Sharecroppers: sharecroppers farm on another farmer’s land and hand over part of the crop or part of the profit. Solo: solo means acting by yourself.

19. Bills That Look Strange

1I am a bearded barbet. Hairlike feathers on my beak look like a beard. My thick beak has pointed parts like teeth. I live in dry parts of Africa and belong to the woodpecker family. I make a nest hole in a dead tree with my beak. I eat fruit and insects, and other birds help care for our chicks.

2I am a crossbill, a finch with a beak crossed like an X. I travel to find pinecones and pry them open for pine nuts. If I cannot find pine nuts, I eat other seeds and insects. I am a rhinoceros hornbill, named for the horn on my beak. I use the horn to knock down fruit, to fight, and to attract a mate. I seal my mate in a tree-hole nest with mud and feed her through a small opening.

3We huias lived in New Zealand until we became extinct. The male had a thick, strong beak, and the female had a thin, curved beak. People once thought we were not related. We had orange wattles and shiny black feathers with white tail tips. I am a sword-billed hummingbird, the only bird with a bill longer than my body. I get nectar from tube-shaped flowers in the Andes Mountains.

4I am a brown pelican with a skin pouch that can hold almost three gallons. I dive into the ocean for fish, and gulls sometimes steal fish from my pouch. I warm my eggs with my webbed feet. I am a shoebill stork, and my beak looks like a shoe with a hook. I live in African swamps and fall onto fish with my mouth open. My mate and I cool our eggs with water and wet grass.

5I am a spoonbill, and my long beak flattens at the end like a spoon. People may mix me up with a flamingo until they see my bill. I sweep my bill in mud and shallow water to catch frogs, shellfish, and small fish. I am a toucan with a huge, colorful beak that is light and full of air pockets. My beak helps me reach fruit in rainforest trees and toss it in a mating ritual. Baby toucans take several months to grow a large adult beak.

6Many birds have weird beaks. Some beaks help birds catch their food. Some beaks help birds eat their food. Other beaks just look incredible. Which beak is your favorite?

20. Born Together as Twins

1You may have a big brother. That brother may be bossy. You may have a little sister. That sister may be sweet. You may have older siblings. You may have younger siblings too. You may be the oldest child in your family. You may be the youngest child. Picture a brother who is your exact age. Picture a sister who is your exact age. Picture the two of you looking identical. That can happen. Your sibling would be your twin. A twin shares your age. A twin can share your looks. The match in age can be exact. The match in looks can be exact too. Some families have a bossy older brother. Some families have a sweet younger sister. Some children are both older and younger than other siblings. Being the oldest is one family place. Being the youngest is another family place. A same-age brother is a twin. A same-age sister is a twin. Identical looks can be the clue. Then you know that sibling is your twin.

2Twins are more than a shared look. Twins are more than a shared birthday. Big differences can show up between twins. Each twin has special qualities of his or her own. Many twins share more than a birthday. Some twins like to share clothes. Some twins like to share a bedroom. School may place some twins in one shared class. Twins are two siblings. They grow inside their mother at the same time. Twins can be two brothers. Twins can be two sisters. Twins can be a brother and a sister. Every baby starts as an egg. The egg is inside the mother. The baby grows bigger as it develops. Birth comes at the end of that growth. One twin is born first. That first twin is slightly older. Twins can look very similar. They can look almost like one person. Twins can also look very different. Today a mother is much more likely to have twins. Years ago, twins were less common. In some families, twins show up more often. A shared bedroom is one extra match. A shared class is another. Shared clothes are another. The older twin leads by only a short time. Looks may match, or looks may not.

3Identical twins often share tastes. Food is one taste they may share. Clothes are another taste they may share. An almost exact look is common for identical twins. They come from one egg. That egg split into two. The pair is always two girls or two boys. Telling identical twins apart can be hard. They often match in height. They often match in weight. They often match in hair color. They often match in eye color. They often match in other features too. Tricks come easily when the twins look this similar. They can trade places at home. They can trade places at school. People cannot tell which twin is which. Count four sets of identical twins. About one of those sets is a mirror-image pair. Mirror-image twins can happen among identical twins. They face each other. Then it looks like a mirror. The sight is like a reflection. Freckles may show up on mirrored sides of the two bodies. Each twin may part the hair on a facing side. One twin may use the left hand. The other twin may use the right hand. Fingerprints are patterns. Your fingertips carry these patterns. Identical twins can look very much alike. Their fingerprints are always different. A picture may mark one print as Twin 1. The other print belongs to the second twin.

4Identical twins can match in still more ways. They might share interests. Music can be a shared interest. Sports can be a shared interest. Favorite foods can match. Their personalities can be similar. They may like the same types of people. Identical twins are not always much alike. Sometimes the look is only slightly similar. They do not look exactly the same. Interests can be completely different. Hobbies can be completely different. Long shared time comes when their interests match. Identical twins occur less often than fraternal twins. A boy and a girl are the most common twin pair. Looks in a fraternal pair can be far apart. Two separate eggs are their start. The pair can be two boys. The pair can be two girls. The pair can be a girl and a boy. A fraternal pair may match in some characteristics. Any siblings can share that same number of characteristics. Picture two sisters with a few years between their births. Both sisters might have light hair. Both sisters might have blue eyes. Another pair of sisters might not match in hair color. They might not match in eye color. Fraternal twins work the same way. They can look very similar. They can look a little alike. They can look very different.

5Personality gaps are more likely in a fraternal pair. Shared traits most likely come from the same age. Growing up together can also create shared traits. As fraternal twins grow, their enjoyments often split. Baby twins have a bond. They do everything together. Twins often grow a special bond. A bond is a relationship. The bond starts early. It can start before birth. Twins often interact while they develop. Development happens inside the mother. They move around. They reach toward each other. After birth, the bond goes on. Infant twins may feel comfort. A nearby sibling brings that comfort. Different personalities are common for fraternal twins. Same age helps explain what they do share. Time together as they grow also helps explain it. The bond is already there in the mother's body. Reaching and moving are early signs of that bond. After they are born, staying near each other still comforts them.

6About 40 percent is the share of young twins with a private language. They talk with a language they made up. Some twins invent that language as babies. They use nonsense words. They invent words that copy sounds. The words help them communicate with each other. A real language usually replaces the made-up one. The made-up language goes away after that. Summer after summer, many twins go to the same town. The town is Twinsburg, Ohio. Most of them dress the same. They spend the day at Twins Day. Twins Day is a yearly gathering. No other yearly twin gathering in the world is bigger than Twins Day. Identical twins look very much alike. A photo can place that pair at the top. Fraternal twins usually look different. A photo can place that pair at the bottom. An almost exact look belongs to only some twins. Other twins match in personality instead. Other twins match in interests instead. Some twins look very different. Some twins act very different. Fraternal twins are an example of that difference. Twins can still share a bond. The bond can begin before birth.

7Twins are not simple copies of each other. Each twin is his or her own person. This is true if the twins are identical. This is true if the twins are fraternal. Some twins may look similar. Some twins may like the same things. Every twin is one of a kind. A bond is a strong feeling. The feeling is connection. The feeling can be friendship. Characteristics are features. Features help people identify a thing. Features can also identify a group of things. Fraternal twins do not look exactly alike. Identical twins look exactly alike. Interact means to work together. It can mean to speak with others. It can mean to do things with others. Personalities are sets of qualities. They are ways of behaving. These ways set one person apart from another. A reflection is an image. Light makes the image by bouncing. The light bounces off a surface. A mirror is one surface that can do this. Siblings are brothers. Siblings are also sisters. A twin is one baby from a pair. The pair is born in a single birth. Looking alike does not make two twins the same person. Each one stays one of a kind.

21. Bugs People Can Eat

1A man tastes a dish of crickets. He cooked the dish himself. People around the world have eaten insects for thousands of years. The thought of eating insects makes some people squirm. Even so, insects are healthy food. They hold the nutrients that people need. This account looks at foods that help the body. It compares crickets with cows. It names popular bugs. It tells ways to eat them. It gives a warning to be careful. It invites you to start cooking. Word meanings come at the end.

2Protein makes up a large part of an insect. Take 100 grams of grasshoppers. They have around 20 grams of protein. The same amount of beef has more protein. The beef has only 6 grams more. The grasshoppers have far less fat. Termites are a great source of iron. Some caterpillars are a great source of iron too. Crickets are high in calcium. Some insects have large amounts of healthy calories. Termite queens are one example. Ant queens are another. A chart compares these foods. The chart uses milligrams for calcium. It uses milligrams for iron. It uses grams for protein. It uses grams for fat. A cricket has 75.8 milligrams of calcium. It has 12.9 grams of protein. It has 5.5 grams of fat. It has 9.5 milligrams of iron. A red ant has 47.8 milligrams of calcium. It has 13.9 grams of protein. It has 3.5 grams of fat. It has 5.7 milligrams of iron. A giant water bug has 43.5 milligrams of calcium. It has 19.8 grams of protein. It has 8.3 grams of fat. It has 13.6 milligrams of iron. A silkworm has 41.7 milligrams of calcium. It has 9.6 grams of protein. It has 5.6 grams of fat. It has 1.8 milligrams of iron. A grasshopper has 35.2 milligrams of calcium. It has 20.6 grams of protein. It has 6.1 grams of fat. It has 5.0 milligrams of iron. Ground beef has 32.0 milligrams of calcium. It has 27.4 grams of protein. It has 26.5 grams of fat. It has 2.1 milligrams of iron. Cod is a fish. Cod has 14.0 milligrams of calcium. It has 22.8 grams of protein. It has 1.0 gram of fat. It has 0.4 milligrams of iron. Many insects are as nutritious as beef. Many insects are as nutritious as fish. Some insects are more nutritious. Choosing insects as food also helps the planet. The human population is growing. Global farming has to expand to feed those people. Farming for billions of people harms the planet in a large way. One cause is a demand for beef. Cow farms harm the planet for more than one reason. Cows give off a greenhouse gas. That gas helps cause global warming. A cattle ranch makes harmful waste. A large insect farm makes harmful waste too. The ranch makes about ten times more of it. Insect waste is a great fertilizer. It is good for the soil. At an insect farm, two men look closely at the worms.

3Much of the world's farmland is used to raise cows. Much of it is used to feed cows. A farm for insects uses much less land. They need much less food to provide the same nutrients. Most cows today eat grain. Grain needs large amounts of land to grow. Grain needs large amounts of water. Clearing land for cattle in South America makes life hard for jaguars. The animals that jaguars used to eat disappear. The jaguars may start hunting cattle. Ranchers sometimes kill the jaguars when that happens. In some parts of the world, farmers clear more trees each year. They want room for cattle. This destroys homes for thousands of species. Those species are already at risk. One strong way to save these forests is a change in food. People could be offered protein from insects. In Thailand, girls enjoy packets of crickets. The crickets are crunchy. The crickets are seasoned. Each packet can taste different from the others. More than 2,000 insects around the world can be eaten. Cooks prepare these insects in many different forms. In Thailand, some outdoor stands sell water bugs. The bugs are crispy. The bugs are fried. People add sauce to them. Other people might put mustard on a hot dog. They might put ketchup on a hot dog. Some people snack on grasshoppers. Some people snack on bamboo worms. Fried grasshoppers are one food people eat.

4Food culture changes over time. Italians once believed a tomato could poison a person. Americans once disliked lobster. They fed lobster to prisoners. Fifty years ago, few cultures ate raw fish. Today, people across the globe enjoy sushi. A French restaurant often starts a meal with snails. The dish is called escargot. You say it es-car-GO. Escargot is the French word for snail. Cooks take the snails out of their shells. They cook the snails. They place the snails back inside the shells. Diners use special tools. The tools pull the snail meat out. In some restaurants in China, cooks boil water bugs. They boil them in a special sauce. The bugs and the sauce are served over rice. When the weather turns chilly, some people eat ant soup. The bowl of soup is steaming. Cooks can use ants in the soup. Cooks can use ant eggs in the soup. Sometimes people dip water bugs in sauce. A man collects ants so he can cook them. He may freeze the ants for later. The minty taste of the ants comes from their food. Queen ants are one kind people cook. One town in Brazil cooks queen ants every October. That town also cooks them every November. This is the season when queen ants leave their hiding place. They had been underground. People collect the ants. They pull off the wings. Some people fry the ants. Other people dip the ants in chocolate. Those people say queen ants taste minty. Most arthropod species are insects. Lobsters are arthropods too. Crabs are arthropods. Spiders are arthropods. Scorpions are arthropods. Many other animals are arthropods. All of these animals wear their skeletons on the outside. People find the good meat inside the body. People can eat some of the outer shells too.

5Some restaurants in Mexico serve a grasshopper tortilla. They also serve fried worms with guacamole. A woman in Kenya sells fried termites. In Mexico, some people enjoy caterpillars cooked like French fries. They enjoy ant eggs covered in butter. For dessert, locusts covered in chocolate are a sweet treat. Worms covered in candy are a sweet treat too. Central Africa has a wide range of insects that people can eat. In Ghana, many people fry termites. Many people roast termites. The termites can be ground into flour. Bakers use that flour. Witchetty grubs can be set out ready for cooking. Fried cocoons of the silk moth are a popular treat. Some people in Australia munch on witchetty grubs. The grubs look like large white caterpillars. These insects have crisp skin. They have a smooth filling. The filling tastes like nuts. In Japan, diners can order fried cocoons of the silk moth. Some stores sell fried grasshoppers. Some stores sell other insects too.

6Some caterpillars have very poisonous bodies. Some have very poisonous spines. The Io moth caterpillar is one of them. These caterpillars can harm people who touch them. They can harm people who eat them. Other insects protect themselves in other painful ways. The blister beetle is one of those insects. Never eat wild insects. Remember that warning. A search in your backyard for a buggy snack can be dangerous. Insects that live outside can be covered with harmful stuff. That stuff might harm you if you eat it. Some insects are poisonous. They can make you very sick. An insect farm keeps its crickets in clean containers. Workers then pack the crickets. Workers ship them out. People who cook insects should buy them from farms. People who eat insects should do the same. Farmed insects eat special diets. Those diets do not hold things that might harm people. In the United States, the Internet is the best place to find insects you can eat. People can find live insects there. People can find meals made with insects. People can find tasty insect recipes.

7David George Gordon is about to cook. His dish uses scorpions. He is a chef from Washington. He wrote the Eat-A-Bug Cookbook. People like his pasta. The pasta has meatballs with six legs. He makes those meatballs with crickets. Fried tarantula is another favorite. Cookies are another favorite. Those cookies use white chocolate. They also use waxworms. Gordon believes insects are a better protein food. He compares them with chicken. He compares them with cows. He compares them with pigs. Insects cost little money. People can raise them in plain fish tanks. A farm full of insects does no harm to the planet. Insects may be the food of the future. These words help with the account. A calorie is a unit. It measures how much energy is in food. A chef is a cook who does that work as a profession. A diet is the set of foods a person eats as a habit. Other animals have diets too. Edible describes something a person is able to eat. An insect is a small animal. It has six legs. It has three body parts. It usually has two sets of wings. A nutrient is a substance a living thing needs. The living thing needs it in order to live. The living thing needs it to stay healthy. The living thing needs it to grow. Poisonous describes something that holds a poison. The poison can harm a person who swallows it. It can harm a person who breathes it. It can harm a person who touches it. A population is every member of one species. A recipe is a set of steps. The steps tell how to make one kind of food.

22. Dogs Carry Medicine to Nome

1Five parts make up this book. A terrible disease comes first. A great sled-dog relay comes second. Delivery by the dogs comes third. Life after the race comes fourth. Word meanings come last. January 1925 sent sled-dog teams racing in Alaska. Wilderness was the setting. A blizzard was the weather. Deep snow blocked the way. Icy wind struck without letting up. Children's lives were the reason for the race.

2Nome, Alaska, was where a terrible disease spread. Two Native children were the first to die. More people then became very sick. Medicine was needed at once. Only one doctor served Nome. That doctor ordered the medicine. Nothing arrived before winter. The port closed for the season first. Anchorage held the needed medicine. That supply was able to save the children. The gap was almost 1,000 miles, or 1,600 km. During the outbreak, Nome's only doctor worked inside the hospital. Saving lives was the work. A small picture shows the medicine package for the town's children. A train could cover only part of the distance. After the final train stop, 674 miles remained. That is 1,085 km. Airplanes then could not fly so far with safety. Native people had already used sled dogs for snow deliveries. Goods were what the dogs hauled. For this medicine, sled dogs were the clear best choice. Distance still caused a problem. One dog team could not cover it all. A relay was the only remaining hope.

3Top mushers in the relay numbered more than 20. Native Alaskans made up most of that group. Sled dogs in the relay numbered more than 150. Posts along the way were handoff spots. Each musher passed the medicine onward to the next team. January 27 brought the medicine off the train. The first dog team was waiting. Heavy fur wrapped the package. Freezing was the danger the fur prevented. The package then changed hands. An Alaska map shows two lines. One line is the railroad. The other is the sled-dog route toward Nome. Towns, rivers, and wild country fill the map. A normal sled run from the final train stop into Nome took twenty-five days. These teams had no time for a normal pace. The clock was the enemy. Rough wilderness met the dogs. High winds met them. Bitter cold met them. Team followed team. Often a musher paused to warm the medicine at a fire. The journey killed some of the dogs.

4Leonhard Seppala drove one team. Fame already belonged to this musher. Danger was greatest on the stretch his team ran. Cold had sunk to -85 degrees Fahrenheit. That is -65 degrees C. A Siberian husky led his team. Togo was the dog's name. Twelve years was the dog's age. Many sled-dog races had gone to Seppala. Togo's help was part of those wins. A shortcut crossed frozen sea. Darkness covered that crossing. The goal was saved time. Snow-packed mountains came the next day. The climb was a struggle. Distance for that team was 91 miles, or 146 km. Every other team ran a shorter distance.

5Hard travel continued for the dog teams. Gunnar Kaasen took the leg just before the last one. Balto, a Siberian husky, was his lead dog. A terrible blizzard surrounded the run. Kaasen served as an assistant to Seppala. Fame later covered both Kaasen and Balto for this journey. Balto led when blowing snow hid the view. A violent gust struck at one moment. The sled flipped. Medicine fell into the snow. Bare hands did the digging. Kaasen searched until the package was found. Fingers were hurt by the fierce cold. The medicine itself stayed safe.

6The next team was supposed to take over at a meeting point. That team was not set to depart. Kaasen chose to continue. Nome would be his own finish. February 2 brought the team into Nome. Dawn had not quite arrived. Balto and the teammates had done it. Five and a half days was the length of the whole journey. Sick children were saved by the medicine. Spread of the disease was stopped. Seppala's love for his dogs ran deep. He treated them as friends. A husky kennel in Maine came later in his life. Raising dogs happened there. Training happened there. Racing happened there. One year after the race, a U.S. tour began. The dogs went with him. Togo was included. Crowds were large. Siberian huskies were still new in much of the country. Seppala was among the first to bring the breed to those other parts.

7That same year, a statue of Balto was built in New York City. Balto and some teammates toured the United States in a dog show, but they were not treated well. A businessman from Cleveland, Ohio, thought the dogs had earned a better life. He had their story printed in a newspaper. In ten days, people gave enough money to bring the dogs to Cleveland. They lived at the Cleveland Zoo for the rest of their lives. Balto is still famous for his bravery. Every sled dog on that journey helped save the sick children when little hope was left. Kaasen and Balto visit the new statue built to honor the sled dogs. A blizzard is a severe snowstorm with cold, heavy snow, and strong winds. Medicine is a drug used to treat an illness. Mushers drive the sleds that dogs pull. A relay moves something in stages from one person to another. A Siberian husky is a breed from Siberia. Thick fur helps it pull sleds. A team is animals in harness together, pulling a vehicle.

23. Dogs for Every Kind of Work

1Pet dogs show all sorts of shapes. Their sizes vary too. They have always offered people company. They have always offered help. They have always offered affection. Have you seen how many kinds of dogs there are? Some dogs are big. Some dogs are small. Some dogs are long. Some dogs are short. Some dogs have lots of hair. Some dogs have no hair. Some dogs have big ears. Some dogs have little ears. Some dogs have big noses. Some dogs have little noses. Some dogs have long legs. Some dogs have short legs. Some dogs have long tails. Some dogs have no tail at all.

2So many kinds exist because there are many breeds. A breed is a kind kept apart when dogs mate. If dogs of one breed mate only with that breed, the pups match it. Dogs like these are purebred. Purebred means the parents match, so the pups match too. A male bulldog and a female bulldog have bulldog pups. Other dogs are mixed-breed dogs. A mixed-breed dog blends two or more kinds. People sometimes call these dogs mutts. A picture shows a wolf. Dogs did not exist in the distant past. Only wolves lived then. People began to live in villages. Their garbage became food for wolves. As time passed, some garbage-eating wolves grew tamer. Those wolves changed slowly. They grew better at living near people. They started to look more like the dogs we know. They started to act more like those dogs.

3Some of the early dogs ran better than the rest. Some hunted better. Some dug better. People wanted a dog even stronger at one of those jobs. They let fast runners mate only with fast runners. They let good diggers mate only with good diggers. Across many thousands of years, the pet dogs changed. They became the breeds we know today. Wild wolves stayed wild. They are still wild. Dogs fall into seven groups. Each group holds many breeds. The pages ahead look at the groups and some members. Hunters got help from sporting dogs that people had bred for that work. Some help the hunter find the game. Game means the animal a hunter is after. After a shot, some sporting dogs locate that animal. They carry it back to the hunter. A picture shows pointers and a setter. Pointers belong with the sporting group. People bred the pointer to spot game. Its nose and body then aim at that animal. The dog locks its body and holds still. One front leg lifts. The nose does the pointing.

4The Labrador retriever is also called a Lab. It is a sporting dog that many people like. It is a good family dog too. People first bred it to retrieve birds. Retrieve means to bring the birds back. Hunters had shot the birds. The birds had fallen into a pond or a lake. A Lab has webbed feet. The webs help it swim well. Its coat keeps water out. The coat holds warmth in cold water. Its jaws are strong. Those jaws can carry a heavy bird. Hounds have their own job. They chase game along the ground. Hounds come in two kinds. A sighthound chases by using its eyes. A scenthound follows a scent with its nose. Scent means smell. A hound may bark when it has cornered the game. That bark tells the hunter. A hound may kill the game itself. The Irish wolfhound is a sighthound. Few dogs on Earth stand taller. At the shoulder it can stand .9 meters high. That height is 3 feet. People bred it to hunt wolves. They also bred it to kill wolves. Speed in the hunt comes from legs that are long and strong. A photo shows an Irish wolfhound beside a terrier friend.

5The bloodhound is the best scenthound. Loose skin covers its face. Its ears are long and floppy. Both traits stir smells into the air. Then the dog can find game animals with ease. Bloodhounds can also find people who are lost. Working dogs are smart. Working dogs are tough. Their jobs include guarding. Their jobs include protecting. Their jobs include rescuing. Their jobs include pulling sleds. Their jobs include pulling carts. The Newfoundland is a working dog. It is large and sturdy. A thick coat covers it. The coat keeps water out. The coat keeps the dog warm. Long ago, these dogs pulled boats through water. They pulled heavy fishing nets too. Today, people in Europe use them for rescue. They save people who are drowning.

6Asia's coldest lands are the first home of the Samoyed. At first, these dogs guarded reindeer. They also herded reindeer. Today they pull sleds. The coat blocks bad weather. Extra thickness holds in warmth. A stocky body helps keep heat inside. Herding dogs move quickly. They are smart. People bred them to control other animals. Sheep are one animal they control. Cattle are another. A herding dog may bark at the animals. It may stare at them. It may nip them. These dogs train easily. They like to work on their own. They are eager at the job. The Welsh corgi is sturdy and quick. Its legs are short. People first bred it to drive cattle. The short body can slip under a cow. Then the dog nips the cow's ankles. It can dart away fast. That speed helps it avoid a kick.

7A movie called Babe made the border collie famous. The story is about a pig. The pig herded sheep. A border collie taught the pig that skill. People bred border collies to herd sheep. They bred them to herd cattle. The dogs still do that work. They are athletic. A very quick run is normal for them. Sheep learn to obey them. Cattle learn to obey them too. Most terriers are small. Some are medium in size. People bred most of them to find fierce pests. They also bred them to kill those pests. Rats are one pest. Otters are another. Foxes are another. Badgers are another. The dogs had to be very brave. A pet terrier may refuse to give in. Training them can be hard. No terrier grows bigger than the Airedale. People first bred it to kill badgers. They bred it to kill weasels. They bred it to kill otters. It watches a home by nature. People have also used it as a police dog.

8Jack Russell terriers grew very popular. They were stars of shows on American television. Frasier was one of those shows. Wishbone was another. This dog does not fit every home. Each day it needs lots of exercise. Each day it needs lots of activity. Without that, the dog turns difficult. It also turns destructive. Toy dogs were bred for a small body. A few toy breeds once hunted pests. A few once killed pests. Most were first bred to keep people company. The papillon is small. It is friendly. It is smart. Its ears are big. Papillon is a French word. The word means butterfly. The ears look like the wings of a butterfly.

9Most of a hairless Chinese crested has no fur. Puffs of hair sit on the head. Puffs of hair sit on the feet. The skin is dark gray. Pink spots mark the skin. Summer sun means the dog needs sunblock lotion. Winter cold means the dog needs a coat. Nonsporting dogs form another group. Many breeds here do not fit the other groups. The old work is no longer what these breeds do. Now the job is mostly to keep people company. The Shar-pei looks unusual. Wrinkles cover its loose skin. Its mouth looks like the mouth of a hippo. People in China first bred it to fight. They also bred it to guard. Loose skin let the dog spin free. Another animal could not keep a lasting hold.

10Dalmatian pups are born all white. Spots show up during the early weeks after birth. People trained Dalmatians to trot next to a coach. Horses pulled that coach. If farm animals blocked the road, the dog moved them aside. Wild dogs were kept off the horses by this breed. Great strength was required. Many miles of trotting were part of the job. A picture here shows a beagle. Purebred dogs can have health troubles. Health is a reason some people pick a mix of breeds. Every size shows up among these dogs. They come in every color. Their personalities are often lively. Taking a dog into a family is a big choice. Regular visits to the vet help keep the dog healthy. Training matters just as much. The dog needs to behave well.

11You now know more about why so many dog kinds exist. Dogs really do come in nearly every shape. They come in nearly every size. Personality differs a great deal as well. A new dog should be chosen with care. Pick one that will fit your family. For a long span of years, a dog can stay close and friendly. The breeds named in this book include the Airedale. They include the Newfoundland. They include the bloodhound. They include the papillon. They include the border collie. They include the pointer. They include the bulldog. They include the Samoyed. They include the Dalmatian. They include the Shar-pei. They include the hairless Chinese crested. They include the Welsh corgi. They include the Irish wolfhound. They include the Jack Russell terrier. They include the Labrador retriever.

24. Elizabeth Blackwell’s Road to Medicine

1Table of Contents. Tough Enough begins on page 4. A New Direction begins on page 5. Against the Odds begins on page 7. This Is the Way to Learn begins on page 9. Champion for Women Doctors begins on page 11. Thank You, Dr. Blackwell begins on page 13. The glossary begins on page 16. The first chapter is Tough Enough. A professor cut into a dead body. His medical students looked on. Some young men blushed. Others looked sick. Others laughed nervously. Elizabeth Blackwell was determined not to show any emotion. She watched the demonstration closely. She pinched her hand as hard as she could. The pain helped her keep her face still. She had to show she was tough enough. The year was 1847, and Blackwell was the first.

2Elizabeth Blackwell’s birth came in 1821. The country of birth was England. Nine children belonged to the family. She was the second child. The home was close. The home was loving. Parents wanted a good education for boys. Parents wanted a good education for girls. Any kind of career was open to an educated white boy. People expected girls to learn a wife’s skills. People expected girls to learn a mother’s skills. Lucky girls learned reading. Lucky girls learned writing. In 1832 her father moved the household. The country he chose was the United States. A new business was his aim. Bad luck came along. People owed Mr. Blackwell money. Those people never paid him. He died in 1838. A short illness came before that death. The family was heartbroken. The family was poor. Daily life was a struggle. The boys found jobs. The girls began a school. Students paid to come. In 1844 she visited a neighbor. The visit changed her path. The neighbor was dying. Male doctors’ care had been awful. She told Elizabeth about that care. A woman physician, in her view, was the right judge of the illness. She called Elizabeth smart. She urged doctor training for Elizabeth. Her first answer was to reject the idea. The idea stayed anyway. She could not forget the possibility.

3American girls and women in the 1840s lived inside strict limits. Those limits are hard to imagine today. Women could not vote. Fathers held power. Husbands held power. Brothers held power. They controlled almost all businesses. They controlled almost all homes. They controlled almost all money. Women had few career options. A woman might work as a nurse. A woman might work as a teacher. Law was nearly closed to women. Medicine was nearly closed to women. Men refused women equal brains. Men refused women equal talent. Those views drove Blackwell on. She aimed to be the first woman doctor. That aim was set in the United States. Three big challenges stood before her. Medical school money was the first need. Human biology was the second need. She needed enough biology for medical studies. A school that would take a woman was the third need. In 1845 she went to North Carolina. South Carolina came after that. One doctor’s family gave her a home. A second doctor’s family gave her a home. She taught music. The lesson money was saved. Medical school was what the savings were for. Free time went to study. She read medical texts that the doctors owned. By 1847 she felt ready. Readiness was for the third challenge. She applied to colleges of medicine. She chose only the best. Some of the colleges were in Philadelphia. Others were in New York City. Helpful doctors still taught her. Sixteen schools rejected her. One adviser told Elizabeth that trying would fail. A letter came in October. Geneva Medical College sent the letter. The college stood in western New York. She braced for another rejection. The college accepted her. She was 26. Medical school was about to start.
