4She did not know why the college said yes. Chance was part of the reason. Professors at Geneva Medical College let the students decide. The question was whether she could enter. The professors expected a no. They counted on an all-male class to reject a woman. Students treated the vote like a joke. Every student voted yes. The faculty felt shock. The school kept its promise. She was the first woman a medical college accepted. That acceptance was in the United States. At the start, some professors limited her courses. They tried to keep her from male anatomy. They called that study improper. Blackwell asked for the full course list. She asked in a gentle voice. She asked in a firm voice. Male students already had that training. She wanted the same training. Professors agreed at last. Classmates agreed at last. Quiet determination won respect. Ability won respect too. Her diary called this learning path the right one. Graduation fell on January 23, 1849. She crossed the stage. A diploma came from the medical college president. He bowed. The local newspaper praised the event. The paper said a painter should capture it. The paper praised her courage. She took first place in the class. From then she was Elizabeth Blackwell, M.D. M.D. means Doctor of Medicine.

5She sailed to Europe for more training. A hospital post in Paris was hers. An accident soon changed her life. It happened not long after she arrived. An eye infection struck a baby in her care. She was washing that eye. Water from the eye hit her face. The same infection then reached her eyes. Doctors took out her left eye. A glass eye replaced it. She wore that glass eye through the years after. She had once hoped to be a surgeon. Bad eyesight made surgery impossible. Her disability did not stop the work. In 1851 she returned to the United States. She opened an office. The office was in New York City. Her small pay barely bought food. Most patients came from poor families. Those families were new to the city. Some of the families came from Germany. Some of the families came from Ireland. At times they could not pay. She treated them anyway. She taught them healthy eating. She taught them good hygiene. Hygiene means staying clean. Hygiene also means not passing disease along. She formed a plan for her own hospital. Women doctors would run it. In 1857 she opened a small hospital. The hospital served poor women. The hospital served poor children. It stood in New York City. Her sister Emily joined her. In 1854 Emily became a doctor. She was the third woman to reach that role. The role was counted in the United States. More medical training was open to women at that hospital.

6She still saw patients. She still treated patients. She promoted medical education for women. She did that work in the United States. She did that work in England. In November 1868 the new school opened. She opened it with her sister. The school was named the Women’s Medical College of the New York Infirmary. The school welcomed women. Doubts had no place there. Questions had no place there. It stood among the first schools to act this way. A year later she went back to England. She practiced medicine in England after that. She retired in 1877. Books followed the retirement. Those books taught girls how to guard health. Those books taught women how to guard health. A photo shows Elizabeth Blackwell around retirement. Rebecca Lee Crumpler enters the story here. She was the first Black woman in the United States to practice as a doctor. She began as a nurse. Massachusetts was where she nursed. A college accepted her in 1860. The school was New England Female Medical College. Boston was its city. Her challenges matched Blackwell’s challenges. Many people thought women lacked a doctor’s strength. Crumpler faced that belief. She also faced racism. She met racism in medical school. She met racism in her work after graduation. White doctors were often unkind to her. They doubted her abilities. Crumpler persisted. The Civil War came to its end. The year of that end was 1865. Her husband moved with her. They settled in Richmond. Richmond is in Virginia. She cared for many poor people. The people she served were people of color. Women were central in that care. Children were central in that care. She published a book in 1883. It gave ways to prevent a baby’s sickness. It gave ways to treat a baby’s sickness. She broke barriers. Black women doctors today follow her lead. A 2020 photo shows Dr. Janice Beacon. She is Black. She is a primary care physician. She gives a child a physical exam. The exam prepares the child to return to school.

7Blackwell died in 1910. Almost 7,400 women doctors were working in the United States then. Today, more than one in every three doctors in the United States is a woman. They practice every kind of medicine. Some treat children. Some save lives in emergency rooms. Some do brain surgery. Elizabeth Blackwell's brave efforts opened the door. Women doctors have followed in her footsteps. A chart shows the percentage of doctors in the United States by gender. Men are labeled on the chart. Women are labeled on the chart. The listed figures are 7.5 percent, 11.6 percent, 17 percent, 24 percent, 30 percent, and 36.3 percent. The sources are the American Medical Association, the American Journal of Public Health, and the Association of American Medical Colleges. In the United States, the percentage of doctors who are women is nearly five times what it was in 1970.

25. Fold and Cut a Paper Snowflake

1In nature, each snowflake that falls has a different design. Your paper snowflakes can all be different, too. Have you ever looked closely at a real snowflake? Their designs can look like lace. You can make your own patterns by folding and cutting paper. This book shows three shapes, and extra cuts can make each snowflake look different.

2You need white copy paper, scissors, and a pencil. Lay the 8½ by 11 inch paper with the long side going left to right. Fold the bottom left corner up to the right until the top edges touch. Crease the diagonal so the top left corner is a point and the top edges line up. Cut off the extra paper on the right and open the triangle into a square. Fold the square in half to make a rectangle.

3Fold this rectangle in half to make a smaller square. Find the corner that is the center and mark it with a pencil dot. Fold the paper one more time along the crease. The new shape is a triangle, and you are ready to cut. Hold the folded paper with the center pointing down. The top edge is the outside, and a straight edge makes square sides.

4A chart shows some of the shapes you can cut. If you cut one shape, you will end up with another shape. Think about how each shape will look when you unfold the snowflake. Leave the outside edge uncut. Cut shapes from the other two sides of the triangle. Cut carefully and leave some paper connected so it will not fall apart.

5When you finish cutting, gently unfold your snowflake. Do you see how the edge cuts made the pattern? You can change the outside edge, or make up your own ideas. For a round snowflake, make the same basic folds. Put the outside edge at the top and cut it in an arc. An arc is a piece of a circle, so the snowflake becomes round.

6Cut shapes from the other two sides of the triangle. You can cut the curved edge, too. Next, cut new shapes from the center to change the design. A chart gives ideas for those center cuts. If you cut one shape, you will end up with another shape. You can also use your own ideas.

7For a star snowflake, make the same basic folds. Cut the outside edge in one of the shapes shown for a star. Then cut the sides and center in any way that seems fun. To make smaller snowflakes, fold paper in four equal parts and line up all the edges. Cut along the fold lines and use each piece to make a snowflake. You can tape them on windows or use them in many other ways.

26. Food Choices for a Strong Body

1Long ago, a snack might have been nuts and seeds from the forest. You might have picked wild berries and mushrooms. You might even have munched on an insect. Now many people get food from cabinets, refrigerators, or vending machines. They might grab fruit, bread, cheese, soda, or candy. There are many food choices, so nutrition, the study of food, matters more than ever.

2How many meals might you eat in your life? Use a calculator to multiply three meals a day by 365 days by 90 years. Wow, that is a lot of meals! A nutrient is any substance your body needs to live, stay healthy, and grow. Nutrients come from what you eat and drink, so the right kinds matter. Proteins build and repair cells, help make strong muscles, and are in meat, fish, eggs, dairy, beans, nuts, and some grains.

3Carbohydrates make energy, and complex ones give energy over a long time. They are in vegetables, many fruits, oatmeal, whole grains, and whole-grain bread. Sugars cause an energy flood and then a crash, which stresses your body. These sugars and starches are in candy, cakes, cookies, white potatoes, bagels, and many breads. In type 2 diabetes, the body cannot manage sugar in the blood. A family history or too many sweets raises risk, and cases rise in the United States.

4Fat helps build cells, absorb some vitamins, and store extra energy. Animal fats are in meat and cheese, and vegetable fats are in olive oil, nuts, and seeds. Vegetable fats from nuts and seeds are better than animal fats from meat and butter. Trans fats are in pre-made foods like snacks, cheese products, fried foods, and desserts. They are called hydrogenated or partially hydrogenated oils, and studies show they are not good. French fries often are cooked with trans fats.

5Vitamins help cells use energy and help the body see, digest food, and fight diseases. Minerals help bones, blood, brain, and nerves grow, work, and send signals. Many people get vitamins and minerals from pills called supplements. Water carries nutrients to your cells, so drink six to eight glasses each day. A calorie is a bit of energy from food, and needs depend on age, body type, and activity. Too few or too many calories for how active you are can lead to health problems.

6Children and teens should get at least sixty minutes of exercise a day, not all at once. These numbers are average calories a 150-pound adult burns in one hour. Watching TV burns 43, cleaning burns 115, table tennis burns 171, and bicycling burns 174. Walking burns 198, washing a car burns 193, swimming burns 288, basketball burns 450, and jogging burns 654. Eat protein, complex carbohydrates, healthy fats, and lots of colorful vegetables and fruits. If a snack comes ready to eat from a bag or box, you probably should not eat much of it.

7Look at a box of cereal, a snack bar wrapper, or a bottle of juice. A Nutrition Facts chart shows the nutrients and the serving size. If a bag of chips has two servings, eating the bag doubles the calories shown. This jar of sauce has 70 calories in one serving. Saturated fats and sugars are listed as less healthy than unsaturated fats and fiber. Ingredients are listed from most to least, like tomato puree, tomatoes, then a little lemon juice.

8The adults in your life may make many of your food choices. Someone may cook breakfast and dinner, or you may eat lunch in the school cafeteria. Your soccer coach or scout leader may provide snacks for practices and meetings. Adults can help you plan meals and snacks. Knowing about nutrients and calories will help you stay healthy as you choose more foods. Eat well, exercise, drink plenty of water, and remember food can feed your health and happiness!

9You can use the Smart Eating section to plan your meals for a day. Include healthy foods from each of the three main food groups. If you eat something unhealthy, eat something healthy at that meal or later that day. Eat a good breakfast each morning. You will have energy all day. You will also do better in school.

27. Gentle Sea Cows

1Long ago, sailors told stories of mermaids. They also called these beings sea sirens. The creatures were beautiful. Each one was half woman. Each one was half fish. Their songs drew sailors toward dangerous rocks. Ships then broke apart on the rocks.

2Early sailors looked at manatees. They believed the animals were mermaids. That belief is hard to picture now. A manatee looks gentle. A manatee looks sweet. A manatee does not look like a mermaid. The manatee is a mammal of the sea. Its group has the name Sirenia. The group name comes from an old belief. People once thought manatees were sea sirens. Some people call manatees sea cows. Hour after hour, a manatee feeds by grazing on plants. A cow grazes in that same way. A manatee eats a huge amount of food. A cow eats a huge amount too.

3A manatee has a rounded body. It has a small head. The tail is shaped like a paddle. That tail helps the animal move through water. Large flippers in front help it steer. The face is pudgy. The face is wrinkled. The face looks a little like a walrus face. The bones are heavy. The bones are solid. Scientists think those heavy bones do a job. They keep the manatee from floating too high. The closest living relative is the elephant. One photo shows a manatee rising to breathe. A smaller photo shows a walrus.

4Manatees come in three kinds. One kind is the Amazonian manatee. One kind is the West African manatee. One kind is the West Indian manatee. That group splits into two types. One type is the Florida manatee. One type is the Antillean manatee. Today's homes for manatees are marked on a map. It marks Florida manatees through the whole year. It marks Florida manatees in warm weather. It marks the Antillean manatee. It marks the Amazonian manatee. It marks the West African manatee. The map labels North America. It labels South America. It labels Africa. It labels Europe. It labels the Pacific Ocean. It labels the Atlantic Ocean.

5A habitat is the place a manatee lives. Daily habits are the things it does. This habitat is 68 degrees F. A manatee has a thick layer of blubber. The open ocean is part of this habitat. A map shows a migration route for the West Indian manatee. Florida is marked on that route map. Rhode Island is marked on it. Maine is marked on it. The Atlantic Ocean lies on the map. Flippers help a manatee move along the floor of the ocean.

6Algae often coats a manatee's body. The coat can look like green fur. Plant food in a single day can pass 45.5 kilograms for one manatee. The same amount is 100 pounds. Seagrass is the meal in one picture. At times these animals swim with the belly up.

7Manatees live as loners. When they meet, they seem to kiss. They nibble. They groom other manatees. In one scene, two manatees play. Other manatees rest nearby. A baby drinks its mother's milk. The baby may keep that milk for up to two years. A few weeks after birth, the baby begins to eat plants. It may still drink milk then. The baby is taken underwater several times. This teaches it how to go underwater. This teaches it how to come back to the surface.

8All manatees are in great danger of becoming extinct. Extinct means none would remain alive. Their numbers are low for many reasons. Hunters have killed them for food. Hunters have killed them for their hides. A manatee can die if a fishing net traps it. In Australia, some die in nets set along beaches. Those nets protect swimmers from sharks. One photo places a pair of manatees beneath a motor on a boat. Another photo shows two manatees at a meal. They eat floating plants mixed with trash. Things dumped into the water can be poisonous. Pesticides can poison the water. Other chemicals can poison it too. Dirty water is a threat to the places they live. Power boats bring one of the biggest dangers. Manatees move slowly. They cannot get away from a boat that speeds. A propeller can injure a manatee. A propeller can kill a manatee. Signs tell boaters to keep out of manatee areas. Many boaters ignore the signs.

9Boats and divers are at Crystal River, Florida. A sign tells boaters to take care around manatees. Sirenia is the mammal group that includes manatees. A sea siren is a make-believe being. She is beautiful. She is half woman and half fish. People also call her a mermaid. Sea cows is another name for manatees. Protect means to guard a thing so harm cannot reach it. A predator hunts other animals. It eats them so it can live. Migrate means to travel from one feeding ground to another. The trip may link summer feeding grounds with winter ones. It may link feeding grounds with mating grounds. A marine mammal is a mammal that lives in water. Extinct means gone from the world. Blubber is fat stored under the skin. That fat keeps marine mammals warm.

28. Helen Keller Finds a Way

1Helen was born at Ivy Green. As a little girl, Helen Keller liked searching for bird eggs in the grass with her friend Martha. Helen could not hear or speak. She used her hands to tell Martha when she wanted to search for eggs. She put her hands together and touched the ground. Martha understood, and the two ran off to hunt for eggs. Using her hands was one of the many ways Helen found to communicate.

2Helen Keller’s birthday was June 27, 1880. Birth happened in Tuscumbia, Alabama. Nineteen months was her age when serious sickness arrived. A high fever came with the sickness. She recovered. Hearing did not return. Sight did not return. Deafness stayed. Blindness stayed too. A scene from around 1887 puts Helen beside her pet poodle. Childhood gave her a way to communicate with nearby people. Signs made from hand movements carried what she wanted. The same signs carried what she needed. The family cook was Martha’s parent. Martha understood the signs sooner than almost anyone. Helen reached age seven with about sixty signs. She and Martha had built those signs. Frustration remained. Other people often missed the meaning. The label deaf-blind person is one wording. That wording is called identity-first language. Many people who are deaf choose it. Many people who are blind choose it too. They treat a disability as a deep piece of identity. Identity means who a person is. Person-first language is another choice. It names the person before the disability. One model puts the word person ahead of deaf and blind.

3Helen’s childhood had schools for deaf students. It also had schools for blind students. Teachers who could teach a child both deaf and blind were rare. A scene from 1892 shows Helen at age 12 with Anne Sullivan. The hiring year for Anne Sullivan was 1886. Helen’s parents made the hire. Sullivan came as the teacher. Her graduation from Perkins School for the Blind was brand new. Laura Bridgman became her friend at that school. Bridgman was ahead of every other deaf-blind person in learning language. Sullivan learned finger spelling from her. Different hand shapes spell the words. Each shape matches a letter. American Sign Language includes those shapes. Deaf people speak this language. ASL is the short form. A scene from around 1893 puts Anne on the right side. Spelling travels from Anne’s hand into Helen’s hand.

4Finger spelling was the lesson Sullivan gave Helen. On one day, water crossed a hand of Helen’s. Sullivan did the pouring. The other palm got the spelled letters W-A-T-E-R. Helen sent the same word back. The letters landed on Sullivan’s hand. Only a few months passed. Hundreds of words belonged to Helen then. Sentences belonged to her too. The film The Miracle Worker retells that lesson. It lets viewers watch Helen meet her earliest word. Braille writing meets Helen’s fingers. A girl’s lips under those fingers show what the girl is saying. Helen saw a new use for touch. Reading could grow from that sense. Braille reading arrived soon. Raised dots form this writing. The dots are grouped in sets on paper. Each set holds one letter. Fingers do the reading. Spoken words could be felt as well. Helen set her fingers on moving lips. Changing lip shapes told her the speech.

5An older Keller entered schools built for blind children. Schools for deaf children were part of her path too. She took joy in learning. College was the goal she chose. Sharp thinking was hers. Strong study habits were hers. Many people still doubted she would succeed. Her senses were how Keller made sense of the world beside her. A cap and gown are what Helen wears once college ends. Radcliffe College was her school starting in 1900. Sullivan attended beside her. Teacher talk became hand signs in Keller’s palm. Sullivan made those signs. Keller typed the lessons later. One machine was a typewriter. Another machine was a Braille writer. Some textbooks had nothing in Braille. Sullivan read those pages. Signs from her then delivered the wording. The lessons passed through Sullivan. Keller herself did every assignment. Her 1904 graduation came with honors.

6Books were Keller’s work after college. So were essays. She crossed the country on trips. Her talks described life for deaf people and for blind people. She noticed poverty around many people who had disabilities. She aimed to help. The year 1924 made her part of American Foundation for the Blind. Work there went on more than forty years. More schools for blind children opened with her help. More Braille books were printed with her help. Travel later circled the world. She went to more than thirty-five countries. Disabled people beyond the United States were part of her aim. Her unusual views confused many listeners. One scene lays Keller’s hand on a piano. She feels music through the wood. Women’s rights were in her fight. Black people’s rights were in her fight. Workers’ rights were in her fight. Help from her in 1920 started an organization. Its job was protecting rights inside the United States. The organization has lasted. Some listeners rejected her views. A common insult followed. Some people treated deafness and blindness as proof she lacked a strong mind. Those people would not hear her out.

7Throughout her life, many people treated Keller unfairly because of her disabilities. People often decided things for her instead of letting her make her own choices. They thought they knew better than she did. Keller had to prove over and over that she could do things for herself. By the time she died in 1968, she had accomplished many great things. She was a capable deaf-blind woman. She wanted the world to know that disabled people can think, learn, and do great things. Helen Keller died on June 1, 1968, at age 87. Braille is a system of printing that uses raised dots to represent letters. Communicate means to share thoughts or ideas. Disabilities are conditions that limit a person ability to do something. Frustrated means annoyed or distressed about being unable to complete a task.

29. Holding Back the Flood

1In 1931, water from the River Thames covered streets in London. A picture shows the city under that flood. An old tale tells of three pigs. Each pig made a house. Straw formed the first house. Sticks formed the second house. Bricks formed the third house. A big wolf came to the houses. His hard breath knocked down the straw house. His hard breath knocked down the stick house. The brick house kept standing. Severe weather can act like that wolf. It shoves hard against the places people live. A hurricane can wreck buildings. A tornado can wreck buildings. Other severe storms can wreck buildings too. Storms like these can take lives. One picture shows a reporter out in a hurricane. The reporter stays in the severe weather to gather news. Severe weather events come more often than they once did. The hazards that ride with them have grown too. People cannot halt these events. Like the pigs, people can still block some of the harm. They can also try to control that harm. A stronger house can survive the blast. Flooding ranks among the hazards people meet most often.

2A layer of gases surrounds Earth. People call this layer the atmosphere. The blanket keeps the planet safe. Heat from the Sun stays inside it. A greenhouse holds heat in a similar way. People call this holding of heat the greenhouse effect. The effect helps make Earth comfortable to live on. Gas is one fuel people burn. Oil is another fuel they burn. Burning these fuels lets out more carbon dioxide. Carbon dioxide is also called CO2. Over many years, CO2 has piled up in the atmosphere. Other greenhouse gases have piled up too. The gases make the air keep more heat from the Sun. Heat that leaves the ground gets caught. That extra heat does not get away into space. Because of this, temperatures on Earth keep going up. People call that rise global warming. Higher temperatures change the planet's weather. They change the climate as well. Climate change has brought more severe weather events. Warmer air pulls up more water from the ocean. People call that pull evaporation. Extra evaporation helps storms grow stronger. A diagram shows a natural amount of CO2. It also shows a large amount of CO2. The large amount traps more heat that leaves Earth's surface. Floods can strike almost anywhere in the world. Heavy rain can start a flood. Melting snow can start a flood. Large waves can start a flood. Storms can raise those waves. Earthquakes can raise them too. The worst floods come suddenly. They also come when nobody expects them. Water that races along can smash nearly all in its way. Not every flood is dangerous. In many places, people wait for floods. They need the water so the soil stays healthy. People have learned to live beside floods. They have learned to steer floods. Some plans even try to halt a flood. Most towns use drains to carry water off. Most cities use sewers for the same job. These systems can take only a limited load. Street drains pull water off hard ground. A paved street cannot soak water up. A parking lot cannot soak it up either.

3Scientists can say when severe weather is likely. They can say where it is likely. Governments can use that news. They can warn people to get ready. They may tell people to leave a place. A warning that comes early can save property. It can save people's lives. A photo shows residents moved to safety after a typhoon. Japan gets strong storms often. Japan gets floods often too. Its government has built many barriers. It has built other tools that control floods. It has built tools that stop floods. In October 2019, a typhoon struck Japan. A typhoon is a severe storm. This one brought powerful wind. It brought heavy rain. Floodwater left more than one hundred rivers. The water was stronger than some barriers. Some of those walls broke. Leaving home was the only choice for many families. Some people died. Japan's storm shows how deadly a flood can be. It also shows why people must control floods. It shows why people must try to stop floods. Lots of families live beside water. Land beside water floods more easily. Some families set their homes on tall supports. Those supports look like stilts. Floodwater can pass beneath a house on stilts. Items left on the ground may still be ruined. The house itself can stay safe. The things inside can stay safe too. A very high flood can still touch a stilt house. Floods that high come less often. People still need to heed flood warnings. One photo shows stilt houses in Galveston, Texas. Those houses wait for the next flood. Water wraps all the way around that city.

4In the Netherlands, people pile sandbags against rising water. A sandbag wall can shield a building. It can shield a small patch of land. Workers fill each bag about halfway. The fill may be sand. The fill may be soil. Then people stack the bags in a tight pile. Each bag rests on another bag. One bag can be as heavy as three bowling balls. The pile blocks most of the water. It does not block every drop. Filling the bags is hard work. Carrying them is hard work too. A useful wall needs a great many bags. Because of this, sandbags help only for a short time. Where floods come often, governments build large structures. The structures guard people. They guard property too. New Orleans is a city in Louisiana, in the U.S.A. The city lies lower than the sea. Water nearly surrounds it. Floods have troubled New Orleans for a long time. Floodwalls ring almost the whole city. Levees ring it too. A levee is a long bank of raised earth. Levees guard land that floods only now and then. Heavy rain far to the north can swell the rivers. Those rivers can then flood. Huge waves from severe storms can drive water into the city. The low city gives that water no way out. In 2005, a hurricane struck New Orleans. The storm wrecked many levees. Water covered several sections of the city. Afterward, the United States government built the walls again. It built the levees again. The new works stood taller. They also stood stronger. Workers finished that project in 2018.

5After the 2005 hurricane, more than fifty levees failed. More than fifty floodwalls failed too. Those works had been guarding New Orleans. One photo shows a broken levee wall at the center. A neighborhood in another photo sits under water. It was only one of many flooded neighborhoods. London is a city in England, in the U.K. The city built a gate system called the Thames Barrier. Thames is said TEMZ. London's middle is split by the River Thames. It keeps going until it meets the ocean. Ocean tides shove water into the river. Tides also draw water back out. A high tide plus heavy rain can jam the flow. Large waves can jam it too. Then water piles up in the Thames. It can spill over the banks. The middle of London can flood. Leaders built the Thames Barrier to halt that kind of flood. Workers finished the barrier in 1982. It has ten gates made of steel. Each gate turns so it can open. Each gate turns so it can shut. A shut gate rises about as high as a five-story building. An open gate lets the river move. Ships can travel upstream. Ships can travel downstream. A gate can be flipped upside down. Crews do this when the gate needs repair. Once a gate is shut, it must stay shut for a time. It stays shut until the water matches on both sides. Heavy rain can set up a flood in London. A high tide can do it. Strong wind that drives seawater inland can do it too. The barrier blocks the ocean from forcing the Thames back upstream. That block keeps a flood from growing worse. A diagram names the gate, the tide, the river, and the riverbed. It shows a gate that is closing. It shows a gate that is closed. It shows a gate that is open.

6Since the 1980s, a perfect record belongs to the Thames Barrier. Hundreds of uses have gone right. The sea is rising. Weather is growing more extreme. Some people in London now want a bigger barrier. They want a higher one as well. The Netherlands lies a short way from London. More than half of that land sits under sea level. The country holds many rivers. It holds many lakes. Storms offshore often raise huge waves. Those waves can bring deadly floods. A map puts England and the Netherlands side by side. Both lands touch the North Sea. Global warming does more than stir fiercer weather. It also lifts the sea. The North Sea rises with it. The map marks Amsterdam. The map marks London. Dutch leaders built the Delta Works to hold back floods. Few builds anywhere are as huge as this one. The works include six big dams. They include many levees. They include other barriers too. With this system, the country can steer most floods. It can halt many floods before they start. A photo from above shows one piece of the Delta Works. Water spilling past a dam shows a gap in height. One side of the dam sits higher than the other. A second structure lets salt water come in. That salt water keeps two upstream rivers from freezing. The same opening gives migrating fish a path.

7Because of climate change, severe weather strikes more often. Place after place on Earth sees the same pattern. Dangers linked to that weather also strike more often. People keep inventing clever tools. These tools help steer the danger. They also help stop it. Floods will stay a hazard that returns again and again. The works already built cannot halt every flood. Still, those works save many lives each year. They save huge sums of money each year. When a bad storm hits, more people can feel calm because of these works. If a flood is coming, listen for storm warnings. Listen for flood warnings too. You may hear them on the Internet. You may hear them on the radio. You may hear them on TV. Go up to high ground. You can also go to an upper floor. Keep away from rivers. Keep away from shores. Keep away from drains. Keep away from any other spot that may flood. Leave pets indoors. Do not walk in water that is moving. Do not swim in that water. Leave alone any wet thing that must plug into a wall. A barrier is an object that blocks movement. It may be natural. People may build it. Dangerous means something that can cause harm. It can also mean something likely to cause harm. A hazard is a possible danger or a risk. A levee is a raised wall along a river. Soil or other material can form it. The wall keeps the river from spilling its banks. Overflow means liquid spills past a rim. Predict means to tell what will happen later. People often predict from what they notice. They also predict from what they have lived through. Severe means extremely bad. It can also mean unpleasant. Stilts are posts that hold a structure up. They can hold it above the ground. They can hold it above the water. A structure is something put together from many parts. A building is one kind. A bridge is another kind.

30. How Cats Became Household Friends

1The parts of this book come in this order. An Odd Pair begins on page 4. Taming the Cat begins on page 6. A Bond That Grew begins on page 8. Cats of Today begins on page 12. Furry Friends begins on page 15. A word list begins on page 16. People keep more cats than any other pet. Cats still stand apart from most animals that people tame. A cat can act friendly toward people. A cat does not rely on help from others. Most of the time, a cat hunts by itself. Most of the time, a cat lives by itself. Long years have produced many cat breeds. Those breeds show many colors.

2This count is for the United States. One home in three keeps a cat. Some of those homes keep two cats. Cats do not labor for people the way dogs do. Cows also do work that cats skip. Horses do work that cats skip. Sheep do work that cats skip. That makes people and cats a pair few would expect. How did cats turn into such close friends? Some scientists say the first tame cats lived in the Middle East. They place that start at roughly twelve thousand years before now. People then began to grow grain. They also stored that grain as food. Mice came to eat the stored grain. Rats came for the grain too. Cats arrived to hunt those mice. Cats hunted the rats as well. People may have begun to feed the cats. They were glad the cats removed the pests. In time, cats that got along with people had many young. That happened fast near towns. Those cats did well. Food was easy to find. Farmers still keep barn cats. Ranchers do the same. The cats still hold pests in check.

3A cat hears well across a long distance. Each ear has thirty muscles. Those muscles let the ear move fast. A cat sees well at night. A cat also sees well up close. Whiskers add to a cat's sense of touch. A cat's teeth are sharp. A cat tastes sweet flavors poorly. That weak sense of sweet leads a cat to choose meat. A cat's sense of smell is strong. A cat can smell fourteen times better than a human. A cat can look adorable. Its body traits also make it a fine hunter. Its strong senses do the same. Some scientists think people simply liked how cats looked. A cat has big eyes. A cat has a round face. Those traits often stir a caring feeling in people. People may have taken cats in because cats look cute. The bond between people and cats grew as time passed. In ancient Egypt, people held a special belief. They believed cats were magical. They believed cats brought good luck. Some Egyptian gods had the body of a cat. One old Egyptian drawing shows a cat. That cat watches geese. One old Egyptian goddess has the head of a cat.

4Ancient Egypt turned the bodies of the dead into mummies. A mummy was a sign of love. It was also a sign of respect. They made mummies of pet cats that had died. Cat mummies show how special these animals were. They were special to the people of ancient Egypt. A person who hurt a cat faced trouble. Experts think tame cats spread fast. They spread through the ancient world. They rode on ships. Those ships carried grain. Crews brought cats aboard to hunt mice. Trade ships carried cats across Europe. The same ships carried cats across Asia. Cats reached China about two thousand years before now. They reached India at about that same time. In Asia, cats kept mice away from important papers. Cats often show up in Asian legends. They also show up in Asian art. One example is a Chinese painting.

5The Middle Ages ran from 500 to 1500 CE. In those years, many people in Europe grew afraid of cats. They thought cats were evil. They held superstitions about wicked people. Those people kept cats. Some stories said those people even turned into cats. Even so, many people still kept tame cats. Cats were useful. They hunted pests. In medieval Europe, people held another belief. They thought cats obeyed orders from evil people. People like cats for the way they hunt. Cats can also cause harm. Scientists think cats helped wipe out birds. They count thirty-three kinds of birds around the world. If your cat goes outside, add a bell. Put that bell on its collar. The bell helps the birds near your home. It helps other animals there too. Indoor pet cats today live in a new way. Their lives differ from cats of earlier times. For a long stretch, tame cats stayed mostly outdoors. House life for pet cats did not start until the 1960s. Cat litter grew more common then. Outdoor cats often live under three years. Indoor cats usually live fifteen years. Some indoor cats live as long as eighteen years.

6A traditional Siamese cat has a well-known look. The European wildcat is a wild cousin of tame cats. Pet cats today look a bit unlike older tame cats. In earlier times, tame cats looked like those wild cousins. Many cats in Europe had stripes. They often had young with wildcats that lived nearby. In Asia, no wildcats lived close enough to mix with tame cats. Different breeds formed there as time passed. The Siamese cat is one of those breeds. Its fur is smooth. Its fur is white. Its paws are dark. Its ears are dark. Its face is dark. The first cat show displayed several breeds. Pictures of those breeds appear with the show. In the 1800s, people in England began a new practice. They bred cats for different colors. The first cat show took place in London. People came to see breeds called fancy. A Persian cat won that show. Other named breeds include the Abyssinian. The British shorthair is another. The Russian blue is another.

7Cats have been friends with people across the world. That friendship has lasted thousands of years. Cats first entered towns while looking for food. Since then, their play has won people over. Their grace has won people over too. More than three million cats go into animal shelters each year. That number is for the United States. Fewer than two million of those cats are adopted. If your family wants a pet cat, go to a local animal shelter. You may find a furry friend there. Here are word meanings from this book. Bred and breeds are verb forms. They mean animals came together and made a baby. Mummies means the dried bodies of dead people or of other animals. Pests means insects or other animals that cause harm. Superstitions means beliefs that do not rest on reason. Fear of the unknown can feed those beliefs. Trust in the supernatural can feed them too. Tame means not wild. It means people control the animal or care for it.

31. How Halloween Came to Be

1A picture from the 1890s shows children on Halloween. They hold jack-o'-lanterns and use them to scare an adult away. A Holiday Both Fun and Scary. People celebrate Halloween in the United States. They celebrate it in Canada. They celebrate it in the United Kingdom. They celebrate it in Ireland, the place where it first began. Streets fill with trick-or-treaters when the date is October 31. Creepy music plays at Halloween parties. People put on costumes. Monsters, ghosts, superheroes, and princesses celebrate side by side. Children join in, and so do adults. Three girls show off costumes in a photo from Cincinnati, Ohio, in 1929.

2A boy looks for a costume in a shop in Montebello, California. The United States is where Halloween is especially popular today. It matters in culture. It also matters in business. The holiday shows up in Hollywood movies. It shows up in well-known songs. American stores do big business because of the holiday. Halloween candy costs people a lot of money every year. Costumes cost them a lot of money as well. Halloween grows out of a very old tradition. It started among peoples called Celts. Their name sounds like KEL-tik. Two thousand years ago, they lived in Great Britain. They lived in Ireland. They lived in northern France. Modern-day druids say a blessing at Stonehenge. Stonehenge stands in southern England. The Celts and Samhain. Every fall the Celts held Samhain. That name sounds like SOW-in. October 31 ended the year on the Celtic calendar. Samhain on that date was a farewell to the year that was ending. A supernatural side belonged to Samhain as well. The Celts believed the spirits of the dead went to the underworld at Samhain. To help the dead on that journey, people lit bonfires. They also gave crops as a sacrifice. They gave animals as a sacrifice too. During this festival, they believed, priests could see what was coming. Plans for the new year depended on the predictions from Samhain.

3How the Holiday Got Its Name. Later, Christianity spread across Europe. The Catholic Church tried to put its own traditions in place of Samhain. One example was All Saints' Day. The Church placed it on November 1. People also called that day All Hallows Day. October 31 comes the day before All Hallows. It was called All Hallows Eve. In time, the name was shortened to Halloween. Many older Celtic traditions stayed alive inside the new holiday. Halloween customs of today come from the Celts. They come from the Catholic Church. They come from old customs of Europe. Poland is where Catholic worshipers walk a town on All Saints' Day. Costumes and Trick-or-Treating. Halloween costumes likely began with the Celts. At Samhain, they dressed as animals. Fairies, witches, and ghosts were costumes people wore while walking English streets. That kind of dressing up was called mumming. Neighbors gave the mummers food. They gave them drink too. People thought the gifts would send the spirits away before they caused trouble. Today we call this custom trick-or-treating. An adult gives a treat. Then each child in a costume leaves without playing a trick. In England, a modern druid marks Samhain in a costume of a stag.

4One big Halloween custom began with the legend of Stingy Jack. Jack-o'-Lanterns. Lots of people carve these glowing pumpkins when Halloween comes. To make one, a person carves a design into a pumpkin. A candle then goes inside the pumpkin. The candle lights up the carving. An Irish story of a man called Stingy Jack is where the custom comes from. Jack liked to trick his friends. When he died, his spirit had to roam in the dark. That roaming was his punishment. To light the way, he cut a hole in a turnip. He set a candle in the hole. Because of that, people in Ireland called him Jack-o'-lantern. In Ireland, people made these lanterns from turnips and beets. People in England did the same. North America became home to many Irish people during the 1800s. They carried the jack-o'-lantern custom with them. Using pumpkins for these lanterns began in the United States. That change started in the mid-1800s. Pumpkins work well because they are large. A carver can cut all kinds of designs into them. Jack-o'-lanterns now matter a great deal at Halloween. Turnips are still carved by some people for this holiday.

5Children in the United States play bobbing for apples during a party. The picture is from the early 1900s. Older Customs. Some older Halloween customs are less familiar now. Bobbing for apples began as a way of telling fortunes. In the game, a player tries to pull an apple from a tub of water. Only the mouth may be used. People said marriage would come first to the player who got an apple first. Many Halloween games no longer include telling fortunes. A fortune-teller might still appear at a Halloween party. A person might meet one at a haunted house. Halloween Now. The Halloween we know is fairly new. Older traditions are often mixed into it. North America is where this is especially true. Wide celebration there did not come until the 1800s. Many early settlers from Europe, living in the New World, frowned on Halloween. Irish settlers who reached the United States during the 1800s brought the change. They brought Halloween customs with them. Jack-o'-lanterns were part of what they brought. Mumming was part of it too. Little by little, more Americans joined the celebration. Sadly, pranks grew common as the holiday grew popular. Pranks had become a serious problem by the 1930s. To improve things, some cities tried to make Halloween a holiday for families. Children could go to trick-or-treating events that many cities held. In the 1930s and 1940s, trick-or-treaters got cookies, fruit, toys, and coins. A photo from 1898 shows American children in Halloween costumes.

6Candy became a trick-or-treat gift in the 1950s. The small Halloween candy bars we know did not show up until the 1970s. Treats have changed as the years passed. The holiday still excites children. Trick-or-treating in 2014 drew more than 41 million children in the United States alone. Where Costume Ideas Come From. Ideas found online make up 31.4 percent. Ideas found in a store make up 26.8 percent. Ideas from friends or family make up 18.1 percent. Pop culture makes up 13.6 percent. Other sources make up 10.1 percent. Fun Halloween Facts. In 2013, people in the United States ate 4.2 pounds of fresh pumpkin per person. Five percent of dedicated Halloween fans start shopping before September. United States spending on this holiday in 2015 came to 6.89 billion dollars. A Big Crowd. Halloween keeps growing more popular. Each year, more people celebrate it in the United States. The same is true in Canada. Some cities pick malls as a spot for trick-or-treating. Some hold it at churches. Some hold it in other places. These events help keep children safe from cars on the street. The crowds are not only children. Many adults celebrate Halloween too. Plans in 2015 put more money toward adult costumes than toward children's costumes. The holiday is fun for kids. Adults do not want to miss out. Children go trick-or-treating at a special event.

7Old Ideas Mixed with New Ones. Today's Halloween does not look like the parties people held in the 1950s. It looks different from the celebrations of the 1800s. It looks different from Samhain, the festival of the ancient Celts. The customs keep changing. They blend what is old with what is new. Halloween now carries traditions from several cultures. People of every age can enjoy them. A photo from 1898 shows two girls in the United States dressed for Halloween. Did You Know? Candy, costumes, and party supplies for Halloween support a whole industry. A survey in 2015 asked Halloween shoppers about candy. Of those shoppers, 93.7 percent said they would buy it. Candy by itself was set to cost those shoppers 2.1 billion dollars. Each household was expected to put about 28 dollars toward costumes. Pets were also going to get costumes in many of those plans. Among people celebrating, 44.8 percent planned to decorate their yards. Among shoppers, 31.5 percent planned to host a party or go to one. People celebrating the holiday planned to spend 74.34 dollars on average. These figures come from the National Retail Federation in 2015. Bonfires are large fires built outdoors. Celebrations, signals, and burning trash are reasons for these outdoor fires. Carving means cutting with care. It can mean shaping a material, such as a sculpture, by careful cutting. Celtic describes a group of people. Those people once lived in ancient Ireland. They also lived in Scotland. They also lived in Wales and in western Europe. Pranks are tricks or practical jokes. Predictions are guesses about what may happen later. Roam means to move about with no set plan. Supernatural means beyond what natural laws can explain. The word often relates to religion. A tradition is a belief or custom handed down year after year, and from one generation to the next. In some myths, dead souls go to a place called the underworld.

32. How Moving Water Shapes the Earth

1Water is the most important resource on Earth. Water is a mighty force. It covers most of the planet. It is always at work. It changes the surface of Earth. Plants need water to survive. Animals need water to survive. Humans need water to survive. People must take care of the water. They do this for the good of Earth.

2You can find water above the ground. It fills lakes. It fills ponds. It fills rivers. It fills streams. It fills oceans. You can also find water under the ground. It sits between rocks. It sits between sand. People call that water groundwater. Air holds water too. Some of it forms clouds. Some of it is an invisible gas. People call that gas water vapor. Here, water appears in different forms. Try to name each form. Much of the water on Earth is moving. Water falls from clouds as rain. Water falls from clouds as snow. Then it drains into streams. It drains into rivers. The land around those streams and rivers has a name. People call it a watershed. Rain and snow fall on that land. Water soaks into the ground. Streams and rivers carry water along. Groundwater collects below. Streams flow into small rivers. Small rivers flow into bigger rivers. The smaller streams and rivers have a special name. People call them tributaries. A tributary flows into a bigger river. Over time, a great deal of this water arrives at the oceans.

3A map shows the Mississippi River. It shows St. Louis. It shows the United States. It shows New Orleans. It shows Natchez, Mississippi. It shows the Gulf of Mexico. It shows tributaries. Letters on the map also spell the Ohio River. A question goes with the map. How many smaller rivers flow into the larger Mississippi River? Flowing water picks up dirt. It picks up sand. It picks up rocks. Dirt and sand carried by water have a name. People call them sediment. Sediment helps water cut into the land. The Grand Canyon is one of the biggest canyons on Earth. A fast river carved it. The carving took millions of years. The river is the Colorado River. The canyon shows the power of water. Its depth is more than a mile. That depth is 1,600 meters.

4Sediment can pile up and make new ground. One pile is the Mississippi River Delta. The delta sits at the Gulf of Mexico. When water slows, the sediment drops. It drops to the bottom of the stream. It drops to the bottom of the river. Those dropped piles become new land. People call some of that land sandbars. People call some of it deltas. A large delta forms where a big river meets an ocean. Floodwater can destroy homes. Sometimes a huge amount of rain falls. Sometimes a huge amount of snow falls. Streams then spill over their banks. Rivers spill over their banks too. The nearby land floods. A flood can cause a great deal of damage.

5After a flood, the water goes back. It returns to the riverbed. Dirt from the floodwater stays on the land. Year after year, that dirt builds a floodplain. Floodplain soil is rich in nutrients. The nutrients come from the river. Crops grow well in that soil. A later flood can ruin the crops. Farm fields can stand on a floodplain. Rivers and streams do more than carve land. People swim in rivers. People boat on rivers. People fish in rivers. For thousands of years, people have used rivers in another way. They move goods from one place to another. Today, large barges carry cargo on rivers. Ships carry cargo on rivers too. Lumber companies float logs on rivers. The logs travel from forests to mills.

6A dam on a river can use the push of water. The dam uses that push to make electricity. Factories use millions of gallons of water every year. They use the water to make goods people need. Farmers send water onto fields so the crops can grow. At home, people use water to clean. At home, people use water to cook. What other jobs does water do in your home? Many living things make their homes in streams and rivers. People drink water from streams and rivers. Other animals drink that water too. Some industries dump waste into rivers. Some large cities dump waste into rivers. The waste pollutes the water. Polluted water harms humans. It harms plants. It harms other animals. Some of those animals live in the water. Some of them need the water to stay alive. Water from factories often pours into local waterways.

7A list gives the lengths of famous rivers. The Nile is in North Africa. It is 6,677 kilometers long. That length is 4,150 miles. The Amazon is in South America. It is 6,280 kilometers long. That length is 3,900 miles. The Yangtze is in China. It is 6,115 kilometers long. That length is 3,800 miles. The Mississippi is in the United States. It is 3,780 kilometers long. That length is 2,350 miles. The Volga is in Russia. It is 3,740 kilometers long. That length is 2,325 miles. The Mackenzie is in Canada. It is 1,802 kilometers long. That length is 1,120 miles. The River Dee is in the UK. It is 110 kilometers long. That length is 70 miles. Running water in rivers changes the shape of the land. Running water in streams does the same. It changes the land in many ways. Rivers and streams matter to humans. They matter to other animals. They matter to plants. They will keep caring for us. That stays true if we care for them. A glossary follows, apart from the account above. A delta is land built by sediment. It forms at a river mouth. A floodplain is low flat land. It floods when a stream overflows. Groundwater is water under the ground. To irrigate is to bring water to land. Pollutes means makes water harmful. The harm reaches animals, plants, and humans. Sediment is rock, dirt, and sand that water carries. A tributary is a smaller river. It flows into a larger river. A watershed is the land around a stream. Water from that land drains into the stream. Water vapor is water as an invisible gas. A word list follows, apart from the glossary. The list names crops. It names deltas. It names drinking. It names floods. It names the floodplain. It names the Mississippi River. It names pollute. It names sediment. It names tributaries. It names water as a force. It names water that is moving. It names water as a resource. It names uses of water. It names the places where water is found.

33. How People Put Wind to Work

1The parts of this book come in this order. The First Sailor starts on page 1. What Is Wind starts on page 2. Sailboats start on page 3. Windmills start on page 4. Electricity from Wind starts on page 5. Cleaner Energy starts on page 6. Problems with Wind Power start on page 6. Wind Power's Future starts on page 7. A word list starts on page 7. More than five thousand years ago, a person tried something new. Cloth was tied onto the mast of a boat. Wind filled the sail. The wind pushed the ship ahead. The ship moved across the water. For the first time, people put the power of wind to use. That moment was only a start. Today, wind helps run many machines. Computers are one example. Cars are another. Around 200 bce, someone carved a ship in marble. The ship is small. It is a Roman ship. It has two masts.

2Wind can act like a friend. It cools people on hot summer days. Because of wind, kites can fly far overhead. Wind can also do great harm. It can knock down power lines. It can knock down trees. It can even knock down buildings. This moving air comes mainly from differences in air pressure. Those pressure gaps come from gaps in temperature. Warm air rises. Warm air spreads out. That rising air is called low pressure. Cold air sinks. Cold air compresses. That sinking air is called high pressure. Air moves from high pressure toward low pressure. That movement happens across the planet. The movement makes wind. A diagram shows warm air rising. That rise makes low pressure. The diagram shows cool air sinking. That sink makes high pressure. The diagram also shows the way the wind goes. Air that flows from a high-pressure place to a low-pressure place forms wind. Air inside a balloon is under higher pressure. That pressure is higher than the air outside the balloon. When you let the air out, it rushes away. It rushes into the place with lower pressure. That rush is wind. Places with high pressure keep moving. Places with low pressure keep changing. Earth spins. Earth also travels around the Sun. Those motions help the pressure places change. Usually, a bigger pressure gap means faster wind. Pressure gaps are most extreme in tornadoes. They are also most extreme in hurricanes. Those storms can make winds that are hard to believe. Hurricane winds can blow faster than 322 kilometers per hour. That is the same as 200 miles per hour. A tornado can blow at 483 kilometers per hour. That is the same as 300 miles per hour.

3The Phoenicians used wind to move their boats. That was more than three thousand years ago. Sailboats were the first big use of wind power. Early sailboats were simple ships. Each had one sail. Wind let people travel in only one direction. That direction was the way the wind blew. Later, people learned how to sail into the wind. They built ships that carried many sails. Those ships could cross long distances. People used sailboats when they fished. They used them when they traded. They used them when they explored. They used them in war. Sails drove the battleships of ancient Greece. Sails helped the Phoenicians travel in the Mediterranean. Sails helped them trade there too. In the 1500s, explorers used huge ships that carried sails. Those ships went all the way around the globe. People who came to the Americas used ships of that kind. Later, engines took the place of sails. Engines became the main way to move ships. Today, people still sail because they enjoy it. No sailing group before the Magellan expedition went all the way around the world. The ships departed Spain in 1519. They came back in 1522. Magellan himself did not finish the trip. His expedition opened the way for later trips of discovery. A map of that voyage marks Asia. It marks Spain. It marks North America. It marks Europe. It marks Africa. It marks South America. It marks Australia.

4Windmills let people use wind power on land. Windmills were probably invented first by people in Persia. Persia is the land now called Iran. They built them well over a thousand years in the past. Some of those windmills still work today. The sails on the earliest windmills were cloth. Later, the sails were wood. The sails caught the wind. Then the sails began to turn. That turn spun a shaft. A stone shifted because the shaft was turning. The stone crushed grain into flour. A diagram of a windmill shows the wind. It shows the shaft. It shows the drive gear. It shows the sails. Grain goes in at one spot. Grinding stones sit in the mill. Ground grain comes out. The earliest windmills had a main job. They ground grain. They also pumped water. Windmills spread across Asia. They spread across Europe. Later they reached the Americas. Besides grinding grain, they ran pumps. Those pumps pulled water up from the ground. Dry places grew easier to settle once wind pumps were used. That land was in the American West. China may have been first to make a wind pump for irrigation. By the close of the nineteenth century, millions of windmills were at work. They stood in many parts of the world. At sawmills, power from windmills was used to cut wood. It pumped water for homes. It pumped water for animals. It pumped water for crops. Windmills supplied water for steam engines. Trains had to fill their water tanks again. They did this while they crossed the country.

5In the late 1880s, people found a better way to use wind. They built the wind turbine. The blades catch as much wind as they can. As the blades turn, they drive an electric generator. The generator makes electricity. A wind turbine can do more jobs than a simple windmill. A windmill does one task. A turbine can power almost anything. A diagram names the propeller blade. It names the shaft. It names the generator. Wind blows toward the blades. The rotor is the blades plus the hub. The rotor links to a shaft. The shaft spins the generator. That spin makes electricity. The Gansu Wind Farm is in China. It is the biggest wind farm on Earth. It has seven thousand turbines. Work on it ended in 2021. It sends power to one million homes. Today, people build wind turbines in groups. A group may hold hundreds of turbines. A group may hold thousands. These groups are called wind farms. They can make huge amounts of electricity. Most wind farms stand on land. Some stand on water. Strong winds at sea stay steady. That makes the sea a good place for turbines to make electricity. The Baltic Sea holds a wind farm out on the water.

6Wind supplies more and more of the energy people use. Experts make a prediction about the year 2050. By then, wind may make more than one-third of world electricity. Why is wind power growing? Wind is a renewable resource. A renewable resource is a natural resource. It can make energy without coming to an end. Using it does not use it up. Wind power is much cleaner than coal. It is much cleaner than gas. It is much cleaner than oil. William Kamkwamba grew up in a poor village. The village is in Malawi. Malawi is in Africa. The area suffered from drought. Farming was hard there. Nobody in his village had electricity. At age fourteen, William chose to change that. He had read about wind turbines. The reading was in a library book. He set out to build a turbine. He used scrap metal. He used parts from tractors. He used parts from bikes. Soon his turbine gave his family electricity. The power ran lights. It also ran a radio. He built a wind pump too. The pump gave his family water so they could farm. A photo shows William Kamkwamba. Another photo shows a windmill William made. Wind power gives clean electricity. Still, no energy resource is perfect. A home beside a huge wind farm is not always pleasant. Wind turbines can be noisy. The noise comes from huge blades that spin. Because of that noise, wind farms often stand far off. They stand far from the places that need the power. In some cases, that distance makes the electricity cost more. Some people think wind farms look ugly. They say the farms spoil the view. Other people object for another reason. Sometimes the blades kill birds. A strike is less likely when a blade is painted black. A chart asks how loud a wind turbine is. People measure sound with a unit called the decibel. The short name is dB. A blender is about 90 dB. A lawn mower is about 105 dB. A midsize window air conditioner is about 50 dB. A refrigerator is about 40 dB. A wind turbine can stand 500 meters from a home. That distance is 547 yards. The gap is about five soccer fields. At that distance, the noise is about the same as a refrigerator. The chart puts that noise at 38 dB.

7Wind power makes energy that people need. It also helps make the planet cleaner. The years ahead hold an even greater promise. Inventions that use wind cover a wide range. Streetlights are one kind. People are even building turbines to catch the winds of hurricanes. Someday those turbines may take in the powerful winds a storm carries. By catching those winds, the turbines might make the storms weaker. The storms are dangerous. Weaker storms could mean less damage. They could also save lives. New wind machines are limited only by human imagination. Far ahead, people will still hunt for fresh ways to use wind. The Greenbird is a vehicle that wind powers. It travels on land. It also travels on ice. It set a new land speed record for the world. Its speed was 203 kilometers per hour. That speed is 126 miles per hour. Here are word meanings from this book. Air pressure is a noun on page 2. It means how hard air pushes in the atmosphere. Compresses is a verb on page 2. It means squeezes tightly together. It can also mean presses tightly together. Generator is a noun on page 5. It means a machine. The machine changes motion into electricity. Harnessed is a verb. It means brought under control. It can also mean put to use. People often harness something so they can make energy. Inventions is a noun on page 7. It means new devices. It can mean new designs. It can mean new processes. Those things did not exist before. Natural resource is a noun on page 6. It means anything in nature that people value. People can use it to make a product. Minerals are one example. Forests are another. Object is a verb on page 6. It means to speak against something. It can mean to stand opposed to it. Turbine is a noun on page 6. It means a machine shaped like a fan. It catches moving water. It can catch wind. It can catch steam. It uses that motion to make an electric current. Windmills is a noun on page 4. It means machines whose blades spin. They use wind to make power.

34. How People Work to Save Wild Tigers

1Among big cats, tigers have the largest bodies. Power like theirs is rare in the animal world. Their trouble may surprise you. These huge cats could die out completely. Saving them is a goal for people in many places.

2Tigers are among the most beautiful animals on Earth. They are important members of the places where they live. Those places are their habitats. Tigers help keep nature in balance. They eat hoofed animals. Deer are one kind they eat. Wild pigs are another. Without tigers, those hoofed animals would chew through too many plants. Other animals would suffer. Those other animals need the plants for food. They need the plants for shelter too. In India, a tiger chases two deer. Their falling numbers are a separate worry. Long ago, nine kinds of tigers lived in Asia. They lived across large parts of that continent. During the 1900s, three kinds became extinct. A fourth kind probably has no wild members left. Wild tigers once numbered more than 100,000. That was one hundred years ago. The wild holds only about 3,500 today. Every kind of tiger that remains is endangered. Extinct means every animal of that kind has died out. Extinct in the wild means something different. No free animal of that kind is still alive. Those animals live only in captivity. A zoo is one kind of captivity. Today, captivity holds many more tigers than the wild does.

3Tigers now live in only tiny pieces of their old lands. The kinds are easier to follow in groups. Three kinds are extinct. The Caspian tiger is extinct. The Javan tiger is extinct. The Bali tiger is extinct. Some kinds still have wild members. The Amur tiger is also called the Siberian tiger. The Bengal tiger is another kind. The Indochinese tiger is another kind. The Sumatran tiger is another kind. Two kinds are nearly gone from the wild. The South China tiger is mostly extinct in the wild. The Malayan tiger is expected to be extinct in 5 to 10 years. The biggest reason tigers are endangered is trade. People buy tiger body parts. People sell those parts for money. To get the parts, some people poach. To poach is to break the law and kill a wild tiger. Tiger bones went into some Chinese medicines for many centuries. They used other body parts too. Some people thought the medicines could cure certain illnesses. They believed healing could come from a tiger's strength. One medicine from China holds powdered tiger bone.

4Hunters use many parts of a tiger to earn money. A pelt can become a rug. It can become a wall hanging. Bones can go into medicines. Bones can also go into alcohol. Teeth can become jewelry. Claws can become jewelry. Organs can go into medicines. Most Chinese medicines do not use tiger parts. Some companies still make medicine from those parts. A poacher can earn a lot of money. The poacher sells a dead tiger to one of those companies. China has banned the sale of tiger products since 1993. Most countries have made the same kind of promise. They agreed not to buy tiger parts. They agreed not to sell tiger parts or products. Poaching still goes on. Poachers care more about earning money. They care less about guarding endangered animals. Loss of wild land is a different danger. A tiger can live only in a certain habitat. The habitat must have hoofed animals. It must have water. It must have dense plants. Habitat loss is another reason tigers are endangered. Tiger habitats are disappearing. Asia's human population is rising quickly. People need more land for homes. They need land to grow food. Houses replace the wild land. Farms replace it too. Roads replace it too. Tigers once made homes on that land. Small separate areas are all that remain for tigers. Those areas sit far apart. Survival becomes much harder.

5Destroyed habitat hurts the animals tigers eat. Those animals have less land. People hunt them as well. Their numbers fall even more. Tigers find less wild food. They search for food closer to people. Sometimes a tiger attacks a person. More often, a tiger attacks farm animals. Cattle are one target. Sheep are another. Farmers shoot tigers they see attacking their animals. In tiger hunting land, cows walk through. Some farmers set out poison for tigers that take their animals. The tigers of India are Bengal tigers. Hard work in many countries aims to keep tigers from dying out. Wild tigers live in fourteen countries. Those countries run many conservation programs. India started a program in 1973. The program is called Project Tiger. Project Tiger began to create tiger reserves. A reserve is a large protected area of tiger habitat. Tiger reserves now number more than fifty. Guards work in the reserves. Their job is to stop poaching.

6Teams of guards work in Malaysia too. They work in other places as well. They protect tigers from poachers. Many Asian borders also have guards on duty. They try to stop tiger parts from crossing a border illegally. They try to stop tiger products from crossing too. Countries pass laws against illegal tiger business. They punish poachers. In Malaysia, the old fine for killing a tiger was large. A poacher could pay up to 31,000 dollars. Now the cost can pass 300,000 dollars. Jail time can be added. Poachers kill tigers so they can sell the pelts. They sell other body parts too. Conservation groups from many nations give help. They work in the countries where tigers live. More reserves are what they request from governments. They want places where tigers will be safe. Money is raised for tiger projects. One project helps tiger cubs that are orphaned. The groups teach people around the world. They explain why tigers need protection. They help scientists and governments plan together. They look for shared ways to save tigers. A return to the wild is possible for some tiger cubs.

7Nobody wants tigers to become extinct. Many people still miss an urgent fact. Tigers do not have much time left. Several changes must happen if tigers are to be saved. Tiger countries must protect more of their land. That land must remain natural. It must remain wild. Pay is needed for more guards. Their work is to stop poachers. Medicines from plants must take the place of tiger medicines. Then poaching has less reason to continue. The buying of tiger parts and products has to end. Sellers must be stopped too. Working as one, people may keep these beautiful animals alive. These words help you read the book. Conservation means protecting wild lands and the living things found there. An animal that could die out completely is called endangered. Extinct means a kind of living thing is completely wiped out. A habitat is the natural environment of a plant or an animal. Hoofed describes an animal with hooves. A hoof is a hard, horn-like cover on the foot. To poach is to kill a wild animal in a way that breaks the law. A reserve is a place that protects wild plants and wild animals. To survive is to stay alive.

35. Hunters of the Sky

1People call a bald eagle's nest an aerie. Aerie is said AIR-ee. The nest can stretch as much as 3 meters across. That width is 10 feet. It can rise as much as 3 meters. That height is 10 feet. From far off, a large bird sees a mouse. It drops in a strong rush of speed. Sharp claws seize the rodent. The bird then lands. Its beak rips the meal apart. Birds of prey are also called raptors. They are predators that impress anyone who watches. Three body features let them hunt. The same features let them eat. Strong feet carry talons. The talons grab the prey. The beak is sharp. The beak is curved. That beak tears food with ease. Their eyesight is excellent. They can spot prey from a long way off.

2A peregrine falcon is a bird of prey. A mountain eagle is one. A great horned owl is one. A golden eagle is one. Antarctica is the only continent where these birds do not live. They make homes in many habitats. A prairie can be a home. A desert can be a home. A few kinds even live in cities. Scientists sort them by when they are awake. Night birds are called nocturnal. Day birds are called diurnal. A tawny owl dives to seize a mouse. Owls are the nocturnal birds of prey. Night is their hunting time. Day is their sleeping time. An owl has a short tail. It has rounded wings. It has sharp talons. The head is large. The face is flat. The eyes are big. The beak curves. An owl has no eyeballs. Each eye is more like a tube. Bones lock the tubes in place. The owl cannot really shift its eyes. It must turn the whole head to follow prey. The head can turn backward. Then the owl sees what is behind it.

3An owl hunts by watching and waiting. It pounces when the moment is right. In full dark, hearing takes over. That hearing is sensitive. Sight is no longer the main tool. Owl feathers are special, unlike the feathers of other birds. The feathers stay silent in flight. Quiet wings let the owl sneak close. They also let it hear the prey move. At the right moment, the owl drops fast. Talons catch the prey by surprise. The beak then crushes the catch. Most owls eat small birds. They eat mice. They eat lizards. They eat insects. In the Americas, no owl is seen more often than the great horned owl. Barn owls got their name because they nested in barns. People have even found them in baseball stadiums. A barn owl body can reach 40 centimeters. That length is 16 inches. The wings can span 110 centimeters. That span is 43 inches. Barn owls live on every continent but Antarctica. A pygmy owl body can reach 18 centimeters. That length is 7 inches. The wings can span 38 centimeters. That span is 15 inches. This owl is among the smallest in the world. It mostly comes out while the sun is up. Its home is western North America. A great gray owl body can reach 84 centimeters. That length is 33 inches. The wings can span 153 centimeters. That span is 60 inches. Great gray owls rank among the tallest owls. One may live to the age of forty. Homes include Canada. Homes include northern Europe. Homes include northern Asia. The horns on a great horned owl are only feather tufts. Its body can reach 63 centimeters. That length is 25 inches. The wings can span 145 centimeters. That span is 57 inches. This owl lives in North America. It lives in Central America. It lives in South America.

4Owls work at night. The other hunters in this group work by day. People call that daytime pattern diurnal. Falcons are one example. They are medium in size. Their wings are long and pointed. Owls sneak up in silence. Falcons hunt by speed instead. Amazing eyes let them see prey from far away. Fast flight comes from those pointed wings. The wings help them turn sharply during a chase. The attack is a headfirst dive. People call that dive a stoop. The falcon hits the prey hard. Talons snatch the prey. The sharp beak kills it. A picture shows a peregrine falcon in flight with its catch. The catch is a seabird called a tern. Kestrels are the smallest falcons. A kestrel body can reach 30 centimeters. That length is 12 inches. The wings can span 63 centimeters. That span is 25 inches. The Madagascar kestrel lives along the East African coast. The laughing falcon got its name from its voice. It yelps loudly at dawn. It yelps loudly at dusk. The yelps can go on for up to five minutes. Its body can reach 53 centimeters. That length is 21 inches. The wings can span 94 centimeters. That span is 37 inches. Central America is its home. No bird of prey is quicker than the peregrine falcon. No animal is faster. One recorded dive reached 389 kilometers each hour. That speed is 242 miles each hour. A peregrine body can reach 49 centimeters. That length is 19 inches. The wings can span 110 centimeters. That span is 43 inches. These falcons live on every continent but Antarctica.

5Eagles, hawks, and kites may be small or large. A strongly hooked beak puts them in one group. They usually kill by squeezing the prey. They also stab it with those claws. Size puts eagles near the top among these hunters. An eagle's eyes are huge. The eyes can fill half of the head. Massive talons grab the prey. The bird usually carries the meal to another spot. An enormous beak then tears it open. Among birds of prey, eagle beaks are the largest. A bald eagle's wings can stretch almost 2 meters. That span is 6 feet. A tall man stands about that high. People say eagle vision when they mean sharp sight. People say hawk eye for the same idea. A hawk has a long tail. Its wings are short and rounded. Fine vision makes it a skilled hunter. A sharp beak helps. Strong talons help. Some hawks slip through branches with ease. They hunt in thick brush. Others like to soar high and search. Many sit in a tree and wait. Many sit on a pole and wait. Kites range from medium to large. Their bodies are light. The head is small. The beak is short. The wings are long and narrow. Many kites have a forked tail. Warm climates are where they spend most of life. Insects are the main food. Some kites are scavengers. A scavenger eats an animal that is already dead. Black kites may soar in circles as a group. They search for prey while they circle.

6The martial eagle is Africa's largest eagle. Martial is said MAR-shull. It can catch prey much bigger than its own body. Lion cubs can be that prey. Baboons can be that prey. Antelope can be that prey. Its body can reach 81 centimeters. That length is 32 inches. The wings can span 260 centimeters. That span is 102 inches. Africa is its home. Harris's hawks usually hunt as a team. The team may hold two birds. It may hold as many as six. A Harris's hawk body can reach 59 centimeters. That length is 23 inches. The wings can span 119 centimeters. That span is 47 inches. They live in the southwest of North America. They live in Central America. They live in South America. Snails are the main food of snail kites. A special beak helps with the shells. The beak pulls the snail out. A snail kite body can reach 40 centimeters. That length is 16 inches.
