The wings can span 116 centimeters. That span is 46 inches. Florida, in the USA, is one home. Cuba is another home. Vultures are scavengers, as some kites are. These birds seldom hunt. Most of them see well. Some have strong talons. They share a meal. Bigger vultures eat before the others. Birds with stronger beaks eat before the others. Smaller birds wait. They take the scraps that remain. The head of a vulture is bare. Bare skin keeps blood from tangling the feathers during a meal. A food pouch called a crop is found on most vultures. The pouch stores a meal for later. A vulture can last a long time between meals. Turkey vultures find food by smell. A turkey vulture body can reach 81 centimeters. That length is 32 inches. The wings can span 178 centimeters. That span is 70 inches. North America is their home. Cape griffon vultures feed bones to their chicks. The bones help the chicks grow stronger. A Cape griffon body can reach 115 centimeters. That length is 46 inches. The wings can span 255 centimeters. That span is 100 inches. Africa is their home.

7A bald eagle can lift a fish straight from the water. The feathers can stay dry. Owls hunt in lands across Earth. Falcons do too. Hawks do too. Eagles do too. Kites do too. Vultures do too. All of them share three important features. Talons are one feature. A sharp beak is another. Excellent eyesight is the third. Look up at the sky. You may catch a fierce predator busy with a hunt. You may see it hunting prey. A bird of prey has sharp talons. It has a strong beak. It hunts other animals. It eats those animals. Raptor is another name for it. Climate means the weather of a place across a long time. A habitat is the natural home of a plant or an animal. Hooked means bent in the shape of a hook. A predator hunts other animals. It eats them in order to live. Prey is an animal that a predator hunts for food. Sensitive means able to notice a small change. Talons are the foot claws of an owl. A hawk has them too. So does an eagle. Vision means the power to see.

36. Inside Rocky Mountain National Park

1A map shows Trail Ridge Road. It shows Wyoming. It shows Colorado. It shows Longs Peak. It shows New Mexico. The parts of this book come in this order. The Best of the Rockies starts on page 4. Young Mountains starts on page 5. From Moose to Marmots starts on page 7. Plan a Visit starts on page 10. A Popular Park starts on page 14. A word list starts on page 16. At Dream Lake, the first light of morning clears the fog away. Rocky Mountain National Park stands in Colorado. People also call the park RMNP. The park shows the finest side of the Rocky Mountains. The park covers 415 square miles. That area is 668 sq km. The land is full of natural beauty. The air feels fresh. The air is clean. Lakes in the mountains mirror the high peaks above them. Mountain lions live in the park. Moose live there. Marmots live there. Many other wild animals live there too. A person who loves nature can enjoy the park from a car. The same person can enjoy it on foot.

2Rocky Mountain National Park is only a small piece of a long chain. That chain is the Rocky Mountain Range. People call these peaks the Rockies. The chain stretches 3,000 miles. That length is 4,828 km. It runs from Canada down to New Mexico. Set beside older ranges, the Rockies are young. Forces pushed them up long ago. That rise began one hundred million years ago. It continued until sixty-five million years ago. Later, glaciers slid downhill. They cut the valleys. Longs Peak rises inside the park. It is among the tallest peaks in the Rockies. The Rocky Mountains make up much of the Continental Divide. The divide is a spine of rock. It runs the length of North America. It sends water two different ways. Water west of the divide flows west. It heads for the Pacific Ocean. Water east of the divide flows toward the Atlantic Ocean. A map shows North America. It shows the Continental Divide. It shows the United States. It shows the Pacific Ocean. It shows the Atlantic Ocean. It shows the Colorado River. It shows the Cache la Poudre River. It shows Mexico. One major river has the name Colorado. It runs through the western United States. The river begins inside the park. It flows toward the southwest. It heads for the Pacific Ocean. On the way, it gives water to several large cities. The Cache la Poudre River begins nearby. The name sounds like KASH LUH POO-dur. This river flows to the east. It joins rivers that are larger. Those rivers run toward the Atlantic Ocean.

3Rocky Mountain National Park holds several kinds of environments. Some areas are wet. Some are forests of pine. Some are forests of aspen. Wildflowers of the mountains dot grassy meadows. In summer those flowers show bright colors. Trees stop at about 11,500 feet. That height is 3,505 m. People call this limit the tree line. Above the line, the ground is too cold for trees. It is also too rocky. Each kind of place is home to its own animals. Elk feed where the forest edge meets open ground. Deer feed there too. Moose wade through wet places. They bite off plants there. Bobcats live in the park. A few mountain lions live there. Black bears live there too. One flower is among the earliest to open each year. Some animals stay above the tree line. Bighorn sheep live there. The bighorn sheep is the state animal of Colorado. Marmots are large members of their family. That family is the squirrel family. On a warm day, people can spot them on rocks. The rocks are in the sun. A marmot gives a loud squeak if danger comes near. In rocky spots, pika race about. A pika looks like a large hamster. A pika is in the rabbit family. A label on one photo reads pika. Horns on a bighorn sheep curl, and they are heavy. One pair can pass 30 pounds in weight. That weight is 14 kg.

4Bird watchers have noted about 280 kinds of birds in RMNP. Eagles ride the winds of the mountains. Ravens ride those winds too. A ptarmigan is skilled at hiding even in the open. The name sounds like TAR-mi-gin. In summer, the feathers are brown. They are also gray. Those shades match the ground. In winter, the feathers turn white. The white matches the snow. The western tanager has strong color. Inside this park, few birds show stronger color. Across the country, few birds show stronger color either. One photo shows a ptarmigan in summer. Another shows a ptarmigan in winter. Time spent here can feel easy and free. It can also feel full of adventure. Roads inside the park give drivers wide views. Trail Ridge Road is paved. No other paved road in a US national park climbs higher. This climbing road crosses the Continental Divide. Its high point sits above 12,000 feet. That height is 3,658 m. Winter closes this road. Drifts of snow can stand more than 20 feet deep. That depth is 6 m. Trail Ridge Road is a highway of the state. It opens in the last week of May. It opens then only if the weather allows.

5Colorado has fifty-eight mountains with a shared nickname. People call them fourteeners. Each of these peaks rises more than 14,000 feet. That height is 4,267 m. Longs Peak is one peak people choose most often. Some visitors want to leave traffic noise behind. For them, RMNP offers hundreds of miles of hiking paths. Some hikers set tents beside mountain lakes. They camp beside creeks that rush. Other hikers follow paths of rock. Those paths lead to the tops of mountains. Longs Peak is the tallest mountain in the park. It stands at 14,259 feet. That height is 4,346 m. Only a hiker in strong shape should try the climb. Each year, a bull elk drops his antlers. Then he grows a fresh set. More points on the antlers mean the elk is older. Autumn is a special season for a visit. Leaves on the aspen trees turn a bright yellow. People also come to watch the elk. They come to hear them. In September, a bull elk makes a high call. He does this in October too. The call draws females near. People call those females cows.

6A photo shows Ute people. The year is about 1905. This section tells how Rocky Mountain National Park came to be. The Ute once lived on this land. They also traveled through it. The Arapaho did the same. Other Native peoples did too. That land is the park today. Some of those people passed the summer in the valleys. They camped there. The valleys were beautiful. This stretch of the Rockies started to see new settlers. People seeking gold arrived too. The arrivals started in the mid-1800s. Settlers going west found these mountains hard to cross. They had to take wagons over steep passes. Other people built towns for mining. Estes Park was one such town. It stood near a place that later became an entrance to the park. A law received the president's signature in 1915. That law created Rocky Mountain National Park. A rush of building came next. People put up lodges nearby. They laid roads nearby. Even so, forest still wraps much of RMNP. Wilderness still wraps much of it too. The Ute Trail in the park keeps an old name. The name honors the people who used the path first. Rocky Mountain National Park draws huge crowds. Among US parks, this one draws some of the biggest crowds. Each year, the visitors number more than four million. They come from many parts of the world. Summer can fill this park with crowds. At times, cars jam the roads. Many visitors now ride a bus inside the park. They leave their cars behind. A graph carries the title Four Million and Counting. The scale marks 4 million visitors. It marks 3 million. It marks 2 million. It marks 1 million. One label on the graph says Total visitors. The numbers come from the National Park Service. The bottom of the graph is marked Year. The graph tracks visitors to RMNP. The count has climbed across a century.

7A person who laces up hiking boots can still find an adventure here. The park is a treasure of the nation. It is a treasure of nature as well. Ice-cold water runs in most of the park creeks. A bridge like the one in the photo helps a hiker cross. For many hikers, a bridge of that kind is a welcome sight. Here are word meanings from this book. Autumn is a noun on page 12. It means the season that follows summer. Winter comes after it. People also call this season fall. Environments is a noun on page 7. It means all the things that shape life. The setting is one particular area. Plants are part of it. Animals are part of it. Weather is part of it. The land is part of it. Glaciers is a noun on page 5. It means huge bodies of ice. The ice stays all year. The ice creeps downhill. National park is a noun on page 4. It means land that a government owns. The land is kept safe. The reason may be importance in history. The reason may be nature and wild animals. Peaks is a noun on page 4. It means mountains that are tall. It can mean the pointed tops of mountains. Range is a noun on page 5. It means mountains lined up in a row. Those mountains join one another.

37. Inside the Middle Kingdom

1Shoppers fill a popular town in China. The town dates from ancient times. Buildings in a traditional style shape its streets. Food from the local kitchens draws people in too. The country's full name is the People's Republic of China. It belongs with Russia among the largest countries on Earth. Canada belongs in that same group. The United States belongs in that group as well. A wide part of Asia is covered by Russia and by China. China is almost large enough to equal the entire continent of Europe. India shares a border with China. Mongolia shares a border with China too. Russia is a neighbor as well. North Korea is a neighbor too. The Pacific Ocean lies east of the country. The South China Sea also lies to the east. The East China Sea lies to the east too. The coast runs for about 14,500 kilometers. In miles, that same coast is about 9,000.

2Beijing's Forbidden City was put up in the early 1400s. In 1420 the emperor's family lived there for the first time. Shanghai counts as one of the large cities. Beijing counts as a large city too. Hong Kong counts as a large city too. Beijing is the capital. The capital sits in the northeast. People have changed the city's name many times. It is still among the oldest cities on Earth. More than three thousand years have passed since it began. The people living there today number more than fifteen million. Buildings from long ago stand beside modern ones. Skyscrapers rise beside structures that have lasted hundreds of years. The Forbidden City occupies the center of Beijing. This palace dates from the fifteenth century. China's emperors made it their home across more than five hundred years. A strict rule produced the name Forbidden City. Every area inside could be entered only by China's emperor. The palace welcomes visitors today as a museum. China's people number more than 1.3 billion. No other country has a larger population. The population of the United States is about 316 million. Placed beside China, that count comes to about one quarter. A village shows one way people live. More than half live in towns, in villages, or on country land. In rural regions, growing crops is common. Raising livestock for food is common there too. In towns and in villages, some people manufacture goods. Other jobs are part of life there as well. Large cities are centers for trade. They are centers for business too. Many ethnic groups share the country. The Han are the largest of those groups. The Hui (HWAY) and the Zhuang (JWAHNG) are other groups. So are the Dai (DY) and the Yao (YOW). The Miao (MEOW) are another group. Tibetans (teh-BET-uhns) and Manchus (MAN-chooz) live here too. Mongolians (mahn-GOH-lee-uhns) are a further group. Mandarin is what most people speak. The word Mandarin means “common language.” Other languages are spoken as well. Cantonese is one of those languages. English is another.

3Some Mandarin words appear as characters. The words are fire, mountain, forest, and woman. Mastering calligraphy can take years. Each line must be spaced in a perfect way. Each dot must be spaced that way too. The spacing shows balance. It also shows beauty. Chinese writing does not follow the English alphabet. What it uses instead is thousands of characters. A character can stand for a whole word. It can also stand for part of a word. Some characters look like the meaning they carry. Three trees side by side are the look of the forest character. Chinese artists have produced amazing works across thousands of years. Calligraphy stands among the more interesting of those art forms. Art and writing meet in this form. Ink is the tool calligraphers use to shape beautiful characters. Joined together, the characters become stunning images. They become powerful poetry as well. Many different landforms appear because the country is so large. The northwest holds deserts. It holds mountains. It holds canyons. One of Earth's lowest spots is in this area too. The spot is a salt lake inside the Turfan Depression. Its surface sits 154 meters below sea level. That depth is 505 feet. Several mountain ranges climb into the sky in the southwest. The Himalayas are the most famous of those ranges. The highest mountain on Earth, Mount Everest, belongs to this range. High plateaus fill parts of the southwest as well.

4The Tian Shan (tsee-EN SHAHN) Mountains rise in the country. A bamboo forest is another part of the land. The Yellow River runs through the land. The east holds flat plains. It also holds wetlands and forests. Farming fits only this flat land. A section of the Yangtze (yank-SEE) crosses this region. Part of the Yellow River crosses it too. Water for the fields comes from these rivers. Rice and wheat are crops the farmers grow. Potatoes and peanuts are crops too. Tea, barley, and cotton are further crops. Many people raise chickens and goats. They raise pigs and sheep. They raise cattle too. Experts place the start of Chinese civilization more than five thousand years in the past. China is where some of the world's oldest artifacts have turned up. People long ago kept careful records. Those records let experts study the country's past. Zhongguo (JOONG-gwah) is the name the ancient Chinese gave their land. "The Middle Kingdom" is what the name means. They believed civilized life had its center in their land. A giant wall was begun by a Chinese emperor. Its purpose was to block invaders coming from Mongolia. The building started around 220 bc. It kept going for nearly 2,000 years. What people call the Great Wall is really many walls linked as a series. Those walls stand in northern China. They also stand in southern Mongolia. One stretch covers about 8,850 kilometers. In miles, that stretch is about 5,500. For a span beyond three thousand years, emperors ruled parts of China. Sometimes one family held power in an area through hundreds of years. People called that long family rule a dynasty. Those dynasties helped China grow into an advanced civilization. The Great Wall of China remains a famous sight.

5Kong Fuzi (KOHNG FOO-tsu) is known by another name, Confucius (kuhn-FYOO-shuhs). He worked as a Chinese philosopher. He also worked as a teacher. His belief was that people should serve others. Government leaders were included in that belief. He held that education was a right belonging to every person. His death came in 479 bc. His lessons are still followed. Discoveries and inventions from ancient China are still in use. Paper was first created by these people. That step came nearly two thousand years ago. The magnetic compass was their invention too. Sailors used it when they needed to find their way. Gunpowder came from China as well. People set it off to blast rocks apart. They also used it to make fireworks for celebrations. Fabric made from silk was first produced here. For many years, silk was the export people wanted most. Buyers traveled a trade route known as the Silk Road. They came from many parts of the world to obtain silk. The fibers begin in the cocoons of silkworms. Ancient workers learned how to pull the fibers free from each cocoon. Spinning turned the fibers into a strong thread. Weaving turned the thread into beautiful cloth. A fire dragon dance belongs to the Chinese New Year. The celebration goes on for two weeks. It arrives in late January or in early February. The Chinese calendar sets the date. During the festival, dancers move through the streets with colorful dragons. They carry lions as well. Firecrackers are lit. Feasts are enjoyed. Strips of red paper, with poetry on them, decorate doorways. Good luck in the year ahead is what these customs are thought to bring. The Lantern Festival is another celebration people love. It follows soon after Chinese New Year. It marks the year's first full moon. Colorful lanterns of paper are hung outside. A riddle is fixed to each lantern. Solving a riddle can earn a small gift. Sweet dumplings made from rice flour are eaten too.

6Dumplings are a favorite in many parts of China. The filling may be meat. It may be vegetables. Many meals start from rice. Other foods used often are noodles and potatoes. Meat, fish, and tofu are used often too. What counts as a typical meal changes from one region to another. In some regions the dishes can be very spicy. The southeast sits near the ocean. Many dishes there rely on seafood as the main food. In the north, people cook with wheat where others would use rice. Fruit of the season is a usual dessert. Candies are enjoyed as well. Pancakes are enjoyed too. Shaved ice with fruit is a dish called baobing (BOU-beeng). Millions of visitors come to the Great Wall of China every year. Many people in China take it as the national symbol. The story of this country reaches back across thousands of years. The people are diverse. They have shaped cultures that are interesting. Those cultures are unique. The country reaches across a great share of Asia. Mountains that take your breath are among its wonders. The Great Wall is one of those wonders. Delicious food is another. Exciting celebrations are another. On a trip to China, which wonder would you choose to meet first?

7There are 1,355,692,576 people. The land area is 9,569,901 square kilometers. That area is 3,694,959 square miles. Beijing is the capital city. Mandarin is the primary language. Daoism is a primary religion. Buddhism is a primary religion too. The yuan (yoo-WAHN) is the currency. The Central Intelligence Agency published these figures in July 2014. The World Bank is the other source. An artifact is an object humans made long ago. It can also be an object humans used long ago. Calligraphy is writing turned into art, beautiful or stylized, by special brushes or pens. An emperor is a man who rules an empire. An ethnic group is a body of people who share traits. Race can be a shared trait. Religion, language, or culture can be a shared trait too. An export is a product sold after it is sent to another country. It can also be sent to another state. Forbidden describes something people are not allowed to have or do. A plateau is a high, wide area of land that stays flat. Silk is fabric whose threads come from a silkworm. A wetland is land that is marshy. It can also be land that is swampy.

38. Jefferson and a New Nation

1Thomas Jefferson played a key role in keeping the Library of Congress alive. Its Main Reading Room sits inside one building. That building bears his name. This account begins with a surprise winner. It looks at a man who loved words. It looks at a hard time of taxes. It looks at writings that shaped the United States. It follows him as a first leader, a second leader, and a third leader. It follows him back to his roots. Word meanings close the account. December 3, 1800, was Election Day in the United States of America. The race for president had four candidates. Counters finished the votes. There was no winner. More than two months passed. The House of Representatives voted thirty-six times. Then the House named a winner. Thomas Jefferson won. As president, he would help the young country grow. Long before that election, he had already served the country. He was one of its Founders. The Founders led the American colonies to independence. They led them away from Great Britain. His thoughts would shape the United States forever. His words would shape it too.

2Thomas Jefferson was born on April 13, 1743. The place was Shadwell. Shadwell was his family's plantation in Virginia. He was child number three in a family of six. His mother was Jane Jefferson. His father was Peter Jefferson. At that time, Virginia was one of thirteen colonies. The colonies stood in North America. Great Britain ruled them. Peter Jefferson belonged to the House of Burgesses. That house was the first assembly in North America with a special job. Voters elected it to make local laws. Peter was a mapmaker. Peter was a land surveyor. Peter owned a large amount of land. He used enslaved people to farm that land. Plantation owners used enslaved people to work their fields. Forty enslaved people were held by Jefferson's father. Jefferson inherited those people when his father died. In time he would enslave several hundred people. Jefferson believed slavery was wrong. He never freed the people he held. He did help write some founding papers of the nation. He did share in ideals about freedom. He did share in ideals about justice. He still did not believe Black people were equal to white people. Slavery ended in the United States in 1865. That year was thirty-nine years after Jefferson died. The Jefferson home held a library. The library had twenty-nine books. A personal library of that size was large for the time. Thomas had read every one of those books by his sixth birthday. Thomas was shy. Thomas had red hair. He loved the outdoors. He loved music. He loved reading more than anything else. He was always reading. He was always thinking. This was true before he started school. He started school at age nine. He liked to write his thoughts down. He liked writing them more than saying them aloud. In 1757, Thomas was fourteen. His father died that year. Thomas was heartbroken. He kept on with school. Three years later, he entered William and Mary College. After college, he studied law.

3In 1767, Jefferson began to practice law. The next year, he joined the Virginia House of Burgesses. He thought through his law cases with great care. He prepared them well. He was not a strong speaker. Inside the House of Burgesses, he stayed very quiet. People came to know him for the important papers he wrote. His career began while tensions were growing. The tensions were between Great Britain and her colonies. British lawmakers placed many rules on the colonies. They made the colonists pay high taxes. The colonists were angry. People far away were deciding how the colonists would live. The colonists wanted their own representatives in the British government. They believed in the words no taxation without representation. The British government refused them a voice. They had no voice in the making of laws. The colonists held many protests. Great Britain sent troops to the colonies. The troops were sent to keep the peace. Jefferson was one person who saw the British government as unfair. He thought a government had a duty to its citizens. The duty was to take care of them. The duty was to help them. Jefferson cast a vote in 1769 against buying goods from Britain. The vote was a protest against high taxes. Great Britain kept placing rules on the colonists. Great Britain kept placing high taxes on them. The colonists grew deeply frustrated. They decided to meet and talk about the problem. Also in 1769, Jefferson began his dream home. He built it on a mountaintop in Virginia. He designed the home himself. He named it Monticello. On New Year's Day in 1772, he married Martha Skelton. He brought her to live at Monticello. Workers were still building the house. Jefferson treated that house as a labor of love. He kept changing the design. He kept adding to the house. He did this work for forty years.

4In 1774, representatives from almost every colony went to Philadelphia. They joined the First Continental Congress. The Congress said the colonies were loyal to Great Britain. At the same time, the Congress argued about taxes. It said Parliament should not be allowed to tax the colonies. Great Britain did not end the taxes. The tensions kept growing. Armed citizens in Massachusetts fought the British Army on April 19, 1775. That fight started the Revolutionary War. A Second Continental Congress was called. A painting shows the first shots of that war. The painting is called The Shot Heard 'Round the World. In the painting, British soldiers march toward Concord, Massachusetts. Thomas Jefferson went to the Second Continental Congress. He represented Virginia. There he met leading thinkers from the other colonies. One of them was John Adams. Adams came from Boston. The two men became close friends. The Congress decided the colonies must declare independence. They would break away from Great Britain. The colonies needed a document of brilliant writing. The document had to inspire the colonists. Adams knew Jefferson was an exceptional writer. Adams insisted that Jefferson write the paper. Jefferson worked in a small room he had rented. The room was in Philadelphia. He finished a draft in seventeen days. The draft was the Declaration of Independence. Many of his own beliefs went into that paper. People still quote one part often. That part says some truths are clear without proof. It says all men are created equal. It says the Creator gives them rights. No one can rightly take those rights away. One of the rights is life. One of the rights is liberty. One of the rights is the pursuit of happiness.

5The Second Continental Congress approved the Declaration on July 4, 1776. That day became America's Independence Day. The Revolutionary War did not end until 1783. After the approval, Jefferson went back to Virginia. Voters elected him to Virginia's House of Delegates. That house used to be the House of Burgesses. He served there until 1779. He ended one unfair law about family wealth. The old law gave all of a family's wealth to the oldest son. The son received it when the father died. Jefferson argued for schooling for every white male. Family background should not block that right. He wrote laws that set up free public schools. Another paper from his pen was the Statute of Virginia for Religious Freedom. He placed many of his own beliefs about religion into that Statute. He believed each person should be free to practice any chosen religion. He wanted the church and the state kept apart. In his view, religion was one thing. Government was a different thing. He did not think either should hold power over the other. These ideas were fairly new at the time.

6In 1789, voters elected George Washington as the first United States president. Jefferson became the first secretary of state. In 1797, his friend John Adams became the second president. Jefferson became the vice president. The two friends argued about what a government should be. Adams believed a strong central government mattered. Jefferson believed the separate states should hold the strongest powers. In a strong central government, the president and Congress hold the most power. Jefferson said that setup felt too much like a king and a parliament. Colonists had fought against that kind of rule. Jefferson and Adams stopped speaking to each other. The silence lasted more than ten years. One picture shows Jefferson standing. Ben Franklin is there. John Adams is there. The three men look over a draft of the Declaration of Independence. In 1801, Jefferson became the third president. He served two terms. Together the terms lasted eight years. In those years, he cut the country's debt in half. He also doubled the size of the country. He did that through the Louisiana Purchase. He bought the land from France in 1803. Never before had two countries traded so much land without a war. The purchase pushed the western border of the United States outward. The border reached the Rocky Mountains. Nations of Native peoples lived on that land. The purchase helped the young country become an important world power. A map of the purchase in 1803 shows several areas. It shows the Louisiana Purchase. It shows the United States. It shows Spanish Territory. It shows British Territory. It shows Oregon Territory. It shows the Pacific Ocean. It shows the Atlantic Ocean. The government bought 820,000 square miles from France. That area is 2,123,790 square kilometers. The price was 15 million dollars. The price comes to 18.29 dollars for each square mile. The land covers all or part of fifteen states that exist today.

7During the War of 1812, the British Army burned the Library of Congress. That library belonged to the country. It stood in Washington, D.C. About 3,000 books were destroyed. In 1815, Jefferson sold his own book collection to the library. He was the man who loved books. The collection held 6,487 books. Those books let the library open again. Today the library holds more than 35 million books. After his second term, Jefferson went home to Virginia. He spent his days reading. He spent his days writing. He cared for his fruit garden. He cared for his vegetable garden. He helped start the first public university in Virginia. Thomas Jefferson died on July 4, 1826. John Adams died on that same day. The date was the fiftieth birthday of the United States. Today people remember Jefferson as a founder of democracy in the United States. His words helped create the country. His actions helped define it. A statue of him stands at the Jefferson Memorial. These words help with the account. The Declaration of Independence announced a break. The thirteen colonies separated from Great Britain. Democracy is a form of government run by the people. The people act through elections that are free. The elections are also frequent. The Founders are the people who helped set up the United States. They helped set up its system of government. A government is a group that holds power. The group can make laws for a country or an area. The group can enforce those laws. Independence means freedom from another group's control. It also means freedom from that group's influence, support, or help. A plantation is a large farm. Many workers labor on it. A representative is a person chosen to act for someone else. The someone else may be one person. The someone else may be a group. The representative may speak for them. The representative may vote for them. A right is a freedom a person can fairly claim. A right can also be a power. A statute is a law. The legislative branch of a government creates it.

39. Laura's Homes on the Prairie

1A map shows the places where Laura lived. In Minnesota, one home was Walnut Grove. She lived there from 1874 to 1876. She lived there again from 1878 to 1879. In South Dakota, one home was De Smet. She lived there from 1879 to 1894. In Wisconsin, one home was Pepin. She lived there from 1867 to 1869. She lived there again from 1871 to 1874. The map marks St. Paul. It marks Pierre. It marks Madison. It marks Des Moines. It marks Lincoln. It marks Springfield. It marks Topeka. It marks Jefferson City. It marks Iowa. It marks Nebraska. It marks Illinois. It marks Missouri. It marks Kansas. It marks Oklahoma. It marks the United States. A key shows state capitals. A key also shows Laura's homes. Independence, Kansas, was home from 1869 to 1870. Mansfield, Missouri, was home from 1894 to 1957. Settlers often rode in covered wagons. Laura Ingalls was born on February 7, 1867. She was born in the woods near Pepin, Wisconsin. When she was only two, Ma and Pa Ingalls packed the wagon. They put all their belongings inside. They set out for Independence, Kansas. That trip was the first of many moves. The family moved often while Laura was a child. They were settler-colonists. They were among the first white settlers in the American West. In the 1800s, settler-colonists streamed west. They cut down forests. They plowed the tough sod. Sod is a mat of grass roots. Sod covered the prairie. They plowed so crops could grow. Settlers had to make almost everything they needed in order to live. They built it. They grew it. They knitted it. They sewed it. They cooked it. This account begins on the prairie and in the big woods. It tells of hard times. It tells of a new home in Dakota Territory. It follows Laura as a young lady. It tells of happy years at Rocky Ridge. It tells of the Little House books. Word meanings come at the end.

2Laura kept vivid memories of the Kansas prairie. She remembered insects singing in the waving grass. She remembered birds singing there. She remembered sunsets coloring the wide horizon. Memories like these later shaped her famous books. The family stayed in Kansas for only about a year. Then they went back to the Wisconsin woods. Their Kansas stay rested on a mistake. They had settled on Osage land. The Osage people had lived on that land for hundreds of years. They lived there before white settlers came. The United States government did not treat the Osage as citizens. In the late 1800s, the government forced the Osage off their land. It sent them to the place now called Oklahoma. In Wisconsin, Laura was four. She found a wonderful new world there. The big woods were dark. The woods were wild. The woods were mysterious. Laura loved the outdoors. On long winter nights, Pa played his fiddle. He played for the whole family. Those were the happiest days of her childhood. Ma Ingalls had the name Caroline. Pa Ingalls had the name Charles.

3Hard times followed the happy years. Laura later wrote about leaving a little house. Everything from that house went into the wagon. The beds did not go. The tables did not go. The chairs did not go. She said Pa was always able to build new ones. Pa Ingalls missed the open prairie. When Laura was seven, he moved the family to Minnesota. At first they lived in a house of prairie sod. Prairie sod is the thick grass that grew all around them. Later, Pa built a house of wood. He planted wheat near Walnut Grove. Laura went to school with her older sister. The sister's name was Mary. Laura loved reading. Laura loved writing. Laura loved reciting. A few happy months passed. Then hardship struck. Grasshoppers came in huge swarms. Each swarm was as big as a thundercloud. The insects ate all the wheat. The crop was gone. The family moved into town. They took odd jobs. Even young Laura worked in a hotel. She cleaned there. She waited on tables. Then the family lost a new baby boy. His name was Freddie. A fever killed him. Spirits rose a little when another baby came. The baby was Grace. Grace arrived in 1877. Life stayed hard. Mary fell ill. The illness may have damaged the nerves in her eyes. A stroke may have done it. No one knows the cause for sure. Mary stayed blind for the rest of her life. Pa chose to look west for a fresh start. He went ahead to Dakota Territory. He took work with the railroad. The rest of the family boarded a train in Walnut Grove. They rode west to join him. A picture from about 1880 shows the sisters. Laura is on the right. Carrie is on the left. Mary is with them.

4A railroad could make a town appear very fast. The growth could seem to happen overnight. Helena, Montana, looked like that in 1869. Settlers rushed in. They built houses in a hurry. They built stores. They built hotels. They built saloons. Dakota Territory had only a railroad stop at first. A town was not there yet. The Ingalls family helped start De Smet, South Dakota. A few other white families were there too. They were the first citizens. More settlers arrived later. The Ingalls family then moved to a homestead. The homestead sat a few miles outside town. Pa built a cabin. Pa built a stable. Pa built a garden. In winter, the family lived over their store in town. The girls would be nearer the school. The United States government wanted white settlers to move west. It offered homesteads. A homestead was a place to live at almost no cost. The land had been taken from Native people. Laura, like many white settlers, held racist views. The views were about the Native people whose land she lived on. Her books often showed those people in a negative way. The books showed white settlers in a positive way. The choice to live over the store was lucky. Blizzard after blizzard hit the new town. The snow blinded the girls. They could not find the way to school. Trains could not bring food. Some families nearly starved. At last the long winter ended. The family went back to the homestead. The Dakota prairie there was in bloom.

5Laura wrote that suffering comes to an end. She wrote that love lasts forever. Mary learned of a college in Iowa. The college taught blind students. Mary wanted to go there more than anything. Laura was old enough to teach school. She knew her pay could help Mary. The pay would go toward college. She took a teaching job. The school was twelve miles from De Smet. Laura was sixteen. She said goodbye to her family. She was brave about it. She still dreaded the months away from home. The first school week ended with a surprise. Almanzo Wilder drove up to the schoolhouse. He was a young homesteader. Laura knew him from De Smet. He arrived in a beautiful sleigh. Horses pulled the sleigh. He offered to drive her home on weekends. On the long prairie drives, a friendship grew. The friendship turned into love. One night he asked her to marry him. She agreed. She gave him a nickname. The nickname was Manly. Laura and Manly moved to his homestead. The homestead was outside De Smet. Soon a daughter was born. They named her Rose. Then bad luck hit the young family again and again. A hailstorm destroyed the wheat. Then Laura became ill. Manly became ill too. Both of them recovered. Manly walked with a limp after that. The limp lasted the rest of his life. Laura gave birth to a baby boy. The boy lived only two weeks. Then the house burned down. Laura was still a settler at heart. She looked for a new place. The family needed a fresh start. She soon found the home she wanted. A picture shows Laura and Almanzo Wilder in 1885. The picture was made soon after the wedding. Before the twentieth century, people had few vaccines. They had very few medicines that worked well. Diseases were common. Diseases were serious. Illness touched many people in Laura's family. That pattern was common in those years.

6Rocky Ridge Farm did so well that a newspaper noticed. The local paper asked Laura to write a column. The column gave farm advice. That column was her first paid writing job. She wrote that sweet, simple parts of life are the true ones. At age twenty-seven, she set out in a covered wagon again. She and Manly bought a small farm near Mansfield, Missouri. The farm sat in the foothills of the Ozark Mountains. The land was rough. The land was wooded. The land was rocky. Laura knew at once that it was home. Hard work changed Rocky Ridge. It became a beautiful farm. It became a successful farm. After so many moves, Laura felt content. In time, Rose left home. Rose went to San Francisco. There Rose became a well-known journalist. Rose earned her living by writing. Laura had always loved writing. Life on the farm was comfortable by then. Could Laura earn a living that way too? Laura began to put her memories on paper. Some nights she wrote until morning. She worked at a small desk. Manly had built the desk for her. Soon a whole book was finished. The book told of her early years. Those years were under the tall trees of Wisconsin. She named the book Little House in the Big Woods. Rose sent the book to a publisher. Laura was sixty-four. She did not expect much to come of it. The time was the 1930s. People had cars. People had electricity. People had radio. She wondered who would want those old stories. The stories told of backbreaking chores. The chores took place in a dark forest. The book became a hit at once. Letters filled the mailbox at Rocky Ridge. Young readers begged for more books. She wrote about the Kansas prairie next. That book is Little House on the Prairie. She wrote about Walnut Grove in another book. That book is On the Banks of Plum Creek. The terrible winter in De Smet became The Long Winter. The time after that winter became Little Town on the Prairie.

7A picture shows Laura Ingalls Wilder signing her books. The time is around 1940. She loved writing back to her fans. She loved trips to readings. She loved book signings. She was always glad to come home afterward. She came home to Manly. She came home to Rocky Ridge Farm. Both of them lived into their nineties. She died in 1957. Her books kept living after that. In 1974, the stories even became a series on television. Tales of settler life still delight young readers. Her own life is now part of American history. These words help with the account. Hardship means pain or loss. It can also mean a thing that causes pain or loss. A homestead was land from the United States government. People got the land if they settled it and farmed it. This was especially true in the 1800s. Income is money that comes from work. Income can also come from another source. A prairie is a wide flat plain. Grasses cover it. A publisher is a person who brings writing to the public. A company can be a publisher too. The writing may be printed. The writing may appear on the Internet. Reciting means speaking words aloud from memory. Settler-colonists leave one place to live in another. They plan to stay. They claim a piece of land as their own. A stroke is a fast loss of brain function. It happens when blood flow to the brain is interrupted. Vivid means very bright. Vivid also means very strong.

40. Life in the Deep Sea

1A deep ocean anglerfish has a spine. The end of that spine is bright. The fish uses the bright end like a fishing pole. The pole helps it catch fish. A sea pig of the deep ocean is small. It moves along the ocean floor. It eats tiny sea animals. Those animals live in the mud. Picture a visit to the ocean at its deepest point. Which sights would you expect on that trip? Would the place be dark? Would the place be light? Would the water be clear? Would the water be cloudy? Would the water be warm? Would the water be cold? Could strange new animals show up? Would you find strange new plants? The bright tip of the spine is a lure. Fish are what the lure can catch. Tiny mud animals are the sea pig's food. The deepest water may be dark. It may instead be light. The water may be clear. It may instead be cloudy. The water may be warm. It may instead be cold. Strange new animals are one possible find. Strange new plants are another possible find.

2Sunlight warms the top of the ocean. That top is the shallow part. The warm reach goes down about 200 meters. That depth is 656 feet. Plants that need sunlight live in this warm layer. Animals that need sunlight live there too. Animals that need warm water live there too. Dolphins live in this layer. Sharks live in this layer. Other animals live deep down. Their water is cold. Their water is dark. Squid live in that deep water. Viperfish live in that deep water. Tide pools are warm and shallow. Species of starfish live in tide pools. Anemones live there. Mussels live there. Tiny crabs live there. The ocean is split into zones. Each zone is a different layer. About 200 meters, or 656 feet, is the sunlit bottom edge. Dolphins and sharks share that warmer water. Squid and viperfish share the cold dark water.

3The deep ocean begins below 1,000 meters. That depth is 3,281 feet. The water there is freezing cold. Water pressure is the weight of the water. Below 39.6 meters, that weight can harm you. That depth is 130 feet. Sport divers wear air tanks. The tanks hold a mixture of gases. The mixture is for safety. Deep divers wear a special suit. The suit is hard. It protects them from water pressure. The deepest trips use a small submarine. People call it a submersible. Two or three scientists ride together. A chart can show a swimmer near the top. The chart measures depth in feet. Marks run from 0 feet to 21,000 feet. The marks step by 1,000 feet. A sport diver is marked deeper than the swimmer. Near 15 meters the water is warmer. That depth is 49 feet. The temperature is about 13 to 21 degrees Celsius. That is about 55 to 70 degrees Fahrenheit. Another mark sits near 39 meters. That depth is 128 feet. The temperature there is about 7 to 13 degrees Celsius. That is about 45 to 55 degrees Fahrenheit. A diving suit is marked much deeper. Near 609 meters the water is colder. That depth is 2,000 feet. The temperature is about 4.5 to 7 degrees Celsius. That is about 40 to 45 degrees Fahrenheit. A submersible craft is marked far down. Near 6,096 meters the water is almost freezing. That depth is 20,000 feet. The temperature is about 0 to 2 degrees Celsius. That is about 32 to 35 degrees Fahrenheit. Ocean scientists are called oceanographers. Say that word as oh-shuh-NOG-ruh-furs. They often live on a ship for months. They work seven days a week. Some of them learn what a sea animal eats. Some study where a sea animal lives. The ocean water itself is the topic for others. Every oceanographer must understand biology. Every oceanographer must understand chemistry. Living things are the subject of biology. Chemistry asks what things are made of.

4A tag can ride on a sea animal. A satellite can help track where it travels. Scientists put electronic sensors on some animals. A satellite reads the signal from the tag. The tag may sit on a fin. The tag may sit on the back. Where the animal travels shows up in the signal. Coral sea fans are a swimming place for lionfish. Their spines are long. Their spines are feathery. A spine can sting a predator that comes too close. Lionfish are native to the South Pacific. United States waters had a first lionfish sighting in the year 2000. People ask which animals live in the ocean. People ask where those animals live. People ask how many there are. Oceanographers spent ten years on those questions. They studied animals. They counted animals. They looked at oceans all over the world. The search took in the North Pole and the South Pole. Census of Marine Life is the name of that report. The report came out in 2010. More than 540 groups of researchers added information.

5The leafy sea dragon can blend with nearby plants. Each eye can move on its own. It can look two ways at once. The male carries the eggs. He carries them for the female. The male gives birth. The Census of Marine Life held surprises. The first surprise was the places where life was found. Life was found everywhere. It was found at every water level. It was found at every temperature. The hottest places in the ocean still held marine species. Marine species lived in the coldest ocean places. Some species lived deep with no light. Some species lived deep with no oxygen. The number of new species was another surprise. The count of described new marine species is more than twelve hundred. Giant tube worms live very deep. Their home is over 2 kilometers down. That depth is 1.3 miles. They live in hot water. A lava pillar sends the hot water bubbling up. The cold Deep Ocean Zone also holds hot volcanoes. Water at a volcano can reach 400 degrees Celsius. That heat is 752 degrees Fahrenheit. The heat is high enough to melt lead. This very hot water holds some shrimp. Some crabs live in it. Some worms live in it. Some bacteria live in it. Sulfur is what lets these animals stay alive. They use sulfur instead of sunlight.

6The Arctic also held sea life in huge groups. They live in freezing water. Some species live under solid ice. The ice is 700 meters thick. That thickness is 2,296 feet. Squid are among those species. Cod are among them. Jellyfish are among them. Many of these species slow their movements. Slow movement saves energy. Saved energy helps them avoid freezing. The Antarctic ice fish lacks red blood cells. Its blood is thinner than most fish blood. A kind of antifreeze is in that blood. The antifreeze lets the fish survive. The water under the ice is frigid. The blood of most fish would freeze in that cold. Scientists learned that life can change more than they first thought. Deep ocean animals have ways to survive. They survive in harsh environments. Most other species could not live in those places. A big-eyed squid lives in cold deep water. The place is the northern Atlantic Ocean. The marine census counted a huge number of species. The count was more than 230,000 species. Species never seen before numbered more than 6,000. Some creatures were see-through. Some had warning lights. The lights turned on. The lights turned off. Some had long feelers. They had feelers instead of eyes. Some had teeth so long they crossed. The teeth overlapped the jaws. Some animals make their own antifreeze. That antifreeze helps them stay warm.

7Which other living things share the deep ocean? Scientists keep finding new species. They keep finding new facts. More looking brings more finds. They think only one-fourth of marine life has been counted. A written description exists for only part of those species. Many surprises are still ahead. Each week, new discoveries join the marine census. Adaptable means able to change. The change helps a living thing fit a new situation. The change can also fit a certain environment. Marine means of the sea. Marine can mean related to the sea. A sensor is a device. It can sense a signal. It can sense a change in conditions. It reacts to what it senses. A species is a group of living things. The members look physically similar. The members can reproduce. A submersible is a small vessel. It can travel under water. It can work at deep levels. Sulfur is a yellow chemical element. Sulfur is common. Burning sulfur smells unpleasant. One-fourth is the share scientists think has been counted. Weekly discoveries still join the census. A submersible is built for deep underwater work. Sulfur is yellow, common, and foul when it burns. Species members are similar and can reproduce.

41. Life on the Indian Peninsula

1A map of the region labels Afghanistan and China. It labels Bhutan and Pakistan. It labels New Delhi and Nepal. It labels Bangladesh and Myanmar, a country also called Burma. Mumbai appears on the map. So do the Arabian Sea and the Bay of Bengal. Asia is labeled too. The Andaman Islands, the Indian Ocean, Sri Lanka, and the Nicobar Islands are labeled. India lies in Asia. Most of it is land nearly wrapped by water, called a peninsula. Pakistan sits on the northern edge. China sits on that edge too. Nepal (nuh-PAHL) is there as well. So is Bhutan (boo-TAHN). Bangladesh (bahng-gluh-DESH) lies east of India. Myanmar (mee-AHN-mar) lies to the east too. Sri Lanka (SHREE LAHNG-kuh) is an island just to the south. The Arabian Sea lies to the southwest. The Bay of Bengal lies to the southeast. Groups of islands belong to India as well.

2New Delhi was built nearly one hundred years ago inside the older city of Delhi. It was created to serve as the capital of British India. New Delhi (DEL-ee) is India's capital. It forms one part of the larger city called Delhi. The two areas do not look alike. Delhi itself is very old. The newer area rose in the 1920s and the 1930s. Small streets in Delhi form a complex maze. Small shops crowd those streets. Wide streets run through New Delhi. Modern shopping centers line them. The two areas together hold more than twenty million people. Across the whole country, the number of people is more than one billion. China is the only country with more. Most Indians live in the countryside. Large cities do exist here. Running water is missing from most homes in rural areas. People walk to a well each day. They carry water back home. Electricity is missing for about half of rural residents too. Growing crops, mainly grains, is how half the people earn a living. Cattle are raised here. Water buffalo are raised too. Other animals are raised as well. Cattle are not kept for meat by most people. Many faiths here hold cows as sacred. Hinduism is one of those faiths. Plowing fields is one use of cattle. Carrying loads and people is another use. Meat comes from sheep, goats, and chickens. The Taj Mahal (TAHZH muh-HAHL) ranks among India's best-known landmarks. Emperor Shah Jahan ordered it in memory of his wife. Her name was Mumtaz Mahal. He gave the order after her death. Forced laborers needed twenty-two years to complete the building.

3The word Bollywood blends Bombay with Hollywood. Its movies are famous worldwide for sets that are elaborate. Singing is part of that fame. Dancing is part of it too. Cities here are extremely crowded in most cases. Apartment buildings hold many of the residents. Very narrow streets run beside those buildings. Demand for electricity has been hard to meet because the population is so large. Power often goes out in some cities. Filmmaking in the Hindi language is centered in Mumbai (mum-BY). The British name Bombay came from saying Mumbai the wrong way. Each year, Mumbai puts out more than a thousand movies. In India and abroad, people call this film world Bollywood. Viewers of those films live all over the world. The land includes the Indus River and the Himalayan Mountains. It includes Mount Everest and the Indo-Gangetic Plain. It includes the Thar Desert and the Ganges River. It includes the Chota Nagpur Plateau and the Sundarban. It includes the Deccan Plateau, the Eastern Ghats, and the Western Ghats. India's northern border is formed by the Himalayas (him-uh-LAY-uhz). These peaks are the highest mountains on Earth. The peaks cut India off from Asia's other lands. Because of that separation, people sometimes call India a subcontinent. A low plain stretches across the north. River floods long ago dropped soil and left this rich ground. Western India is the site of the Thar Desert.

4The Ganges is a river Hindus treat as sacred. Bathing in its water is a common act. The Ganges (GAN-jeez) runs through the northern plains. The Indus runs there too. Hindus travel from every part of the country to step into the Ganges. South of the Indus and the Ganges, higher ground covers more than half the country. In May or in June, winds move north off the Indian Ocean. Those winds carry rain. People call this rainy stretch the monsoon season. On almost every afternoon, rain falls. This hot, wet season supplies most of the country's rain. The Hindu goddess of wealth is the figure Diwali honors. It is a festival of lights. Many special days are celebrated here. Diwali (dee-VAH-lee) goes on for five days. In that time, people from many religions light small candles. Firecrackers are set off. Sweets and other gifts go to friends and to family. Holi (HOH-lee) is a Hindu festival of spring. People know it as the festival of colors. People of every religion now join Holi. They use it to mark winter's end. Colored water is thrown. Colored powder is thrown too. Children spray friends from water guns loaded with colored water. There is singing. There is dancing. Special foods and drinks are enjoyed. Balloons full of colored water are thrown by many children.

5India's civilization reaches far back. One ruler had mostly united this land's people about 2,300 years ago. Muslims founded a kingdom about 1,300 years ago. Delhi stood at its center. That Muslim rule continued until the 1600s. Europeans had come ashore as early as 1498. In that year Vasco da Gama (VAHS-koh DAH GAH-mah) sailed here. He was an explorer from Portugal. Great Britain had control of the country by 1858. During the 1920s, the lawyer Mohandas Gandhi (moh-huhn-DAHS GAHN-dee) joined the effort against British rule. He asked for protests that were nonviolent. Nonviolent means peaceful. Freedom came by 1947. Many obstacles have appeared since independence. Growth has continued even so. Indira Gandhi became the first woman to serve as prime minister. The year was 1966. Forty-one years after that, in 2007, the country chose its first woman president. A large picture shows Mohandas Gandhi. City streets often have monkeys on them. People give them food. The food is why the monkeys remain. An alarmed cobra spreads its hood. Many exciting species live in the wild here. No other country has both lions and tigers. Two kinds of leopards live here as well. High in the Himalayas, the snow leopard is very rare. Among reptiles, the cobra is one of the most famous. Small animals are the prey of the Indian cobra. Rats are among that prey. Mice are among it too. Towns and villages are often where that prey lives. Bites to humans have happened.

6Strength is a mark of the Indian elephant. In earlier times, people had it lift logs in the forests. Crops were carried by elephant too. People were carried as well. Some workers still get help from elephants. Wild Indian elephants are found across most of the country today. Loss of habitat has left them endangered. Near the Bay of Bengal, mangrove forest is a special home. Sea turtles use that home. Sharks use it. Crocodiles use it too. The same saltwater swamp is the biggest home of Bengal tigers anywhere. A male can reach 3 meters in length. That length is 10 feet. His weight can reach 230 kilograms. That weight is 500 pounds. These tigers often swim the swamp while they hunt. This big cat is endangered. Scientists are working hard so it can be protected. India produces spices for the whole world. The share is about 70 percent. Women sell those spices at a market. The history here is long. The population is very large and diverse. Leaders are trying to care for people and still protect natural beauty. Fascinating animals help make the country unique. Interesting people do too. There is much to learn about it.

7The population is 1.4 billion. The land area is 2,973,193 square kilometers. That area is 1,147,956 square miles. New Delhi is the capital city. Hindi is the primary language. Hinduism is the primary religion. The rupee (ROO-pee) is the currency. The Central Intelligence Agency published these figures in February 2022. The World Bank is the other source. Endangered means a living thing might die out for good. Hindu refers to India's major religion. That faith has many gods. It includes belief in reincarnation. A mangrove is a tree of the tropics. It can grow in a swamp. It can grow in shallow salt water. A monsoon is a storm tied to a season. It brings rain in heavy amounts. Muslims are people of the Islamic faith. A peninsula is a long reach of land. Water wraps almost all the way around it. Sacred means linked to religion or to the spirit. A subcontinent is a big piece of land inside a continent.

42. Mammals With Almost No Hair

1Chinese crested dogs may have no hair. They may also have long hair. Mammals share a few traits. The body stays warm on its own. The young are nursed. Hair grows on most mammals. Some grow a great deal of it. Others grow almost none. Breeding has removed the hair from some mammals that normally have it. Common pets include breeds without hair. There are dogs of this kind. There are cats of this kind. There are guinea pigs of this kind too. Wild country has mammals without hair as well. Take a look nearby. Animals with little hair show up all around you.

2Dogs that lack hair have a history that reaches far back. One breed is the xolo (SHOW-low). More than three thousand years is how long it has been around. Some people think xolos traveled with the first humans to enter Central America. Mexico now claims the xolo as its national dog. A Mexican xolo has the look of the breed. Hairless dogs called Inca orchids live in Peru. Few breeds anywhere are rarer than this one. Most of the hairless Inca orchids wear a small tuft on the head. The tuft can seem almost like a wig of fur. Hair is present on some Inca orchids. A single litter can hold both hairy pups and pups with none. How is that possible? The reason sits in the genes. Genes are the instructions your cells follow. They help decide the person you become. One result is the color of your hair. Another result is the height you will reach.

3A chart of genes shows how a hairless kitten can appear. A cat that receives a hairless gene from only one parent can later give that gene to kittens. A cat that receives it from both parents will grow no hair. The chart labels one gene as regular, the hairy kind. It labels another gene as hairless. Two parents with hair are shown. Among the young, one kitten has no hair and three have hair. Parents hand genes down to children. Every child does not receive the same ones. Brothers and sisters often look a bit like both parents. The likeness shows up in different ways. A hairless dog is also explained by genes. A hairless cat is explained by genes too. Pass the same hairless gene along for many years, and a new breed can appear. Breeds like these sometimes need help staying alive. A coat of hair keeps a mammal warm. Sweaters are what many owners use on pets that have no coat. Sunburn is a danger those owners also prevent. Most cat breeds without hair go back only a short time, measured in decades. A gene changed on its own. The beautiful sphynx came from that change. A new breed of cat began with it. Among cats with no hair, the sphynx is the one people choose most. A sphynx is this hairless breed. A gene can shift when no one expects it. That shift is known as a mutation. Most breeds without hair started from a mutation of this sort. The gene was meant to order the body to grow hair. The order changed. Young animals inherited the changed gene. A breed without hair grew from that line.

4The peterbald, a cat without hair, comes from Russia. It might be mistaken for a grouchy creature from space. Friendly is what it really is. Playful is what it really is too. So much activity gives it a high body temperature. That heat makes the cat a warm one to hold. A seat on a lap, if the lap is warm, is what these cats look for. Fur in a heavy layer is absent. A very fine coat is still present on most of them. A stroking hand meets a surface like a fuzzy peach. A peterbald shows this sleek shape. A guinea pig is a rodent people like to keep. One hairless kind is known as the skinny pig. It looks thin because the thick fuzzy coat of other guinea pigs is missing. Care must be special. Furry relatives do not need that same care. With nothing covering the skin, injury comes easily. Illness comes easily too. The safe place for them is indoors. A skinny pig stands without a fuzzy coat.

5A furry rat can be paired with a rat that has no hair. The fancy rat is another rodent kept without a coat. Most of the time, rats are social animals. Social here means they like other rats close by. Fancy rats without hair do better when furry rats live with them. The company supports their happiness. It supports their health too. An allergy is the body reacting to something it does not want. Sneezing can be one result. Itchy eyes can be a result. A sore throat can be a result. Pets with hair trouble some people. Dander is the usual cause. Tiny flakes of skin are what dander is. No pet is free of dander. Hairless pets have no thick fur where those flakes can collect. Frequent washing of the pet can lessen sniffles and sneezes. Washing the home can help as well. Pet allergies are what cause those signs. Animals in the wild can live with little hair too. Their bodies have adapted to that kind of life. Odd looks mark a rodent known as the naked mole rat. It lacks the thick fur seen on most rats. Pink skin full of wrinkles covers the body. A true mole is not what it is. A true rat is not what it is either. Fully bare is not quite right either. Hairs grow between the toes. While tunnels are dug, those hairs brush dirt away.

6When two naked mole rats need to pass in a tunnel, a habit takes over. The smaller animal usually drops low. The bigger one can crawl across the top. Every part of life happens under the ground. The setting is a world of tunnels. Body heat is managed by moving from spot to spot. On hot days they head to deeper tunnels. Those tunnels feel cooler. On cold days they press together in a group. Each animal shares warmth with the others. An Asian elephant shows how little hair some giants have. Certain mammals have adapted to places where a heavy coat is not required. Elephants grow thick skin. Rhinoceroses grow thick skin too. Sun and heat are blocked by that skin. The ocean is home to whales. It is home to dolphins too. A thick fat layer called blubber holds their warmth in cold water. Hair is not the covering that does this job. People have very little hair of their own. Cold days call for coats. Hot days call for shorts. Houses are warmed through the winter. They are cooled through the summer. Humans have adapted to sparse hair, as those other mammals have. A beluga whale uses blubber to stay warm at sea.

7You can find animals with little hair, or with none, in many places. Wild animals are one group. Pets you can cuddle are another. Some of the breeds go back thousands of years. Some of the breeds are new. The next animal without hair is impossible to predict. They would question anyone who calls them hairless. Pets with no coat have a defender in the Peruvian Inca orchid. The Mexican xolo defends them too. A Chinese crested will do the same. Adapted means a living thing changed to fit a certain place or a new one. Breeds are matching groups of animals or plants. The match comes from people guiding how they reproduce. Bred means someone raised an animal or a plant to get a chosen kind of offspring. Genes are tiny packages of information. They move traits from an older generation to a younger one. A mammal is a warm-bodied animal with a backbone. Hair or fur is part of the body. The young are nursed. Babies arrive by live birth. A mutation is a gene change between one generation and the next. Growth of the offspring may change because of it. What the offspring can do may change too.

43. Maria Tallchief and American Ballet

1Six parts make up this book. A national treasure comes first. Early years come second. A New York star comes third. A Chicago teacher comes fourth. Her legacy comes fifth. Word meanings come last. The year 1999 brought Maria Tallchief a major honor. National Medal of Arts was the honor. Dance history gives her a place unlike any other. America had never had a prima ballerina before her. A prima ballerina is the woman who leads a ballet. Famous ballet dancers in her era came from Europe. Talent was hers in large measure. Hard work was hers too. Those gifts made her American ballet's first prima ballerina. She also helped the United States fall in love with ballet. The story opens on a reservation. Oklahoma is the state. Native peoples were the people of that land.

2A childhood picture shows Betty Marie. The birth name was long. It is Elizabeth Marie Tall Chief Osage. January 24 was the day. The year was 1925. Fairfax, Oklahoma, was the town. Betty Marie was the childhood name. Ki He Kah Stah Tsa is the family name. Her father belonged to the Osage tribe. OH-sayj is how to say Osage. Her mother was a white woman. Home was the Osage reservation. That land was kept for Osage people. Oil was discovered on the reservation during her father's youth. Wealth followed for him. A 1930 family picture has three people. Betty Marie is on the left. Marjorie is on the right. Their mother stands with them. The year 1956 shows Marjorie's sons. They are watching the sisters dance. Marjorie was the younger sister. Music came easily to both girls. Piano lessons started when they were small. Dance lessons started then too. Los Angeles became home in 1933. California offered more room to study dance. A dance school was found before any house was bought. Ballet was one subject. Tumbling was another. Tap dancing was another. Spanish dancing was another.

3Vaslav Nijinsky ranks among great dancers. Bronislava Nijinska, his sister, ranks among them too. Age twelve brought a decision from Betty Marie. Piano lessons stopped. Ballet would be the only study. Madame Nijinska soon became her teacher. Say the name nuh-JIN-skuh. This was the first ballet teacher who truly mattered. Study with her lasted from 1937 to 1942. Power entered her dancing in those years. Grace entered it. Feeling entered it. High school ended in 1942. Ballet, she knew, was the right path. A Russian ballet company toured in 1938. Shows went all over the United States. New York City was their base. Los Angeles got a visit. Betty Marie saw great ballet then for the first time. A career in ballet grew even more exciting. The Song of Norway was a 1944 show she prepared to dance. New York came after high school. Many companies refused her. The Russian company she had watched said yes. A new name was chosen. Maria Tallchief was that name.

4George Balanchine joined her company two years after she did. He was a choreographer of rare gifts. Ballet, in his hands, became works of art. Tallchief gave those works a living form. The pair practiced as partners. She later described her Firebird night in writing. City Center, she said, roared. A stadium after a touchdown was the comparison. The year 1948 brought a brand-new company from Balanchine. New York City Ballet was its name. Tallchief became a member. Success for the company had her help. Fame arrived with a leading role. Firebird was the ballet. She danced the lead. Prima ballerina was a rank no American woman had held until then.

5Some of Balanchine's loveliest ballets were made for Tallchief. Sugar Plum Fairy was one role. The Nutcracker is that ballet. Swan Princess was another role. Swan Lake is that ballet. Travel came during the New York City Ballet years. Nineteen other countries saw her dance with the company. Television saw her dance. A movie saw her dance. One scene pairs her with a partner in Swan Lake. Another scene shows the Swan Princess. Celebration followed her wherever she performed. People prized the feeling inside the steps. The style was grand. The style was free as well. Other ballerinas did not dance in that same mix.

6The year 1965 found Tallchief at age forty-one. Hard work had worn her out. Marriage to Henry Paschen had come nine years earlier. A daughter was theirs. Performing stopped. Chicago became home so the family could be together. From 1975 until 1987 she served as creative director. Two Chicago ballet companies were under her lead. Students of hers later rose high in ballet. A scene shows her dancing with daughter Elise. More time also went to Balanchine's ballets. His death came in 1983. Other ballerinas he had trained joined Tallchief. They recorded sections of his works. Students could learn from the recordings. The late 1990s brought several important awards. Death came at age eighty-eight. April 11, 2013, was the date. Chicago was the city. The world still remembers the life. The world still remembers the talents. A timeline lists the marks. Birth was January 24 in the year 1925. A family move to Los Angeles came in 1933. Nijinska lessons began in 1937. High school graduation came in 1942. That same year she reached New York City. She entered a company there. Ballet Russe de Monte Carlo was the company. Balanchine arrived there as choreographer in 1944, two years after she joined. Membership in New York City Ballet began in 1948. She was that company's first prima ballerina. Firebird was performed in 1949. The Paschen marriage was in 1956. Elise was born in 1959. Retirement from performing came in 1965, still at forty-one, along with the Chicago move. From 1975 to 1987 she led two ballet companies in Chicago as creative director. Death came on April 11 in 2013.

7The United States had its first prima ballerina in Maria Tallchief. A new ballet style grew with her help. American ballet had its first Native star in her. Doors opened for people of every background. Grace carried the art forward. Talent carried it forward. Achievements carried it forward. One dancer summed her up in a single word. Grand is the word. The White House saw her dance in 1964. President Clinton made a presentation in 1999. The honor he gave was the National Medal of Arts. His words credited her with a change. An art form had belonged to Europe. She made it belong to America. He called the match a fitting one. The person who did it was a Native American woman. Word meanings come next. Achievements are successes earned by effort. Ballet is a dance form. Exact formal steps mix with light leaps and with turns. A choreographer sets a dance's style. That person also arranges the steps and the moves. A company is a group of artists working and performing as one group. Dancers can belong. Actors can belong. Grace is ease plus charm, in movement or in behavior. A reservation is land where Native peoples had to live. The government of the U.S. forced that, long ago.

44. Pavel Shows His Museum Home

1Welcome to the State Hermitage Museum in St. Petersburg, Russia. My name is Pavel. Let me show you my home. It is the greatest museum in the world. It has a rich history. It holds more than three million works of art. It has survived many disasters. All of these characteristics make my home great. Just you wait and see.

2The Winter Palace stands on the bank of the River Neva. That river flows through St. Petersburg. The palace is known for its unique green color. The Hermitage cannot show all three million works at once. Storage holds many of them. The museum is open for about eight hours each day. It is closed for many holidays. Picture two minutes spent on each work of art. Only open hours would count. More than forty-three years would pass before the whole collection was seen. From 1762 to 1796, Catherine the Great was Russia's empress. An empress is like a queen. My great-grandmother's grandmother knew Catherine well. They lived in the Winter Palace together. The Winter Palace is the largest of the seven buildings that make up the Hermitage today. Hermitage comes from French. It means a place of seclusion.

3The Hermitage began as Catherine the Great's own art collection. She began by buying paintings. In 1764, she bought 225 paintings by Dutch and Flemish masters. Like other collectors, she kept expanding the collection as years passed. Famous artists made several of those paintings. Rembrandt was among them. Rubens was among them. Some of those paintings can still be seen here. During her reign, no visitor was allowed in to see the collection. She said once that only she and the mice could admire all of it. For hundreds of years, mice have been a problem in this museum. Cats were brought inside to patrol. Their job was to stop mice from ruining the collections. About fifty cats still walk these halls. More than 200 years have passed since those first purchases. The collection grew from them into one of the largest anywhere. It is also one of the most impressive. Come along and see a few pieces with me.

4Two of the most loved paintings here are probably by Leonardo da Vinci, the Italian artist. Madonna with a Flower is one title. He painted it in 1479. It entered the collection in 1914. Madonna and Child is the other title. He painted it in 1490. Being small lets me get close to both. A crowd almost always stands around them. In this collection, my favorite works come from Gauguin, the French artist. A whole room is given over to him. Picasso has a room of his own. Matisse has a room of his own. I could stay in the Gauguin room for a full day. I picture myself on Tahiti, the island where he worked. Many pieces in this collection come from that stay. Every painting from his Tahiti trip delights me. One of them dates from 1891. The shapes he chose earn my admiration. The colors earn it too.

5The museum building itself is another favorite work. Elaborate decoration covers many rooms. Paint covers them too. Pavilion Hall makes me feel I have walked into a wedding cake. Feelings like that keep my interest alive. I want every room explored. I want every hall and every corner explored. One room lets me pretend I am Peter I. His rule in Russia ran from 1689 to 1725. His clothes are part of what is on view. His furniture is part of it. His books are part of it. His tools are part of it. Other everyday objects are part of it. Peeking into a real Russian emperor's life is fascinating. Most days, tourists explore beside me. Pride comes from a choice made by the Russian emperor Nicholas I. Public visitors were allowed in because of him. The year was 1852. A new building was his project. New Hermitage was the name he gave it. Hiding this beautiful collection from everyone would have been terrible.

6The first disaster was survived by my great-great-grandmother. The year was 1836. A large fire in the Winter Palace put the collection at risk. Palace soldiers carried precious items outside. They carried those items out to the square. Speed was everything. Almost all of it was saved. Restoration came to many rooms after the fire. The War Gallery of 1812 is one of those rooms. Stories of the second disaster came from my grandmother. The year was 1917. Russia went through a revolution. The palace no longer held the royal family. Nicholas II was forced out with them. The art was moved by the new government. Moscow, the capital, was where it went. Nobody truly knew the art's future. By October 1917, that government had lost its power. A second new government brought the art home. The Winter Palace and the New Hermitage became a state museum. The third disaster was survived by my mother. The year was 1941.
