German troops launched an attack. Russia was pulled into the Second World War. More than a million works were kept safe from the fighting. Art filled two special trains. A remote part of Russia received them. The Ural Mountains are where they went. St. Petersburg was cut off by the Germans for 900 days. Leningrad was the city's name then. The siege killed hundreds of thousands of people. Workers from the Hermitage were among the dead. Sand in the basement buried many sculptures. That sand kept them safe through the war.

7The collection endured. A small group of paintings was the start. One Russian empress loved them. Several disasters were survived. It grew into one of the oldest art collections. It is among the largest. It is among the most impressive. People across the world enjoy it now. The Hermitage is where I live. My great-grandmother's grandmother lived here before me. Now you can see why I call it the world's greatest museum. It is also a fine place to live. Characteristics are features that help you recognize a person or a thing. A collection is a set of similar items someone chooses to keep. A disaster is a terrible event that strikes suddenly. Elaborately means with many parts and many details. Endured means lived on through a hard challenge. Impressive means able to leave a strong, lasting effect. Precious means greatly valued and deeply loved. A revolution forces one government out and puts another in its place. During a siege, an enemy is surrounded. The surrounding often lasts a long time. Attacks are common too. Food and other supplies are blocked during a siege.

45. Popcorn From Ancient Times to Now

1The parts of this book come in this order. A Snack from Long Ago starts on page 1. Popcorn Before Written History starts on page 2. Popcorn in a New Land starts on page 3. Popcorn Takes Off starts on page 4. Popcorn and Films starts on page 5. Popcorn Now starts on page 6. Lots of Flavors starts on page 7. A word list starts on page 7. A photo from 1912 shows two young boys. They enjoy popcorn. They bought it on the street. Do you eat popcorn when you watch a film? Since the early 1900s, movie theaters have been a place to eat this snack. Few snack foods anywhere are older than popcorn. Films are much newer than this snack. The tale of popcorn goes back thousands of years.

2A picture compares the size of corncobs. It shows an ancient cob. It shows a modern cob. Five thousand years of farming made corncobs much bigger. The change came little by little. People first grew corn as food in Mexico. That start was eight to nine thousand years before now. From Mexico, corn spread across the Americas. Ancient people in the Americas ground corn into flour. They also heated corn until it popped. They ate the popped corn as a snack. Old corncobs have lasted inside caves. Popcorn kernels have lasted too. Those caves lie all over the Americas. One cave in New Mexico is called Bat Cave. It held corncobs. Those cobs may be as old as four thousand years. A cave in Utah held popped corn that scientists uncovered. That popped corn is one thousand years old. Some of this old popcorn looks almost fresh. Would you take a bite after a thousand years shut inside a cave? People first went into Bat Cave in the 1940s. Bat Cave is in New Mexico. It held the oldest corn anyone has found in North America. A small picture shows one ancient ear of corn. The age of that ear is above six hundred years.

3Settlers from Europe received food from some Native Americans during the 1600s. Corn was part of that food. The food helped the settlers live. Explorers from Europe met a surprise in the Americas. The meeting happened in the 1600s. Popcorn was a snack they had never seen. Yet they found people eating it in many places. Near the Great Lakes, Native people stirred popcorn into soup. People in Peru toasted popcorn. They ate the toasted corn as a snack. Colonists from Europe loved popcorn too. A fire was how they heated corn until it popped. They ate it as a snack. Some ate popcorn in the morning. They added cream. They added sugar. That morning dish may have tasted a bit like cereal does now. A photo from the late 1800s shows a man. He sells popcorn from a wagon. A crowd stands in Denver, Colorado. As years passed, popcorn grew more popular. People usually made this snack themselves. They made it at home. They made only a small amount. Popping corn inside a kettle hung over flames was slow work. Everything changed in 1885. Charles Cretors was a businessman. He worked in Chicago. He invented the first machine made to pop corn. His machine popped corn fast. The work was easy. The machine had wheels. Because of the wheels, people could sell popcorn on the street. Sellers worked at fairs. They worked at circuses. They worked at sporting events.

4Cracker Jack grew popular with people who liked baseball. For many decades, every box held a small toy surprise. People began to eat popcorn in many places. New popcorn flavors began to appear. In the late 1890s, a person who made snacks invented Cracker Jack. The treat used popcorn. It used peanuts. It used molasses. People seemed unable to get enough popcorn. At the start, one group was not fond of this crunchy treat. That group was the people who owned movie theaters. In the early 1900s, popcorn was not allowed inside those theaters. Owners feared the snack made too much noise. They also feared it made a mess. A photo shows people in the United States standing in a line. They wait for bread. The city is New York City. The time is the Great Depression. Owners of the first theaters tried to bar this snack. The ban did not last. During the 1930s, life in the United States grew very hard. People call that time the Great Depression. Many people lost their jobs. Money was very scarce for most families. Why does a kernel of popcorn pop? Water is the answer. A little water sits inside each kernel. Heat turns that water into steam. The steam pushes on the inner wall of the kernel. When the kernel is hot enough, it bursts. The burst kernel becomes a fluffy snack.

5People who sold popcorn needed a way to earn a living. Other workers needed that too. The sellers began to walk into theater lobbies by themselves. They moved through the lobby. They sold popcorn there. The plan worked very well. The popcorn tasted good. The popcorn did not cost much. During the Depression, most people could pay for only a few snacks. Popcorn was one snack they could still buy. Popcorn eating rose past every earlier level. A photo from 1935 shows a group of children. They wait to go into a movie theater. The theater is in Waterbury, Connecticut. Later, theater owners saw a chance to earn money. They could sell the popcorn themselves. Theaters began to sell popcorn. They sold other snacks too. The sales went very well. During World War II, popcorn grew even more popular inside movie theaters. During that war, sugar was hard to find. Candy was hard to find too. People ate still more popcorn. They chose it instead of sweet snacks. A photo shows a girl in New York. She eats popcorn. She watches a film. The time is the early 1940s.

6A home bowl of this snack is simple to enjoy now. You can eat it while you face a TV. During the 1950s, fewer people reached for popcorn. The same dip came in the 1960s. More and more families owned a TV. They often stayed home and watched it. They went to a movie theater less often. A top photo shows microwave popcorn. It takes little time to make. It is simple to make. A bottom photo shows a store shelf. The store sells many kinds of popcorn. Someone invented microwave popcorn in 1981. All at once, the kind of popcorn sold at theaters was simple to make at home. Huge numbers of people did make it at home. Home kitchens now make close to three-fourths of all popcorn eaten. A film is still a time when large amounts of popcorn get eaten. Some of that popcorn is eaten at home. Some of it is eaten in a theater.

7Flavors of popcorn today include butter. They include cheddar. They include s'mores. They include caramel. They include hot and spicy. They include chocolate peanut. Other flavors exist too. No other country now eats as much popcorn as the United States. No other country eats a larger share. Popcorn comes in many flavors. Blue cheese is one flavor a buyer can choose. You can buy popcorn that tastes like mushrooms. You can buy popcorn that tastes like cupcakes. You can also eat popcorn in the most common way. That way uses salt. It also uses butter. Which flavor do you like best? Here are word meanings from this book. Allowed is a verb on page 4. It means someone let a thing happen. Colonists is a noun on page 3. It means the residents of a colony. It can mean people who start a new colony. Explorers is a noun on page 3. It means people who visit places that are new to them. They learn about those places. Kernels is a noun on page 6. It means the softer inner part of a seed. It can also mean that part of a nut. Kettle is a noun on page 8. It means a pot made of metal. The pot has a handle. It has a spout. People use it when they heat a liquid. Machine is a noun on page 8. It means a device that uses energy. The device helps a person with work. Microwave is a noun on page 14. It means a closed space that cooks food with energy waves. People also call that space a microwave oven. Movie theaters is a noun on page 4. It means buildings. Films are shown inside them. Survived is a verb on page 6. It means stayed alive. It can mean kept on existing.

46. Pumpkins with a Light Inside

1People carve pumpkins into all kinds of creative designs. Some carvings look spooky. Some look funny. Some look like careful works of art. Who knew a pumpkin could become so many things? A carved pumpkin is called a jack-o'-lantern. These lanterns make a festive sight in a window. They also glow on a doorstep or a porch. Around Halloween, they are a favorite decoration. A light set in the hollow middle makes the lantern glow. It shines in the autumn night.

2Some artists enter contests to carve the finest jack-o'-lantern. Some try to carve the biggest one. Children like turning pumpkins into lanterns, and adults do too. A simple lantern may have eyes in the shape of triangles. It may have a grin full of teeth. Other lanterns have frightening faces. Some artists cut designs that are full of detail. Some of those designs feel festive. Pumpkin carving goes back more than a hundred years. Stories say the custom started even sooner. It started in Ireland. There, people carved lanterns from turnips or from potatoes. Why did people in Ireland begin this custom? What is the origin of that name? Many tales explain where it started. A well-known tale from the nineteenth century is about a man called Stingy Jack. The tale says Jack was cheap. That habit gave him his nickname. He also liked to trick everybody. Jack was so mischievous that death brought a curse. He had to wander the land forever. He had to live by himself in the dark. A turnip with a light inside showed him the way. Jack of the Lantern was the name Ireland gave his ghost. Later, they shortened the name to Jack-o'-lantern.

3Someone carved this turnip into an old-style jack-o'-lantern. Jack-o'-Lanterns in Later Years. The tale of Stingy Jack later joined a night known as All Hallows' Eve. Across Europe, people lit bonfires on that night. They also put on costumes to keep wandering ghosts away. In Ireland, people scooped out turnips and potatoes. They cut scary faces into them. They set a light inside. The light was meant to keep troublesome spirits, such as Stingy Jack, away from the house. Those carved vegetables were the earliest jack-o'-lanterns. Before long, people in Scotland carved vegetables on this night. People in England did too. In England, the vegetable was often a beet. All Hallows' Eve later took the name Halloween. People from Ireland, England, and Scotland immigrated to the United States. They brought the lantern custom along. Pumpkins grew in the United States, and they worked well as lanterns. By the 1920s, carving pumpkins was common all across America. Today, a carved pumpkin is a key part of Halloween. So is trick-or-treating. So is wearing a costume. Each autumn, visitors crowd pumpkin patches. Farms and vegetable stands get crowded too. They search for just the right pumpkin. Each year, growers in the United States raise about 1.5 billion pounds of pumpkins. That weight is more than 680 million kg. Children like to walk the rows and choose a favorite pumpkin.

4A basic carving kit holds a thin saw. It also holds a scoop. It also holds a stencil tracer. How to Carve One. A good pumpkin for carving is firm. It is smooth. It has no marks. A pumpkin that sits upright is the safer pick. If the pumpkin leans, the light inside may tip over. Some people buy a kit that includes tools and art patterns. The kit helps them make a fine lantern. Other carvers bring tools and designs of their own. Some carve freehand, with no pattern at all. The first cut makes a hole in the top. That piece becomes the lid. Always ask a parent to help when you use a knife. Step 1 is cutting off the top. Next, scoop out the soft inside and the seeds. Stop when the inside is clean and the pumpkin is hollow. Many people roast the seeds and eat them as a healthy snack. The soft inside can become pies, breads, and soups. Nothing has to be thrown away. Step 2 is scooping out the inside.

5Step 3 is drawing the design. Draw a design on the outside of the pumpkin. Stencils can help, and paint can help too. Step 4 is the carving itself. When the drawing is done, the carving can start. A carver might cut a face, an animal, a pattern, or a message. Each jack-o'-lantern is its own work of art. After the cuts are done, a candle or another light can go inside. Then the lid goes back on. It is wise to show the lantern within a few days. After that, the pumpkin may spoil and lose its shape. Many cities hold festivals so people can see artistic pumpkins. Some pumpkins show the old scary faces. Skilled carvers have also shaped cartoon characters, animals, and other designs full of detail. Hudson Valley, New York, builds one special show from thousands of these lanterns. The show uses sound effects and music. Together the pumpkins form snakes and dinosaurs. The show even includes a pumpkin planetarium. Visitors know this Hudson Valley show as the Great Jack-o'-Lantern Blaze. The spot is Sleepy Hollow, home of the Headless Horseman legend.

6Artists and sculptors hand-carve more than five thousand pumpkins each year. Los Angeles hosts the Rise of the Jack-o'-Lanterns. A huge show there includes cars made of pumpkins, dinosaurs, and carvings of stars from sports, television, and movies. Visitors can watch the artists turn pumpkins into lanterns. One artist carved a jack-o'-lantern among the largest ever recorded. It weighed more than 1,800 pounds, or 816 kilograms. That record pumpkin weighed as much as a cow. He beat his own Guinness World Record for the biggest jack-o'-lantern, which he had set five years before. Keene's yearly Pumpkin Festival held the world record for the most lit jack-o'-lanterns many times. Recently the town of Keene, New Hampshire, set that record once more. The town lit thirty thousand jack-o'-lanterns at once. Carved pumpkins are even shown underwater. Aquarium workers place them in large ocean tanks each autumn. Visitors like the festive sight.

7A long time may be needed to carve a jack-o'-lantern. The finished lantern is worth the work. The Custom Goes On. Turning a pumpkin into a jack-o'-lantern is a fun thing to do. People of every age can take part. A pumpkin might wear a scary face. It might show detailed artwork. It might be one piece of a bigger display. No matter the style, these lanterns send a clear message. Halloween is near. Only the carver's imagination limits the design. Which jack-o'-lantern would you choose to make? Cursed means hit by trouble or harm. A supernatural power is often the cause. Designs are patterns made of lines, shapes, pictures, or colors. Festive means tied to a celebration or a holiday. It can also mean cheerful. Immigrated means a person moved from one country to another in order to settle. A mischievous person likes playful trouble. A planetarium is a building. Inside, a picture of the night sky is shown on a curved ceiling. Stencils are thin, stiff sheets. Designs or letters are cut out of the sheets. People use the sheets to mark those shapes on a surface. Stingy means a person does not like to spend, give, or share. A tradition is a belief or custom passed along from year to year and from one generation to the next.

47. Races Through the Mud

1A mud-run course may include a climb on a cargo net. A scramble over tires may be included. A pit of mud may be included too. You might cross on a log. You might do a caterpillar crawl. You might crawl through a pond. An under wall can appear. A hay mountain can appear. A crawl through a tunnel can appear. A slide into water can appear. Imagine you are out on the course. Some runners are in front of you. Some runners are behind you. Tired faces are everywhere. Sweat covers everyone. Mud covers everyone from head to foot. The whole event has been a mix of crawling, running, and climbing. All of it happens in mud. You feel as if this is the finest time of your life. You have found the mud run. The name tells you the truth about it. People race, and the path is mud. Americans have joined these races for decades. Children have grown fond of them in recent years. Adults have grown fond of them too. Indiana is one place where kids race in the mud.

2These events now take place across the whole country. No two are quite the same. Professional athletes enter some of them as real competitions. Fun is the only goal of others. Children alone may enter some races. A whole family may run others together. Two things are true of every one of them. Great exercise is certain, whichever race you pick. Very, very dirty is also certain. Someone has to fall into the mud before anyone else. A woman in Pennsylvania races with mud all over her. Mud is hard to run through. It grabs your shoes. Your pace drops because of that grab. Forward motion takes real effort. Organizers of the races know this fact. They build fitness challenges out of the mud.

3The mud itself is not the only test. Strength is required. Bravery is required. A willingness to get dirty is required. Obstacles demand those qualities. Cargo nets call for strong arms. Climbing walls call for strong arms too. A balance beam calls for patience. It also calls for careful attention. On harder courses, people hang from ropes with their heads down. Monkey bars are another way they swing across. You cannot crawl these paths and stay clean. Nearly every muscle works during a crawl. The crawls force you down into the mud. Tunnels may have to be slithered through. Bravery is tested by some obstacles. A steep hill may drop you into water. A river may have to be crossed. Fire may have to be jumped. Training therefore uses many kinds of exercise.

4Picture mud and obstacles joined with zombies. A themed race can do exactly that. People dressed as zombies must be outsmarted. They must be outrun too. Run for Your Lives is one race with this theme. Racers get two choices. Being hunted by zombies is one choice. Becoming a zombie and chasing others is the other choice. Which role sounds better to you? Zombies close in around one racer at this event. Costumed runners leap a barrier of fire. Themes are not on every course. Costumes still show up, worn just for the joy of it. A nurse is one outfit you might pick. A superhero is another. Your grandma is another. Only imagination sets the limit. Have photos taken before you start. Have them taken again at the end. The two groups of pictures will not look alike at all. Mud covers the outfits completely. Tearing is common before the finish. Some outfits are ruined. That wreck is part of the pleasure.

5Racing alone is not required. Family can join you. Friends can join you. Together you finish the course. A relay might use a caterpillar crawl. A relay might use a slither through a tunnel. An obstacle can also be faced as a group. Some races make teammates push one another. They also make them pull. The goal is to get up and over a wall. Slippery mud coats that wall. The day is challenging. Teamwork lets everyone have a better time. Georgia hosts a race open only to women. A team of women takes part. Clean clothes mark the start. The clean look does not last. You need not be a professional athlete. Every age can find a race. Every fitness level can find one too.

6Some courses welcome children whose age is only three. Runners of that youngest age usually leave the start first. Older children follow them. Adults follow after that. Distance is shorter for the children. Obstacles are still there. Mud is still there. Adventure is still there, as on the adult courses. A wall built for kids might be climbed. A tunnel built for kids might be wiggled through, on the way to the finish. Idaho is where a young boy clears an obstacle in a race. A good time waits in any run you pick. Some children like the course enough to repeat it. Two times is common. Three times happens too. A man in a superman outfit celebrates once he is done. Your first mud run is almost over. You head for the finish. Then a squelch sounds as your foot finds a huge pool. The mud in it is wet and sticky. Shoes are sucked at by the mud. Clothes are stuck to by the mud. You keep going anyway. The finish line is crossed. No costume is on you today. A superhero is still how you feel. Not every costume is wise for a race. Pieces that stick out from the body get in the way after the start. Fairy wings are one risky choice. Swords are another.

7Top tips can make a mud run easier. Choose old clothes. Choose clothes that feel good. Pack something warm. Wet and muddy is what you will be. Boots are a better pick than sneakers. Trail-running shoes are a better pick too. They hold the mud with a stronger grip. Cleats are banned at most races. Leave cotton at home, including jeans and T-shirts. Wet cotton feels very cold. Leave pockets off your pants. Leave loose clothing off too. Mud slips inside clothes. The weight then drags you down. Water should be drunk in large amounts. Valuables should stay at home. The biggest tip is to enjoy yourself. An adventure is an experience that feels unusual or exciting. Athletes train for sports, games, or other work that needs strength. Speed and skill are needed too. A course is the path racers follow while they compete. An obstacle is anything that blocks forward movement and makes the going hard. Themed means the look follows one subject or one idea. A zombie is a made-up monster. It is a dead body that still moves.

48. Rooms You Will Never Forget

1A world map locates hotels that are hard to believe. North America is marked. Europe is marked. Asia and Africa are marked. South America and Australia are marked. The Atlantic Ocean is marked. So is the Pacific Ocean. So is the Indian Ocean. Number 1 on the map is Skylodge Adventure Suites, in Cuzco, Peru. Number 2 on the map is Hôtel de Glace, in Quebec, Canada. Number 3 on the map is Giraffe Manor, in Nairobi, Kenya. Number 4 on the map is Treehotel, in Harads, Sweden. Number 5 on the map is Sala Silvermine Hotel, in the town of Sala, Sweden. Number 6 on the map is Marina Bay Sands Hotel, at Marina Bay in Singapore. Key Largo, Florida, holds number 7, Jules' Undersea Lodge. Number 8 on the map is Tundra Lodge, in Manitoba, Canada. The tour covers unusual hotels. They are amazing hotels too. Sleep will happen on four continents. You will swim down to the floor of the sea. You will sleep inside a cave. You will meet animals that are endangered. These hotels do not match one another. They still share a single gift. Each one offers a stay that is hard to believe. Pack a bag. Then the tour can start.

2A see-through room can give a wide view. The opening stop is Skylodge Adventure Suites. The city is Cuzco, in Peru. The valley around it is called Sacred Valley. Getting there is difficult. One route is a hike up a mountain. Another route is a ride on zip lines. The room comes only after that climb. Sleep is inside a clear capsule. The capsule hangs high over the floor of the valley. Solar power runs every capsule. Sacred Valley can be seen across 300 degrees. Dinner is eaten in a clear capsule. Breakfast is eaten there too. Birds move in the sky above the table. You may feel you are spending the night in a giant bird nest. The next hotel is built of ice, yet a warm fire can burn inside. Even the cups are ice. Leave Cuzco and you reach Hôtel de Glace. The name is said O-tel DEH GLAHS. It sits only minutes outside Quebec City, in Canada. Cold is the point of the place. The building is a work of art, and the material is ice. Ice is carved into the rooms. Ice is carved into the halls. Ice is carved into most of the furniture. Each winter the build takes about forty days. Artists do the work. Other workers do it too. When the weather turns warm, the hotel melts away.

3Bring heavy clothes made for winter. The indoor temperature is only 23°F to 27°F. In Celsius that is –5° to –3°. The chill matches a freezer. Beds are thick slabs of ice, so sleep requires a special bag. The bag is extra warm. Guests can curl up on the ice bed. Three giraffes visit Giraffe Manor at breakfast time. The tour moves on to Nairobi, in Kenya. Breakfast there is shared with guests of a special kind. Forests around Giraffe Manor shelter Rothschild’s giraffes. These animals are among the tallest giraffes on Earth. An adult may measure 20 feet from the hooves to the crown of the head. The same measure is 6 meters.

4Pellets on a guest’s plate become food for a hungry giraffe. At the start of each day, the herd leaves the forest. The animals walk to the hotel. Heads come in through windows that are open. Heads come in through open doors too. Guests hand over pellets. Dry grass goes into the pellets. Corn goes in. Molasses goes in. Giraffe Manor is not only a pleasant place to sleep. Once costs are paid, the remaining money is profit. That profit goes to a local center. The center protects wildlife. Up in the branches, try to spot the Mirrorcube. After Kenya the route reaches Harads, in the north of Sweden. Treehotel is a group of strange houses held in trees. A guest sleeps in one room out of six. Each room is a separate tree house. They hang from 14 feet to 20 feet off the ground. That span is 4 meters to 6 meters. Every room is unique. Three of the rooms are special beyond the rest. Glass wraps the outside of the Mirrorcube. The glass throws back the image of the woods. The room is nearly impossible to see.

5Visitors enter the UFO tree house by climbing a ladder. A tangle of branches covers the Bird’s Nest tree house completely. It could be the home of a bird of enormous size. The UFO is an unusual room. Builders shaped it like a flying saucer. Lights for landing sit on the outside. Night is when you can pull the ladder indoors. The scene looks as if someone from far away chose Earth for a holiday. Candles glow in a dark underground room of stone. Stay in Sweden and you can visit the town of Sala. The deepest hotel room on Earth is in that town. Sala Silvermine Hotel sits above a silver mine that has closed. Mine tunnels run past 12 miles in length. The length is 19 kilometers. Their depth is 980 feet. The depth is 300 meters. The Eiffel Tower reaches this same height. Most rooms sit near where the mine opens. For a rarer night, sleep hundreds of feet below the ground.

6The Mine Suite lies more than 500 feet under the surface. That distance is 152 meters. A guide with special training leads you down. The room contains a bed. It contains chairs. It contains a bathroom. It contains snacks. Warm clothes belong on this visit. Air in the mine itself is about 28°F. That reading is –2°C. Heat brings the room to 64°F. That reading is 18°C. Singapore is the home of Marina Bay Sands Hotel. A swimming pool sits on the roof. No rooftop pool in the world is larger. The length is almost 500 feet. That length is 152 meters. From the water, guests can study the city view. They are 650 feet above the ground. That height is 198 meters. A round window called a porthole lets people watch for fish. Jules’ Undersea Lodge is the last stop. It is in Key Largo, Florida. Wet clothes are part of the plan. The lodge sits 21 feet down in the water. That depth is 6 meters. The bottom of a lagoon holds it. The lagoon’s name is Largo Sound. Scuba gear is how you reach the door. Inside are two bedrooms. Inside is a common room as well. Snacks are kept in that shared room. Breakfast is kept there too. Pizza can be the dinner order. A worker from the hotel swims it down to you. The box around the pizza does not let water in.

7From Kenya's forests to the sea's floor, amazing hotels can be found. A single night's bed is only the start of what they give. The memory of the stay lasts. Conservation is the care of wild land. It is also the care of living things found there. Continents are the chief pieces of land on Earth. Endangered means at risk of being gone for good. Hotels rent rooms to people one night at a time. Incredible describes something so unusual that belief comes hard. Amazing is a second meaning. Profits are the money left after a business pays every cost. Slabs are broad pieces that are thick and flat. A slice of that kind counts too. A tour is a trip taken so you can learn a place. The shape of Tundra Lodge is like a train. Huge wheels support it. Snow is what the whole hotel crosses. The ride lets guests come very close to polar bears. Unique describes a thing that is one of a kind. No other thing matches it.

49. Sea Life Left by the Tide

1Tide pools hold a lot of sea life. The tide is the ocean rising and falling. This happens where the ocean meets the land. Two times each day, the water level goes up. It falls two times each day. When the tide goes out, small pools of seawater stay behind. Those pools are tide pools. Low spots hold most of them. They line rocky shores. Small tide pools can also form on sandy beaches. Any shore spot that traps water can become a tide pool.

2Gravity is the force that causes tides. The sun has strong gravity. The moon has strong gravity too. That gravity pulls on Earth. It pulls on the land. It pulls on the water. Only the water can move when gravity pulls. The pull of the sun and the moon shapes the ocean. The water gathers into two mounds. One picture shows a dock full of boats. The tide is high. Another picture shows the same dock. The tide is low. Earth's rotation makes the tides rise and fall. Rotation means spin. Land moves into a mound of water. Then the land moves out of that mound. The water level slowly goes up. It slowly goes down. Every 24 hours, Earth spins once. That spin brings two high tides. It also brings two low tides. The moon travels around Earth. That trip changes the tides. Twice each month, the moon and the sun line up. They pull on the ocean at the same time. Together, their gravity builds higher mounds of water. Those mounds make the month's highest tides. They also make the month's lowest tides. The name for these tides is spring tides. In other parts of the month, the sun's pull fights the moon's pull. Then a high tide is not as high. A low tide is not as low. These smaller tides are called neap tides. Say neap like neep. A diagram of neap tides shows the sun and the moon pulling apart. Their pull makes the tides smaller. A diagram of spring tides shows them pulling together. That shared pull makes very high tides. It makes very low tides too.

3A diagram uses arrows to show where the intertidal zone starts and stops. One edge is high tide. The other edge is low tide. The intertidal zone is the shore between those two levels. Seawater covers the zone at high tide. Air meets the zone at low tide. The strip closest to the sea is bare only at the lowest tides. The strip farthest from the sea is wet only at the highest tides. Each part of the zone is home to its own creatures. The place depends on what the creature needs. Some need air. Some need water. A sea star rests in a tide pool. Sea life crowds these pools. You can see the creatures after the tide goes out. More hiding spots usually mean more animals. More places to hold on mean more animals too. Rocky tide pools usually have the most animals. The material under the animals has a name. It is called the substrate. Animals live in the substrate or grip it. The substrate is usually rock. Sometimes it is sand.

4Life in the intertidal zone is hard. Picture seawater washing into your home every day. Then picture it washing out again. Tide pool animals must face crashing waves. An animal that does not hold tight can be thrown about. It can be battered on the rocks. It can be swept out to sea. When the tide comes back up, waves crash over the pools. Water is not the only danger. Air is dangerous. The sun is dangerous too. Animals that must stay wet face two big problems in the air. Drying out is the first problem. Predators are the second problem. So how do the animals last? Anemones have ways to live in a tide pool. Sea stars have ways too. Animals of the intertidal zone have adapted to this life. They are used to the tide leaving and returning each day. They can cling to rocks. They can find cover from crashing waves. Their bodies have ways to stay wet. They can stay away from predators. How an animal survives depends on movement. Many animals are stuck to the substrate. They do not travel. Staying put keeps them from being washed away.

5Some animals grow very strong fibers. The fibers look like hairs. Mussels are one example. The fibers grip the substrate. Other animals make a hard glue. Barnacles do this. Say barnacles BAR-nick-els. The glue holds them to rocks. These animals cannot flee the sun. They cannot flee the air. They cannot flee predators. They cannot move. A picture shows barnacles packed on rock. Other tide pool animals are mobile. Mobile animals can move. They can run. They can swim. They can crawl. Some are sea animals trapped by low tide. They cannot reach the sea again until high tide returns. They can still look for shelter. They can hide from the air. They can hide from the sun. They can hide from predators. They can do this because they move. A crab travels across the pool. Anemones need moisture or they cannot live. Most animals that stay in one place have a way to avoid drying out. Some have hard shells. Barnacles are one example. Oysters are another. At low tide they can close the shell. Some crawl inside the shell. Enough moisture stays inside. They remain wet until high tide comes back. Soft-bodied animals that stay put face the greatest drying danger. A sea anemone is one of them. Say anemone ah-NEM-oh-nees. Close to the ocean is where these animals usually live. Low tide does not leave them bare for long. When the water is gone, they shut their bodies. They shut them as far as they can. That helps them keep their moisture.

6Mobile animals in a tide pool have a better chance to survive. They can move out of the sun. They can move out of the drying air. Many dig down into the sand. Others hide under rocks. Some hide under seaweed. One group of crabs is very thin. People call them slider crabs. A slider crab can push its flat body into a crack between rocks. Slow animals have another trick. A marine snail can hide inside its shell. It can shut a small trap door. The door seals the snail inside. Most of the time, a tide pool still holds enough water. Mobile animals can swim in that water. They can crawl in it too. Wet sand holes can hide a crab. A picture shows them there. Eating and being eaten happen in every habitat. Tide pool animals have special ways to find food. Their ways of eating are interesting too. Oysters trap tiny animals that float in the water. Clams do this too. They use parts that look like hairs. Anemones sting tiny animals. They use their tentacles. Jellyfish sting with tentacles too. An octopus catches other animals with its strong arms. Suction cups on those arms help. Crabs pick up food with their claws. Lobsters do the same. One picture shows crabs eating tiny sea creatures.

7Staying off the menu takes amazing tricks too. Often the same trick fights drying and predators. The animal slides under a rock. Or it slips into a shell. Other animals escape by running. Some scuttle. Some swim. Some jump. Some animals match their surroundings. Matching helps them hide from predators. Almost every small sea animal is food for a seagull. The most common tide pool animals are invertebrates. An invertebrate has a soft body. It has no backbone. Sponges and corals are in this group. Octopuses and sea stars are in it too. It also includes anemones and jellyfish. It includes worms, crabs, and shrimp. It includes snails and urchins. An invertebrate may stay in one place. It may be mobile instead. Drawings show kinds you often see. A sponge has a lumpy body. The shape is irregular. Small holes cover the body. A sponge stays in one place. It sticks to the substrate. A flat worm has a flat body. The body is shaped like a worm. A flat worm is mobile. A segmented worm has a body in sections. A section may be flat. It may be round. A segmented worm is mobile.

8A coral has a hard skeleton. Some corals have a hard shell instead. The soft body has tentacles. A coral stays in one place. An anemone has a body shaped like a flower. Stinging cells cover its tentacles. An anemone is usually stuck in place. It is not always stuck in place. A jellyfish has a body like a bell. Some look like a dome. Long tentacles hang from the body. The tentacles have stinging cells. A jellyfish is mobile. Sea stars have spiny skin. Sea urchins have spiny skin too. Sand dollars have spiny skin too. Their body parts sit in a circle. They can move, but only slowly. A snail has a soft body. A slug has a soft body too. Each one crawls on a single foot. A clam has a soft body. An oyster has a soft body too. Each one has a hard shell with two sides. They usually stay in one place. In an emergency, they can move. An octopus has a soft body. It has a hard beak. It has eight arms. It can swim very fast.

9A barnacle stays in one place. A hard shell sticks it to the rock. It has legs in segments. Shrimp have legs and bodies in segments. Crabs and lobsters do too. A hard outer shell covers them. They are mobile. A kelpfish swims in a tide pool. Fish are another group often found in tide pools. A fish has a backbone. Many kinds of fish have adapted to tide pool life. A tide pool is small. Fish that live in one are usually small. Other fish are trapped when the tide leaves. They are not used to such a small space. The returning tide is what they wait for. Then they swim out to the open sea.

10Many land animals visit tide pools too. The water is shallow. That makes sea creatures easy to find. It makes them easy to eat. Many birds eat oysters. They eat mussels. They eat fish. Seagulls are one kind of bird that does this. Ravens do it too. Some mammals scoop animals out of the pool and eat them. Raccoons do this. Monkeys do this too. A shorebird eats a shellfish. Plantlike living things also fill tide pools. Marine algae is the group name for most of them. People also call them seaweed. Seaweed comes in many types. Only some types live in tide pools. Seaweed gives animals good places to hide. It can also be a substrate. Some animals attach their bodies to it. Some attach their eggs to it. One picture shows marine algae, which is seaweed. Interesting creatures fill every tide pool. Exploring them is fun. A tide pool is like an aquarium packed with sea creatures. If you want tide pools, start on a rocky shore. Watch how the tide rises. Watch how it falls. Newspapers near the ocean usually print the tide times. They also print how high the tide will be. A lower tide lets you see more. Move in a slow and gentle way. Then you will not step on an animal. You will not crush one.

11Stop and study one small spot. Pick a spot that interests you. A longer look makes it more likely you will see something. Remember that the pool is a home for many creatures. If you lift a rock to look underneath, lift it gently. Always set the rock back where you found it. An adult should say yes before a tide pool animal goes into your hands. Some of these animals can sting. Put water in your hand first. The water keeps the animal moist. Always return a sea creature to the place where you found it. Animals need to remain in their tide pool homes. Pets are a poor match for them. Taken out of the pool, they are likely to die. Handle tide pool animals with care. These words help you read the book. Neap tides are the month's smallest tides. They come when the sun's pull and the moon's pull oppose one another. The word adapted means changed to fit one kind of life. Algae are living things that are like plants. Seaweed is one kind of algae. Predators are animals that hunt other animals and eat them. Gravity is a force that pulls things. It pulls them toward very large objects. Rotation means spinning. Spring tides are the month's greatest tides. They come when the sun and the moon line up. The intertidal zone lies between the highest tide and the lowest tide. Invertebrates are animals that have no backbone. The substrate is what tide pool animals grip or live in. It is usually rock or sand. Mobile means the animal can move around.

50. Sights in the United States Capital

1A map shows where Washington, D.C., sits. The map places it in North America. The map shows the United States. The map shows Canada. Maryland lies beside Washington, D.C. Virginia lies beside it too. The parts of this book come in this order. A Start begins on page 4. Buildings of Government begin on page 5. The National Mall begins on page 9. More Places to See begins on page 11. A Closing begins on page 15. A word list begins on page 16. Washington, D.C., is not a state. It is a city and a district. That district lies between Maryland and Virginia. The United States of America keeps its capital in Washington, D.C. The capital holds many sights worth a visit. It also holds many things to learn. Would you like to learn about a few of them? The place is named for George Washington. No one held the presidency of the United States before him. The letters D.C. stand for District of Columbia.

2The White House is where the US president works. It is also where the president lives. The president leads the country. Room count inside the White House is 132. It has 6 floors. It has 32 bathrooms. It has 412 doors. It has 147 windows. It has 28 fireplaces. The Oval Office, built in 1909, is the main work room for the president. Each day, about five thousand tourists come to the White House. Window count for the Capitol Building is 658. Of those windows, 108 are in the dome alone. Congress works with the president. Together they make the laws. Congress meets inside the Capitol Building. The Capitol roof is a dome. The dome is made of iron. A statue stands on top of the dome. The statue is called Freedom. Freedom wears a helmet. Freedom carries a sword. Freedom also carries a shield.

3Buildings stand across from the Capitol. The Library of Congress uses those buildings. The library holds over 164 million items. Each day, more than 12,000 new items are added. Those items include books. They include maps. They include pictures. They include other items too. The books fill 883 miles of shelves. That length is 1,349 km. That shelf length matches the trip from Washington, D.C., to Florida. People ask the Supreme Court to hear about ten thousand cases a year. The court hears about eighty of those cases. The Supreme Court Building sits near the Library of Congress. The Supreme Court outranks every other law court in the country. Art on the building is a sign of law. That art also stands for justice. The huge front doors are metal. They weigh about 13 tons. That weight is 12 mt. The doors show eight pictures. Those pictures are of justice. Many other government buildings stand in the city. More of them stand around the city.

4A long park in the city is called the National Mall. It starts at the Capitol Building. Celebrations sometimes bring crowds to that park. People also gather there for protests. Marches happen there too. On October 16, 1995, many people marched. That march was the Million Man March. The marchers wanted to stand with African Americans. Many memorials line the National Mall. Monuments line it too. George Washington is the person honored by the Washington Monument. It stands a little over 555 feet tall. That height is 169 m. When it opened in 1884, no building in the world was taller. Climbers face more than eight hundred steps before the top. The Lincoln Memorial honors Abraham Lincoln. Lincoln was president number sixteen in the United States. The memorial holds a statue of Lincoln. He sits in a chair. The statue measures 29 feet tall. That height is 8.8 m. Other monuments line the mall as well. Other memorials do too. They honor important leaders. They honor people who fought in wars. The Martin Luther King, Jr. Memorial has a large statue. The statue shows that leader. His work pushed laws toward fairness for every person. The Vietnam Veterans Memorial lists names. It lists more than fifty thousand Americans. Some of those Americans died in the Vietnam War. Others went missing in it.

5Washington, D.C., has many more sights. The Smithsonian Institution runs museums. It runs gardens. It runs galleries. It runs the National Zoo. Visitors inside National Air and Space Museum can touch a rock. That rock came from the Moon. Astronauts carried Moon rocks back to Earth. They brought the rocks from space. Dinosaur fossils can be viewed from close range inside National Museum of Natural History. They can see giant dinosaur skeletons there. Museums show art that amazes people. Galleries do the same. The Smithsonian American Art Museum holds work by many American artists. Those artists are not all alike. Other galleries show modern art. Some show African art. Some show Asian art. Paintings there show Americans who did important work. The paintings include presidents. They include inventors. Sometimes a special show is on view. One show had a buffalo hide. Visitors were allowed to touch that hide.

6A visit to any Smithsonian Institution museum is free. A visit to its galleries is free. A visit to the National Zoo is free. The National Zoo keeps more than 1,500 animals. They live on 163 acres. That area is 66 ha. Visitors can watch animals being fed. They can also watch training. Those times change through the day. These sights are only some of what Washington, D.C., offers. People can see still more. People can learn still more. A measurement can be hard to picture. A comparison with something else can help. Here are some measures from this book. Door weight at the Supreme Court Building is 13 tons. That weight is 12 mt. That is about as heavy as two large elephants. Washington Monument rises 555 feet. That height is 169 m. That height matches nearly one and a half American football fields. The Lincoln statue, with its base, is 29 feet tall. That height is 8.8 m. That is about as tall as a building with three stories.

7A map of places to visit marks these spots. It marks the Smithsonian American Art Museum. It marks the White House. One label reads National Museum of Natural History. It marks the Vietnam Veterans Memorial. One label names the Supreme Court. The rest of that label says of the United States. It marks the U.S. Capitol. It marks the Lincoln Memorial. It marks the National Mall. It marks the Washington Monument. It marks the Smithsonian Institution. One label reads National Air and Space Museum. It marks the Library of Congress. It marks the Martin Luther King, Jr. Memorial. It marks the Thomas Jefferson Memorial. It marks Washington, D.C. It marks the Potomac River. It marks Virginia. Millions of visitors arrive in Washington, D.C., every year Seeing the sights is one reason they come. They come to learn US history. They come to learn how the US government works. Which sight would you choose in Washington, D.C.? Here are word meanings from this book. Capital is a noun on page 4. It means the city where a government keeps its main offices. Congress is a noun on page 6. It means the top group that makes laws for the US government. Court is a noun on page 8. It means a place that hears cases under the law. Domed is an adjective on page 6. It means a roof that is round and curved. It can also mean a covering of that shape. Galleries is a noun on page 11. It means rooms that show art for the public. It can also mean buildings that do that. Justice is a noun on page 8. It means being fair. It means being honest. It means being right. It can also mean the act of those things. Memorials is a noun on page 9. It means things that stand for the memory of a person. A memorial can also recall a place, a thing, or an event. Monuments is a noun on page 9. It means buildings made to honor a person. It can also mean statues. It can also mean other structures. A monument can honor an event too. Protests is a noun on page 9. 51. Small Ants with Big Power

1Ants keep moving all the time. You have seen them march through grass. You have seen them cross sidewalks. You may have seen them on a kitchen counter. You may have seen them join a picnic. They show up in several colors. Red is one color. Yellow is one color. Brown is one color. Black is one color. These ants are tiny. These ants are strong. These ants are awesome. A grass march is a common sight. A sidewalk crossing is a common sight. A kitchen counter can be their path. A picnic can be their next stop. Red, yellow, brown, and black are ant colors. Tiny size does not stop their strength. Awesome is a fair word for them. They keep moving wherever people look. Daily paths are full of these insects. Grass can hold a marching line. A sidewalk can hold a marching line. A kitchen counter can hold them. A picnic can draw them in. Tiny size still leaves room for strength. Awesome is a fair name for that strength.

2One fossil shows a giant male ant. More than 49 million years have passed since that ant became a fossil. About 100 million years is how long ants have existed. They crawled under the dinosaurs. Early mammals lived in that same time. Ancient birds lived in that same time. Ants have changed a lot since then. Old ants looked more like wasps. Those old ants had shorter jaws. More than 10,000 species live today. They live in deserts. They live in rainforests. They live in swamps. They live in cities. Every continent except one has ants. That one continent is Antarctica. Their bodies help them last so long. Their way of living helps too. Over the years they have adapted. They adapt when environments change. Three parts make an ant body. One part is the abdomen. One part is the thorax. One part is the head. A microscope can show a leg. The leg has hooks for climbing. Ants are insects, so they have six legs. Insects also have three body parts. The abdomen is the back part. The abdomen holds the heart. The abdomen holds the stomachs. Some ants have stingers. Some ants have poison sacs. Stingers and sacs protect them from predators. Three pairs of legs join the thorax. The thorax is the middle body part. Each leg has three joints. Each leg has little hooks. The hooks let ants climb almost anything. Ants have no ears. They still hear. They feel vibrations through legs and feet.

3The head holds the brain. The head holds two eyes. The head holds antennae. The head holds jaws. The jaws work in the same way scissors do. Jaws cut. Jaws carry things. Jaws dig. Jaws hunt. Jaws are the most important tool an ant has. An ant can lift a heavy load. An ant's lift is greater than twenty times its own weight. Other mouthparts chew food. Other mouthparts are for drinking. Antennae can taste a grape. The two eyes are called compound eyes. Each compound eye is made of many smaller eyes. So many eyes still do not see well. Eyesight is not very good. Antennae help ants get around. One antenna does the work of many sense tools. Ants taste with antennae. Ants smell with antennae. Ants hear with antennae. Ants communicate with antennae. That communication uses touch. The antennae are long and thin. They bend in the middle. The bend is like a person's elbow. The antennae keep moving. Ants have no lungs. Air enters through small holes. The holes cover the body.

4Four ants may touch antennae to share a message. Ants communicate in three ways. Touch is one way. Sound is one way. Smell is one way. Different antennae touches send different messages. Ants can make noise. They rub their legs together. They rub their legs on their bodies. Scientists learned the meaning of these special noises. The noises call for help. Leaf-cutter ants carry leaves to the colony. A leaf can weigh far more than the ant. Smell is the main way ants communicate. They release special chemicals. The chemicals are called pheromones. Say pheromones as FAIR-oh-mohnz. Other ants smell them after the first ant has gone. Different smells send different messages. A crushed ant releases chemicals. Those chemicals draw other ants. Some chemicals act as an alarm. The alarm says danger is near. The chemicals make other ants attack. They attack what is near if it is not a colony ant. An ant that finds food lays a trail. The trail is made of chemicals. Other ants follow the trail to the food. The trail also leads back to the colony. More followers make the trail stronger.

5An ant colony can hold millions of members. The colony is full of activity. Some ants stay active all year. Other ants hibernate in winter. Some colonies are built in logs. Some colonies are built in trees. Most colonies are built underground. Some ants strengthen the entrance. They mix twigs into the dirt. The mix helps keep floodwater out. A colony has many rooms. Each room has its own use. Some rooms store food. Some rooms store eggs. Some rooms are for care of the young. Some rooms are for rest. One special chamber is for the queen. A cast can show the upper part of a big colony. Scientists think that colony went very deep. The depth was over 12 meters. That depth is 39 feet. A soldier ant is usually much larger than a worker. Every ant in the colony has a job. Special skills match that job. Worker ants find food. Soldier ants find food. They also protect the nest. Ants are social insects. They work as a group. Worker ants are the ones usually seen above ground. Those workers are the older ants. Other ants build the nest. Other ants care for the young. Other ants do still more jobs. Inside workers tend to be the younger ants. All of these ants are female. Only the queen may lay eggs. Every ant in the colony is related. A male ant has one main purpose in most colonies. He mates with the queen. After that, most males die.

6Many smaller ants spend life caring for the queen. The queen leads the colony. She does not give orders to the other ants. She is the largest ant in the colony. She is a machine for laying eggs. A single day can bring thousands of eggs from some queens. In most colonies, the queen count is one. A new queen may hatch. She must fly away. She starts another colony. She loses her wings when egg laying starts. A queen can live a long time for an insect. The life can pass twenty-five years. The colony dies when the queen dies. Some males can fly. Queens can fly too. A flying male may land near a colony. Worker ants then rip his wings off. They rush him to the queen. The pair mates. The male dies after that. Water does not stop every ant. Some ants climb onto one another. They link legs. The linked bodies can make a raft. The linked bodies can make a bridge. Ants are small. They can still do awesome work. Thousands of ants can work as one. Millions of ants can work as one. Together they can build a bridge. The bridge crosses a gap in the path. Together they can form boats. The boats float them across rivers. Together they can overpower a much bigger animal. That animal may be hundreds of times their size. They do this to get food.

7A trail of ants usually has a meaning. The ants may be moving to a new home. Or they found something very tasty. Next time you meet an ant trail, stop. Watch the ants. You can also follow them. Do not step on their trail. The little ants you see are female. Watch what they can do. Then their power will seem awesome. Antennae are thin feelers. Some animal heads carry these feelers. They help with touch. They help with smell. A colony can be a group that lives together. A colony can be the place ants live. Some other social insects live in colonies too. Communicate means to share information. Pictures can share it. Language can share it. Other means can share it too. An environment is the set of conditions around a living thing. The living thing is in a specific area. Plants are part of those conditions. Animals are part of them. Water is part of them. Soil is part of them. Weather is part of them. Landforms are part of them. Air is part of them. Insects are small animals. They have six legs. They have three body parts. They usually have two sets of wings. Mammals are warm-blooded. Mammals have a backbone. Mammals have hair or fur. Mammals nurse their young. Mammal babies are born live. Pheromones are chemicals animals release. The chemicals stir other members of the species. The chemicals can also send a message. A species is a group of living things. Members of a species are physically similar. Members of a species can reproduce. Vibrations are trembling movements. They are small rapid shakes.

52. Smells That Help Living Things Survive

1A calm Tasmanian devil does not smell bad. Danger brings a strong odor. Glands make that odor. The glands sit near the start of the tail. A skunk odor is not pleasant. That odor is terrible. Plants can smell bad. Animals can smell bad. A stinky plant uses its odor to draw insects. Those insects help the plant reproduce. An animal gives off odor to draw a mate. An animal gives off odor so other animals stay away. Plants use these odors to survive. Animals use these odors to survive. The smelliest living things in nature depend on odor. Skunks are famous for a disgusting odor. Some animals smell worse than a skunk. The devil smells fine only while it stays calm. Only danger starts the strong odor. The glands near the tail do that work. Insects come because the plant smells bad. Their visit lets the plant reproduce. A mate may come because of an animal odor. Other animals may leave because of an animal odor. Survival is why these smells exist. A smell worse than a skunk odor does exist in nature.

2The skunk is probably the most famous animal for its smell. A skunk uses odor so enemies stay away. An enemy may step close. Then the skunk growls. It stomps its feet. Those acts may not frighten the enemy. The skunk then lifts its tail. It sprays a liquid with a terrible smell. The liquid shoots out. It comes from the area where the tail starts. Striped polecats are cousins of skunks. A striped polecat may smell even worse. Its spray can cause burning pain. A striped polecat may bark at enemies. It may fluff its tail to scare them. Rough fur covers a lesser anteater. That fur pushes ants away. This happens during an attack on an anthill. Other furry animals stink besides skunks. Striped polecats are not the only other ones. A lesser anteater is four times as stinky as a skunk. It also shoots a smelly liquid. That liquid leaves from the area where its tail starts. An opossum fakes death. It does this when an enemy is near. People call the trick playing possum. The opossum stays still. It lets out green slime. The slime stinks. Opossums are cousins of kangaroos. They are cousins of other pouched animals too.

3Wolverines are larger than any other weasel on land. A wolverine can smell animals it hunts. Those animals can be hidden under snow. The snow may be twenty feet deep. A smelly liquid can mark a spot. The spot shows where a wolverine has been. Some people call the wolverine a nasty cat. It pees on food it wants to save. The pee goes on before the food is hidden. That smell lets the wolverine locate the saved food. The same smell turns other animals away from the food. Musk oxen grow two layers of hair. The hair keeps them safe from the cold. They shed the undercoat. They do this when winter ends. A scent can be let out as a warning. Male musk oxen fight one another. The fight is over mating with females. Males send a signal with stinky urine. The signal tells other males to keep back. Urine sticks in their long belly hair. The hair then smells terrible.

4The corpse flower is the smelliest flower in the world. Rotting flesh is the smell of this plant. The smell is disgusting. It draws flies. It also draws beetles. Those insects eat rotting animals. The visitors do not find the food they want. They do pick up pollen. Later they visit other corpse flowers. Then they spread the pollen. That spread helps corpse flowers reproduce. These flowers make their own heat. They also make steam. Heat and steam carry the odor a long way. The stinking root parasite smells different. It does not smell like rotting flesh. It smells like poop. This plant lives in Africa. Inside the flowers is where the bad smell starts. The smell brings dung beetles. Those beetles pollinate the plant. Dung means poop. Dung beetles love dung. A hoatzin is able to fly. It still does not fly often. Say the name hoatzin as wat-SEEN. That name is the real name of the stinkbird. This bird mainly eats leaves. Breakdown of the food happens in the crop. The crop sits under the throat. Only then does the food reach the stomach. Eating in this order gives the hoatzin an awful smell.

5Bad odors are normal for vultures. Their food is flesh from dead animals. That flesh stinks. An enemy may come near a vulture. Then the vulture throws up. The vomit is extra nasty. Rotten meat is in the vomit. Stomach acid is in it too. The acid is strong enough to cause burns. Strong stomach acid keeps a vulture healthy. That help matters when the bird eats rotten meat. A bombardier beetle makes two chemicals. Both chemicals stay inside its body. The chemicals explode when they mix. Some bugs use odor to stay safe. A bombardier beetle cannot fly away from enemies. It has a strong weapon instead. It can let out a hot cloud. The cloud is a stinky spray. The spray is deadly. The heat can kill many enemies. The heat can burn humans.

6A smelly liquid comes off many kinds of millipedes. A touch can make them do this. The liquid can make skin burn. It can also make skin itch. Millipedes are not fast. Stink makes up for that slow speed. They cannot race away from danger. They need another way to stay safe. An enemy may come near. Then a millipede curls up. It gives off a stinky liquid. Some millipedes shoot that smelly stuff. People know the common musk turtle by a second name. People call it a stinkpot. It smells bad if other animals disturb it. A yellow liquid comes out. The liquid stinks. It leaves from the back of the body. A common musk turtle can live a long time. The life can last from forty to sixty years. The mink frog uses scent so enemies stay away. People have compared the stink to rotten onions. This nasty smell is how the frog stays safe. The safety is from other animals. At one time a female mink frog may produce from 500 to 4,000 eggs.

7Africa is home to the green woodhoopoe. Its home is south of the Sahara Desert. The female protects the nest by using odor. She sprays a dark liquid. The liquid stinks. The spray keeps enemies away from the nest. Being stinky helps many plants stay alive. It helps many animals stay alive too. Insects come to plants because of the odor. Those insects help the plants reproduce. For animals, the odor often keeps enemies away. The odor can also help an animal get food. It can help an animal get mates. Steam can be one stinky helper. Spray can be one too. Vomit can be one too. Slime can be one too. Urine can be one too. These smelly ways help living things survive. A liquid is something that can flow. It can change shape. It still keeps the same size. An odor is the scent of something. An odor is also the smell of something. To reproduce is to make offspring. Those offspring are similar to the first living thing. Rotten can mean very bad. Rotten can mean unpleasant. A scent is a smell. People often use scent for a pleasant smell. Stinky means a smell is strong. Stinky can also mean a smell is bad.

53. So Many Things to Save

1This book has six parts, and they come in this order. The introduction comes first. Collecting Is Cool comes second. Common Collections comes third. You Collect What comes fourth. Start Your Own Collection comes fifth. The glossary of word meanings comes last. Stuffed animals rank near the top for kids who collect. Adults love them as well. Counting pieces can be part of the fun. A book is where some children store a collection. Plants and leaves of different kinds can fill such a book. As a hobby, collecting works well. 5Nature offers sticks to some collectors. It offers shells to others. Woods hikes are one search. Beach walks are another. Seashells can consume a lot of time. The effort can still pay off.
