54. Star Athletes of the Summer Olympics

1Sports fans everywhere prepare for a big event. They do this every four years. The event is the Summer Olympics. The modern Games gather more than 10,000 athletes. Those athletes travel from 200 countries. They compete in 300 events. Running is one kind of event. Jumping is another kind. Swimming is another kind. Many more events fill the Games. A winner may take home a gold medal. A winner may take home a silver medal. A winner may take home a bronze medal. The record books may one day list some of these athletes. A few may join the legends in this book.

2Abebe Bikila lived from 1932 to 1973. He ran the marathon for Ethiopia. Fans know him as a shoeless wonder. This Olympic race begins like other races. Runners get a ready call. Then they go. Here they must keep going. The course stretches a very long way. The length is 26 miles (42 km). A picture shows Bikila running barefoot. Walking that far might take about eight hours. Breaks would add more time. Runners finish in a little more than two hours. They take no breaks. People still talk about the Olympic marathon of 1960. Bikila was second late in that race. Less than a mile remained. Then he pulled far ahead. He won by 200 meters. His barefoot time was an Olympic record. He trained with bare feet. His runs crossed the Ethiopian countryside. Ethiopia lies in Africa. Shoes hurt his feet, he said. He won again four years after that. Socks were on his feet for the second win. Shoes were on his feet too. He set a world record that day. The city was Tokyo in Japan. Doctors had removed his appendix just six weeks earlier.

3In 1972 Mark Spitz was World Athlete of the Year. Fans called him the golden boy. His birth year was 1950. Swimming is his sport. The USA is his country. He had dark hair. He wore a mustache. Before the 1968 Olympics he bragged. He promised himself six gold medals. He won two gold medals instead. He also won one silver medal. He also won one bronze medal. The result was quite a feat. It was still not enough for him. He trained hard through the four years after that meet. He did not brag once the 1972 games began. He entered four individual swims. The butterfly was his specialty stroke. He raced three team relays as well. Seven gold medals were his. That total set an Olympic record. Every race also brought a world record. He finally had a reason to brag. Next comes Daley Thompson. Daley Thompson was born in 1958. His event is the decathlon. He represents the United Kingdom. That country is also Great Britain. Few athletes could match him. His first decathlon title came in 1980. His second title came in 1984. Just one person had won this Olympic event twice before him. In 1988 he was 30 years old. For this sport, 30 sounded old. He still came in fourth. The decathlon is the most grueling event in sports.

4Decathlon athletes are called decathletes. They may be the best at many skills at once. Sprinting shows their speed. Hurdles show their speed too. Longer races need their endurance. They are strong enough for the javelin. The discus needs that strength too. The shot put needs it too. They can long jump. They can high jump. They can pole vault. A photo catches Thompson in the long jump. He flies through the air. Most Olympic athletes practice for hours each day. The practice lasts for years. They want to excel in a single event. Track practice is for running. Field practice is for throwing and jumping. Decathlon training must cover ten events. Jim Thorpe lived from 1888 to 1953. The heading calls him the greatest one among athletes of the 20th century. He was Native American. He was a famous college football hero. He joined the Summer Olympics in 1912. He won the decathlon, a contest of ten events. He also won the pentathlon. That contest has five events. World records were his in both. The records held for many years. Each contest is difficult. Each contest is demanding.

5One photo shows Fanny Blankers-Koen after the 1948 Olympic Games. Her daughter is in her arms. Fanny lived from 1918 to 2004. She sprinted and jumped for the Netherlands. People called her a super mom. In 1936 she was 18 and still Fanny Koen. She placed sixth in the high jump. The place disappointed her. She was sure the next chance would be better. That chance took 12 years. The Games were cancelled in 1940. They were cancelled in 1944 too. World War II caused the wait. She was 30 in 1948. She was married. Two children were hers. Her will was strong. People said she should stay with her family at home. She ignored them. She won at 100 meters. She won at 200 meters. She won the 100 meter relay. She won the hurdles at 80 meters. No woman had taken four gold medals at one Summer Olympics before her. Many people believed six gold medals were within reach. She then held the long jump world record. She held the high jump world record too. Women could enter only three individual events under the rules. Another photo shows those 80 meter hurdles. She is racing in London in 1948.

6Jesse Owens and Carl Lewis were super speedsters. Each collected four gold medals during one Olympics. In the 1936 Summer Olympics, Owens faced prejudice. He pushed back against it. A true champion comes from the person within. Skin color does not create a champion. Next comes Nadia Comaneci. Nadia Comaneci was born in 1961. She does gymnastics for Romania. Crowds treated her as everyone's darling. The 1976 crowd roared with pure delight. Nadia was only 14. A ponytail tied her hair. She swung on the uneven bars. Her grace surprised people. Her agility surprised them too. The whole audience could tell the work was great. Say Comaneci like co-man-EECH. She also competed in the floor exercise. Viewers wondered about the score. The board was able to show 1.0 only. A score of 10 would not fit on it. Her work was still perfect. A gymnastics event tops out at 10. Nobody had scored a perfect 10 before she did.

7Nadia went on charming judges at the Olympic Games. The audience stayed charmed too. She earned six further marks of 10. They came in other gymnastic events. Three gold medals went to her. One silver medal went to her. One bronze medal went to her. She was the all-around champion. The 1980 Games added two gold medals. They added two silver medals too. Her uneven bar routine is in a photo. It brought her a perfect 10. Use the glossary for harder words. Agility is quick movement done with ease. Endurance is the power to continue when pain may be present. To excel is to perform extremely well. A feat is an act full of amazing skill. The index names Abebe Bikila and Carl Lewis. It names Cathy Freeman and Daley Thompson. It names the decathlon and Fanny Blankers-Koen. It names gymnastics, the high jump, and hurdles. It names Jesse Owens and Jim Thorpe. It names the marathon and Mark Spitz. It names Nadia Comaneci, the pentathlon, and relay races.

55. Stone Faces on the Mountain

1A map shows Minnesota. It shows North Dakota. It shows Montana. It shows South Dakota. It shows Wyoming. It shows the Black Hills. It shows Nebraska. In the early 1900s, more people in the United States began to settle in the West. Many of them had never visited that region. In 1924, Doane Robinson had an idea. He was a historian in South Dakota. He believed a huge memorial cut from rock would draw visitors. He already knew which sculptor should do the work. He asked the artist Gutzon Borglum to take the job. This account starts with that big idea. It tells of a message about America. It tells of blasting rock. It shows how a hard job brought results. It looks at the mountain today. It looks ahead. Word meanings come at the end.

2Robinson wanted the memorial to honor people who mattered to the West. He suggested explorers. He named Meriwether Lewis. He named William Clark. He suggested Native leaders. He named the Lakota chief Red Cloud. He named the Lakota chief Crazy Horse. Borglum chose a different plan. He wanted faces of American presidents. He spent a year looking for the right rock. Then he picked Mount Rushmore. The mountain stood on important Lakota land. People call that land the Black Hills. The United States government took the land from the Lakota by force. Gold had been found there in 1874. The rock in that place was special. People call it granite. Granite is good for carving. Before the big project, Borglum practiced. He carved a head of Abraham Lincoln. Two of the planned faces were George Washington and Thomas Jefferson. Borglum thought hard about which presidents to carve. He wanted each face to show the strength of the United States. He chose George Washington first. Washington was the first president. He chose Thomas Jefferson second. Jefferson bought Native land from France. That purchase made the United States larger.

3He chose Abraham Lincoln third. Lincoln led the country through the hard years of the Civil War. He chose Theodore Roosevelt last. Roosevelt pushed the United States to build a major waterway. The waterway was in Central America. It made trips shorter between two oceans. One ocean was the Pacific. The other ocean was the Atlantic. Roosevelt was a special pick for one more reason. He had a home in South Dakota. Cutting a work of art into rock is not easy. Borglum began by making small models. He made one model for each president. Workers carried the models up Mount Rushmore. They would copy the models in the rock. Workers used dynamite to blast rock away. Then workers went down the face of the mountain. They hung from cables. They drilled the shapes out of the stone. A picture shows workers on the memorial. Another picture shows workers carving the face of Abraham Lincoln.

4The workers met many problems. One day they blasted rock for a nose. The nose belonged to Thomas Jefferson. They found a split in the rock. Because of that split, they had to move Jefferson's head. Workers patched the crack. The crack now crosses his cheek. It does not cross his nose. A crack in the nose could have broken the stone. More problems came up during the carving. The United States faced hard times in the 1930s. Money for the memorial was scarce. Work stopped many times. Work started again when people raised more money. A picture shows Gutzon Borglum with some of his crew.

5In the 1930s, many Americans could not find jobs. The memorial gave work to 400 crew members. A timeline lists important years. From 1789 to 1797, Washington is president. From 1801 to 1809, Jefferson is president. From 1861 to 1865, Lincoln is president. From 1901 to 1909, Roosevelt is president. In 1927, the carving work starts. In 1929, the Great Depression begins. In 1941, Borglum dies. In 1941, the work is finished. The memorial took twelve years to complete. Crews used a few more years for the last details. Borglum died before those last details were done. A short way from Mount Rushmore is Thunderhead Mountain. A model shows the Crazy Horse memorial as it will look when finished. In 1940, Chief Henry Standing Bear made a request. He asked a sculptor to carve a Lakota memorial in that mountain. The sculptor chose Crazy Horse. Crazy Horse was an important Lakota chief. He fought the United States military. The military was trying to take control of Lakota land. The memorial is also home to a center. The center teaches Native culture. The center is a place of learning.

6Visitors stop for photos. They stand outside the entrance to Mount Rushmore. The place has changed over the years. Roads now lead people to the memorial. A special theater stands at the foot of the mountain. More than two and a half million people visit each year. Some visitors come in protest. They want the government to give the land back. They want the land returned to the Lakota people. Each year, crews clean the memorial. They look for cracks. They look for damage. Rock wears away as time passes. The memorial needs repairs often. Repair crews use a special material. The material fills cracks. The material mends broken rock. The workers hang from cables. The first crews hung the same way in the 1930s. Water can enter a crack. The water can freeze in winter. The ice then expands. The expanding ice breaks the rock.

7Today, technology helps preserve the memorial. Special computers locate spots that need repair. This care should keep the memorial standing. Future generations will be able to see it. The computers tell workers where to work. The workers use special tools. The tools help them reach the right spot. Then they do the needed repair. These words help with the account. The Black Hills are a mountain range. They lie in South Dakota and in Wyoming. A generation is a group of people. They are born and live at about the same time. Granite is a hard rock. Its color is light. People use it for monuments. People use it for buildings. A memorial stands for a memory. The memory may be of people. The memory may be of events. The memory may be of places. To preserve a thing is to keep it from ruin. A sculptor is an artist. The artist shapes wood. The artist may shape clay, stone, or metal. The artist may shape some other material.

56. Stories Carved in Cedar

1It takes hard work to cut a bird shape out of wood. Now imagine animals cut into a log as long as a tree. Native peoples of the Pacific Northwest Coast did this kind of work. They have done it for hundreds of years. People call the carvings totem poles. This coast runs from southern Alaska down to Oregon.

2Carvers use different styles. They also carve for different reasons. Figures on a pole carry special meanings. The figures help keep history. A memorial pole can tell a person's life. It can tell a special event. Other poles tell the story of a clan. A clan is a family group. Still other poles greet visitors. A pole can rise 40 feet. That height is 12.2 meters. Cedar trees supply the wood. One photo shows a pole beside a painted house front. The building is a house used for a potlatch. It stands in Totem Bight State Historic Park. The park is in Ketchikan, Alaska. Some poles belong to a house. Carvers cut house poles into doorways. They may stand at the outside corners. They may hold up the roof. Such poles display the family's wealth. They display the family's status.

3A mortuary pole is like a gravestone. Its carvings honor someone who has died. They tell important events from that life. They tell about family ties. Some poles greet people who come to a village. These poles are usually one human figure. They stand near a beach. They may stand near a stream. They tell guests they are welcome. Canada has a park that holds this story pole. The carving explains a choice made by the Killer Whale clan. Black Bear became the clan crest. A crest is a special symbol. In the Tsimshian language, the clan is called Gispwudwada. Tsimshian is said TSIM-shee-an. This nation lives in British Columbia. One day a hunter walked into the mountains. Mountain goats were what he hoped to find. Black Bear captured him. Black Bear took him to a den. The man lived there with Black Bear. The two became friends. Even so, the man missed his village. Two years later, Black Bear let him leave. By then his body had the look of a bear. Village people ran from him. At last, a kind man rubbed medicine on his body. Soon he looked human again. He chose Black Bear as his crest. Black Bear, his friend, always guarded the family. The pole shows the hunter. It shows the kind man. It shows Black Bear. It shows Black Bear in human form.

4Certain figures show up often on these poles. Tales about them change from one culture to another. Still, each animal keeps some matching traits. A straight beak marks Raven. Raven is a bird. The role Raven plays is trickster. He can turn into almost anything. He might become a bird. He might become a person. He might become a speck of dirt. Bear has round ears. Bear has many teeth. Bear is caring. Bear is a godlike being. Bear can take a human form. Beaver has long teeth in front. The tail is flat. Crossing lines mark that tail. Beaver carves with skill. A hard slap of the tail can start an earthquake. The slap can also make Beaver vanish. Watchmen are men in a crouch. Their hats are very tall. They are human figures. They can see danger from far off. Curly feathers on Thunderbird look like horns. For many Native peoples, this bird ranks among the strongest spirits. It brings thunder. It brings lightning. A whale could fit in its talons. Pointed ears mark Wolf. Wolf has a long snout. Sharp teeth fill the mouth of Wolf. People do not find Wolf friendly. Wolf knows secret ways of healing.

5In earlier times, a clan hired a master carver. The hired artist would create the pole. Museums now request poles. Businesses ask for poles. People far away ask for poles. First they hire a carver. Together they plan the pole. The carver seeks a tree with no knots. The tree must have no bends. The carver cuts the tree down. Branches come off next. Hauling brings the log to a workshop. Soft wood on the outside is chipped away. In Alaska, a Tlingit man cuts a pole. Tlingit is said TLING-kit. David Boxley carves a pole 22 feet tall. That height is 7 meters. His workplace is the National Museum of the American Indian. That museum is in Washington, D.C. The Tsimshian tribe of Alaska is Boxley's own tribe. A team often shares the work. Each design is sketched onto the log by the master. Charcoal makes the lines. The master carves the bottom figure. That figure is the biggest. It is the most important. People can see it from close up. Helpers carve the other figures. Helpers paint them. A large pole can take nine months to finish.

6Wood is still the material for some Native artists of this coast. They make art in the old way. Saxman Native Village lets visitors watch carvers shape poles. The village sits near Ketchikan, Alaska. Tlingit people settled it in 1894. Guests from many countries come each year. They study the village's beautiful poles. They watch the carvers. Nathan Jackson carved many of those poles. He won many awards. He helped Northwest Coast art live again. Other artists took courage from his work. Chicago's Field Museum is getting a pole from three men. Jackson is one of them. His son Stephen is one of them. In the photo, Jackson stands on the right. Stephen stands in the center. When a pole is done, people carry it to its place. A potlatch marks the raising. A potlatch is a special ceremony. People eat at the gathering. They dance. They sing. They give gifts. They tell the events carved on the pole. Someone may say prayers for the pole. Someone may say blessings for it. Prayers may also be for the owners. Blessings may be for the owners. In 1884, British Columbia's government passed a law. The law made a potlatch illegal. Most Native peoples then stopped carving poles. In 1951, leaders dropped that law. Carving of poles began again. A carver helps lift a pole in Alaska. The pole stands 40 feet. That height is 12 meters.

7The 1800s brought European explorers to this coast. The poles they found impressed them. Many poles later stood in museums. A fresh law from the United States arrived in 1990. The law said Native peoples could reclaim property. That property had been stolen. Because of the law, many poles are going home. They return to the makers. One painted pole spreads its wings. A carver is someone who cuts shapes into a hard material. Wood is one such material. A clan is a family group. A memorial pole keeps a memory. The memory may be a person. It may be a place. It may be a thing. It may be an event. A mortuary pole honors the dead. A potlatch is a ceremony for big events. A totem pole is a wood pole cut from cedar. Native peoples of the Northwest Coast make them.

57. Storms That Spin

1What can pull a roof off a building? What can throw a house into the air? A tornado can do both. Tornadoes are not all one size. Across, a small tornado may be just a few feet, or 1 m. A large one can spread wider than 1 mile, or 2 km. Some touch the ground for only a short time. Some keep going for hundreds of miles. In 2011, a strong tornado hit St. Louis, Missouri. It nearly ripped the roof from a home. It also turned an SUV over in the driveway.

2A supercell storm crossed Nebraska in June 2004. Why do tornadoes form? Scientists are not sure. They do know where strong tornadoes begin. They begin in huge storms called supercells. A supercell starts when warm, moist air rises. That warm air mixes with cold, dry air high in the atmosphere. In some supercells, air at different temperatures may start to spin. The spinning air builds a cloud shaped like a funnel. When that funnel reaches the ground, it is a tornado. Some scientists think a tornado helps even out heat and moisture in the air. This funnel cloud later grew into a strong tornado. That tornado hit Kansas in 2004.

3How a Tornado Forms. Cool air moves in. Warm air rises. The air keeps spinning inside the thundercloud. Then rain and hail begin to fall. A funnel cloud takes shape. The funnel cloud touches down and travels over the land. The tornado damages anything in its path. Debris flies through the air. Measuring Tornadoes. Scientists have a system to estimate a tornado's strength. They use the Enhanced Fujita Scale, or EF Scale. Wind speed is what the scale uses to rate strength. Scientists look at the damage a tornado does. From that harm, they estimate the wind speed. An EF-0 has wind gusts of 65 to 85 miles per hour (105 to 137 kilometers per hour). An EF-1 has winds of 86 to 110 miles per hour (138 to 177 kilometers per hour). An EF-2 has winds of 111 to 135 miles per hour (179 to 217 kilometers per hour). An EF-3 has winds of 136 to 165 miles per hour (219 to 265 kilometers per hour). An EF-4 has winds of 166 to 200 miles per hour (267 to 322 kilometers per hour). An EF-5 has winds of more than 200 miles per hour (322 kilometers per hour). EF-5 tornadoes are the most powerful of all.

4Where Tornadoes Form. Tornadoes can be hard to measure. Still, scientists know the places they are most likely to strike. A tornado can happen in any part of the world. Most of them form in the middle of the United States. People call that region Tornado Alley. The number that forms there each year is above one thousand. Tornado Alley takes in South Dakota, Nebraska, Colorado, Kansas, Oklahoma, and Texas. Canada lies to the north. Mexico lies to the south. Cold, dry air comes down from the north. Warm, moist air comes up from the south. In 2013, a huge tornado hit Moore, Oklahoma. It caused a great deal of destruction. In Tornado Alley, northern air mixes with southern air. Those conditions are perfect for a tornado to form.

5Famous Tornadoes. A tornado in 1925 traveled through Missouri, Illinois, and Indiana. It killed 695 people. Two huge storm systems came in April 1974 and April 2011. Each one produced hundreds of tornadoes across the Midwest and the Southeast. In May 2011, an EF-5 tornado struck Missouri. It destroyed more than seven thousand homes. A tornado that hit Kansas in 2003 was so powerful that a child's bicycle wrapped around a tree. In March 2012, a giant tornado swept through Henryville, Indiana. It was 150 yards wide, or 138 meters. It traveled 52 miles, or 84 kilometers. Tornado Safety. Tornadoes are very dangerous. Weather scientists do their best to warn people before a tornado arrives. There is no way to be sure that a tornado will strike. The National Weather Service tries to help people stay safe during tornadoes. They may declare a tornado watch.

6This underground shelter was built to provide protection from tornadoes. The safest place to be is indoors. A basement is best if you have one. The next safest places are closets or bathrooms. Stay away from windows. A tornado can blast through the glass. Get down low to the ground. If you can, go under a heavy table or a desk and cover your head. You can even get into a bathtub. A sign alerts people that a shelter is nearby. They can find safety there if a tornado comes. Students practice for a tornado by going into a hallway and covering their heads. The spinning cloud causes things to fly around. Cover yourself with a mattress or blankets to protect your head. If you are in a tall building, find the stairs. If you are in a car and cannot escape, park the car. Leave your seatbelt on and lean forward.

7Closing Words. Tornadoes are scary examples of the power of nature. Weather scientists are working on better ways to tell when a tornado is coming. Many questions remain. What really causes a tornado? What is it like inside a tornado? Perhaps one day we will find out. A Doppler on Wheels is a van. Scientists follow storms in that van and learn more about tornadoes. Glossary. Atmosphere means a layer of gases around a planet, a star, or a moon. Damage means harm done to someone or something. A funnel is a cone-shaped tube that is wider at the top. People often use a funnel to pour liquid or powder into a small opening. Moist means slightly wet, or damp. Strike means to happen suddenly, or to appear. Tornadoes are fast-spinning, funnel-shaped clouds that touch Earth's surface.

58. The Highway Called Route 66

1Route 66 carries drivers across the whole American West. A First Look. The year is 1957. You fold the top down on a red sports car. Rock and roll pours from the speakers. Nothing but open road lies ahead. Could the drive be any better? Then an ice cream cone comes into view. It is three stories tall. Your mouth starts to water. Should you stop? Wait. A cheeseburger as big as a bus is coming up. A quick lunch sounds better. At night, you park the car. A teepee is the shape of the hotel room beside you. The day on the road was full of fun. No one knows what the next day will bring. You have just made a road trip. The highway is Route 66, a favorite road in America.

2America's Main Street. Picture a map of roads in the United States. The lines look a lot like a spiderweb. Wide highways zigzag from one coast to the other. They link nearly every city along the way. Travel is simple. Get in a car and go. A hundred years ago, a trip across the country was much harder. Most roads were only small trails of dirt. Cars often broke down. They sank in mud. Sharp rocks made tires pop. In the 1920s, the government of the United States built new highways. The new roads made driving easier. The length of one highway was 2,400 miles. That is 3,862 km. It started in Chicago, Illinois. It ended in Los Angeles, California. People named it Route 66. A fond nickname was the Main Street of America. The United States met hard times in 1929. Many banks failed. People lost the money they had saved. They lost their jobs. They lost their homes. The years that followed are called the Great Depression. Then long dry spells ruined many farms. Those years are called the Dust Bowl era. All at once, many families had to leave home. A lot of them hoped to find work in California. Thousands packed what they owned and headed west. They traveled on a new road. The road was Route 66. A Book about the Dust Bowl. The Grapes of Wrath is a classic novel. John Steinbeck wrote it. The book came out in 1939. It follows a family leaving Oklahoma in the Dust Bowl era. They travel a long way on Route 66. They hope to find work in California.

3California gained 1.2 million people in the 1930s. The Dust Bowl pushed out over half a million of them. World War II drew in the United States on December 8, 1941. Soldiers from every part of the country had to travel. Military bases in the West were their destination. They rode Route 66. Restaurants opened along the road to serve them. Gas stations opened too. Hotels opened too. The war ended in 1945, and Americans celebrated. Many people bought new cars. They drove west to see places such as the Grand Canyon. Route 66 was the way they went. The Road in Songs and Shows. After World War II, Route 66 had some of its best years. Shiny new cars rolled along the road. Yellow cabs did too. Route 66, a song, reached the top in 1946. The words told people to get their kicks on Route Sixty-Six. Fame for the highway grew because of that song. In 1960, a television show named Route 66 went on the air. It followed two friends driving across the country. They were hunting for adventures. By 1970, a newer highway had taken most drivers off the old Route 66. The old road was bypassed. Even then, Route 66 stayed part of American popular culture. The first famous recording of that song came from the Nat King Cole Trio. They sang it in 1946. The television show Route 66 ran from 1960 to 1964.

4John Lasseter took his family along Route 66 in the year 2000. He led Pixar Animation Studios. Bright art along the road won his heart. The buildings won his heart as well. He chose to turn that trip into a movie. The Disney-Pixar film was called Cars. It came out in 2006. Lasseter sent a team along Route 66 to study the road for the film. Some roadside art they saw became characters in Cars. Real sights from the road appear in the movie. Holbrook, Arizona, has the Wigwam Motel, and that motel is one example. In the film, that motel is the Cozy Cone Motel. After families saw Cars, many of them took a vacation on Route 66. The trips helped the road grow popular again. A rusty tow truck in Galena, Kansas, became a character in the film. The character is Tow Mater. Sights on a Map. The highway crosses Illinois, Missouri, Oklahoma, Texas, New Mexico, Arizona, and California. Chicago is at one end. Los Angeles is at the other. Canada lies north of the road. Mexico lies south of it. The Pacific Ocean is to the west. The Atlantic Ocean is to the east. These stops sit along the way. The Gemini Giant is in Wilmington, Illinois. Paul Bunyon is in Atlanta, Illinois. The Brooks Catsup Bottle Water Tower is in Collinsville, Illinois. The Red Rocker is in Fanning, Missouri. The Blue Whale is in Catoosa, Oklahoma. Slug Bug Ranch is in Conway, Texas. Cadillac Ranch is in Amarillo, Texas. The Wigwam Motel is in Holbrook, Arizona. Elmer's Bottle Tree Ranch is in Oro Grande, California. The Cabazon Dinosaurs are in Cabazon, California. Chicken Boy is in Los Angeles, California. Sights beside the Road. Museums, motels, and shops line Route 66. The road is also full of art you will not see anywhere else. Old gas stations still stand. Theaters still stand. Travelers can watch live shows. One show is a Native American dance. Another show is a rodeo. You can be sure of one thing. A trip on Route 66 will not be boring.

5Cadillac Ranch stands in a pasture. Cows graze around it. Cadillac Ranch. In 1974, a group of artists buried ten cars in a field. Each car points nose down. Visitors have covered the cars with spray paint of many colors. Close by, in Conway, Texas, is the VW Slug Bug Ranch. Five Volkswagen Beetles, painted in bright colors, stick up from the ground. As a boy, Elmer Long saved bottles. He also saved other things people had thrown out. Elmer's Bottle Tree Ranch. Elmer Long, a folk artist, built Bottle Tree Ranch. He used bottles that could be used again. He used parts from cars. He used other objects he found. Wind passing through the open bottles fills the air with music. A Blue Whale. What gift suits a woman who already has everything? Hugh Davis gave his wife a blue whale. The whale is 80 feet (24.4 m) long. He built it to mark their anniversary. Catoosa, Oklahoma, is where this Blue Whale stands.

6Chicken Boy stands in Los Angeles, California. The Gemini Giant stands in Wilmington, Illinois. Paul Bunyon stands in Atlanta, Illinois. A Land of Giants. Route 66 really is a land of giants. A factory in California sold thousands of huge statues. Stores and other businesses used the statues in advertising. Workers at the factory added costumes. The costumes made the giants look like cowboys. They made them look like astronauts. They even made them look like chickens. A lot of the largest statues along the road are shaped like food. A giant orange can make you hungry. A giant donut can too. A giant cheeseburger can too. A giant ice cream cone can too. Look out a car window in Illinois. You may see the biggest catsup bottle on Earth. It is the Brooks Catsup Bottle Water Tower in Collinsville, Illinois. Dinosaurs in a Row. Dinny cost 300,000 dollars to build. Roadside materials were used to make Dinny. Prehistoric creatures of every shape and size live along Route 66. One of them may be the biggest concrete dinosaur in America. Length for this dinosaur is 150 feet. That is 45.7 m. A dinosaur park in California is where it stands. Claude Bell built the park. He wanted people to stop at his restaurant nearby, the Wheel Inn. Mr. Rex is a Tyrannosaurus rex three stories tall. Visitors love to see him at the dinosaur park.

7The Red Rocker stands in Fanning, Missouri. It is one of the sights on Route 66. Keeping the Road Alive. More and more people drove Route 66, and the heavy traffic hurt the highway. From 1956 through the 1980s, builders made new roads around it. The new roads were fast highways with many lanes. As drivers switched to those highways, shops on Route 66 lost customers. A lot of the shops shut down. Peeling paint became a common sight. So did windows covered with boards. A law from the United States Congress came in 1999. The law was written to protect Route 66. Money from the law helps businesses. It also helps towns keep important places open. Route 66 is growing popular once more. The best way to feel the spirit of this road is to drive it. You will have an American experience you will not forget. Advertising is a public notice. It tells people about a product, a service, or an event. It draws interest and helps sales. Bypassed means gone around or avoided. People often do this to make a job faster or easier. Cabs are cars or similar vehicles. The drivers carry passengers from place to place for pay. A highway is a main road linking towns or cities. Jagged means a shape or edge that is sharp and uneven. Military bases are places the military owns. Soldiers and equipment stay there. A road trip is a journey in a car or another vehicle. People take it to see places away from home. A rodeo is a contest. People show how they handle cattle and horses. Spirit is the mood of a person. It can also be the mood of a group or a place.

59. The Long Story of Chewing Gum

1The book parts follow this order. An opening says chew on this. Earliest chewers come next. Gum machines come next. Gum control comes next. Riches from gum come next. Steps for making chewing gum come next. A conclusion is last. Dogs chew bones. Cows chew cud. People chew a different thing. The thing is gum. You might ask where gum starts. You might ask how people make it. You might ask who chewed it before anyone else.

2Americans sometimes get credit as the first people who chewed gum. The credit is mistaken. Gummy plant stuff was already being chewed in many countries. That habit was old before Europeans reached North America. Chewers in Greece used mastiche. Native peoples of South America used chicle. Betel was a favorite in India. Stains appear on teeth after betel nuts are chewed. Stains also appear after the leaves are chewed. A sticky goo pleased native peoples who lived in North America. Spruce-tree sap supplied the goo. A picture includes a spruce tree. Europeans who came to settle learned the goo in North America. They gave it a try. They gave it approval. Spruce gum might have disappointed you. Flavor was nearly absent. Change waited until 1850. John Curtis put flavor in. He hoped to spruce up sap taken from spruce. He called the result Maine Pure Spruce Gum. Other settlers in North America were his buyers. A picture shows sap dripping out. The drip leaves a cut on a spruce tree.

3Chewing gum was widely popular by 1871. Thomas Adams built a machine for making it. He worked in New York. He also sold gum. More machines appeared not long after. A coin in a slot caused gum to come out. Some machines gave balls of gum. Other machines gave small sticks. Glass globes sat on certain gumball machines. Balls inside looked bigger than their true size. A machine today can resemble the early 1900s models. Adams was earliest with Tutti-Frutti gum. He sold those pieces in subway stations. New York City was the place. 1888 was the year. Inventors missed a trick children would learn. Fingers went into the machine. A gumball came out free. Adams corrected the error. He placed the earliest vending machines along the New York subways. The year for that step was also 1888.

4Getting rid of chewed gum is still a leading problem. Sidewalks receive a lot of spit gum. Desks receive gum stuck underneath. Chairs receive it too. Worse spots receive it too. A nasty mess can result. The early 1900s gave one railroad this trouble. New York Central Railroad was that line. It hired someone whose whole job was removing gum. The role was full-time. Each night the worker gathered what people had stuck down. The place was New York Central Station. Seven to eight pounds came off in a night. Since 1960, chewed gum has been pressed on an old brick building. San Luis Obispo, California, holds that building. People call the spot Gum Alley. The wall is very tacky. A picture shows the alley.

5Downtown Chicago is the site of the Wrigley Building. Naming rights came from the Wrigley family. William Wrigley Jr. put extra flavors into the gum he made. The taste grew better. His earliest flavor names were two. Lotta Gum was a name. Vassar was a name. He wanted the public to try a piece. He sent a piece to each person on a huge list. The list held one and a half million names. Every phone-book listing in the United States was on it. The mailing year was 1892. People loved what they tasted. Sales followed. The money let him buy a professional baseball team. That team was the Chicago Cubs. He built a stadium for the team. The stadium was Wrigley Field. He later bought an island for rest. Enjoyment was part of the plan. The island’s name is Catalina Island. It sits offshore from Southern California. A picture shows the Chicago Cubs during a home game.

6Would homemade chewing gum interest you? The job may be hard. Begin with the ingredient called a gum base. The usual base is one of four materials. Latex is a material. Chicle is a material. Beeswax is a material. Paraffin wax is a material. Melt that base until it pours like thick maple syrup. Send the pour through a fine mesh screen. A mixing vat catches it. Flavoring is the next add-in. Coloring is the add-in after that. Move the mixture onto a cooling belt. Let cooling finish. Let setting finish. Rolling is the step after that. Cutting makes the pieces. Keep the pieces aside for 24 hours. Pack only when the hours are done. Did the order stay clear? A bought piece of gum may be simpler for you.

7Pause the next time a gum stick is in your mouth. You might instead be waiting on a machine. A gumball has not dropped yet. Let the pause raise questions. Ask where gum began. Ask how the making works. Ask how many years people have liked it. Discard the piece the right way. Use the garbage. Some people use Gum Alley instead. Cud is food that a cow digests. The first digesting happens once. The cow brings the food back up. Chewing happens again. Discard means you get rid of something. Throw it away and the word still fits. Reject it and the word still fits. Latex begins as sap from particular plants. Contact with air makes it solid. Mesh may be threads. Mesh may be wires. They are woven in a loose pattern. Paraffin wax is water joined with carbon wax. The join makes a solid. Sap is a plant’s fluid. Food moves through the plant on that fluid. Tacky can describe something sticky. Tacky can describe a missing sense of style. A vat is a container of large size. Liquids stay in it. A wad is soft stuff gathered in a small mass. Chewed gum that people throw away can become a nuisance. Singapore is an island nation that faced this trouble. It outlawed gum during 1992.

60. The Music Life of Yo-Yo Ma

1In 2012, Yo-Yo Ma played with a high school choir in Chicago, Illinois. He was born in 1955 in Paris, France. Music filled the family. His mother sang. His father played the violin and taught music. Yeou-Cheng is his older sister. YOO-CHANG is how to say her name. She had played the violin since she was small. Both parents were born in China. France became their next home because of a teaching job at a music school. At age seven, the family moved again. New York City was the new home. His father then created the Children's Orchestra Society. Classical music for children was its purpose.

2His parents gave him a violin when he was only three. Ma remembers a terrible sound. He believed skill was missing. Yeou-Cheng remembers broken instruments. More than one violin was smashed. Anger was the cause. He quit quickly. A 1961 practice break shows the children playing for orchestra members. Yo-Yo is six. Yeou-Cheng Ma is ten. Music pulled him back before long. About a year after he quit, his parents took him to a concert in Paris. A double bass caught his eye. That was the instrument he wanted. A double bass reaches about 6 feet. In metric measure that is 1.8 m. His parents said a small child could not handle a size that big. They chose the next-biggest stringed instrument. A cello was the gift. CHEL-oh is how to say cello.

3Both children were pushed to play difficult music. Their father offered a method for any hard task. Split the task into smaller pieces. Those pieces are easier work. Their mother wanted the notes learned past the point of thinking. Feeling inside the music could then take the mind. In 1962, seven-year-old Yo-Yo played for President John F. Kennedy. The following year, brother and sister played on television. Millions watched. Children of that age often meet music in a simpler way. A recorder's first notes are the usual start. Ma was already famous worldwide. He stood among the best-known musicians.

4High school ended early. The age was fifteen. Juilliard was the college he picked. It ranks among the finest music schools on Earth. A switch came soon. Other subjects, not only music, were what he wanted. The cello was not supposed to be his entire life. Adulthood found him already on stage. Many of the world's top orchestras had shared those stages. A summer music festival came before the new school. Jill Hornor was there. Love followed. She was in college. She ran the festival. Return visits filled later summers. Music was one reason. Time with Jill was the other. Marriage came later. Two children came too. Sweden was a 2012 trip. Jill Hornor went. The children went. The honor was for his work.

5Back pain arrived while he was still a young man. Graduation was followed by a forced stop. Cello playing had to wait. Surgery was planned for his back. A condition was bending the spine the wrong way. Risk was high. A bad result might end cello playing forever. Skipping surgery might mean pain for a lifetime. He judged the risk worthwhile. The operation succeeded. Six months went by inside a body cast. Moving around was impossible. Cello playing was impossible. The period was very hard. Freedom from the cast sent him back onto stages worldwide. Album recording began at twenty-three. The total now is more than seventy-five albums. Among living classical artists, he is one of the top sellers. The cello itself dates to 1733. Its worth is $2.5 million. A 1999 taxi ride went wrong. The cello was left behind. The driver brought the cello back. Ma then made a recorded reminder. Every taxi in New York played that reminder. Riders were told to look for items left at the ride's end. A 2011 performance includes the Silk Road Ensemble. Charles Lil Buck Riley, a dancer, is with them.

6Yo-Yo Ma plays at an opening in 2002. The event is the Winter Olympic Games. Sting, a pop musician, performs beside him. Chinese ideas helped form his music. French ideas helped form it. American ideas helped form it. European classical music was the starting point. Other styles came soon after. Bluegrass is one. Argentine tango is one. Jazz is one. Traditional music from China is one. Traditional music from Japan is one. Traditional music from Brazil is one. Movie songs are part of his playing. Broadway musical songs are part of it too. An open mind, he says, matters. It deepens a person's grasp of music. It deepens a person's grasp of people. The year 1998 called for a group. The sound should match his blend of cultures. Silk Road Ensemble is the group he founded. Twenty countries send its musicians and its composers. They make music as a team. Traditions from around the world feed that music. Hundreds of years ago, Asia had a trade route called the Silk Road. Ideas traveled that route among many peoples.

7The year 2001 shows Yo-Yo Ma preparing to play. Hard work is the success story many musicians tell. Natural talent is another story they tell. Yo-Yo Ma offers neither story. He cannot say why popularity found him. He is among the world's most popular musicians. Accident is the word he once used for himself. Credit, he says, is widely shared. Parents helped. Teachers helped. His sister helped. His wife helped. His children helped. Public playing while he was young helped shape him. Paris life helped shape him. New York life helped shape him. Alter any of those pieces, he feels, and success might not have followed. A giant double bass still sits in that story. He was four when he saw it. Miss that sight, and the whole life would have turned out differently. Word meanings come next. A cello is a big string instrument from the violin family. The player sits and holds it upright between the legs. Classical can mean European music of the eighteenth century. It includes the nineteenth century too. It can also mean older Western works, including symphonies and operas. Composers write music. Cultures are the customs and ideas of different groups. Each society keeps its own. An ensemble performs together. Actors may be in one. Musicians may be in one. A festival can be a celebration. It can be a planned run of cultural events. An orchestra is many musicians playing as one large group. String instruments and classical music are common there. Surgery is a doctor's treatment that opens the body. Damaged parts may be repaired. They may be removed. A violin is a string instrument. It rests under the chin. A bow makes the sound.

61. The Pirate with the Burning Beard

1Picture a pirate. You may think of an eye patch. You may think of a parrot. You may think of a black hat. That colorful picture is not only from cartoons. It is not only from movies. In the early 1700s, a real pirate sailed the seas. He had a dark beard. He had wild eyes. Sailors felt fear when they heard his name. People called him Blackbeard. No picture of him is known to exist. Artists draw him from written descriptions. They also study pictures of other pirates. This account looks at the man behind the old tales. It tells how he became Blackbeard. It shows how he ruled the sea. It tells about his last fight. It looks at the legend that grew later. A list of word meanings comes at the end.

2Few facts are known about the man people called Blackbeard. He may have been born in Bristol, England. The year was around 1680. The name Edward Teach was his real name. Sometimes people spelled that name Thatch. Sometimes they spelled it Teache. A map of his seas shows North America. It shows North Carolina. It shows South Carolina. It shows Georgia. It shows Florida. It shows the Atlantic Ocean. It shows the Gulf of Mexico. It shows the West Indies. It shows the Caribbean Sea. It shows Central America. It shows South America. It shows the Pacific Ocean. One area on the map is marked for a reason. Most pirate attacks happened there. Trading ships from many countries crossed the Caribbean Sea. Those ships often carried stolen people. They often carried stolen goods. Pirates could reach the ships with ease. Edward Teach became a sailor. The life was very hard. The pay was small. He soon became an English privateer. He robbed enemy ships for England. That work led him to Captain Benjamin Hornigold. Hornigold was a famous English pirate. Many countries hired sailors as privateers during wars. The law let privateers attack enemy ships. They added the best captured ships to their own country's navy. They sold the goods those ships carried. Privateers received a small share of the profits. They had to follow rules of war. Many privateers chose to become pirates. Pirates did not have to follow rules of war. A pirate could attack any ship. A pirate could keep all the treasure.

3Teach was fearless. He was bold. Hornigold gave him command of a ship the men had taken. The two men worked as a team. They attacked ships. They stole cargo. Before long, no pirates on the ocean scared people more. In November 1717, Teach took command of a large French ship. Someone had already captured that ship. Its name was La Concorde. He gave it a new name. The new name was Queen Anne's Revenge. He added more cannons. The ship was now heavily armed. Hornigold retired. Teach took over. Within a year, the sailor Edward Teach had become the pirate Blackbeard. During the 1700s, European countries set up new towns. Some towns were in the Caribbean. Others were in North America. Others were in South America. English cargo ships sailed back and forth. French cargo ships did the same. Dutch cargo ships did the same. Spanish cargo ships did the same. Pirates could reach those ships with ease. Queen Anne's Revenge was large. It was fast. It had great cannon power. Blackbeard himself was a fearful sight. He was a huge man. He wore beads in his thick black beard. He wore ribbons twisted into that beard. He stuck lit fuses into it. The beard looked as if it was on fire. Most of his victims were too scared to fight. They gave up.

4Blackbeard's black flag showed a skeleton. The skeleton was stabbing a heart. The skeleton held an hourglass. The hourglass meant time was short. A victim had to give up quickly. If the victim did not, Blackbeard would attack. He captured ship after ship. He ended with four ships. His crew grew to more than 350 men. In May of 1718, the four ships reached Charleston, South Carolina. They waited outside the town. For one week they robbed every ship that arrived. They robbed every ship that left. He captured people from those ships. He told the town to pay if it wanted the people back. How did Blackbeard gather such a big crew? A pirate crew often received part of the treasure. Most sailors received little pay. They received little respect. When pirates attacked, many sailors chose to join them. Some enslaved people joined for a reason. Joining was a way to escape slavery. Blackbeard planned a new base on the Outer Banks. The Outer Banks are a chain of islands off North Carolina. On the way, Queen Anne's Revenge stuck on a sandbar. The sandbar was near Beaufort. He told his men to move the treasure. The treasure went aboard the other ships. He had heard news from England. England would let pirates go free if they turned themselves in. He sent one captain to learn if the news was true. While that captain was away, Blackbeard left many men behind. He left them on a nearby island. Then he sailed away. He took the loot with him. Some people say he drove the ship onto the sandbar on purpose. They ask if he tricked the crew. Then he would not have to share the treasure with so many people.

5A map marks Virginia. It marks the northern Outer Banks islands. It marks Bath. It marks North Carolina. It marks Beaufort. It marks the southern Outer Banks islands. It marks South Carolina. It marks Charleston. It marks the Atlantic Ocean. It marks Georgia. It marks Florida. It marks Cape Hatteras. It marks Ocracoke Island. It marks Beaufort Inlet. The map shows where the wreck of Blackbeard's ship was found. The wreck lies near Beaufort Inlet. That inlet is off the coast of North Carolina. Searchers found the wreck of Queen Anne's Revenge in 1996. It seemed that Edward Teach had taken his loot. It seemed that he had stopped sailing. North Carolina's governor forgave the crimes Teach had done before. Blackbeard soon returned to his old tricks. He offered to share his loot with the governor. In return, the governor would let him rob ships. The ships were off the Carolina coast. With that support, Blackbeard began to attack cargo ships again. He used the one ship he still had. That ship was called the Adventure.

6Alexander Spotswood was the governor of Virginia. He grew tired of Blackbeard's looting. He sent Lieutenant Robert Maynard to find Blackbeard. Maynard was also ordered to capture him. On November 22, 1718, the crew of Maynard's ship spotted the Adventure. Maynard's ship was called the Ranger. The Adventure was near the island of Ocracoke. People still tell the story of that final fight near Ocracoke. Even young Benjamin Franklin wrote a poem about that battle. Blackbeard fought for many hours. Cannons on the Adventure fired at Maynard's ship. Thick smoke covered the fight. Maynard's men hid below. The ship looked as if its crew had been killed. Blackbeard thought he had won. He climbed aboard the Ranger. Maynard's men ran up on deck. The fight became hand to hand. Blackbeard fought hard. Maynard had too many men. Blackbeard was shot five times. Then he fell dead. Maynard's men threw the body into the sea.

7The name Blackbeard lived on after his death. Many pirates were remembered as heroes outside the law. People remembered Blackbeard in that same way. His life may stand behind characters in Treasure Island. It may stand behind characters in Pirates of the Caribbean. It may stand behind other tales. The next time you hear about a pretend pirate, remember a real one. The next time you read about a pretend pirate, remember him too. The real man behind the legend is Blackbeard. These words help with the account. Cargo means the goods a large vehicle hauls. A legend is an old story that people believe. No one can prove that the story is true. A pirate attacks ships at sea in order to rob them. A privateer serves on a private ship during wartime. The law lets that ship capture enemy ships for a country. To sail is to cross water by boat or by ship. Treasure is something very special. Treasure can also be something of great worth.

1Michelle Obama works with children in a vegetable garden. The garden grows at the White House. She was a First Lady. In the United States, the president's wife holds that title. The work is not a normal job. These women do not get paid. The public watches them closely. That lets them speak to many Americans. They can talk about causes and projects they care about. Across the years, they have worked hard. They have helped Americans who are poor. They have helped the land and water. They have stood up for schools and libraries. They have tried to make life richer for many children. People ask a question. If a woman wins the presidency, what name will her husband have? People will call him the First Gentleman.

She said no one can make you feel small unless you let them. She was one of the strongest women in this role. Franklin led the country in a hard time. They entered the White House in the Great Depression. Those years were the 1930s. Many people had no work. Wealthy parents raised Eleanor. She grew into a hard worker. She worked as a reporter. Later she edited books in New York. Betty Ford's husband was Gerald R. Ford Jr. Leaders named him vice president in 1973. In 1974 he became president. 63. The Quiet Room in the Yellow House

1In 1888, Vincent Van Gogh moved from the Netherlands to Arles, France. He liked the calm beauty of Arles. Night stars glittered on the Rhone River. Sunlight shone on yellow wheat in the rolling hills. He painted fields, sky, and sunflowers outside, which was new then. Most artists before him had painted inside a studio.

2Vincent was a man of many strong feelings. At times he felt sadder, angrier, or happier than other people. His feelings led him to create some of his greatest paintings. He used color and brush strokes to show those feelings. Starry Night shows a night sky swirling with energy. Yellow stars and the moon twirl in a navy blue sky.

3His moods changed often. He needed a calm and cheerful place to live and work. He rented a bright yellow house in Arles. The left window with closed shutters was his bedroom. In October 1888 he wrote to his brother Theo about the room. The walls were pale violet, the bed was butter yellow, and the bedspread was scarlet.

4A painting of a night cafe made that Arles cafe famous. Vincent painted Cafe Terrace at Night in September 1888. In the mornings he painted all kinds of pictures. In the evenings he sat in outdoor cafes and chatted with people. Later he enjoyed coming home to his comforting bedroom. He also painted twelve sunflowers and a sunny lawn in a public park.

5In 1888 and 1889 Vincent created five works of his bedroom. Three were bright oil paintings, and two were sketches. The three oils are sometimes called the Bedroom series. He was very close to Theo, who supported his life as a painter. Theo saved the letters, and 847 are in an online museum today. One letter included a sketch and said his eyes were still tired.

6His paintings show a simple, restful, cheery bedroom haven. No indoor plumbing existed, so he kept wash water and hung clothes on hooks. Radios, televisions, and CD players had not been invented yet. Vincent painted for fun and made more than 2,000 works of art. In Arles he painted many landscapes and portraits. Each bedroom painting showed newer pictures on the walls.

7The three paintings are similar, but some details are very different. Vincent painted the first one in October 1888. The Rhone River flooded the yellow house and damaged the painting. Theo liked it and encouraged Vincent to paint a new one. He created the second painting in Saint-Remy in September 1889. How do the two paintings show what Vincent was feeling?

8Many people agree you truly see a painting only by standing before it. To see these few paintings you must travel across Europe and North America. The easiest way is to look in art books and on the Internet. In the summer of 1889 he made smaller versions of favorite paintings. The bedroom was a favorite, so the third version was smaller and different. His style used large swirls, thick short strokes, and colors that show feelings.

9Vincent Van Gogh loved color and used it to express his feelings. He loved the colors of the sky, butter, wheat, and sunlight. Those bedroom colors made him feel content and glad to be home. Do bright colors make you feel happy? Can dark colors make you feel sad? If this were your bedroom, what colors would you use?

64. The Scientist Who Lived with Chimps

1After a talk in 2013, Goodall speaks with fans. The talk was in Nairobi, Kenya. Jane Goodall grew up in Bournemouth. Bournemouth is a seaside town. It is in the United Kingdom. In those years, people expected a quiet life for most young English girls. The girls were expected to stay home. England is her birthplace. The year was 1934. Learning about animals pleased her when she was a child. She liked watching the way they behaved. She loved bright stories about wild animals. The animals in the stories lived in Africa. She dreamed of living there herself. Jane felt sure about her future work. Animals were the work she hoped for. She wanted to write stories about them.

2A photo shows young Jane with her dog. The dog's name is Rusty. Jane loved playing with her pets. She loved climbing trees. She loved reading. She wanted a life full of travel. She wanted adventure. At eighteen, Goodall finished high school. She became a secretary. The path from that job to Africa was unclear to her. She kept working anyway. By 1957 she had saved enough money for a trip. She went to Kenya. Kenya is in eastern Africa. A famous scientist met her before many months had passed. His name was Doctor Louis Leakey. Doctor Leakey studied apes and humans. He studied the ways they are alike. He studied the ways they are different. He needed a new assistant. Goodall had no training in science. He hired her even so. She learned quickly. She loved the work. A special field study was Leakey's idea one day. The study would be about chimpanzees. Scientists in a field study stay close to the animal under study. Leakey needed a patient person for this study. The person also had to be curious. The person had to feel at ease in the forest. Chimps lived in that forest. He asked Goodall to do it. For her, the offer was a dream come true. Each chimp received a name from Goodall. She watched every chimp in each family. She watched how a family acted together. She watched how families acted with other families.

3Goodall's age in 1960 was twenty-six. That year she traveled to the Gombe Forest. Say Gombe like GOME-bay. The forest is in Tanzania. Chimpanzees lived there. At Gombe she sat quietly on a hill. She watched the chimpanzees every day. She watched all day. She watched even when rain fell. She wrote careful notes. She saw that chimps always stayed with their family group. She used a naming plan. In one family, every chimp's name began with the same letter. Fear of her lasted a long while among the chimps. Then an older chimp came to her camp. She had named him David Greybeard. He was curious. He was looking for food. A banana waited under her shirt, hidden from sight. She let him find the banana. His trust in her grew. Into the forest he led her. He led her to his family. The family let her sit nearby and watch. She came back day after day. Her early reports upset old ideas in science. The ideas were long-held beliefs about chimps. Some scientists doubted what she reported. They did not believe chimps had feelings. They did not believe chimps had intelligence. Personal names for study animals seemed wrong for a serious scientist.

4At that time, people believed chimps ate plants only. Goodall watched the family of David Greybeard eat meat. Later she watched him use a stick. He poked the stick into a termite mound. Out came the stick. He ate the termites as a snack. Goodall understood two new things. First, a tool helped David reach his food. Second, the tool was one he had made. He pulled the leaves off the stick first. A bare stick could fit into the small hole. Her report shocked people and started a scientific debate. The report also brought her important support. It brought her funding. A chimp at Gombe eats a snack of termites. In 1976, Goodall watches wildlife with her family. Her husband is there. Their son is there. Photos of the chimps were seen around the world. Chimp movies traveled the world as well. Because of David Greybeard, the other chimps learned to trust her. She could follow whole life cycles. She watched from birth. She watched until death. She saw actions no researcher had ever seen before. She wrote about those actions.

5Goodall said each chimp had a personality of its own. Humans have personal personalities too. She found a wide range of emotions in chimps. Chimps could be happy. They could be sad. They could be angry. They could be loving. They could be jealous. She saw chimps hold hands. She saw them laugh. She saw them throw tantrums. Across many years, her discoveries gave people new ideas about chimpanzees. Her reports did the same. Her work pushed many scientists to think again. They questioned the line between humans and other animals. She saw that every chimp was unique. In many ways, chimps were like people. A mother chimpanzee guards her baby. This happens in the Gombe Forest in Tanzania. In time, Goodall saw a dark future for the Gombe chimps. Many people there are very poor. Some people cut down the chimps' forest. They wanted the land for houses. They wanted the land for crops. Some people used the trees as firewood. Hunters brought another danger. Some hunters stole baby chimps. They sold the babies to zoos. They sold them to circuses. They even sold them as pets. Most adult chimps do not make good pets in a house.

6Goodall saw what would follow if the harm continued. The Gombe chimpanzees could die out completely. She decided she had to leave Gombe. She had to leave her chimps if she wanted to save them. She began traveling the world. Important groups heard her explain the work. She requested aid so the chimps could be saved. She requested aid so their needed forests could be saved. The chimps need those forests in order to survive. She spoke to people who could change what the public believed. Her work drew attention from around the world. Gombe drew attention too. The work she started still goes on. Everything familiar was left behind when Jane set out. She went to live among another species. She wanted to learn about that species. Her first Gombe Forest trip was more than sixty years ago. The work from that forest still continues. October 1, 2025, was the day Jane Goodall died. She was ninety-one years old. People will remember her work to protect chimps. They will remember her work to preserve chimp habitats. How people see all animals was changed by her research. Her patient and caring work uncovered important new facts. She shared those facts with the world.

7In 2018, Goodall visits a center that rescues chimps. The center is in Uganda. Sadly, chimp numbers keep falling in Gombe. They keep falling across much of Africa. Work to protect the chimps still goes on. In 1977, Goodall started the Jane Goodall Institute. The institute gives money to scientists. Those scientists study chimps that live in the wild. It teaches people who live near wild places. Local people learn ways to guard the wild animals near their homes. In 1991, Goodall started Roots & Shoots. The program started in Tanzania. It began with only twelve local teens. Today the program works in more than 130 countries. It has thousands of members. Teaching young people is its main goal. Young people learn about troubles close to home. These words help you read the book. Only one continent on Earth is larger than Africa. It lies south of Europe. Apes are furry animals. Chimpanzees are apes. Gorillas are apes. Apes are close relatives of monkeys. They are close relatives of humans too. Behavior means how a person acts. It also means how an animal acts in one situation. Chimpanzees are great apes of middle size. They live in Africa. They spend time on the ground. They spend time in the trees. A field study is a science project done outside. It is not done in a laboratory. It is not done in a classroom. It happens in a natural setting. An observation is a careful watch of something as it happens. The watcher wants information. To preserve something is to care for it and keep it for the future. A scientist is a person who studies science. The study may cover one field. It may cover more than one field. A species is a group of living things. Members look alike in their bodies. They are able to reproduce.

65. The Story of Hillary Clinton

1Field Elementary School is where Hillary Rodham went. That school is in Park Ridge, Illinois. Big Dreams. Hillary Clinton was once a young girl, long before she led people in the United States. She had big dreams. The year of Hillary Rodham's birth was 1947. Chicago, Illinois, was near the place she grew up. She was the oldest child in a family of three children. She became a top student. Girl Scout awards were among her prizes. In high school, she served on the student council.

2Hillary grew up when many Americans held old ideas about jobs. They thought only men should be firefighters. They thought only men should be soldiers. They thought only men should be politicians. In most families, men were expected to earn the money. They were expected to lead the household. Women were often expected to remain at home. They were expected to look after children. Many of these ideas were beliefs of Hillary's father. He saw something else too. Hillary was very smart. He wanted her to be free to follow her dreams. Her mother said Hillary would need to be tough. She would need to stand up for herself. A family photo from the 1950s shows Hillary Rodham with a big smile. Beside her are her father, Hugh, and her mother, Dorothy. Her younger brother, Hugh Jr., is there too. Did You Know? Hillary Clinton never became an astronaut. NASA stands for National Aeronautics and Space Administration. Many women from the United States have gone to space. Sally Ride was the first U.S. woman to do it. Her space shuttle flight was in 1983. After that flight, more than forty-five other women from the United States have gone to space. Many more women have trained to be astronauts. As a girl, Hillary liked sports. She also played the piano. At age thirteen, she wrote a letter to NASA. She asked what she must do to become an astronaut. NASA sent an answer. The letter said there would be no female astronauts. That answer made Hillary even more determined to follow her dreams.

3Lawyer, Wife, and Mother. In 1965, Hillary left for college in Massachusetts. The more she learned, the more she wanted to help people. She wanted to guard them from unfair treatment. His name was Bill Clinton. Years later, voters would elect him president of the United States. At Yale, the two law students turned into friends in the early 1970s. The two started dating. She liked that her intelligence did not scare him. Both of them had large plans. Her hope was a lawyer's job in Washington, D.C. He hoped to enter politics in Arkansas, his home state. In 1974, she moved to Arkansas so she could be with Bill. They married the next year in Little Rock, Arkansas.

4In 1978, voters elected Bill Clinton governor of Arkansas. Hillary became the first lady of that state. Chelsea, their daughter, arrived in 1980. Hillary became a successful lawyer. As first lady of Arkansas, she worked hard to improve the schools. A photo shows Bill and Hillary Clinton celebrating his win in the Democratic primary. The day was June 8, 1982. The place was Little Rock, Arkansas. Later that same year, voters chose him again as governor of Arkansas. Bill Clinton was governor of Arkansas for twelve years. He sought the presidency of the United States in 1991. He won the election of 1992. His wife helped him win it. In 1993, Hillary Clinton urged Congress to pass new laws on health care. Life as First Lady. No first lady before her, in this country, had kept a career of her own. President Clinton asked her to take charge of a group. The group would try to make U.S. health care better. Congress did not like the plan. Many Americans did not like it either. Support fell short, so the plan never became a law.

5Some Americans thought Hillary Clinton spoke out too much as first lady. Even so, voters chose Bill Clinton as president again in 1996. She spent her energy fighting so women and children would have their rights. While Bill was president, the Clintons faced many hard times. They faced those times together. His time in politics was coming to an end. Her time in politics was just getting started. In 1997, she spoke about the right of women to an education. Olympic athlete Jackie Joyner-Kersee listened. Astronaut Sally Ride listened too. In 2006, Hillary Clinton greeted supporters. A second term as senator from New York was hers. Senator and Secretary of State. The year 2000 was when she ran for senator from New York. She won by a wide margin. She was the first woman to serve as a senator from that state. Terrorists attacked New York City on September 11, 2001. Senator Clinton gave her help to the city once the attacks had happened. Voters chose her again in 2006.

6The following year, she said she would run for president of the United States. Her chances looked strong. She and Barack Obama both wanted to be the Democratic nominee. The race between them was very close. Obama beat her by a small margin. He then won the presidency. She helped him do it. In 2007, Senator Barack Obama and Senator Hillary Clinton debated. Each hoped to be the Democratic candidate for president. In 2010, Secretary of State Hillary Clinton spoke at the United Nations. The speech was in New York City. President Obama chose her as his secretary of state. The job is a very important one. She was in charge of how the United States dealt with other countries. She traveled and met leaders in 112 countries. She left the job in 2013. She wanted one more chance to become president.

7Supporters crowded to a rally during her presidential run in 2016. A Woman of Many Firsts. In April 2015, she told Americans she would run for president again. She gave many speeches in places across the country. In November, Americans voted. She lost to Donald Trump. He was the candidate of the Republican Party. Across her life, Hillary Clinton has done many things that no one had done before. She did not win that election. Even so, her life and her work set a strong example. Girls and women who come after her can follow that example. Congress is the top lawmaking body in the U.S. government. The Senate is part of it. So is the House of Representatives. First lady can mean the wife of a president. It can also mean the wife of another leader. The words can also mean the leading woman in a job or activity. A lawyer gives people advice about the law. A lawyer can also speak for them in court. A nominee is someone an organization picks. The person may run for office. The person may also be chosen for an award. The secretary of state leads a department of the U.S. government. That department handles ties with other countries. A senator belongs to a group of people. The group talks about laws and makes laws for a country, a state, or another group.

66. The Track Life of Jesse Owens

1A photo shows Jesse Owens in Berlin. He sets an Olympic record in the long jump. The year is 1936. The meet is the Olympic Games. This book has six parts, in this order. The Fastest Kid in School starts on page 4. The Buckeye Bullet starts on page 6. The 1936 Olympics starts on page 8. Back in the States starts on page 12. A Champion for All starts on page 14. A word list starts on page 16. In 1921, a shy boy sat down in a classroom. He was nine years old. The school was in Cleveland, Ohio. The teacher smiled and asked for his name. He said, J. C. Owens. She heard the name wrong. She wrote Jesse Owens on the class list. James Cleveland Owens took the name Jesse from that day on. Years later, Jesse became one of the greatest track and field athletes of the twentieth century.

2The birth year of James Cleveland Owens was 1913. His father worked as a sharecropper. His grandfather had been enslaved. As a boy, Jesse picked cotton. The fields were in Alabama. His brothers worked beside him. His sisters worked there too. When Jesse was nine, the family left the South. They moved to Cleveland, Ohio. They were looking for a better life. Life in Cleveland was not what the Owens family had hoped. Jesse took jobs for part of the day. The pay helped cover family bills. He still made time for sports once school ended. Jesse ran his first race at age thirteen. He was a thin athlete. He was strong. Track was the sport he did best. He ran fast. His stride looked smooth. By his last year of high school, he had matched a world record. The race was the 100-yard dash. Jesse was now one of the best athletes in the country. Many colleges wanted that high school star. They wanted him on a track team. Jesse chose to stay near home. He went to Ohio State University. People shorten that name to OSU. The school is in Columbus. Owens had great talent. Even so, the school did not give him an athletic scholarship. African American students were barred from those scholarships in that era. The offer he got was work-study employment. He took a job for part of the day. He ran a freight elevator. The pay helped cover school costs. He also pumped gas. OSU costs were partly covered by that job. Owens met other discrimination too. The track team traveled across the country. They raced teams from other colleges. Many businesses refused African American customers. At times, Owens could not eat in a restaurant. At times a hotel would not house him with the rest of the team. He did not let this slow his running. He trained hard. At OSU he set many more records.

3One of the greatest feats Owens had came in his second year at OSU. A championship meet was set for 1935. The meet was in Michigan. A few days before it, Owens hurt his back. He could barely bend. He could barely reach his knees. His coach said he might leave the meet. Owens still wanted to race. He said he would handle one event, then the next. First, he matched the world record once more. The race was the 100-yard dash. His back felt fine after that. The long jump came next for him. He took a single jump. The jump set a new world record. It was longer by almost 6 inches. That added length is 15 cm. After twenty minutes, he lined up for the 220-yard final. He set one more record. Last, he ran the hurdles. His time was 22.6 seconds. No person before him had gone under the 23-second mark. In under an hour, he matched or broke four world records. Reporters gave those minutes a title. They called them the greatest 45 in the history of sports. A photo shows Owens while he was at OSU. He won the 100-yard dash there. He also matched the world record in that race. Owens entered the Olympic Trials during July of 1936. He earned a place on the United States team. The Olympic Games were planned for Berlin. Berlin is in Germany. At that time, Nazis controlled Germany. Adolf Hitler led Nazi Germany. He held beliefs that were racist. Those beliefs made many people angry. To the Nazis, people of color did not count as fully human. Eighteen African Americans were on the Olympic team, Owens among them. Officials in Germany mocked the United States. They jeered because black athletes had traveled with the team. Most people in Germany still cheered the athletes. Many of them asked Owens to sign his name. One photo gathers the American Olympic team in a walk. Owens stands fourth from the right. They are out walking in London, England. The year is 1936.

4A photo shows Owens winning a third gold medal. He also beats the record in the 200-meter dash. The meet is the 1936 Olympics. Hitler had wanted the world to see one thing. He wanted the German team to look like the best. Athletes from America spoiled that plan. Owens and his teammate Ralph Metcalfe took the top two places. Both runners were African American. They finished first and second in the 100-meter dash. Next, Owens beat Luz Long in the long jump. Long was from Germany. The name Luz is said LUTS. Long was tall. His hair was blond. He told Owens the win was well earned. Owens later talked about that moment. He said Long showed courage by treating him as a friend. Hitler was right there watching. Owens said the sight of their hug must have enraged Hitler. A photo shows Adolf Hitler. He was Germany's leader. He watches the Olympic Games of 1936. Another photo lines up the long jump medal winners in Berlin. From the left, the bronze medal belongs to Naoto Tajima of Japan. The gold medal belongs to Jesse Owens of America. The silver medal belongs to Luz Long of Germany. Long gives a Nazi salute. Owens had a rough start in that long jump. His early tries did not count. He had fouled. Only one jump remained. He needed it to reach the final round. Luz Long offered help. He told Owens where to begin the run. The last jump Owens took was perfect. Owens went on to win gold. Long won silver.
