The two men became close friends. Their homes were far apart. They wrote letters to stay in touch. World War II lasted from 1939 to 1945. In that war, Long had to fight. He served in Germany's army. North Africa is where Long died. The two friends did not meet after that. The last letter from Long asked Owens for a favor. Long wanted Owens to find his young son. The boy's name was Kai. Long asked Owens to tell Kai about his father. Long wanted Kai to know the years before the war. He wanted Kai to hear how men on Earth can treat one another. Owens did what Long asked. He became Kai's friend. He even stood as best man when Kai was married. Owens and Kai kept that friendship going. It had started years earlier. It started with a kind act from Long. A photo shows Luz Long beside Jesse Owens.

5Hitler felt no pleasure about those results. After the Games closed, Hitler offered no handshake. He refused every African American winner. Owens did not dwell on that snub. He went on and won two more gold medals. Owens said he had not traveled to Berlin to shake Hitler's hand. In all, the men's track team from America won eleven gold medals. African American athletes won six of them. Owens won three gold medals in events he ran or jumped alone. One more gold came in the relay known as 4 x 100. One chart celebrates the Olympic gold triumph of Jesse Owens in 1936. At those Games, Owens broke or matched nine Olympic records. He also set three world records. In Berlin in 1936, he won four gold medals altogether. One gold was for the 100m. In that race he matched his own world record. The time was 10.3 seconds. One gold was for the 200m. In that race he set a new world record. The time was 20.7 seconds. One gold was for the long jump. In that event he set a new world record. The distance on the chart is 8 meters. The chart also lists 26.5 in. One gold was for the 4 x 100m relay. In that race the team set a new world record. The time was 39.8 seconds. The American team came home from the Olympics in Berlin. Owens was greeted as a hero. New York City gave him a huge parade. A party came next at a large hotel. Owens was African American. For that reason, he could not ride the main elevator. That elevator went up to the hotel ballroom. He had to use the freight elevator. He rode it up to his own party. A photo shows fans packed along the streets. They watch a parade for his Olympic wins. The city is New York, New York. The year is 1936.

6At first, being famous did not make Owens rich. Today, a hero of the Olympics may be paid to endorse a product. In the 1930s, pay of that kind did not come. It was even rarer for an African American athlete. Owens was in a hard place. Weak grades meant he could no longer race for OSU. He was offered money to race a horse. The race was in Cuba. The pay was 2,000 dollars. He said yes. Owens won that race. He also raced cars. He raced trucks. He raced dogs. He later said those races upset him. He said the pay was at least honest work. He said he needed to eat. A photo from 1948 shows Owens in a race. A horse runs against him. The place is San Mateo, California. Owens still had trouble earning enough. He worked as a janitor in a park. Then he opened his own company for dry cleaning. Chicago became his home in 1949. He started a company for public relations. The next year, newspapers gave him a title. They picked him as the leading track star of the early twentieth century. People wanted Owens everywhere. He was put in charge of a state group. The group was the Illinois State Athletic Commission. He gave talks in many parts of the country. In the 1950s he traveled to Asia. He went there in the 1960s as well. He spoke about why sports matter. During 1973, the United States Olympic Committee added him. He worked hard to gather money. The money paid for training. It also paid for gear for athletes. After Berlin, President Franklin D. Roosevelt never sent him praise. That silence bothered Owens for the rest of his life. Years later, three presidents honored him. The honor did not come from only one. In 1965, Owens coached runners. The team was the New York Mets.

7A photo from 1975 shows Owens at home in Phoenix, Arizona. Medals and awards sit all around him. President Gerald R. Ford gave him the Presidential Medal of Freedom in 1976. In 1979, President Carter gave him the Living Legends Award. After Owens died, President George H. W. Bush honored him with the Congressional Gold Medal. As Owens grew older, he kept up his exercise. Each day, his walk covered two miles. He also swam, and he lifted weights. Owens smoked. In 1979 he died of lung cancer. He was sixty-six years old. A photo shows President Ford with Owens. Records and medals by themselves did not make him great. He used his gift for sports to stir other people. Owens said that all of us hold dreams. He said a dream turns real only through hard inner work. That work needs determination, dedication, self-discipline, and effort. Here are word meanings from this book. Athletes is a noun on page 4. It means people trained for sports, games, or other activities that need strength, speed, or skill. Determination is a noun on page 15. It means the drive to keep working toward a hard goal. Dignity is a noun on page 4. It means being worthy of respect. Discrimination is a noun on page 6. It means unfair treatment of a person or a group because of gender, race, age, religion, or some other difference. Grit is a noun on page 4. It means courage and strength to keep going when the work is hard. Olympic is an adjective on page 8. It describes an international sports contest and festival. Racist is an adjective on page 8. It describes a belief that some races rank above others. Sharecropper is a noun on page 5. It means a farmer who works land that belongs to someone else and gets part of the crop profit. Track is a noun on page 4. It means a sport made of races run on a track.

67. The Voyage of the Mayflower

1A picture shows a landing on shore. An artist of the nineteenth century imagined the scene. The picture puts the Pilgrims at Plymouth Rock. The year in the picture is 1620. The parts of this book come in this order. Looking for a New Home starts on page 4. A Hard Winter starts on page 7. Help from Nearby People starts on page 9. More English Come starts on page 12. A Closing starts on page 14. A word list starts on page 16. In September 1620, a ship left a port in England. The ship was the Mayflower. The port was Plymouth. About 132 people were on the ship. They were on their way to America. Their plan was to join the Virginia Colony. Some people left England because of faith. They were not free to follow their religion there. Earning money was the hope of the others. Every person on board hoped America would offer a better life. People today call this group the Pilgrims. The crossing took sixty-six days. The water they crossed was the Atlantic Ocean. Bad storms blew the ship away from its planned path. The Pilgrims came to land near Cape Cod. Cape Cod is coastal land in today's Massachusetts. They were about 200 miles north of the Virginia Colony. That distance is 322 km.

2Leaders had told the Pilgrims to make their home in Virginia. If they stayed in this new spot, they might break the law of England. They also knew nothing about the land around them. They chose to sail onward toward Virginia. Rock along the coast nearly sent the ship under. They turned back to Cape Cod Bay. A map traces the path of the Mayflower. Their hoped-for landing sat beside the Hudson River. That place is now inside New York. In 1620, that area belonged to the Virginia Colony. The Pilgrims signed a paper dated November 11, 1620. The paper was the Mayflower Compact. Loyalty to England's king was part of the promise. The promise showed they did not plan to break laws. The compact also set up a government. The Pilgrims agreed to heed the leaders of the colony. They agreed to obey the laws. A picture lets readers view the Mayflower Compact in copied form. The Pilgrims then chose to build where they already were. The Wampanoag Nation was already living on that land. The name sounds like wom-puh-NOH-ag. Today a visitor can go to Plimoth Plantation. It stands in Plymouth, Massachusetts. Plymouth Colony is what the site recreates.

3A map carries the title Wampanoag Territory Prior to 1620. Green on the map marks Wampanoag land. That was their territory when the English came. In December 1620, the Pilgrims picked a site for a home. The western shore of Cape Cod Bay was the site they chose. They named the place Plymouth Colony. They built on ground where an older village had stood. The Patuxet people had lived in that village. The Patuxet had cleared the ground. The ground was ready for crops. Most of the Native people who had lived there were dead. A sickness had killed them. Settlers who came from Europe had carried that sickness. The Pilgrims used the ground the Patuxet had cleared. They used corn fields the Patuxet had already planted. The Pilgrims coated the inner walls of each house with a mix. The mix held clay. It held mud. It held grasses. The mix helped keep cold air out. Air could not slip in as easily. The mix also left a smooth face on the wall. The Pilgrims then faced a bitter winter. The weather was very cold. Many of them fell sick. They took food from a Native village that stood empty. The village was close by. Even so, hunger killed many of them. More than half of the Pilgrims died. Their deaths came before spring in 1621. When the days grew warmer, one problem remained. The people still alive needed a strong harvest. Success hung on that harvest. They knew little about the land. They knew little about the plants that grew there. They did not know which crops would grow.

4Tisquantum met the Pilgrims during the spring of 1621. His name sounds like tih-SKWON-tum. He belonged to the Patuxet tribe. That name sounds like puh-TUHKS-it. His help mattered a great deal. He helped the Pilgrims stay alive. Years earlier, sailors from Spain had made him a slave. They sold him in Europe. While he was there, he learned the English language. Then he got away. He returned to his home. People had carried diseases out of Europe. The diseases moved fast. They reached Native peoples. When Tisquantum came home, his whole tribe was gone. Those diseases had killed every member. He had suffered greatly. He still chose to help the Pilgrims. He taught them how to put corn in the ground. He taught them other crops as well. He showed them places to fish. He showed them places to hunt. He worked with Native leaders who lived nearby. Together they tried to keep a battle from starting. Squash was a key crop for many Native peoples in this region. Corn was a key crop too. Beans were a key crop too. People name these three crops the Three Sisters. Farmers often set them side by side. The three plants grow well as neighbors.

5The feast likely held twice as many Wampanoag guests as Pilgrims. Help from Tisquantum brought a good harvest that year. That autumn, the Pilgrims shared a large meal. They shared it with Massasoit. His name sounds like ma-suh-SOH-it. He was a chief of the Wampanoag. His people sat at the feast too. People now look back on that meal as the first Thanksgiving. Our Thanksgiving meal is not the same as theirs. Their gathering followed an old custom. They met to honor the harvest. The gathering lasted three days. Many people eat turkey now. The food at the Pilgrim meal was likely different. It likely included goose that had been boiled. It included eel. It included lobster. It included clams. A map shows tribal lands in the year 1675. Before English settlers forced Native peoples off their land, many groups lived in this region. The groups were not all the same. Across the next fifty years, an old friendship grew weak. Pilgrim settlers and the Wampanoag were the two groups in that bond. More people from England reached America. They wanted Wampanoag land. They kept wanting still more of it.

6The English also carried more disease to America. The new diseases could kill. They spread with great speed. They killed most Native peoples in New England. Today the Wampanoag meet once each year. Other Native peoples meet with them. The day they choose is Thanksgiving Day. The place is Plymouth, Massachusetts. They do not treat the day as a party. For them, the day is set aside for mourning. They mourn their peoples. They mourn the suffering those peoples endured. A photo shows a man in prayer. He offers a prayer for healing. The event is called the National Day of Mourning. It takes place in Plymouth, Massachusetts. Another photo shows a crowd. The people stand around a statue. The statue is of Massasoit. The coming of the Pilgrims to New England was a turning point. It was an important change in history. The Pilgrims changed the land. English settlers who arrived later changed it too. They broke into the lives of Native peoples. Those peoples were already living there. At the beginning, the Wampanoag gave the Pilgrims help. Peace stood between the two groups then. A visitor today can stand where the Pilgrims came ashore. The place is in Plymouth, Massachusetts. Plymouth Rock marks that spot.

7A work of art shows a first meeting. The Pilgrims meet Samoset. Samoset was a chief among the Abenaki people. He brought the Pilgrims together with Tisquantum. He also brought them together with Massasoit. Both of those men were Wampanoag. Without the knowledge those people shared, the Pilgrims would have had a much harder time. The knowledge came from Tisquantum. It also came from the Wampanoag. Many more of the Pilgrims might have died. As years passed, diseases from England killed most Native peoples in the area. War killed many of them as well. Their story is not a simple one. English settlement in America cannot be understood without this story. Here are word meanings from this book. Colony is a noun on page 4. It means a place where people have settled. A different country rules that place. The place can also belong to that country. Crops is a noun on page 8. It means plants that farmers raise. The plants may become food. They may have other uses. Feast is a noun on page 11. It means a big meal. People share it to mark a special event. Harvest is a noun on page 8. It means how much of a crop people gather. They gather it during a season. Pilgrims is a noun on page 4. It means the settlers who began Plymouth Colony. That colony was in New England. The year was 1620. They wanted freedom to practice their religion. Survival is a noun on page 9. It means the condition of being alive. It can mean staying alive. It can mean continuing to exist.

68. Tillie Spends a Day at the Fire Station

1My name is Tillie. I am in third grade. My uncle is a firefighter. The fire station is where I visit him today. Captain Chuck Williams shakes my hand. Firefighting has been his job for 31 years. Uncle Jason waits at the door. He greets me with a giant hug. The other firefighters meet me through Uncle Jason. The station looks like a house. One thing is different. Shiny red fire trucks park in the garage. Shifts are how the firefighters work. One shift lasts twenty-four hours. Sleep is possible for Uncle Jason if no emergency call comes. On a busy shift he may stay awake all night. He would fight fires. He would help people.

2Uncle Jason shows me around the station. The station has a kitchen, just as my house does. This kitchen has three refrigerators. Food for each shift sits in its own refrigerator. Each shift locks that refrigerator. Then other firefighters will not eat the food. I think the locks are kind of hilarious. The kitchen has plenty of ice cream. Black cherry is the favorite flavor. The firefighters act like a family at meal time. They shop for food together. They cook dinner together. Tonight they will eat salad. The salad has chicken strips. They take turns washing the dishes. They take turns keeping the station tidy. Right now the crew is busy with chores. They tinker with the communication radios. They work at the kitchen table. The radios must work properly. An emergency call may come through the radios. It may come through the station speaker. The crew needs to hear that call loud and clear.

3Uncle Jason's bedroom comes next. The room has a television. It has a couch. It has a bed. Fresh sheets go on the bed once he is done sleeping. The next firefighter will need a clean bed. Then he shows me the day room. Firefighters relax there after the chores are finished. I climb into a big comfy chair. I watch television with the crew. Sometimes the crew plays video games. Most of the time they are busy helping people. They also make sure the equipment is ready.

4At last we reach the most exciting room. It is the garage. Two fire trucks wait there. They are ready for any emergency. The water truck is usually Uncle Jason's to drive. The truck is bright red. A gigantic water tank rides on it. Two thousand gallons of water fit in the tank. That much water could fill forty-eight bathtubs. One thousand gallons can pour out in a single minute. They use the truck's water when no fire hydrant is close. Hooking a fire hose to a hydrant is only step one. From the hydrant, water flows into the truck. Pumps on the truck control the pressure of the water. The water leaves the truck through a second hose. Its work can start at that moment. The truck also carries the tools the crew needs. Medical supplies are on the truck. A snake box is on the truck too. We live in the desert. People call the station about snakes in their yards. If a snake is venomous, the crew catches it. They use a metal pole. The pole is called a snake grabber. They lock the snake safely in the box. Later they let the snake go. They choose a place far from houses. They also stay far from businesses. A rattlesnake is the snake Uncle Jason lets me see. It gives me the creeps. It is also cool.

5The firefighters show me the clothes they wear in a fire. I get to put the gear on. There is a special coat. There are special pants. There is a helmet. There is an oxygen tank. It feels like a winter coat worn in summer. The gear is heavy. The gear is hot. Then I sit in the fire truck. A cord is there for me to pull. The air horn blasts. The garage holds more than fire trucks. Exercise equipment stands there too. I ask what the equipment is for. My uncle says the crew likes to stay fit. Carrying a person out of a burning house takes a lot of work. Uncle Jason gives me a warning about a fire at home. I must never hide under my bed. I must never hide behind my curtains. Hiding there would make me hard to find. While I sit in the truck, he lets me try the headphones.

6Suddenly a call comes in. The call is a fire. Gear is grabbed by the firefighters. They race off with sirens blaring. The station is where I stay. My dad stays with me. The crew goes to fight the fire. They come back exhausted. They come back dirty. Uncle Jason tells me the whole story. The man had been fixing his car. He was in his garage. A fire started there. He was lucky. He escaped to the sidewalk. His wife escaped too. His daughter escaped too. Their poodle, Fluffy, was stuck inside. Hoses came out in the hands of the other firefighters. They went to work on the flames. Uncle Jason searched the house. Fluffy was under a bed, and Uncle Jason found the dog there. He also found a fishbowl. He found two parakeets. The birds were in a cage. The day was saved by Uncle Jason and the rest of the crew. They rescued the pets. They put the fire out. The car was destroyed. Everything else in the house stayed safe. Back at the station, the crew stows the gear. They get the trucks ready in case another call comes.

7Putting out fires is the work everyone knows them for. Firefighters also help in many other ways. They come when a person is sick. They come when a person is hurt and must be rushed to the hospital. A car crash is another common call. They may come if a hiker has a bad fall. They may come if a swarm of bees stings someone. Their help each day takes many different forms. Uncle Jason says one part of the job is the toughest. He is away from his wife. He is away from his son. His son is four years old. He misses them while he works. Helping people is still the part of firefighting he loves. Home is calling me for the evening. Dinner is waiting. The firefighters start cooking their own dinner too. They are like one big family. These words help you read the story. An emergency is a sudden problem that is very serious. It needs action right away. Equipment means the tools used for work or for play. A firefighter is a person whose job is putting out fires. Rescued means saved from danger. A shift is a planned block of work time. During a shift, a group works together. The word can also mean the workers on duty in that time. A tour is a walk through a place. You take the walk to learn about the place.

69. Tiny Worlds Under the Lens

1A credit card carries a raised number. Magnify it. Tiny imperfections appear. The book climbs from barely visible things to stronger lenses. Simple microscopes from the start come first. Compound microscopes come next. A 20 to 30 times view comes next. A 50 to 200 times view comes next. A 200 to 1,500 times view comes next. A one-million-times view comes next. A closing follows. Word meanings come last. Make yourself tiny, and a green blowfly would look unlike itself. A human hair is tiny enough to test the eye. It sits among the smallest things sight can catch. Imagine a shrink so deep that the hair seems arm-wide. What appears then? Leg hairs on a fly would seem like giant spikes. The fly would seem like a monster. Giant eyes would stare. A microscope offers that same kind of close look.

2A closer look at the world has always been a human wish. Ancient Greeks filled glass balls with water. Objects then looked bigger. Romans noticed a useful glass shape. Thick middle and thin edges made things look larger. That shape was the earliest magnifying glass. Europe in the 1600s learned to form better lenses from glass. The microscope grew from that skill. At the beginning it was only a fancy magnifying glass. Five times bigger is a common power for a magnifying glass. Ten times bigger is also common. First simple microscopes shared that power. One simple microscope from 1780 folded away. Its case was pocket size. A modern compound microscope has named parts. A body tube is one. An eyepiece lens is one. A focus knob is one. Objective lenses are others. Three objective lenses of different powers sit on one common model. A stage is part of it. A mirror is part of it. A base is part of it. Early users lined up a pair of magnifying glasses. Lens two made the view from lens one even bigger. The view grew again. One instrument with both lenses became the compound microscope. Today's compound microscope can enlarge an object as much as 1,500 times.

3Life at 20 to 30 times size starts to show hidden detail. Robert Hooke used a microscope early. Careful notes were his habit. He was among the first to keep them. A book held the notes and many drawings. A louse appears up close in that book. A flea appears up close too. Fly eyes in his drawing showed detail no one had drawn before. The flea drawing sits in the 1665 book. Plants and seeds were sketched as well. Cork went under the lens. Tiny rooms made up the cork. Cells was the name he gave those rooms. Later the word widened. It came to mean the tiny blocks inside all living things. A thin cork slice, magnified, shows the rooms. Life at 50 to 200 times size reveals still smaller things. Historians believe Hooke's book pushed Antonie van Leeuwenhoek further. LEY-vunhook is how to say the name. Merchant was his work, not science. Fabric that he bought and sold got a close look through magnifying glasses. Hooke's drawings sent him into lens making of his own.

4Pond water hides tiny living things. A microscope can uncover them. Beach sand is a mix of many bits. A lens can sort those bits. Van Leeuwenhoek found a path to better lenses. His microscopes enlarged objects past 200 times. Lake water, under those lenses, held tiny life. A naked eye cannot see that life. Nobody before him knew it was there. Soon microscopes showed tiny life in every kind of place. At 200 times, things once invisible can be seen. Sand is a simple case. Normal sand from a beach looks plain and ordinary. The lens tells a different story. Tiny shells are in it. Rocks are in it. Coral is in it. Early microscopes could warp a picture. A fun-house mirror warps in a similar way. From 200 times up to 1,500 times, early compound microscopes still failed in places. Blur was one failure. Stretch was another. Edge colors shifted. Real detail and lens error were hard to tell apart.

5Louis Pasteur worked as a chemist in France. Say the name pass-TUR. Heat, he found, could make milk safer. Tiny life in the milk could sicken people. Heat could kill that life. Early in the 1800s, a new lens arrived. The person who invented it was Joseph Jackson Lister. Stretching disappeared with that lens. Color changes disappeared too. Power rose. Trust in the image rose. Science gained many discoveries from the change. Medicine gained many too. Mid-century in the 1800s, Pasteur tied spoiled milk to tiny life in the air. Doctors, with the better microscope, could see germs in people who were sick. Medicine changed completely because of that. Snowflake Bentley is the nickname of a man who photographed snowflakes. A camera sat on his microscope. The sharper compound microscope led to a strong conclusion. Each snowflake differs from every other snowflake. Wilson Bentley earned the snowflake nickname through this work. His photos belong to the late 1800s and the early 1900s. The count of snowflake photos passed five thousand. Crystals showed up under microscopes as well. Some natural things grow in those special shapes. Salt crystals are cubes. Building blocks are a fair comparison. Table sugar grows crystals too. Each sugar crystal slants at the ends. Sugar crystals run longer than salt crystals. Their regular form is rectangular.

6Tiny detail can explain how ordinary things work. Burrs offer a clear case. George de Mestral walked a field. Burrs clung to his clothing afterward. A microscope showed him the reason. Tiny hooks covered the burrs. Loops in his pant fabric were what the hooks grabbed. He set out to copy the design. Fabric was his material. Velcro is the cloth that resulted. One picture shows the natural hooks on a burr. Another shows Velcro hooks beside Velcro loops. Two fabric pieces make Velcro. Thousands of tiny hooks cover one piece. Thousands of tiny loops cover the other. Hooks catch loops. The pieces then stay together. A dust mite is a tiny eater. Flakes of human skin are its food. Light sets a ceiling on the compound microscope. Scientists answered with microscopes that skip light. Electrons are the energy one kind uses. No other microscope magnifies as far. The reach is one million times. The figure is 1,000,000 times.

7A fish scale can be seen up close through a microscope. Greater power has taught people many new facts about the world. Nearly every area of science has gained discoveries this way. Clear cells are hard to notice. Other tiny clear objects are hard to notice too. Stains and dyes are the fix. An object then stands apart from its background. Seeing it gets easier. A sick human liver, stained, looks different under the lens. Orange marks the healthy cells. Purple marks the damaged cells. Damaged cells are replacing healthy ones. Word meanings come next. Compound describes a thing built from two or more parts. Invisible describes what cannot be seen. Lenses are curved glass or curved plastic. They focus light for a bigger view or a clearer one. Crystals are solids. Atoms inside them sit in tight, regular patterns. A detail is a small piece of a larger thing. It can be one exact bit of information. Magnified means something was caused to look larger than its real size. A magnifying glass is often round. A handle is common. People use it when they want a larger look. Electrons are atom particles. They move around the nucleus. Their electrical charge is negative. Fabric means cloth. Weaving is the usual way it is made. A microscope lets people view objects that are tiny. Germs are tiny life that can bring sickness. Reliable means the result stays steady. You can depend on it.

70. Tools That Make Music

1Music has likely mattered since the earliest people lived. They shaped instruments from things they used every day. A person may have found music by chance. A strike on a tree trunk can make a tone. A strike on a rock can make a tone. Wind can sing as it passes through a shell. It can sing through a reed. It can sing through a piece of bamboo. One picture shows a frame drum with a mallet.

2Music still matters to us now. We can make it with our voices. We can make it with instruments. We can move our bodies to the sound. We can simply listen. Music adds pleasure to a day. It adds joy too. Calm, quiet music can settle a person. Loud music can stir a person. It can also give energy. People can create a sound for any mood. They can find a sound for any mood. A photo shows a taiko drummer in Japan. Music comes in many types. Instruments come in many types too. Each instrument has its own character. Each one makes its own sound. Each one sets its own mood. A sound may be soft. A sound may be loud. It may be gentle. It may be harsh. It may be wild. It may be soothing. A saxophone is one instrument in the pictures.

3Shape changes the sound an instrument makes. Design changes the sound. Size changes the sound. The stuff used to build it changes the sound. The way a person plays it changes the sound too. The number of instrument kinds is amazing. Each kind can please the player. Each kind can please the listener. A mandolin is made from wood. A gong is made from metal. A tambourine uses skin. An orchestra gathers many of these instruments in one place. It is a group of musicians. They play set instruments together. Most big cities have an orchestra. Many smaller cities have one too. The music they usually play is called classical music. One picture shows a conductor leading an orchestra.

4Strings make up the largest section of an orchestra. This section is a family of instruments. A player draws a bow across the strings. These instruments all make a similar kind of sound. Smaller ones sound higher. Bigger ones sound deeper. Violins sit in this family. Violas sit in it. Cellos sit in it. Double basses sit in it. A large orchestra can place over seventy musicians here. Pictures show a violin, a cello, and a double bass. One more string instrument is not bowed. The player plucks it. That instrument is the harp. The harp is quieter than many orchestra neighbors. It usually plays while few others play. Then listeners can hear it. Sometimes the harp plays solo. Solo means it plays alone.

5Woodwinds form another important family in an orchestra. Flutes belong to this family. Oboes belong to it. Clarinets belong to it. Bassoons belong to it. Other relatives may join an orchestra too. The piccolo is a smaller cousin of the flute. The English horn is a larger cousin. The bass clarinet is a larger cousin. The double bassoon is a larger cousin. A picture shows a flute. A player blows air into a woodwind to make sound. Many holes sit along the tube. Covering a hole makes one note. Opening a hole makes another note. Sometimes fingers cover the holes. Sometimes the player presses keys. The keys cover the holes. A clarinet appears in a picture too.

6Brass is another section of the orchestra. Trumpets belong here. Horns belong here. Trombones belong here. Tubas belong here. These rank among the strongest voices in the group. A picture shows a French horn. Brass players blow into their instruments, as woodwind players do. A trumpet has valves. A horn has valves. A tuba has valves. The player pushes valves to change the note. A trombone has a metal tube bent like the letter U. It also has a slide. A new note comes when the player shifts the slide. Percussion is another key part of an orchestra. Players strike these instruments. They hit them in some way. Some are made of skin. Some are made of metal. Some are made of wood. Certain percussion makes musical notes. A xylophone is one of these. Other percussion makes sound but no notes. Castanets do this. Most drums do this. Percussion adds bangs to the orchestra. It adds splashes too. A picture shows a kettledrum.

7The piano is a keyboard instrument. The organ is a keyboard instrument. A closed case hides what is inside. Strings inside a piano vibrate. An organ uses wind to make its sound. These instruments carry many keys. Few other instruments can match how many notes these keys can sound. A picture shows a baby grand piano. A band is a different kind of music group. A marching band uses brass. It uses woodwinds. It uses percussion. It leaves out strings. Other kinds of bands are common too. A guitar, or several, often joins them. A guitar has strings. Guitars matter to many bands. The violin family matters to an orchestra in a similar way. Amplifiers often make band guitars louder. A picture shows an electric guitar.

8A rock band may use electric guitars. It may use bass guitars. It may use drums. It may use an electronic keyboard. Other instruments may join these. A jazz band may use many of those same tools. It may add woodwinds. It may add brass. Latin bands often add more percussion. The marimba is one added instrument. A marimba is related to a xylophone. A picture shows a marimba. Bluegrass bands may use guitars. Country bands may use them too. They may use banjos. They may use mandolins. They may use autoharps. Spoons can be part of their percussion. Washboards can be part of it. Bucket basses can be part of it. These bands also use fiddles. A fiddle is a violin played in a fresh style. That style is very unlike orchestra playing. A picture shows a five-string banjo.

9A kalimba is also called a thumb piano. This one comes from Africa. Peoples around the world play instruments unlike the ones named so far. In any land, most instruments answer to four main actions. One way is blowing. One way is scraping. One way is plucking. One way is hammering. Later pages show an example of each way. Blown instruments include the woodwinds. Each one forces air down a tube. Photos show blown instruments from many lands. One is a Native American flute. One is the Scottish bagpipes from Scotland. One is the panpipes. Panpipes are found in Greece. They are found in Latin America. They are found in the Far East.

10The violin family is a set of scraped instruments. Players often use a bow. Sound starts when the bow scrapes the strings. Many scraped instruments make music in other ways. Two unusual ones are shown. The gusle comes from Eastern Europe. The hurdy-gurdy comes from Europe. Plucked instruments have strings. Fingers can pluck those strings. Other objects can pluck them. A few plucked instruments from many lands are shown. The lute comes from the Middle East. It also comes from Europe. The koto comes from Japan. The sitar comes from India.

11Hammered instruments include drums. They include other percussion. Sound starts when something hits them. Peoples of the world have many kinds. A few appear here. One is a drum from Africa. One is the shekere, a beaded gourd shaker, also from Africa. One is castanets from Spain. In many ways, a person's voice can work like an instrument. It may be the most flexible of all. A song from it may include words. A song from it may leave words out. Many different sounds can come from it. It costs nothing. A person can use it in almost any place. A picture shows a barbershop quartet.

12Music brings great joy into life. It helps people show how they feel. It fills the world with beauty. You might sing. You might dance. You might play an instrument. You might sit and listen. Keep music as part of your life. A picture shows a saxophone player. An amplifier is electronic gear that makes an instrument louder. A bow is a bendable rod. Hairs from a horse stretch from one end of the rod to the other. Players use a bow on the violin family. Brass names a set of metal instruments. Trumpets are in that set. Trombones are in it. Horns are in it. Tubas are in it. Keyboard can name a set of instruments. The piano is in that set. The organ is in it. The harpsichord is in it. Keyboard can also name the rows of keys on these instruments. Percussion names instruments played by hammering or banging. A drum is one of them. String describes instruments that have strings and use a bow. Violins fit this group. Violas fit it. Cellos fit it. Double basses fit it. Woodwinds name a set that holds flutes. Clarinets are in that set. Oboes are in it. Bassoons are in it.

71. Training with Empty Hands

1Karateka means a person who practices karate. Karate can build amazing strength. Karate can build speed that is lightning-quick. Karate can build discipline. Karate can build respect. People gain these things by practicing karate. Karate is one kind of martial art. Practicing people use their hands. They use their feet. They use their forearms. They use their elbows. They use their knees. Karate uses no weapons. The word karate comes from Japanese. In Japanese it means empty hand. A karateka trains with an empty hand. Strength and lightning-quick speed can grow from the art. Discipline and respect can grow too. No weapon is part of karate. Empty hand is the meaning of the Japanese word. A karateka keeps training in this art. Strength can come from the practice. Speed from the practice can be lightning-quick. Discipline can come from the practice. Respect can come from the practice. Strikes use the body alone. Weapons stay out. Empty hand is the idea in the name.

2No one knows for sure how karate began. Many people still share a belief about it. They place the start in the 1600s. They place the start on Okinawa. Say Okinawa as oh-kee-NAH-wah. Okinawa is a tiny island. The island sits between China and Japan. China and Japan often fought over the island. Japan took control of the island. The people living there lost a right. They could no longer have weapons. They made karate so they could defend themselves. Karate reached Japan in the 1920s. Few martial arts draw more people around the world today. Men were practicing karate in 1938. The place of that practice was Okinawa. Today the island is part of Japan. Students train at a dojo. A dojo is a gym. A student bows the head when first entering. The bow shows respect. Shoes come off on the mat. Bare feet are another sign of respect. Bare feet are cleaner. Bare feet are safer for some moves. The teacher in a dojo is a sensei. A sensei holds a great deal of knowledge. That knowledge is passed to students. Students learn respect for the sensei. Students learn respect for classmates. Students learn respect for dojo rules. A sensei helps students practice moves. The practice happens inside the dojo. The 1600s on Okinawa are the believed start. A weapon ban under Japan led people to invent karate. The 1920s brought karate to Japan. A 1938 practice took place on Okinawa, now part of Japan.

3A new belt comes when the sensei says the student is ready. Training starts only in the right clothes. Every karate student wears a special robe. The robe is called a gi. A colored belt goes around the waist. The belt is worn on the gi. More training raises a student's standing. Belt color shows that standing. A new belt is not only a set of new moves. Karate is much more than a sport of the body. Students grow in mind. Students grow in spirit. Students grow in body. Students learn discipline. Students learn respect. Students learn patience. These skills can be used in other parts of life. Good habits matter as much as the moves. A sensei watches for those good habits. Good habits can earn the next belt. The gi and the belt are the clothes of class. Mind, spirit, and body all get training. Patience sits with discipline and respect. Life outside the dojo can use those same skills.

4New belt colors come in a set order. An old story gives each color a meaning. The story follows a plant as it grows. Belt colors stand for the plant. Belt colors stand for the Sun. Belt colors stand for the sky. A young plant is a picture of how a student grows. Time lets a karate student mature. Time also brings more knowledge. A beginner starts with a white belt. Yellow comes next. Orange comes after yellow. The student moves through each color. The black belt comes at the end of that line. Learning karate is a long journey. The journey is exciting. Great discipline is required. Three main steps lead to mastery. People call the steps the three Ks. The steps are kihon, kata, and kumite. Kihon means basics. Kata means form. Kumite means meeting of hands. All three words are Japanese. White is the first belt. Yellow and orange follow white. Black is the last belt in the color line. A young plant is the picture of a growing student.

5The kihon set holds the basic moves. Beginners must learn kihon first. Students learn correct breathing. Students build a strong posture. Students learn kicks. Students learn punches. Students learn stances. Students learn blocks. Students learn thrusts. Each skill is learned by itself. Later the moves are joined. Patience is the most important lesson. Students repeat the moves many times. They stop only when the moves are perfect. The next step waits until then. Kihon keeps the lesson to one move at a time. Many different katas can be learned. Kata comes after kihon. A kata uses the moves from kihon. It sets those moves into a routine. Students picture attackers. They picture fighting the attackers off. Kata moves are used for defense. A kata often joins several kinds of moves. Punches are in the mix. Kicks are in the mix. Blocks are in the mix. Turns are in the mix. Stances are in the mix. Students also practice flow. Flow is the link that joins one move to another. Power matters in kata. Speed matters in kata. Balance matters in kata. Timing matters in kata. Attention matters in kata. Breathing matters in kata.

6Many students also practice bunkai. They practice it while they learn kata. In bunkai, a kata is taken apart. Students learn why each move is useful. Bunkai teaches them to pay attention. They notice what they are doing. They are asked to do more than copy the moves. They slow down. They think about each move. Bunkai also turns their thoughts to an opponent. They think about what the opponent will do. One use of kumite is sport. Kumite is also self-defense training. Students reach kumite after kata is strong. Hard work and discipline get them there. In this last step they use all they have learned. Students work in pairs. They practice moves on an opponent. Kumite is the closest step to a real fight. Safety comes first. Proper form comes first. Breaking a kata apart is the bunkai task. Usefulness of each move is the bunkai lesson. Slow thinking replaces empty copying. A paired opponent makes kumite feel close to a fight. Safety and proper form stay ahead of winning.

7For centuries people have learned through karate. The learners are students. The learners are adults. They live all around the world. They learn discipline. They learn respect. They learn self-improvement. They work hard so they can progress. They work hard so they can earn new belts. The skills can remain. They remain long after a person leaves the dojo. Some students may become karate masters. Every student can have fun while trying. Many people call karate a lifelong journey. The journey is one of improvement. A further lesson is always waiting. Defend means to protect oneself. The protection is from harm. The protection is from attack. Discipline is an ability. It means keeping on toward a difficult goal. A dojo is a school for training. The training is in Japanese martial arts. Karate is a method of self-defense. Hands do most of the striking. Feet do most of the striking too. The strike is aimed at an opponent. A martial art is one sport among several. Students practice fighting. Students practice self-defense. Patience is an ability to wait. Patience is also dealing with challenges. A patient person does this without getting upset. Respect is an understanding about honor. Honor is the right way to treat a person or a thing. The treatment should also be careful. Self-improvement means doing better than before. A sensei is a teacher. A sensei can be a leader. The role is often in Japanese martial arts.

72. What Fossils Show About Dinosaurs

1Displays hold many dinosaur fossils. Natural history museums are where you can see them. What shows that dinosaurs lived on Earth? Scientists called paleontologists find the clues. They discover fossils. The fossils sit in rocks. Those rocks are found all over the world. Study of the fossils teaches them about dinosaurs. One lesson is what dinosaurs looked like. Another lesson is where dinosaurs lived. Another lesson is what dinosaurs ate. A museum fossil began as a find in rock. Paleontologists search the world for those rocks. Looks are one fact a fossil can give. Home is another fact a fossil can give. Food is another fact a fossil can give. That search is how we know dinosaurs lived here. Museums then put many of the fossils on display. Visitors can see what the scientists found. Rocks from many places hold dinosaur fossils. Paleontologists are the people who study them. Shape, home, and food are the lessons. Each new fossil can add one of those lessons. The display in a natural history museum shares the find.

2A bone fossil starts to form after a dinosaur dies. Layers cover the remains as time passes. A layer can be mud. A layer can be sand. A layer can be dirt. Cover protects the bones from animals. Cover also protects them from rain. Other weather is blocked too. The bones stay underground. Discovery may come millions of years later. Sometimes the bones have turned to stone. Sometimes hard mud surrounds the bones. In that case the bones themselves have decayed. Paleontologists can study the empty spaces. The bones once filled those spaces. They can build models of the bones. Each model follows the shape left in the space. Many kinds of dinosaur fossils have been found. Bones are one kind. Footprints are one kind. Teeth are one kind. Nests are one kind. Eggs are one kind. A picture may show a tooth, bones, and eggs. Paleontologists dig a leg bone fossil out of the ground. They dig bone fossils with great care. They record information about each fossil. Then they wrap the fossils. The wrap keeps the fossils safe. Next they carry the fossils to a museum. At the museum they examine the fossils. They study shape. They study color. They think about the place of the find. They think about the depth of the find.

3Paleontologists compare a new find with other fossils. They try to name the kind of dinosaur. Sometimes bone fossils can be fitted together. The fitted bones can make a whole skeleton. A whole skeleton is rarely found in one place. Putting one together later is also rare. Hundreds of bones were usual in a dinosaur body. Finding every bone is hard. Other dinosaur skeletons become the models for scientists. The models guide the skeleton they are building. Triceratops bone fossils get fitted by a scientist. A triceratops skeleton can be set beside a horse skeleton. Some shapes match between the two skeletons. Other kinds of animal skeletons help too. Paleontologists use them to learn about dinosaur skeletons. Dinosaurs are extinct. Many animals alive today still share some traits with them. Some dinosaur bones are bigger than bones of living animals. The shape of certain bones can still be related. The way those bones fit can be related too. Hundreds of bones make a full set hard to find. A model skeleton fills gaps in the build. Triceratops and the horse share some skeleton shapes. Extinct dinosaurs can still be compared with living animals. Bigger size does not erase a related shape. A related fit of bones can remain too.

4Many kinds of dinosaurs once roamed Earth. Fossils help paleontologists learn about movement. Some dinosaurs walked on two legs. Some dinosaurs walked on four legs. Some dinosaurs used wings to fly. A picture can show a walking dinosaur. A picture can show a flying dinosaur. Moving dinosaurs left tracks in mud. Paleontologists study those fossil footprints. A print can show that a dinosaur moved fast. A print can show that a dinosaur moved slowly. Footprints also give clues about size. The clues describe the dinosaur that left the tracks. In China, people uncovered a big group of footprints left by dinosaurs. Two legs were enough for some walkers. Four legs carried others. Wings carried the fliers. Mud saved the tracks as fossils. Speed is one clue in a footprint. Size is another clue in a footprint. China is the place of that large track group. Paleontologists read movement from the prints. A walking dinosaur and a flying dinosaur moved in different ways. The tracks still show how the walker went. Fast or slow can be told from the space of the prints. The size of the print hints at the size of the dinosaur.

5Fossil teeth tell paleontologists what dinosaurs ate. Many dinosaurs grew new teeth. New teeth replaced teeth that wore down. New teeth also replaced lost teeth. Razor-sharp teeth were common on carnivores. Those teeth helped cut meat. The teeth were strong enough to crush bone. Herbivores usually had sharp beaks. The beaks gathered plants. Some herbivores also had flat teeth. Flat teeth ground tougher plant parts. Other herbivores had sharper teeth. Those sharper teeth chopped food. Still other dinosaurs had both tooth shapes. They had flat teeth. They had sharp teeth. The teeth in one picture belonged to a carnivore. Some of those teeth match a human hand in size. Nests and eggs teach about dinosaur babies. Dinosaur eggs had many shapes. They had many sizes. Some eggs were small and round. Some eggs were large and oval. Over twenty eggs could come at one time from some dinosaurs. Other dinosaurs laid only a few eggs. Sitting on a nest was possible for some dinosaurs. They may have stayed until the eggs hatched. Most birds sit on a nest in this way. One fossil nest holds large dinosaur eggs. Those eggs have turned into fossils.

6Dinosaur fossils share their rocks with fossils of other kinds. Plant fossils are among them. Fish fossils are among them. Insect fossils are among them. Fossils of other animals are among them. A picture may show a frog. A picture may show a fish. A picture may show an insect. These fossils show what Earth was like. The time was when dinosaurs lived here. More of these other fossils have been found. Fewer dinosaur fossils have been found. The other fossils are usually smaller. They are usually more complete. Paleontologists can tell their age more easily. They use that age to estimate dinosaur fossil age. A picture can label fossils newer, older, and oldest. A deeper fossil is an older fossil. Tests can find the age of a fossil. Older fossils lie deeper than newer fossils. More recent fossils lie in rocks nearer the surface. Around 230 million years ago, the oldest dinosaurs were alive. Those dinosaurs were only as big as large dogs. Giant dinosaurs came later. The giant time was around 145 million years ago. Then giant dinosaurs roamed Earth.

7Paleontologists hold many different ideas. The ideas try to explain a disappearance. Around 65 million years ago, the dinosaurs were gone. Fossil study gives them clues. The clues help them understand what happened. Footprints of a large dinosaur were found in Germany. That dinosaur had three toes. Decayed means broken down slowly. It can mean decomposed by natural processes. Dinosaurs were large groups of reptiles. They roamed Earth until they died out. Their extinction came over 65 million years ago. Extinct means no longer living. Extinct means completely wiped out. Fossils are remains of plants. Fossils are remains of animals. The remains turned to stone over time. A museum is a building for keeping important things. A museum is also for showing those things. The things can matter to history. The things can matter to science. The things can matter to art. Paleontologists study plant fossils. They study animal fossils. Around 65 million years ago is the time of the disappearance. The German prints came from a large three-toed dinosaur. Stone remains are what people call fossils. Over 65 million years ago is when dinosaurs became extinct. A museum keeps history, science, and art on display.

73. Where a Flush Goes

1Swoosh! You hear that sound each time a toilet is flushed. Have you ever wondered where the things you flush end up? The job of removing waste is often complex. In a city or town, the process can stretch across many miles. It can also run underneath those miles. How a Flush Works. Step 1 is the flush. The valve opens. Clean water leaves the tank and enters the bowl. Dirty water goes down the trap. The labeled parts are the tank, the valve, the bowl, and the trap. At step 2, the tank is empty, so the valve shuts. The tank and the bowl start to fill again. Step 3 shows both the tank and the bowl full once more.

2What Is in Home Water Use? Across the United States, a survey in 2016 checked 23,749 homes. Toilets accounted for 24 percent. Showers accounted for 20 percent. Faucets accounted for 19 percent. Clothes washers accounted for 17 percent. Leaks accounted for 12 percent. Other uses accounted for 4 percent. Baths accounted for 3 percent. Dishwashers accounted for 1 percent. Where Waste Starts and Where It Goes. Human waste is called sewage. In most homes, the toilet helps make sewage. The sink does too. So does anything else that makes water dirty. If that sewage reached the water we drink, the water would not be safe. For more than two billion people worldwide, waste piles up on the land. Or the waste runs into lakes and rivers. Millions of people die each year. They are sick from drinking water that sewage has made unsafe. Experts in sewage treatment are working hard on this problem. The job is huge. In our homes, the pipes do not dump sewage into drinking water. In many cities and towns, the dirty water goes to the local sewer instead. A sign tells people to stay away from a river that is polluted. Two Systems under the Ground. One set of pipes carries household sewage from toilets, sinks, and so on. A catch basin collects water. A sewer pipe carries waste. A storm sewer pipe carries rain. Waste rides sewer pipes until it reaches the plant that treats sewage. Rainwater also reaches the plant through some storm pipes. Other storm pipes send rain straight into creeks. They also send it to ponds and other waterways.

3Some sewer pipes are wide enough for a person to stand inside. Inside the Treatment Plant. Small sewer pipes join and become larger pipes. Those larger pipes join and become larger still. Sewage travels through them to a sewage treatment plant. Workers clean the water there. Anything in the water that could make a person sick is taken out. Treatment systems are not all the same. One common system cleans sewage in these steps. Step 1 uses a big screen. Picture a giant strainer from a kitchen. The screen pulls out rocks, sticks, and garbage. Then those things cannot jam the machines. Step 2 leaves a mix called sludge. Sludge is water that is thick and dirty. Bacteria are helped to grow in the sludge at the plant. The bacteria feed on the sludge. Feeding on it breaks the sludge down. Water then flows into a clarifier tank. That tank is step 3.

4The Water Cleaning Steps. These are the basic steps that clean the water. Tanks at many city plants are enormous. One plant in San Francisco, California, handles 60 million gallons in a day. That amount is 227 million L. A rainy day raises the load to 250 million gallons. That amount is 946 million L. Step 3 sends the broken-down sludge to another machine. In that machine, solid waste sinks to the bottom. Water at the top goes to a filter. The filter cleans the water. Once the water is safe, it may go to a stream. It may go to a lake. It may go to the sea. In step 4, solids go into a huge tank. The tank is called a digester. A second kind of bacteria feeds on much of the sludge there. The digester breaks the solid sludge down once more. It also pulls more water out of the sludge. About half of the sludge changes into gas. Some of that gas helps run the machines at the plant. Drying is what happens to the remaining half of the sludge. Farmers often spread it on fields to help crops grow. Reclaimed water is used on a golf course. The course sits in the desert southwest. Using sewer water again saves fresh water that is precious. A single day on that desert course can demand a huge share of water. A family of four uses that same amount over five years.

5A Septic Tank at Home. In the drawing, liquid is filtered in a yard or a field. It shows a septic tank. It shows groundwater. The tank sends liquid out to the soil. The soil filters that liquid. Solids remain in the tank. Many country homes sit far apart. A pipe system big enough to link homes so far apart would not make much sense. Each of those homes can send human waste to its own septic tank. The tank is a small treatment system for one house only. The burial spot is the yard close to the house. Waste from the house runs into the tank. Inside, solids fall to the bottom. Liquid leaves through pipes under the ground. The liquid spreads into the yard and into the soil. The soil cleans the liquid while the liquid passes through. Harmful things are pulled out before they can reach drinking water. All the solid sludge stays in the tank. Over time, the sludge builds up. When the tank holds too much sludge, a septic business has to pump it out. A gurgling toilet can mean it is time for that pumping. A slow drain can mean the same thing.

6Waste in Earlier Times. Do these systems sound yucky? Think again. People built the first sewerage systems thousands of years ago. Some of those places were in the Middle East. The early systems moved human waste away from cities. The waste carried harmful bacteria. It went straight into water nearby. That water made people sick. An old sewer canal can still be seen in Greece today. Even in the 1800s, big cities struggled with waste. New York was one of them. Houses had no toilets. Sometimes a home had only a hole in the ground. There was no flush. Waste piled up in the hole. Some people let the waste spill. It washed into the streets. It collected where people walked. It collected where children played. The smell was terrible. Before modern pipes, many people used an outhouse like the one in the picture. Some outhouses had two floors. Some had two seats.

7Maryland in 1915 is the setting of a picture of people building a sewerage system. Experts came to see a link. Streets full of waste were making people ill. New York City started to build sewerage systems. The waste would have a place to go. Close to 850 miles of sewer lines were in place by 1914. That length is 1,368 km. Toilets and sinks in the United States today make waste seem to vanish. It can look like magic. You now know the real path it takes. The next time you flush, pause for a moment. Be glad for the modern sewerage system. Bacteria are tiny living things made of one cell. They sometimes cause disease. A digester is a large tank at a sewage treatment plant. Tiny living things in it break down solid waste. A filter is a material full of small holes. Liquid or gas passes through it, and the filter takes out something unwanted. A process is a set of actions in a row. The actions may happen in nature. They may also be steps planned to reach a goal. A septic tank is a container buried underground. Sewage collects inside it. Sewage is human waste. A system of pipes carries it away from buildings. A sewage treatment plant is a place that cleans sewage. The cleaning makes the waste safer for the world around us. A sewer is one pipe, or more than one, that carries off water and sewage. Sewerage systems are the drains and pipes that carry sewage away.

74. Wild Storms and How to Stay Safe

1An introduction opens the book. Weather's building blocks come next. They are temperature and air pressure. They are wind and moisture too. Later come thunderstorms and tornadoes. Then come hurricanes, typhoons, and tropical cyclones. Blizzards and storm safety follow. A glossary ends the book. Weather may be calm and peaceful. It may be fierce and violent. Storms are the fierce kind. Every place on Earth can get a violent storm. Causes come before storm types. A picture shows threatening clouds over a road.

2Sunlight, air, and moisture build weather. Sunshine stands for a good day. The Sun causes bad weather and all weather. It changes temperature and air pressure. It changes wind and moisture. Those are the building blocks. Weather is change in the atmosphere, the air around Earth. Sun energy hits the ground and weather begins. Light may be absorbed or reflected. Absorbed light becomes heat and leaves the ground, as heat leaves a stove, and the air warms. A diagram shows the surface heating the air above.

3Sunlight hits only half of Earth at once. Earth is a giant turning ball, so heat is unequal. The sunny side warms. The dark side loses heat. Dark ground absorbs rays faster and gets hotter. Land heats faster than water. Put black paper and white paper in the Sun. Wait a couple of hours. Compare how each feels before that wait and after it. See which is warmer. A thermometer on each shows the gap. Straighter rays near the equator heat water and land more. Slanted pole rays are absorbed less, so the poles stay cold. Pale snow and ice absorb poorly. The slant leaves the poles cold. It leaves the equator warm.

4Cold air weighs more than warm air. Atmosphere air has weight. Gravity pulls it to Earth, making it push. That push is air pressure. Cold air's harder push is high pressure. It flows toward warmer lower-pressure air and lifts the warm air. Wind may be soft or wild because Earth heats unequally. A warmer air mass has different pressure, so the air moves. Moving air is wind. Wider heat or pressure gaps between two large air masses bring stronger winds along the edges.

5The Sun heats lakes, rivers, and oceans most. Water evaporates into invisible water vapor that shapes weather. In outdoor winter cold, breath makes a tiny cloud the same way real clouds form. Warm moist breath meets cold air and vapor becomes tiny drops. A million cloud droplets make one raindrop. Warm air holds more moisture, and cold air pushes it up. Rising air cools, and vapor is squeezed out into cloud droplets. Gravity pulls large ones down. That fall is precipitation: rain, snow, or ice. Temperature in the clouds and between the clouds and the ground sets the type. Extreme heat, pressure, wind, or moisture brings violent storms.

6Rain needs above-freezing air in the clouds. Air under them may be above freezing or below it. Snow needs below-freezing air in the clouds and under them. Sleet and freezing rain may start as ice, melt, and freeze again near the ground. The lower cloud may be above freezing. The cloud top may be below freezing. Upward winds in the cloud are also needed for hail. About 40,000 thunderstorms hit the world each day, and lightning hits someone daily. A photo shows prairie thunderclouds. These common storms like hot humid days, when warm damp air rises fast into tall clouds and vapor turns to drops quickly.

7Wind lifts big drops high until they freeze. They fall, pick up moisture, and may rise again for more ice. Repeat trips add size until hail falls. A stone may be as big as a baseball or a grapefruit. A photo ruler shows one. Thunder and lightning name the storm. A flash can jump between clouds, down from a cloud, or up from the ground. The hot flash heats the air, which expands fast into a shockwave. That sound is thunder.

8Lightning is hotter than the Sun's outer face. Seconds between flash and thunder give distance. Five seconds means 1.6 kilometers, or 1 mile. Ten seconds means 3.2 kilometers, or 2 miles. No Earth wind beats a tornado. Speed may run from 322 kilometers an hour up to 480, or 200 to 300 mph. Few last past thirty minutes. They can flatten buildings, lift cars and trucks, and pull up trees with the roots. Fast-rising warm air starts them. Cold air rushes in, spins, and makes a funnel cloud. The United States gets about 100,000 thunderstorms and about 1,000 tornadoes a year.

9Waterspouts often look beautiful. A tornado over water is called that. Dust devils are whirlwinds. Hot air whirls up off bare soil and off deserts. They are not tornadoes or waterspouts. Those spin down from clouds. In warm months, huge piles of warm damp air sit over warmer ocean water. Cooler air moves in below the rising warm air. The pile swirls into a huge storm. Names change by place. The map shows typhoon once, hurricane twice, and tropical cyclone twice.

10Hurricanes cause wide damage. Some span hundreds of miles. Three parts make each storm. The central eye is calm. A swirling cloud wall holds violent winds past 322 kilometers an hour, or 200 mph. Rain bands, part three, are thunderclouds that stretch from the wall and drop heavy rain. Wind and floods do the harm. Shore mounds of water can wreck boats and buildings. Heavy rain adds bad floods. A snowflake can be made of 50 or more ice crystals clustered together. The fiercest winter storm is a blizzard, with heavy snow and winds over 56 kilometers an hour, or 35 mph. Seeing only snow is a whiteout. Driving is very dangerous. Several feet can fall fast and drift to 15.2 meters, or 50 feet.

11In a bad blizzard, buildings may fall. Cars and trucks may be buried. Electrical power may fail. Mountain snow can lead to an avalanche, a huge snow wall that breaks free and rolls downhill. Villages have been buried. A photo shows a buried car. Right steps can save your life. For lightning, shelter in a building or a car. Do not pick trees. They draw lightning. With no roof, do not lie flat. A hit spreads over the ground. Keep feet together, crouch, and curl your head low.

12Avoid hilltops and open fields. Get out of the water or off it. Do not carry metal. Do not use the landline. A strike can reach you through the handset. Use a cordless phone to call. A cell phone works too. Skip plugged-in appliances, since a bolt can follow the cord. Avoid sink, tub, and shower pipes. They can take a charge. Tornadoes mean down. Use a basement, or a closet or bathroom. Hide under heavy furniture or in the tub, with arms over head and neck. Leave a mobile home. It may collapse or blow off its base. Find a basement or lie in a ditch, and shield head and neck. Stay out of vehicles. Keep back from windows and doors.

13Before a hurricane, board the windows, clear the yard, get a radio on batteries, and store water. If the storm is coming, go far inland to higher ground. If you must stay, enter a concrete building on high land. Stay indoors, away from doors and windows. Air pressure is the force of air pulled to Earth by gravity. The atmosphere is a layer of gases around a planet. An avalanche is a wall of snow that slides swiftly down a mountain. A blizzard is a severe snowstorm with cold air and heavy drifts. Dust devils whirl air up off the ground. Hail is round ice from thunderstorms. Precipitation is moisture that falls to the ground. Rain, snow, hail, and sleet are forms. Temperature is how hot or cold a thing is. A thermometer measures it. Water vapor is water as a gas. A waterspout is whirling air over water. It may look like a funnel or a tube. The tropics are usual. A whiteout is a severe blizzard. Sight is poor. Everything looks white.

75. Words That Hid a War

1July 1944 finds Cecil Trosip at a radio. He is a Navajo code talker. He works on Saipan. That island sits in the Pacific Ocean. Japan is on one side of it. Hawaii is on the other. February 19, 1945, is when United States troops unload supplies. The place is the island of Iwo Jima. On that same day, Marines reach the beach there. Iwo Jima also lies in the Pacific Ocean. If the enemy learned the plans, everything could be lost. The marines carry a code that is secret. They can pass messages no one else is able to read.

2The United States was already in World War II by 1942. Soldiers in different groups traded messages in code. The goal was to keep them safe and prepared to fight. Those messages kept falling into enemy hands. Breaking the codes was something the enemy did very well. Many American deaths followed in some of those cases. Philip Johnston spoke to the military about a code drawn from Navajo (NA-vuh-hoh). He had spent his childhood near Navajo people. He knew the language was clear to few people beyond the tribe. He knew something else about it. Navajo stayed a hidden language. Speech was its only form. Written symbols did not exist for it. Those points made it fit for messages of the highest secrecy. The military said yes to a trial. A separate 1944 photograph shows Philip Johnston. A map then places the Navajo Nation on the land. Utah is nearby. Nevada and Colorado are nearby too. Las Vegas is marked. Flagstaff is marked. New Mexico, Albuquerque, and Arizona are marked, within the United States. Reservations are home to many Native peoples. A reservation is land the United States government once forced them to occupy. The largest reservation is the Navajo Nation. West Virginia is about the same size. The people name themselves Dine' (dih-NEH). The meaning is The People. An earlier photograph, from 1904, puts Philip Johnston in the center. He was about twelve then. In 1896 he and his family took up life on the Navajo reservation.

3Hidden messages go back to ancient days. In the beginning, only two people knew the trick. One was the sender. The other was the person meant to receive it. Invisible ink was one trick. Once the ink touches paper, it seems to vanish. Heat could make it show again. Chemicals could do that too. Then someone could read the words. Another trick started with a shaved head. Words were tattooed onto the skin. Hair had to grow back and cover the words before the man traveled. He traveled to the one who was meant to receive it. A new shave let the receiver read the words. Modern codes usually leave the message itself in view. What stays secret is the meaning. Enemies may capture the message. Understanding still requires knowledge of the code. Take the line Send help now. Two methods can hide it. Swapping replaces every letter with a different letter. Each letter shifts three places left. A matches X. B matches Y. C matches Z. D matches A, and the same shift continues through the alphabet. The line turns into PBKA EBIM KLT. Scrambling is the other method. Letters are set on two lines, taking every other letter. The first line is S, N, H, L, N, and W. The next line is E, D, E, P, and O. The lines are then joined. The line turns into SNHLNW EDEPO. Early in World War II, American forces replaced their codes again and again. Failure followed every time. Students from Japan had studied American English inside the United States. Code breakers in Japan could open a message nearly as fast as it was sent.

4The Choctaw Telephone Squad appears in a scene from World War I. Secret messages in a Native language were not a fresh idea. During that first war, the United States already used several Native languages this way. Reusing those codes in the second war was unwise. After the first war, Germany sent its students abroad. Japan sent students as well. Their destination was the United States. Those students learned the languages in question. Navajo would have stalled almost any student. Its language family is not the family of many other Native languages. Marine leaders trusted that a Navajo code would be dark to anyone outside the country. A group of twenty-nine Navajo soldiers helped design the system. Messages were coded from their own language. Two parts made up the system. Part one let Navajo words stand in for English letters. Bear is shush in Navajo. The letter B was therefore written as shush. Two soldiers send orders by radio. The Navajo code is what they speak. Some soldiers already knew each other. The same reservation had been their home. In one case the two men were cousins.

5A word list pairs letters with Navajo. For A, the word is wol-la-chee. Ant is the meaning. For B, the word is shush. Bear is the meaning. For C, the word is moasi. Cat is the meaning. For D, the word is be. Deer is the meaning. For E, the word is dzeh. Elk is the meaning. For F, the word is tsa-e-donin-ee. Fly is the meaning. Everyday things had code names too. A battleship was called lo-tso. Whale is what that word means. Bombs were called a-ye-shi. Eggs is what that word means. A commanding officer was called hash-kay-gi-na-tah. War chief is what that means. A dive bomber was called gini. Chicken hawk is what that means. An enemy listener would have been badly confused. Part two came next. It attached military meanings to a set of Navajo words. The set held 211 words. Appearance or job often decided which word was picked. Owl meant a kind of plane. The word for shark was used for a kind of warship. Iron fish was the name used for a submarine. From childhood, Navajo people were trained to hear their people's stories. The new code was easy for these soldiers to learn. A test once measured how quickly they could send a radio message. Amazement was the military response. The same task took other coders half an hour. Seconds were enough for the code talkers. A December 1943 photograph shows Henry Blake Jr. at a radio with George Kirk. Blake is the man on the left. They stand in jungle on an island. The island is northeast of Australia.

6Use of the code started quickly. Training of more code talkers started too. Service appealed to many Navajo men. The Pacific saw more than four hundred of them serve. Their service was brilliant. The Battle of Iwo Jima was among the war's largest fights. Six code talkers worked that battle. They handled more than eight hundred messages. Not a single message held an error. Keith Little was among the six. He called his language his weapon. He said it probably saved lives beyond counting. A crowd hears him speak in 2009. Death came in 2012. He was 87. Sense could not be made of the code by Japan. September 2, 1945, is when Japan gave up. Secrecy around the code continued after that. Talk about the work was not allowed. The quiet lasted for years. Hard lives waited after the war. Work was scarce for many men after they left the army. Discrimination often met Native peoples. Voting stayed closed to Navajo people and to other tribes in Arizona until 1948. New Mexico had the same limit until 1948. Utah's tribes could not vote until 1957. A 1948 scene in New Mexico shows Native peoples signing up to vote. It is their first chance.

7President George W. Bush shakes hands with John Brown Jr. They met in 2001. The event honored Navajo code talkers. Silence around the program finally broke in 1968. After that, the world heard of the amazing work these men had done. The Navajo code is the only spoken military code that has never been broken. A short glossary explains words from this book. To break a code is to solve it or figure it out. A code is a plan of letters, symbols, or signals. Secret messages travel by that plan. Code talkers were soldiers from America. In World War I and World War II they used a native language as their code. Discrimination means treating a person or a group unfairly. The cause may be gender, race, age, religion, or some other difference. A marine belongs to the U.S. Marine Corps. That corps is one branch of the United States military. Navajo can name a person from a Native people of the Southwest. It can also name their language. To serve is to work for a group or for a government. Military branches are a common place for that work. A symbol is a picture or a sign. It can stand for an idea, a letter, or a word. To transmit is to pass a thing from one person across to another.

76. Young Minds, New Ideas

1In 1934, a teenager named George Nissen created the trampoline. What do Popsicles, snowboards, and trampolines share? Kids invented them. An invention can be a new product. It can be a new process. It can be a new service. It might be the only one of its kind. It might improve something older. Many inventors who succeed begin while they are young. Benjamin Franklin was only twelve. He invented swim fins. Margaret Knight was twelve too. She invented a safety device for people in factories. The device kept machines from injuring their fingers. Young Ben made fins for his hands. He made fins for his feet. He wanted swimming to be easier. What else have kids invented? These pages show the power of young makers. One story tells of cold winters in Maine. One story tells of a frozen treat. One story tells of a very early start. One story tells of a way to keep babies cool. One story tells of a fix for broken crayons. One story tells of a new way to say hello. One story tells of putting the sun to work. One story tells of a small light. The last part tells how a person becomes an inventor. Word meanings come at the end.

2Earmuffs today look much like the first pair. That first pair is more than one hundred years old. Chester Greenwood's childhood home was a farm. That farm was in Maine. He worked outdoors in winter. Bitter cold turned his ears red. The cold made his ears hurt. In 1873, Chester was fifteen. He built a wire headpiece. The headpiece covered his ears. He asked his grandmother to sew bear fur onto it. He wore the headpiece. His ears stayed warm. At first, his friends laughed. Soon those friends wanted ear protectors too. A patent for earmuffs came to Chester in 1877. The patent proved the invention was his. No one else could use the idea without permission. Many neighbors worked with Chester. They made earmuffs in their homes. They sold the earmuffs to stores. Popsicles are still a favorite treat. People buy more than two billion of them each year. What tastes better on a hot day than a frosty Popsicle? An eleven-year-old boy in California invented this treat. In 1905, Frank Epperson ran a soda stand. The stand was in a small amusement park. He sold drinks made from soda water. He mixed flavor packets into the soda water. Frank wondered about a frozen drink. He wanted to know how it would taste. On one cold night, he left a glass outside. The glass held flavored soda. A wooden stirring stick stood in the glass. The soda froze. Frank brought his creation to school. His classmates said it tasted good. When Frank was grown, he made the treats to sell. He called them Ep-sicles. His children suggested a different name. They wanted the name Popsicles. People have used that name ever since.

3Gitanjali Rao told her parents about a goal. Her name sounds like gih-TAHN-juh-lee ROW. She wanted to research tests for pollution in water. She was ten years old then. Her parents arranged a tour for her. She toured the facilities at Denver Water. The visit led to another invitation. She could use the lab for research. In time she built a device people could afford. The device tests drinking water for lead. She named the invention Tethys. Tethys was the Greek goddess of fresh water. The device holds a battery.
