It holds hardware for wireless communication. It holds chemical sensors. All of those parts fit in a small box. A 3D printer made the box. The device sends data to an app. The app reports how much lead is in the water. Rao later made an app called Kindly. Kindly helps stop cyberbullying. TIME named her Kid of the Year in 2020. She showed her water-testing device to people when she was eleven. Bishop Curry V grew up in Texas. Heat there can rise to a dangerous level. This fifth-grader heard stories about babies. The babies had died in hot cars. Adults had left them alone. Bishop wanted to help. He thought of a new cover for a baby car seat. If heat puts the baby in danger, the device sends a message. The message goes to the phones of the baby's caregivers. The device then blows cool air over the baby. It keeps blowing until help comes. His invention puts many technologies into one device. His father raised money online. His father then worked with manufacturers. Together they built a prototype. Bishop said his grandfather's death taught him a hard lesson. He learned how it feels to lose someone in the family. He hopes the invention will spare other people that pain.

4Cassidy Goldstein was eleven. A school project was due. She had only broken crayons to use. She needed a holder for the small pieces. Then she could draw with them. The holder had to be the right size. It had to grip the pieces tightly. She remembered roses from a flower shop. Each rose comes with a small tube of water. The water keeps the rose fresh. She used one tube as her first model. A model like that is called a prototype. In 2002, she received a patent for the crayon holder. People named her Youth Inventor of the Year. Her father started a company after that. The company helps other kids build inventions. The company helps them sell the inventions. Ryan Patterson was seventeen. He sat in a restaurant. He was choosing a science project. Some deaf customers came in to order food. The workers did not know sign language. The customers needed an interpreter. Ryan wondered how they would have ordered with no interpreter. His science project gave an answer. His glove reads finger movements. The movements belong to American Sign Language. The glove turns the movements into written English. He called the invention the Sign Language Translator. The glove reads the finger alphabet of that language. Letters from the glove appear on a small screen. A deaf person and a hearing person can then talk through writing. Ryan won several awards. One award was a college scholarship. The scholarship was 103,000 dollars.

5Xóchitl Guadalupe Cruz López grew up in southern Mexico. Her name sounds like SOH-cheel gwah-dah-LOO-peh CROOZ LOH-pez. At age eight, she built a water heater. The heater ran on power from the sun. She built it from materials people had used before. The device uses plastic bottles painted black. It uses black hoses. It uses black nylon. It uses recycled glass. A person can set it on any rooftop. There it catches heat from the Sun. It can make water hot enough for a shower. It can do that even in cold weather. The device could bring hot water to homes. Those homes are in rural parts of Mexico. It could also help other places with no electricity. She got the idea from something she saw. Villagers nearby were cutting down trees. They wanted the wood for fires. Her heater could save trees. It gives people a different way to heat water. She has entered science competitions since she was four. Blair Breazeale could hardly wait for sixth grade. She wanted a school locker of her own. She looked into the locker. It was dull. It was dark. She felt let down. She shopped for decorations. Nothing matched what she wanted. She decided to make a decoration herself. Her parents encouraged her. She created LockerLites. A LockerLite is a magnetic light. The light is an LED. The lights come in colors. A student can stick one anywhere inside the locker. The lights turn on when the door opens. The lights turn off when the door closes. Blair shows a locker dressed with her own inventions.

6A talk with another person can improve an invention. Becoming an inventor may look impossible. It is not impossible. Many inventors began in a simple way. They noticed a problem. They tried to solve it. After an idea comes, a new inventor often talks about it. The inventor may talk with parents. The inventor may talk with friends. The inventor may talk with teachers. Ask for help when you need it. If you cannot do a task, find a person who can. A common next step is to build a model. The inventor might show the model at a school science fair. A bigger gathering may come next. One kind of gathering is a Maker Faire. A Maker Faire is like a large show-and-tell. People of every age can join. Inventors share ideas there. Inventors share things they have made. Braeden Benedict was 14. In 2011, he won a title. The title was America's Top Young Scientist. He invented a sensor that does not cost much. The sensor warns about a football player's head. The player may have a serious injury. The injury can be hard to notice. Students in grades five to eight can enter a yearly contest. One contest uses that same title. First place pays 25,000 dollars. Peyton Robertson won the title at age eleven. His invention was a better sandbag. Sandbags can shield a place from a flood. Heavy sandbags are hard to carry. Setting out enough of them takes a long time. Peyton's sandbag weighs less than a regular one. It grows when it soaks up water. It gives better protection from flooding.

7Building robots is another way young inventors grow their skills. The National Robotics Challenge welcomes students. It is open from elementary school upward. Students in grades four to eight may enter another contest. That contest is the U.S. FIRST LEGO League championships. In these contests, teams build robots. The robots must finish a series of tasks by themselves. The winning team is the one with the most successful robot. These contests teach teamwork. They teach collaboration. They teach creativity. They teach problem solving. Inventing is an awesome activity. Kids use inventions to solve everyday problems. The inventions make life better. The inventions make life easier. You might be a great inventor one day. Students in fifth grade watch a robot run a course. Students in sixth grade watch too. The event was the Iowa FIRST LEGO League Championship in 2010. These words help with the account. An app is a computer program. It may be built for a mobile device such as a cell phone. Creativity is the skill of making something new. A person may design it, invent it, or imagine it. A device is a thing built to do a job. Invented means someone created a thing. The person designed it or built it. The thing had not existed before. A model is a copy of an object. The copy is usually smaller. It is made to look like the real object. A patent is a paper. The paper gives a person the right to earn money from an invention. A prototype is the first form of a thing. Later makers use it as the pattern. To research is to study a question. A person may do it to find new information. A person may do it to find facts. A sensor is a device that notices a physical condition. It may measure that condition. It may react to it. Heat is one condition. Motion is another.
